# ATCS6 Desired Design Model

This document defines the target refactor contracts and changed cross-component behavior for the current ATCS6 codebase.
It is intended to guide an engineer refactoring the existing implementation, not to replace the code as a complete standalone implementation spec.
Unchanged entity-local fields, helper internals, and serialization details continue to follow the current code unless this document states otherwise.
If current code conflicts with a changed contract stated here, this document wins.

## About this document

This is a design document used by AI agents (and human reviewers) to write code for a train control simulator. It describes the v1 system as it should be built, not the history of how it got here. Read with these constraints in mind:

- **`TrainController` mirrors a real-world train computer**, which is a multi-module unit (ATC, ATO, EB, cab interface, mode manager, operational state). Its breadth is intentional domain modeling. Sub-modules are discrete concerns even when adjacent prose mentions several together.
- **Latches model hardware that cannot release work mid-motion.** A latch is a deferred command that persists in controller-owned state until a gating predicate (typically `is_stopped()`) lets it commit. A flag is not a latch. Adding a new latch requires identifying the gating predicate first.
- **The runtime is single-threaded.** All API, UI, and admin requests run between ticks on the same thread as the per-tick phase loop. Every "always recompute live" rule in this document depends on this; breaking the threading model invalidates several core design choices.
- **v1 means v1.** Anything not in v1 must not appear in this document, even as "removed," "legacy," or "deprecated" prose. The absence of a row in a schema table is itself the statement that the thing does not exist. Use git for design history.
- **Performance has been validated for the v1 scope.** Compute-live patterns (`lookahead`, `received_path_plan` projection, derived properties) are intentional, not performance bugs to optimize.
- **Tests are out of scope for this document.** It defines contracts that code generation must satisfy. Test plans live elsewhere.
- **The doc iterates fast.** When a design changes, every cross-reference is swept in the same edit. Readers never see a partial update; if you find one, that is a bug, not history.

## Authoring Rules

These rules govern every edit to this document. Reviewers must reject any patch that violates them.

1. **This document is v1. There is no "removed in v1," "legacy," "deprecated," or "no longer used" prose anywhere in this file.** If a field, method, state, or flag is not part of v1 it must not appear here at all. The absence of a row in a schema table is itself the statement that the thing does not exist. Any patch that introduces tombstone language ("removed in v1," "no longer used," "kept for compatibility," "deprecated," etc.) is incomplete and must be rejected.

2. **No cross-reference drift.** When any rule changes — ownership, payload field, state transition, callback signature, latch consumer, recovery gate — every site in the document that references the old rule must be swept and updated in the same edit. A patch that updates only one section is incomplete. Reviewers must reject any patch that leaves divergent restatements of the same rule anywhere in the document.

3. **One canonical home per concept.** Each shared schema, latch, predicate, and helper has exactly one canonical definition site. All other sites reference it by name. If two sites describe the same concept differently, the patch is incomplete.

4. **Latches are described in the canonical Latch Table.** Every latch in the document must appear in the Latch Table (in the Core Runtime Loop section) with: writer phase + entity, consumer phase + entity, the "consumed by the last entity that uses it in the same 3-phase period" rule, and any multi-tick survival conditions. Latch behavior described elsewhere must cross-reference the table, not restate its rules.

5. **Phase boundaries are described in the canonical Phase Table.** The Phase Table (in the Core Runtime Loop section) is the only place that defines which entity acts in which phase. All other sections cross-reference it by phase name and entity.

6. **No single-use-case branches in shared contracts.** If a workflow step is written as a per-case branch, it must either be expressed as a general mechanism or explicitly justified as the only case the general mechanism cannot cover.

7. **Diagnostic-only distinctions belong on events, not enums.** Distinctions that have no functional consumer must be expressed through `NotificationCenter` event payload, not by adding enum values to control-flow types. Enum values that are written by structurally different code paths (and could plausibly become functional) may stay; enum values that exist purely so the UI can render a different label must be moved to event payload.

## Scope

Primary files reviewed:

- `simulation.py`
- `train.py`
- `train_controller/` (package: `__init__.py` façade plus `atc.py`, `ato.py`, `commands.py`, `eb.py`, `manual.py`, `notifications.py`, `ops.py`, `path_projection.py`, `physics_sync.py`, `route_hooks.py`)
- `train_physics.py`
- `railway_platform.py`
- `station.py`
- `block.py`
- `block_manager.py`
- `path.py`
- `path_dispatcher.py`
- `route_executor.py`
- `train_route.py`
- `track.py`
- `switch.py`
- `network.py`
- `speed_profile.py`
- `block_power.py`

Terminology:

- `path`: a dispatcher-managed reserved movement authority between blocks.
- `route`: a higher-level travel plan made of `stop` / `waypoint` / `dwell` tasks that ultimately resolve to concrete block targets.
- `route run`: the per-train runtime instance of an assigned route. It references one shared `Route` definition but owns that train's mutable execution state: waypoint cursors, the current resolved target status, dwell timing, and active-path correlation. Current-code implementation may still keep the class name `RouteExecutionState`, but the canonical concept is `RouteRun`.
- `block`: an occupancy and reservation unit.
- `track tile` / `TrackSegment`: one physical rail tile on the grid, identified by its `(x, y)` coordinate and local 6-bit geometry. Despite its name, a `TrackSegment` is not a collection of tiles and is not a block; a `Block` owns an ordered collection of these tile IDs.
- `platform`: the concrete physical stopping place on the track. Dispatcher targets are always expressed against a concrete block traversal frame, never against a logical station group; when a waypoint resolves through station/platform data, the platform supplies the linked blocks and traversal context used to derive that block target.
- `station`: a logical grouping of one or more platforms used only for route-level stop selection. When a route targets a station-level stop, `RouteExecutor` must first resolve one concrete platform for that attempt and then derive the dispatcher request contract from that resolved platform.

## Out of Scope

This section lists features and concerns that v1 deliberately does not include. Its purpose is to make absences explicit so that readers (AI agents and humans) can confirm an absence is a decision rather than an oversight.

A separate Out of Scope entry is not a wishlist and not a promise to implement later. It records that the question was raised and answered "no" for v1. Three discipline rules govern what goes here:

1. **Out of Scope is an explicit "no," not a brainstorm.** An entry only goes here when a designer has actively decided not to include a concern for v1. If the question has not been raised, do not speculate.
2. **Out of Scope is not a graveyard.** Concerns that were once in scope and got removed do not belong here; they go nowhere, per the "removed is removed" rule in the Authoring Rules. Only never-was concerns belong.
3. **Out of Scope does not promise v2.** Listing something here is not a roadmap. v2 will have its own scope conversation.

| Concern | v1 stance | Why Out of Scope for v1 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-threaded runtime | Single thread; all API/UI/admin requests run between ticks on the same thread as the per-tick phase loop | The "always recompute live" projections (`lookahead`, `received_path_plan`, derived properties) depend on between-tick mutation only. Adding threads invalidates the read-consistency contract. |
| Lookahead caching / projection caching | Recompute live on every read | Performance is acceptable for v1 train counts. Caching adds invalidation surface that is not needed. |
| Persistence / save-load of runtime state | In-memory only | No use case in v1. |
| Schema migration, backward-compat shims, dual-write transitional fields | None | v1 is the first design; "v1 means v1." |
| Hardware fault dynamics — signal aspect failures, EB fails to engage, and stuck-switch recovery (auto-retry, re-throw, or rolling `Switch.position` back after a failed throw) | Not modeled; happy hardware assumed for these | Fault simulation is its own design problem. Switch throw *confirmation* is in scope and owned by **Power**, Switch power-controller role; the safe state on loss of control is also in scope, owned by **Power**, Hardware interface boundary. |
| Hardware sensors as a fault-detection, verification, or recovery source | Block current detection drives block occupancy (in scope); using any sensor to detect a fault, verify a commanded action, or trigger recovery is out | Block-occupancy authority is the block current detector per **Power**, Block occupancy detection. Switch-alignment authority is the commanded `Switch.position`, and switch position feedback is diagnostic only. Acting on a sensor *disagreement* — fault detection, verification, recovery — is fault dynamics, held out by the row above. |
| Collision modeling | Two trains in one block is a fatal runtime failure via `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`, not a physics interaction | Physics does not model contact. |
| Reverse motion under cab | Forbidden; wrong-direction cab forces the distance-0 ATC limit to `0` | Physics never produces backward motion. |
| Authorization / RBAC / multi-user model | All API requests trusted | No user accounts in v1. |
| Logging beyond `NotificationCenter` | `NotificationCenter` is the only runtime sink | Component-local logging is forbidden by design. |
| Idempotency keys, retry/dedup wrappers on API commands | None | Single-threaded between-tick drain makes them unnecessary. |
| Health checks, metrics export, distributed tracing | None | Operators read `NotificationCenter` and live projections. |
| `Path` / `RouteRun` / notification retention caps | Unbounded for v1 | Retention policy will be added when leak becomes measurable. |
| Per-train safety margin tuning | Two global constants (`TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`, `PLATFORM_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`) | Per-train tuning is a future calibration concern. |
| Per-segment speed limits beyond `Block.speed_limit_kmh` | Block-scoped only | Sub-block speed restrictions are not modeled. |
| Live editor mutation during simulation | Forbidden; editor and runtime are binary modes per session | Geometry stability is required by every "always recompute live" rule. |
| Multi-network in one process | One network per `SimulationController` instance | Cross-network interaction has no use case in v1. |
| Replay / time-travel debugging | None | Use `NotificationCenter` monotonic ordering for after-the-fact reconstruction. |
| Schedule adherence / ETA / SLA tracking | None | The schedule-adherence layer itself (expected-vs-actual comparison, `delay_class` reporting, ETA/SLA dashboards) is out of scope. The underlying `RouteTaskRun.started_at` / `finished_at` timestamps are NOT informational-only — they are the route-progress cursor inputs, written and read per the **RouteTaskRun schema** and **Route-run state** contracts. Only the adherence/ETA/SLA layer built on top of them is out of scope. |
| Pluggable physics or speed-profile backends | None | `SpeedProfile` is the single calibration model. |
| Route-level runtime progress summary | None; `RouteRun` is the only runtime progress store | No route-level summary shape has a v1 consumer; UI and API read per-train `RouteRun` progress. |
| Configuration files, env vars, runtime tunable injection | None | Globals are constants in code. |
| Async/streaming notification protocols (websocket, SSE, push) | None | Frontend reads `NotificationCenter` via polling/projection. |
| Cross-tick latch durability across process restart | None | Process state is volatile. |
| Watchdogs / alert notifications for stuck trains, no-progress | None | Operators read live diagnostics from `lookahead.speed_curves[0].atc_limit_reason_code` and `RouteRun.last_error`. (The hardware output fail-safe watchdog that de-energizes track and point motors on loss of control is a different mechanism and is specified in **Power**, Hardware interface boundary.) |
| Train removal contract | Not specified in v1; do not implement removal | Surface, teardown order, and pending-latch handling are not specified for v1. |
| Bulk operations (multi-train commands, batch route assignment) | None | Operators issue per-train commands one at a time. |

## Glossary

This section defines vocabulary that is overloaded across the document — terms that mean similar but distinct things in different layers, or that appear in prose without a stored field backing them. Defining each term once here lets every other site cross-reference by name rather than restating.

Three discipline rules govern this glossary:

1. **If a word appears in prose but not in any schema, it goes in the glossary or it does not appear in prose.** No informal nouns.
2. **If two words mean the same thing across layers, pick one as canonical and note the other as a synonym.** Do not let two words drift apart over time.
3. **If a word means different things in different contexts, qualify it every time it appears.** Never bare `forward` — always "block-relative forward" or "train-relative forward."

**Group A — Action verbs.** Each row names the entity acted on, who initiates the action, and the resulting state.

| Verb | Entity / initiator / resulting state |
| --- | --- |
| `cancel` | `Path` (`status: ACTIVE -> CANCELLED`); initiated by `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. Writers and gating per the **Latch table** (`c832`) and the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**. |
| `complete` | `Path` (`status: ACTIVE -> COMPLETED`); initiated by `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` per the **Active-path completion procedure** |
| `finish` | `RouteRun` (`current_route_status.state -> FINISHED`); initiated by `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` per the terminal route completion procedure (`879a`) |
| `deactivate` | `RouteRun` (`current_route_status.state -> DEACTIVATED`); initiated by `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` from the Phase-3 abort/power-off/deactivate consumers |
| `abort` | `RouteRun`; operator-driven teardown via the `pending_route_abort` latch per the **Latch table** (`bc5d`) |
| `revoke` | `Path`; system-driven cancel via `pending_path_cancel_request(revoked)` from route reconciliation or admin route deactivation |
| `deviate` | `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` (derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"`); writer and consumer per the **Latch table** (`75cb`) |
| `depart` | `TrainController.ops.state -> RUNNING_MOVING`; initiated by `TrainController.depart()` called by `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` when live authority exists |
| `dwell` | `TrainController.ops.state -> RUNNING_AT_STATION`; initiated by `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` called by `RouteExecutor` Phase-1 dwell-entry step |
| `throw` | `Switch` (moves the point motor to a new alignment and writes the commanded `Switch.position`); owned by `SwitchManager` per the **Switch entity** mutation workflow and **Power**, Switch power-controller role |
| `skip` | `RouteRun` (`task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` set, the route-progress cursor advances past a broken task); initiated by Phase 1's broken-task handling. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `stall` | `RouteRun` (`no_reachable_task = True`, derived `display_state == "STALLED"`, the train holds idle); initiated by Phase 1 when every task on a `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` walk is broken. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |

**Group B — Geometry terms.** Each row names the frame of reference. Always qualify the term in prose.

| Term | Frame of reference |
| --- | --- |
| `forward` | Block-relative (matches `Block.allowed_travel_directions` and `Block.ordered_tiles` order) OR train-relative (the cab end the train is heading toward). Always qualify which. |
| `reverse` | Block-relative or train-relative. Always qualify which. |
| `head` | Train-relative; the physical end of the current span on the authority side, i.e. closer to `authority_end_block_id`. |
| `tail` | Train-relative; the physical end of the current span farther from `authority_end_block_id`. |
| `cab` | Operator-selected control end of the train; `A` or `B` or `None`. The cab is on the head side when the train is moving forward in the authority frame. |
| `frame` | Generic term for a directed coordinate context; always qualify with one of: block-relative, traversal, terminal, or travel. |
| `traversal direction` | Block-relative; `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for one block as walked by a path. |
| `travel direction` | Network-relative; the train's current motion direction in the network frame, derived from cab and span. |
| `terminal frame` | The directed `path_tiles` / `entry_edge` / `exit_edge` of the terminal block in a `Path.resource_sequence`; used for braking and for completion direction match. |

**Group C — Aggregate nouns.** Each row names the type, or marks the term as informal-prose-only with the canonical concrete artifact.

| Noun | Type / canonical schema |
| --- | --- |
| `path` | Stored entity (`Path` record); canonical schema in **Path field schema**. |
| `authority` | Informal prose for "the cleared corridor ahead of the train"; not a stored field. Use `Path` or `received_path_plan` when the concrete artifact is needed. |
| `plan` | Informal prose synonym for `received_path_plan`; canonical schema in **received_path_plan schema**. |
| `run` | `RouteRun` runtime instance; canonical schema in the RouteRun section. |
| `walk` | The directed task index sequence of a `RouteRun` (the ordered list of `task_runs[i]` indexes); not a stored field. |
| `task` | `RouteTask` authored entry (with `task_kind`); runtime instance is `RouteTaskRun`. |
| `start mode` | Which stop a fresh `RouteRun` begins at (`FIRST` / `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`); route-level default `Route.start_mode` plus a per-assignment one-shot input. Canonical home **Run start-mode seeding**. |
| `broken task` | Informal prose for a `RouteTask` whose `task_location` ID is absent from the network (resolves with `failure_kind == missing_target`); it is skipped, never waited on. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `unreachable task` | Informal prose for a `RouteTask` whose target is present but cannot be served this tick (`failure_kind == target_unavailable`, or a live `PathAcquisitionFailureKind`); it is waited on, not skipped. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `valid task` | Informal prose for a `RouteTask` whose `task_location` ID is present in the network, whether or not it is currently available — the complement of a broken task. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |

**Group D — Electrical / switch-hardware terms.** These disambiguate the two voltages and the two notions of switch alignment.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `track voltage` | The DC voltage applied to a block to drive the train on it. One value per train is applied to every block that train holds — the blocks it occupies and its reserved-ahead corridor — so a boundary crossing finds the next block already energized rather than dead (see **Power**, Block power-controller role). Canonical home `TrainPhysics` (see **TrainPhysics**); emitted to the block outputs by `BlockPowerController` (see **Power**). |
| `throw voltage` | The DC drive applied to a switch point motor to move it; its polarity selects the target alignment. Emitted by `SwitchPowerController` (see **Power**). A separate output from `track voltage` — the two are different wires and are never compared to each other. |
| `electrical continuity` / `node` | Two blocks are electrically continuous (share a `node`, and therefore a voltage) when the switches between them are aligned to bond them. Consumed by the switch throw continuity check (see **Power**) to confirm a switch reached its commanded `Switch.position`. |
| `physical channel` | The pin, shift-register bit, or expander port a logical `block_id` or `switch_id` maps to on a given hardware build. Owned only inside a concrete `OutputInterface` / `SwitchOutputInterface` / `InputInterface` implementation (see **Power**, Hardware interface boundary); no other layer names it. |
| `safe state` | All block `track voltage` at `0` and every switch point motor de-energized. Commanded by `enter_safe_state()` on the output interfaces, and reached by a real-hardware watchdog on loss of control (see **Power**, Hardware interface boundary). |

## Design Direction

**Ownership split**

- `TrainController` owns live operational state: ATC/ATO/manual mode, handle position, future manual `power_percent`-mode inputs, and the canonical live lookahead data series. "Target speed" and "speed-limit reason" MUST NOT be stored as standalone fields anywhere; they are the value and reason metadata at position 0 of the lookahead series.
- `TrainController` also owns live power/cab state: the canonical `state` machine (which includes `OFF` for power off), active cab selection, and any live direction-selection inputs tied to that active cab. The convenience read `power_on` is derived from `state != "OFF"`. After the runtime 1:1 `Train` <-> `TrainController` pairing is established, those values are not re-synced from `Train` on every tick.
- `Train` owns persistent/configuration state only: identity, consist, route assignment, and non-live train configuration. Live position/span, live speed, ATO/ATC/path-runtime state, power/cab state, and operational state are not mirrored on `Train`. Initial live placement comes through `SimulationController.add_train(...)`: it constructs the `Train`, creates or obtains the `TrainController`, derives the initial span from the placement payload, and writes the initial `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before the first simulation tick. The frontend/API input shape (`id`, optional `position: {x, y}`, `direction`, `max_speed`, `length`, and other train-configuration fields) is unchanged. Bootstrap data must enter through `add_train(...)` or explicit controller commands.
- The new manual model uses handle plus power-percentage; there is no `manual_target_speed` field.
- Manual control has two controller-owned submodes:
  - `simulation`: user drives with handle notches; the handle command flows to `TrainPhysics`, which integrates it into speed change over time.
  - `power_percent`: user drives with percentage power; the controller passes raw percentage power to `TrainPhysics`, which immediately converts it via train/profile calibration data: `track_voltage` through the inverse of `SpeedProfile.voltage_power_curve` and the calibrated current speed through `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve`, when no braking override is active.
- `TrainPhysics` and `BlockPowerController` MUST NOT decide manual-mode policy. They execute whichever command the controller produces and perform command-to-speed conversion and voltage emission.
- Percentage-power mapping data lives in `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` as part of train calibration and is read by physics.

## Layered Architecture

**Layered responsibilities**

- `PathDispatcher` executes the route plan by acquiring future blocks, locking future switches, and managing movement authority. It plans for the controller by providing the dispatcher-owned authority geometry and current authority-end block; the controller projects the actual braking target from that boundary using its safety-margin policy.
- `PathDispatcher` is the sole owner of dispatcher path records. `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` is a read-only property that returns the dispatcher's current movement-authority projection for that train; it is not controller-owned state, has no setter, and does not store its own copy.
- `PathDispatcher` keeps each path record keyed by `path_id` in a dispatcher-owned registry such as `paths_by_id[path_id]`.
- `PathDispatcher` exposes a derived read `active_path_id_by_train(train_id) -> Optional[str]` that scans `paths_by_id` for the one path whose `train_id` matches and `status == ACTIVE`. There is no separately stored registry to maintain; the index is a projection of `Path.status`. Each train has at most one `ACTIVE` path at a time, enforced by `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` rejecting same-train requests when one already exists.
- Cancelled or completed paths may remain in `paths_by_id[path_id]` for history; they are not active authority (their `Path.status` is no longer `ACTIVE`, so `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` skips them and the controller's `received_path_plan` property returns `None` or the next active path). In v1 there is no path-owned deferred-release flag. `PathDispatcher` owns only future authority resources: blocks and switches the train has not yet entered. When the train head enters a resource, that future reservation/lock is consumed and physical occupancy becomes resource-local physical state through dispatcher-mediated writes. Cancellation and completion release only not-yet-entered dispatcher-owned reservations and locks and transition `Path.status` synchronously; the active-path read switches to `None` (or to a different `ACTIVE` path) by definition because of that status transition. Physical train motion after completion/cancellation is not blocked by retained path rows: `TrainPhysics` keeps moving the train according to live network geometry/state, and physical `occupied_by` state is cleared only by later tail-vacate release events (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches). `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` rejects a same-train request while that train already has an `ACTIVE` path — the rejection rule, its no-side-effect guarantee, and its `failure_kind` are owned by step 1 of the **`create_path()` flow** and the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**. Once the old path is no longer `ACTIVE`, later creation is governed only by ordinary live resource conflicts.
- Controller progress reports identify the moving train by `train_id` only; they do not carry a caller-supplied `path_id`. `TrainController` owns the canonical stored span and speed. `TrainPhysics.tick()` computes the next physical span/speed plus the tile-level delta for that movement, then calls `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` once per crossed resource boundary and once more for the final no-boundary callback for that tick. `span_update` carries the new `TrainSpan`, the new `current_speed_kmh`, ordered `added_tiles`, ordered `removed_tiles`, and the traversal frame used for each added/removed tile. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` atomically applies the new span/speed to controller-owned storage, runs the **Reactive speed-based state evaluation** synchronously (so any READY↔RUNNING_MOVING transition fires in the same tick that physics produced the speed change), interprets the tile delta against `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan.resource_sequence` when authority exists plus canonical lookahead, and appends one ordered `ControllerProgressReport` to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). Phase 3 is the only place that drains that mailbox, calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each report and then clearing it. The mailbox is a within-tick transient, not retained across ticks, and is empty before the next per-train transaction begins. `PathDispatcher` resolves any active path from `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`, decides whether reported resources belong to that path, and then commits active progress. After authority has been cleared, later physical release must not depend on a retained path row surviving: block release is driven by `block_id` plus `direction` through `BlockManager`, and switch release is driven by the vacated switch resource's train-owned physical occupancy/protection state.
- `TrainController` executes the path plan by calculating ATC safety limits, ATO/manual commands, and an active handle or future power command. It plans for physics by producing the active control command for the current mode.
- `TrainPhysics` is an executor only; it MUST NOT decide manual-mode policy.
- `PathDispatcher` updates the `Path` record in its own registry whenever a path is created or extended. The controller's `received_path_plan` property reads a movement-authority projection of that record live, so the controller automatically observes any update on the next read. The controller brakes to the current authority boundary using its canonical safety margin and derives speed restrictions from lookahead over the network and dispatcher authority structure, not from per-block speed limits supplied as separate data. The requested target (`requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`) is stored on the dispatcher-owned `Path` record and is not included in the controller projection. Dispatcher completion enforces the requested-target rule internally: the active path completes only when the live authority end block equals `Path.requested_destination_block_id` and the terminal authority traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`. The controller reads only the canonical **received_path_plan schema**; only stop-completion in `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` branches on the requested-target fields.
- The dispatcher-owned path record is the single source of truth for controller authority while it is active. Cancelling or replacing a path in `PathDispatcher` is sufficient to give the controller a clean slate.
- External requests (HTTP/API/UI/admin) enter through thin route handlers (`routes/`) that delegate to a service layer (`services/`), which is the home for the request-side logic that translates an external request into the domain operations above — enqueuing controller commands, writing the controller-owned Phase-3 handoff latches, or calling the `Route`-assignment entry points (`Route.assign_train(...)` / `Route.unassign_train(...)`). Path create/extend are owned exclusively by `RouteExecutor` in Phase 1 and are never called from a request handler/service. The service layer is where the "request handlers must not mutate dispatcher/route/resource state inline" rule (see **Operator-command processing slot** and the **Procedure / phase ownership table**) is enforced: services issue commands and set latches, and let the per-train phase loop commit the actual dispatcher/route/resource mutations. The service layer carries no runtime state of its own; it is a stateless adapter between the transport (route handlers) and the domain entities. Services raise typed errors (not-found / validation / conflict) that route handlers map to HTTP status codes; bad input surfaces as a validation error rather than an unhandled exception.

## Core Runtime Loop

**Phase structure**

### Per-train tick transaction

1. Advance `simulation_time`.
2. `RouteExecutor` owns the runnable-train ordering for the tick. `SimulationController.update(...)` advances time, calls `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` to refresh route-managed scheduling state, then passes the candidate train IDs to `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(...)` and consumes the ordered list it returns. Route-managed trains are ordered first by run phase, then by descending effective priority within each phase tier: a `RouteRun` already executing its route (`RouteRun.is_starting == False`) is ordered ahead of one still completing its first task (`RouteRun.is_starting == True`), and within each tier higher effective priority comes first (priority values are integers where `9` is highest, `0` is lowest, and default priority is `5`). This is the canonical runnable-train ordering rule; every other site that mentions the ordering cross-references it rather than restating it. Trains with no current route-managed Phase 1 work, including one-off/manual trains, participate in the same ordering as running-tier (not-starting) runs at effective priority `5`; a train that is powered off at the start of its transaction uses the same phase tier and effective priority it would use when powered but skips Phase 1 because route planning cannot run before the command drain has had a chance to accept `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()`. Ties use stable existing iteration order. Ordering established runs ahead of starting runs keeps trains already moving on their route flowing through shared track and lets a fresh departure take the gaps in that movement rather than making a running train wait behind a not-yet-moving one. `SimulationController` still owns executing each train transaction in that returned order. During each train transaction, `SimulationController.update(...)` calls `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` as the entire Phase 1 route-executor tick for that train before it calls `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)`.
3. For each train in that order, run one full three-phase transaction to completion before starting the next train:
  - **Phase 1 - Setup (top-down planning for that train)**: `RouteExecutor` runs the canonical Planning Kernel steps `9c4f`–`66aa`; the step chain, its ordering, and its gating rules are defined once in the **Planning Kernel** section, and the **Phase Table** lists the same chain by step ID. After successful path creation, authority growth, or a successful `blocked_no_growth` target refresh, `PathDispatcher` has updated its own `Path` registry; the controller's `received_path_plan` property automatically returns that updated record on its next read — there is no separate controller write step. Phase 1 may retry or loop locally in the same tick only while it stays inside Phase-1-owned procedures such as covered-task index advance, target resolution, candidate selection, and `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` acquisition-and-rollback. If live-path target re-resolution or a route/controller invariant check concludes that the current live path must be replaced, Phase 1 must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly and must not keep a hidden desired-target override attached to the still-live path: it latches `pending_path_cancel_request` for Phase 3, stops planning for that train for that tick, and waits for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel → callback → route deviation cleanup flow. Operator retargeting and explicit route swap go through "abort current run + create new run" instead (see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**), driven by `pending_route_abort` plus its inclusive cancel. After authority cancellation has committed and the train reaches the stopped state, the next eligible Phase 1 replans from current physical progress and requests a brand-new path; eligibility for this fresh create/replan branch uses the canonical Phase-1 eligibility predicate defined in the **Planning Kernel** section.
  - **Phase 2 - Execute (controller + physics for that train)**: `TrainController.tick(...)` begins by draining that controller's queued operator commands. That command-drain substep runs after Phase 1 has completed for the train and before any power check, ATC/ATO/manual decision, or physics movement. The controller reads `lookahead` live whenever it needs it (each read recomputes from current inputs); there is no separate "pre-physics consume" or "post-physics refresh" step. The command-drain substep may set `pending_power_off`, write `pending_path_cancel_request`, and immediately clear `ato.running` for accepted stopped power/cab/mode/EB commands, but it does not commit path completion or dispatcher cancellation directly. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` then computes movement for that same train and must call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` at least once for that tick. Phase 2 must not call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, must not read `RouteRun` task-walk state directly, and must not write any `RouteRun` field. Dwell entry, dwell exit, route-deviation cleanup, and all `RouteTaskRun` timing writes are owned by `RouteExecutor` in Phase 1 and Phase 3; the controller transitions to `RUNNING_AT_STATION` only when `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` from Phase 1, and to the `READY` state only when `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` from Phase 1.
  - **Phase 3 - Feedback and cleanup for that train**: `SimulationController` runs one general feedback/cleanup pass for that train. The canonical step ordering (a)–(f) is defined in the **Phase ownership in v1 is strict** bullet immediately below; this prose cross-references that list rather than restating it. Notes specific to Phase 3 behavior: each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` call consumes at most one task per call as defined in the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**. If a local progress report cancels the active path, that movement-derived cancellation wins immediately, inline during the mailbox-drain step (`3e9b`). Phase 3 owns `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)`, and `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. Dwell entry, dwell-complete timing, and route-deviation cleanup are owned by `RouteExecutor` in Phase 1 (see the Phase 1 description above); Phase 3 does not run a dwell-complete check.
4. After all trains have completed their per-train transactions, return every block whose holder cleared this tick and is still unheld (no `occupied_by` and no `reserved_for_train`) to `0` `track_voltage` — `SimulationController` drains the blocks `BlockManager` queued as their holders cleared (see **Power**, Block power-controller role) — then update signals from blocks once for the tick.

- The three phases are therefore interleaved per train, not as three world-wide passes. One train's Phase 1/2/3 transaction fully commits before the next train's Phase 1 begins.
- Phase ownership in v1 is strict:
  - Phase 1 owns downward resolution, the planning-side `RouteRun` mutation, and same-phase retries. `RouteExecutor` is the single owner of all `RouteRun` mutation; the only `RouteRun` writes it makes outside Phase 1 are the `task_runs[i].finished_at` stamps in its Phase-3 head-advance and stop-completion callbacks, per the Phase 3 bullet below. The owned steps are the **Planning Kernel** steps `9c4f`–`66aa` plus route reconciliation and ordinary departure decisions that do not require cross-phase cleanup. Every Phase 1 step that needs the resolved next target must invoke `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh after any cursor-changing step (deviation cleanup, dwell-complete, covered-task advance) — the helper's last result is not reused across cursor changes.
  - Phase 2 owns controller-side observation and execution for that same train: the operator-command drain at the start of `TrainController.tick(...)`, ATC/manual/ATO command generation (each consumer reads `lookahead` live as needed), physics integration, and the controller-owned span/speed update triggered by `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)`. The **operator-command processing slot** is the first substep of `TrainController.tick(...)`; it is not a separate `SimulationController` drain method. Phase 2 does not call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, does not read `RouteRun` task-walk state directly, does not write any `RouteRun` field, and does not run any dwell-entry, dwell-complete, or route-deviation step.
  - Phase 3 owns commit/cleanup of dispatcher and physical state, in this canonical order: (a) (`3e9b`) same-transaction progress-report consumption by draining and clearing the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), including all movement-derived deviation handling, physical entry/tail-vacate writes, callback-by-callback route-progress checks against the current task (each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` for the consumed task); (b) (`c6f1`) normal stop-arrival/completion evaluation (`PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` callback writes `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` for the just-served stop); (c) (`7b40`) explicit `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption (gated by `TrainController.is_stopped() == True` per the Latch table; latch survives across ticks while the train is rolling); (d) (`5d2c`) explicit `pending_route_abort` consumption (gated by `pending_path_cancel_request is None` per the Latch table — i.e. the inclusive cancel has either committed earlier in this same Phase 3 pass or was never written because no live path existed at write time); (e) (`9a81`) explicit `pending_power_off` consumption (gated by `pending_path_cancel_request is None` per the Latch table); (f) (`4f6e`) `pending_route_deactivate` consumption (gated by `pending_path_cancel_request is None` per the Latch table). Terminal route completion is not on this list: when Phase 1 detects the terminal walk, it calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside `tick_train_phase1(...)` rather than handing off through Phase 3. Latches written *during* this Phase 3 pass (e.g. by the cancellation callback in step c) are not consumed in the same pass; they wait for the next tick's Phase 1 (route-deviation cleanup) or next tick's Phase 3 (dispatcher cleanup). Phase 3 must not arm a fresh planning target, create fresh authority, run dwell entry/exit, or perform route-deviation cleanup; those wait for the next eligible Phase 1 for that train.

**Operator-command processing slot**

- Operator commands targeted at a train (`TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()`, `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_mode(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_manual_mode_type(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_handle(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent(...)`, `TrainController.eb.activate()`, `TrainController.eb.release()`, `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`) are queued onto the targeted train's `TrainController` as they arrive from the API/UI layer. They are drained by `TrainController.tick(...)` as its first substep for that train, before any controller decision. For powered-on trains this substep runs after Phase 1 has completed planning; for powered-off trains Phase 1 is skipped and this Phase-2 tick entry still runs so `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` can be accepted. Operator intent is therefore reflected in the same tick's controller commands and physics step when a train is powered, while route planning after power-on waits for a later Phase 1 tick.
- Within that drain slot, queued commands run in the canonical order below so lower-urgency configuration is observed by higher-urgency commands on the same tick:
  1. `power_on_cmd()`
  2. `set_mode(...)`
  3. `set_cab(...)`
  4. `set_manual_mode_type(...)`
  5. `set_handle(...)` / `set_manual_power_percent(...)`
  6. `eb.activate()`
  7. `eb.release()`
  8. `power_off_cmd()`

  `set_mode(...)` (slot 2) always runs before `set_cab(...)` (slot 3) and `set_manual_mode_type(...)` (slot 4) because both require `control_mode == "manual"` to accept (operator cab selection is manual-only in v1, and `set_manual_mode_type(...)` needs manual mode), so a same-tick "switch to manual then pick a cab" batch is accepted in one drain. `set_cab(...)` (slot 3) runs before `set_handle(...)` (slot 5) because the latter requires a selected cab.
- Each command's stopped-only / power-on / EB-not-latched / cab-selected preconditions are re-checked at the moment it is drained, against the controller state established by earlier commands in the same drain. A command that fails its preconditions is rejected (`success=False`) and does not block later commands in the same queue.
- Commands that write Phase-3 latches (`pending_path_cancel_request` from operator-issued and ATC-derived EB paths, `pending_route_abort` from explicit route-abort requests, `pending_power_off` from `power_off_cmd()`) write those latches during this tick-entry command drain or, for ATC-derived EB activation, from later inside the same `TrainController.tick()` call; the same train's later Phase 3 consumes them in the canonical Phase-3 latch-consumption order. External API/UI/admin operations must honor this same internal workflow: they enqueue controller commands or set the controller-owned Phase-3 handoff payloads and then let the per-train phase loop commit dispatcher/route/resource mutations. They must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clear controller authority, remove route runs, or release resources inline from the request handler. `TrainController.eb.activate()` is the canonical operator-issued EB command; ATC overspeed escalation — including the no-authority overspeed branch in step `826a` where the canonical lookahead has dropped to `atc_limit = 0` because `received_path_plan` is `None` — can also raise EB from inside `TrainController.tick()`. Both paths perform the **canonical EB activation effects** defined in the **EB workflow state machine** so Phase 3 commits any resulting cancellation through the same dispatcher cancel-and-release path used by every other cancellation kind.
- API/UI command submission has a separate result from command execution. A request handler returns success once the command has been appended to the targeted controller's command queue, or once a direct Phase-3 handoff such as `pending_path_cancel_request` has been written; it does not block waiting for the later tick-entry drain. The real command result is produced when `TrainController.tick(...)` drains the command: each drained command emits one `command_result` notification through the runtime `NotificationCenter` (`command_id`, command name, `train_id`, `success`, optional `reason`, and `event_time`) for UI/API consumers. If a command fails its stopped-only, power, EB, cab, or mode preconditions at drain time, that later result is `success=False` with the diagnostic reason; the original enqueue response remains an accepted/pending submission. `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` may be accepted with EB latched when the stopped-only shutdown precondition is satisfied; EB release is not a prerequisite for shutdown.
- `TrainController.eb.release()` is also latch-based. It may clear `eb.active` only after the EB-triggered path cancellation has already committed and live authority has been cleared. The acceptance check reads its own `received_path_plan` (must be `None`) and its own `pending_path_cancel_request` (must not still contain an EB payload). If either condition fails, `TrainController.eb.release()` rejects with a diagnostic command-result notification. On acceptance it transitions to the **`READY` state**. It must not call `RouteExecutor` directly and must not mutate `RouteRun` directly; route continuation/replanning is discovered by the normal Phase 1 route-deviation cleanup (which fires from `path_cancelled`, set by the EB cancellation callback) and the next eligible Phase 1 planning pass.

**Runtime notifications**

- `NotificationCenter` is the only runtime notification owner in v1. It is exposed through `SimulationController.notification_center` and is responsible for storing, sequencing, and projecting runtime notifications to frontend/API consumers. Controllers, dispatcher, route executor, and admin surfaces emit notifications by calling this class; they do not keep component-local frontend notification queues.
- Every notification carries at minimum `notification_id`, `event_time`, `source`, `kind`, `severity`, optional `train_id`, optional `path_id`, and a structured `payload`. `NotificationCenter` assigns monotonically increasing `notification_id` values in emission order and is the only component that decides retention, read/ack state, and frontend delivery mechanics such as polling, streaming, or websocket fanout.
- Command execution results are notifications with `kind = "command_result"` and payload `{command_id, command_name, success, reason}`. Path cancellation/completion diagnostics, fatal runtime failures, route-run errors, and operator-visible planning failures also flow through `NotificationCenter`; the originating component still owns the underlying state mutation, while `NotificationCenter` owns only the user-facing event record and delivery surface. Runtime warnings emitted from skipped no-op release paths and from any other component-side runtime diagnostic also flow through this same `NotificationCenter` surface as `kind = "diagnostic"` notifications; component code does not log these to a parallel sink. The canonical structured cancellation event is `path_cancellation`, defined in the **Cancellation** subsection.
- Request handlers must not read controller internals to discover later semantic command failures. They return accepted/pending for enqueue or latch writes, then frontend/API consumers observe later results through `NotificationCenter`.

**Phase 1 departure-request writes (manual + ATO)**

- For both manual and ATO modes, Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly on the first eligible Phase 1 pass that observes live authority, regardless of whether the driver has already started rolling manually. This guarantees that route-managed manual operation also goes through the canonical depart-acceptance path; manual driver pre-depart movement is allowed (and protected by ATC) but the route-side "depart accepted, route progress can complete a stop" gate (`controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`) is not skipped just because the train moved early. If `TrainController.depart()` rejects (e.g. EB latched, no cab in manual mode, `pending_power_off` set; see the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract** for the full precondition list), Phase 1 may retry on the next eligible tick.

**Phase handoff rule**

- A phase may retry immediately only when every operation stays inside that same phase.
- If logic in one phase needs a later tick/pass to mutate live runtime state, it must write a per-train retained latch instead of calling across phases directly. The only v1 same-transaction Phase-2-to-Phase-3 data handoff is `ControllerProgressReport`, which uses the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) described below and is a within-tick transient, not retained across ticks.
- **Same-transaction commit-callback exception.** Within one train's per-tick transaction, Phase 1, 2, and 3 run sequentially and Phase 2 does not run again before the next tick. Phase 3 commit code may therefore synchronously call controller and route reaction hooks that mutate Phase-2-owned and Phase-1-owned fields, because there is no live Phase 1 or Phase 2 reader for that train left to observe a partial state. The closed list of allowed same-transaction cross-phase calls is: `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` calling `TrainController.on_path_complete()` and then `SimulationController.handle_path_completion(...)` (which delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`); `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` calling `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (which delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`); `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` calling `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` and `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` / `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(...)` (which fan out to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` / `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)` and `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` / `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)`); `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` calling `TrainController.finish_dwell()`; the Phase 3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer (and Phase 1 dwell-complete step) calling `TrainController.finish_dwell()` per the **`current_route_status` write contract**; and the Phase 1 authority-acquisition step calling `TrainController.depart()` and the Phase 1 dwell-entry step calling `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`, which set the route-driven `RUNNING_MOVING` / `RUNNING_AT_STATION` / `ato.running` transitions. These last two are Phase-1 forward calls and are allowed even though the Phase Table lists those controller fields under Phase 2's canonical writes: Phase 1 runs before Phase 2 in the same per-train transaction, so "Phase-2 canonical writes" means Phase 2 may also write them, not that Phase 1 is forbidden from setting the route-driven transition. Cross-train and cross-tick mutations remain latch-only.
- The per-train Phase 3 retained latch / flag table is controller-owned in v1. Its canonical home is the nested aggregate `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches: PhaseLatches`, documented in the **Latch table** (see the Naming-convention paragraph) in this section.
- The v1 retained latch table contains:
  - `pending_path_cancel_request`
  - `pending_route_abort`
  - `pending_power_off` (controller-owned latch consumed by Phase 3)
  - `pending_route_deactivate` (controller-owned latch written by `route.deactivate()` admin handler per assigned train; consumed by Phase 3)
- `ControllerProgressReport` values are intentionally excluded from this retained latch table. Phase 2 appends them to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), and that train's Phase 3 pass drains and clears the mailbox immediately. The mailbox is a within-tick transient — written in Phase 2, drained and cleared in Phase 3, never surviving across ticks — so it is not a retained latch.
- `pending_path_cancel_request` is an optional payload, not a bare boolean. It is the **canonical owner** of "cancel my path" — the only latch that ever drives `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. Every cancel-needing writer (EB activation, route-edit/replan revocation, operator/admin path cancellation, route-abort, power-off, route-deactivate) routes through `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` directly, or through one of the higher-level helpers (`set_route_abort`, `set_power_off` via `_apply_power_off_request`, `set_route_deactivate`) that fans out to it per the **Writer-helper rule**; once written, the writer walks away. Phase 3 commits exactly one cancellation per pass for that train. Ordinary movement-derived deviation does not use this latch; it is committed inline while Phase 3 drains the controller-owned progress mailbox. The payload's canonical fields are `initiator: str` (free-form label such as `"eb"`, `"operator_cancelled"`, `"power_off"`, `"deviation"`, `"revoked"`, or `"idle_hold_release"`, populated by the writer for the `path_cancellation` notification) and `reason: Optional[str]`. It does not decide whether the `RouteRun` should be removed. If multiple same-tick writers set `pending_path_cancel_request`, the most recent payload wins; Phase 3 still resolves the actual live path from `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` and commits at most one explicit latched cancellation for that train on that tick. Functional behavior is identical regardless of which `initiator` ends up in the slot — the path is cancelled. Consumers must not depend on a particular `initiator` being present after a multi-writer same-tick race. Because v1 allows at most one active path per train, this payload does not need a required `path_id` routing key.
- `pending_route_abort` is a separate Phase-3 bare-bool latch for explicit route-run teardown. API/UI/admin "abort route" requests call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)`, which sets `pending_route_abort = True` and (when a live path exists) fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "operator_cancelled"` so Phase 3 cancels authority before removing the run. Route edit, retarget, reconciliation, EB, and ordinary path revocation that should continue the same `RouteRun` write only `pending_path_cancel_request` and must not set `pending_route_abort`.
- Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly when route conditions are met. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` invokes `depart()` with no intervening latch; the call may fail (e.g. EB latched, no cab in manual mode, power off; see the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**) and Phase 1 retries on a later tick. There is no `departure_requested` latch in v1. Dwell entry and dwell exit are also direct calls: `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` directly when the dwell-entry preconditions are met (the dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at`), and calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` directly (after writing `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at`) when `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` is satisfied. The controller never reads `RouteRun` to decide either transition.

**Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption: stopped-train gating and no-active-path skip**

- The `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer is the **canonical owner** of "is there a live path to cancel?" branching. All other latches (`pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate`) "assign and forget": their writers wrote `pending_path_cancel_request` through the inclusive-latch helpers, and Phase 3 commits that cancel before reaching their consumer steps. They themselves do not branch on active-path emptiness.
- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption step, it first checks `TrainController.is_stopped()`. If the train is still rolling, the latch is preserved and reconsidered on the next eligible Phase 3 pass — Phase 3 must NOT call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` against a moving train, because that would release future block reservations and switch locks ahead of the still-rolling train. Cancellation that needs to commit while a train is rolling is the EB workflow: EB itself stops the train, and once `is_stopped()` is true the same `pending_path_cancel_request(EB)` latch is consumed on the next Phase 3 pass. Multiple writers in the rolling window overwrite the same single payload slot (most-recent payload wins per the latch table); no extra blocking is needed.
- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption step and the train IS stopped, but `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` is already empty (because a movement-derived deviation cancellation already ran inline during local progress drain, because the path completed normally earlier in the same Phase 3, or because no path ever existed for that train), the dispatcher cancel mutation step is skipped with a single `NotificationCenter` diagnostic such as `latched cancel for <train_id> initiator=<initiator> skipped: no active path`. The latch is still consumed on that same Phase 3 pass. Route-run teardown occurs only from `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate`, the Phase-1 terminal-walk direct call to `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)`, or train removal.
- When the `pending_path_cancel_request` latch survives across ticks because the train was rolling at inline-deviation-cancel time, no cross-phase coordination is needed: the train's own ATC sees `received_path_plan is None` (the inline cancel cleared authority) and brakes the train under the no-authority limit. Once `is_stopped() == True`, the next eligible Phase 3 explicit-cancel step (`7b40`) consumes the latch through the same no-active-path skip rule above. The train protects itself; `PathDispatcher` does not reach across phases to clear the latch early.
- Phase 3 must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` on a `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED` retained path row. That call would return failure with `"already completed"` / `"already cancelled"` and is unnecessary because the latch's intent has already been satisfied by the earlier inline mutation or by normal completion.
- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_route_abort` consumption step, the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` has already been processed earlier in the same pass (or correctly skipped when no live path existed). The abort step performs only its own teardown: call `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` so the route assignment is fully removed; the helper writes `state = DEACTIVATED` and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` itself before tearing down the run. Clear `pending_route_abort` after `remove_train_route_run(...)` returns. The abort step does not branch on active-path emptiness — that decision was already owned by the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer.
- `pending_power_off` is a separate Phase-3 latch. `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` is allowed while EB is latched if the train is stopped; EB release is not a prerequisite for shutdown. `power_off_cmd()` is stopped-only at acceptance, so the `is_stopped()` gate on the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` is trivially satisfied at the same Phase 3. When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_power_off` consumption step, the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` has already been processed; this step performs only its own teardown: call `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` followed by the controller-local OFF cleanup (clear cab, EB latch, `pending_power_off`, `ato.running`). The power-off step does not branch on active-path emptiness.
- Terminal route completion is handled by a direct call from Phase 1, not by a Phase 3 latch consumption step. When `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` detects the terminal walk (the final task has been finished and `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns `None`), it calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside that same Phase 1 invocation. By that point, the matching live path has already been cleared by the normal stop-completion path during the prior Phase 3 pass (terminal completion only fires after the last stop+dwell pair finishes), so this direct call does not need a dispatcher cancel. `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` performs the canonical terminal teardown: set `RouteRun.current_route_status.state = FINISHED`, and leave the run as a historical `FINISHED` row in the route registry. `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` automatically returns `None` once the run's `state` flips out of `RUNNING`. If the route is `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` and has no remaining assigned trains, `route.deactivate()` may be invoked here; that triggers the `pending_route_deactivate` workflow for any other still-assigned trains, but since this branch only fires when no other trains remain it is effectively a no-op beyond setting `Route.is_active = False`.
- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_route_deactivate` consumption step, the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` has already been processed earlier in the same pass. The deactivate step performs only its own teardown: call `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` (the helper writes `state = DEACTIVATED`, unassigns the train from the run's `route_id`, and — when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION` — calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first), then, when the structural-edit teardown recorded a held re-assignment for this train, apply it (`Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the recorded route) and clear the stash per the **Shared-route edit teardown**, then clear `pending_route_deactivate`. `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` returns the re-assigned route after a structural-edit re-assignment, and `None` otherwise. The deactivate step does not branch on active-path emptiness.

**Procedure / phase ownership table**

| Procedure | Detect / request in | Commit in | Cross-phase handoff |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| covered-task index advance (for already-covered `waypoint` tasks and covered stopped-at-destination `stop` tasks), next-target resolution via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, `create_path(...)`, `extend_path(...)`, broken-task skip / no-reachable-task stall (per **Unreachable and broken task handling**), same-phase retry (`d09d`) | Phase 1 | Phase 1 | none |
| head-advance task consumption within Phase 3 drain (`39da`) | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; each callback resolves the next non-finished task via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and consumes at most one task per call |
| explicit authority cancel / revoke request (`148a`) | API/UI/admin request handler or Phase 1 reconciliation code | Phase 3 | Set the targeted controller's `pending_path_cancel_request`; request handlers return accepted/pending once that payload is written and never call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` inline. This latch cancels authority only and never removes a `RouteRun`. |
| explicit route abort request (`911d`) | API/UI/admin request handler | Phase 3 | Call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)`, which writes `pending_route_abort` and fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` when a live path exists. Phase 3 flips the `RouteRun` to its historical state (via `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`) only from this abort latch after any active authority has already been cancelled or found absent. |
| operator retargeting / route swap (`d592`) | API/UI/admin handler | Phase 3 (via `pending_route_abort` consume) + Phase 1 RouteRun (re)allocation | Workflow owned by the operator-retargeting bullet under **Route Edit And Reconciliation**: the handler atomically sets the abort latch (with its inclusive cancel) and moves the assignment, then returns; Phase 3 tears the old run down; the next eligible Phase 1 allocates a fresh `RouteRun` against the new `route_id` per the **Phase 1 RouteRun (re)allocation** step. |
| EB activation (operator-issued via `eb.activate()`, or ATC/no-authority escalation inside `TrainController.tick()`) (`651a`) | tick-entry command drain inside `TrainController.tick(...)` for the operator-issued path; later Phase 2 controller logic for the ATC/no-authority escalation path | Phase 3 | Performs the **canonical EB activation effects** from the **EB workflow state machine**; the resulting `pending_path_cancel_request(EB)` is committed by Phase 3 |
| usable-authority stop check (`85a5`) | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; query post-physics controller state directly |
| dwell-complete timer check / `finish_dwell()` (`6f77`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` | Phase 1 | none; query route/controller dwell state directly |
| terminal route completion (no remaining tasks on directed walk) (`879a`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` terminal-walk path | Phase 1 | none; Phase 1 calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly to set `current_route_status.state = FINISHED`, unassign the train, and (for `ONE_OFF` routes with no remaining assigned trains) trigger `route.deactivate()`. The `RouteRun` is preserved as a historical record. The matching live path has already been cleared by stop-completion in the prior Phase 3, so no dispatcher cancel is needed. |
| admin route deactivation (`route.deactivate()` while runs are in flight) (`7f52`) | API/UI/admin request handler | Phase 3 | Per assigned train, call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(...)`, which sets `pending_route_deactivate = True` and (when a live path exists) fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "revoked"`. Phase 3 of each train sets `current_route_status.state = DEACTIVATED`, unassigns, and (when applicable) exits dwell. The `RouteRun` is preserved as a historical record. |
| route deviation cleanup (clear `RouteRun.path_cancelled`; derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` projection clears) (`d111`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` | Phase 1 | none; multi-tick latch gated by `controller.is_recovery_clean` |
| `power_off_cmd()` acceptance (`a536`) | Phase-2 operator-command drain | Phase 3 | `pending_power_off` |
| operator command acceptance (`power_on_cmd`, `set_cab`, `set_mode`, `set_manual_mode_type`, `set_handle`, `set_manual_power_percent`, `eb.release()`) (`f482`) | Phase-2 operator-command drain | Phase-2 operator-command drain | none |
| route/controller departure acceptance (`990e`) | Phase 1 | Phase 1 | none; `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly whenever live authority exists for the current target, regardless of `control_mode`. Reading depart-acceptance state by other layers uses `controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING` (set by any canonical transition into that state in the Controller state machine table — a successful `TrainController.depart()` call or the reactive speed-based evaluation observing motion under live authority). |
| dwell-entry transition after stop completion (`baf0`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-entry step | Phase 1 | none; `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` directly when the route's next task is `dwell`, the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true. The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at`; this step does not need to write it. |
| physics boundary / no-boundary movement observations (`9b6e`) | Phase 2 | Phase 3 | controller-owned per-train progress mailbox `physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`, drained and cleared in Phase 3 (within-tick transient, not retained across ticks) |
| deviation caused by movement (`593a`) | Phase 2 report creation | Phase 3 | controller-owned per-train progress mailbox `physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`, drained and cleared in Phase 3 (within-tick transient, not retained across ticks) |
| resource entry / tail-vacate release (`7e74`) | Phase 2 report creation | Phase 3 | controller-owned per-train progress mailbox `physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`, drained and cleared in Phase 3 (within-tick transient, not retained across ticks) |
| dispatcher path completion (`685d`) | Phase 3 post-physics controller check | Phase 3 | none; `SimulationController` calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` directly |

**Phase Table**

Each phase is a strict execution boundary within one train's per-tick transaction. Code must not cross into another phase's entity chain. Retained cross-phase communication uses only the latches defined in the Latch Table below; same-transaction movement progress uses the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) and is drained and cleared in Phase 3 before the train transaction ends.

**Why this shape.** Phases are interleaved per train, not run as three world-wide passes, so one train's full Phase 1/2/3 transaction commits before the next train's Phase 1 begins. That gives each transaction a single coherent view of network state at the moment it runs and keeps cross-train reads always-current. The strict ownership split — Phase 1 owns route planning and the planning-side `RouteRun` mutation, Phase 2 owns controller and physics, Phase 3 owns dispatcher and resource commit (plus the `task_runs[i].finished_at` stamps that `RouteExecutor`'s head-advance and stop-completion callbacks make) — means each field has exactly one owning component, and because one train's Phase 1/2/3 run sequentially there is never a concurrent writer, which is what makes "always recompute live" reads safe across the rest of the document.

| Phase | Entry point | Entity chain | Canonical writes | Must not |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Phase 1 — Setup** (`17ab`) | `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` | (`9c4f`) RouteRun (re)allocation → (`5fc7`) route-deviation cleanup → (`70c3`) dwell-complete timer check (with `TrainController.finish_dwell()`) → (`c481`) helper lookup via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` (skip to step `3a37` when `task_kind == "dwell"`) → (`a5c6`) covered-task index advance → (`3a37`) dwell-entry transition (with `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`) → (`592a`) re-resolve next target via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` → (`66aa`) `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` (steps `592a`–`66aa` gated by the **Planning Kernel** eligibility precondition) | At `9c4f`: new `RouteRun` (`route_id`, `started_at`, `direction = "forward"`, allocated `task_runs` — with the skipped prefix pre-finished per **Run start-mode seeding** when start mode is `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`, `current_route_status.state = RUNNING`); at later steps: `RouteRun.direction`, `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` for the `walk_position` returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)` on the successful create/extend (written by step `66aa` only; never written by `_find_next_task_target`), `RouteRun.task_runs[i].finished_at` (written by `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` from the dwell-complete and covered-task-advance steps — that helper writes `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`; the derived `current_waypoint_index` and the next task's derived `started_at` reflect the new `finished_at` rather than being separate write targets), `RouteRun.path_cancelled` (cleared by deviation cleanup), `RouteRun.current_route_status.state` for direct `RUNNING` / `FINISHED` writes; `RouteRun.no_reachable_task` (set/cleared by **Unreachable and broken task handling**); `pending_path_cancel_request` when reconciliation demands a cancel | Call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly; clear controller authority directly; read Phase-2 or Phase-3 outputs from this same tick |
| **Phase 2 — Execute** (`e59a`) | `TrainController.tick(delta_seconds)` (operator-command drain first), then `TrainPhysics.tick(delta_seconds)` | Command drain in canonical order → ATC/ATO/manual command generation (each consumer reads `lookahead` live) → physics integration (emitting the train's `track_voltage` to `BlockPowerController` per **Power**, Block power-controller role) → one synchronous `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` callback per crossed resource boundary plus a final no-boundary callback per tick (each callback applies the new span/speed to controller-owned storage and appends one `ControllerProgressReport` to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`)) | `TrainController` state fields, `eb.active`, `ato.running`, `pending_path_cancel_request` (EB / ATC escalation), `pending_power_off` (`power_off_cmd()` acceptance), plus same-transaction `ControllerProgressReport` appends to the controller-owned progress mailbox | Call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly; mutate `RouteRun` (including any `task_runs[i]` field); call `RouteExecutor` planning methods; call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`; run dwell-entry, dwell-complete, or route-deviation cleanup |
| **Phase 3 — Feedback** (`282a`) | `SimulationController` Phase 3 cleanup pass for that train | Drain and clear the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) via `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` → route callbacks via `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` (writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` for the consumed task) → `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` (its callback writes `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at`) → `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption → `pending_route_abort` consumption → `pending_power_off` consumption → `pending_route_deactivate` consumption | Block/switch occupancy and lock state via `ResourceCoordinator`, `Path.status`, `Path.current_resource_index`, `RouteTaskRun.finished_at` (only via head-advance and stop-completion callbacks, each routing through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, which writes `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`; `RouteRun.current_waypoint_index` and the next task's derived `started_at` reflect the new `finished_at` rather than being separate write targets); `RouteRun.current_route_status.state = DEACTIVATED` (via `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` invoked from the abort/power-off/deactivate consumers); the four latch clears (`pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate`); controller-local OFF cleanup writes when the power-off consumer fires (`state = OFF`, `cab = None`, `eb.active = False`, `ato.running = False`). Controller's `received_path_plan` automatically returns `None` once `Path.status` transitions to `COMPLETED` / `CANCELLED`. See the **`current_route_status` write contract** for the per-consumer write rules. | Create or extend authority; call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`; arm future targets; write `RouteTaskRun.resolved_target` (Phase 1 only); run dwell entry/exit, dwell-complete timer, or route-deviation cleanup (those are all Phase 1) |

**Latch table**

**Why latches exist.** Latches model hardware constraints. The underlying real-world action — releasing track resources, cancelling authority, powering off — cannot always commit when requested. The train may still be rolling, hardware may be busy, or another condition may need to clear first. A latch persists the *intent* of the action in controller-owned state and waits for its gating predicate to allow commit. A latch is therefore a deferred command, not a flag. Adding a new latch is only correct when there is a gating predicate that defers the action across one or more ticks. If the action can always commit immediately, do not add a latch — write a flag or call the action directly.

**Naming convention.** The four controller-owned Phase-3 latches live nested under `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches: PhaseLatches`, with the field names `path_cancel_request`, `route_abort`, `power_off`, and `route_deactivate`. The aggregate keeps the four together so the latch discipline (canonical writers, fan-out, consumers) lives in a single class. The exception is `path_cancelled`, which is `RouteRun`-owned rather than controller-owned and uses past-tense form. New controller-owned Phase-3 latches must be added to `PhaseLatches`. Throughout this document the flat names `pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, and `pending_route_deactivate` are prose shorthand for these nested fields (`phase_latches.path_cancel_request`, `phase_latches.route_abort`, `phase_latches.power_off`, and `phase_latches.route_deactivate` respectively) — they are not separate flat fields on the controller. Every reference, whichever spelling it uses, denotes the one nested field; it is written only through the `phase_latches.set_*(...)` writer helpers and read/cleared only through the Phase-3 `consume_*()` accessors.

Rule: each latch is consumed by its canonical consumer in its canonical phase, and the last entity that uses a latch in the same 3-phase period is the one that clears it.
