Within one phase, steps run in canonical order, so a latch written by an earlier step in that phase may be consumed by a later step in the same phase (this is what step ordering is for). Cross-phase handoffs always wait for the next eligible run of the consumer phase. Gating predicates may further defer consumption to a later tick's run of the consumer phase. v1 defines no Phase-1-to-Phase-2 retained latch — Phase 1 either commits its work in Phase 1 itself or writes a Phase-3 latch, and Phase 2 reads only operator-command queue entries plus controller-owned state.

**Writer-helper rule.** Every latch has exactly one canonical writer helper. That helper is the only assignment site for the latch field; all other code routes through it and never assigns the field directly. Each helper is also the canonical site that declares its sub-latch fan-out — i.e. when a higher-level latch implies a lower-level latch, the helper applies that fan-out from inside itself. The helpers are:

| Helper | Latch written | Sub-latch fan-out applied inside the helper |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` | `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` | none — leaf latch |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(initiator, reason=None)` | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request` | none — leaf latch |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(reason, has_active_path=...)` | `phase_latches.route_abort = True` | calls `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "operator_cancelled"` when `has_active_path` is true |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_power_off(reason, has_active_path=...)` | `phase_latches.power_off = True` | calls `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "power_off"` when `has_active_path` is true |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(reason, has_active_path=...)` | `phase_latches.route_deactivate = True` | calls `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "revoked"` when `has_active_path` is true |

Callers pass `has_active_path = controller.path_projection.received_path_plan is not None` so the data class itself stays free of controller dependencies. The `power_off` setter does not clear `controller.ato.running` (that cross-field side effect lives on `TrainController._apply_power_off_request(...)`, which wraps the latch setter so every power-off path stays consistent).

Public callers route through these helpers — they never assign latch fields directly:

- API/UI/admin handlers call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` directly between ticks. There is no separate `TrainController.request_route_abort()` wrapper in v1; every caller routes through the same writer-helper on `phase_latches`.
- `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` calls `_apply_power_off_request()`, which clears `ato.running` and forwards to `phase_latches.set_power_off(...)`.
- The `route.deactivate()` admin handler calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(...)` per assigned train.
- EB activation calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "eb"` when a live path exists (skipped during dwell where there is no path to cancel — see the **EB workflow state machine**).
- Operator-driven Phase 1 cancel-and-replan calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "operator_cancelled"`.
- System Phase 1 cancel-and-replan (route reconciliation, structural extend failure) calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "revoked"`; the idle-hold release of a stalled best-effort hold (see the **idle-hold maintenance and release** rule in Planning Kernel step `66aa`) is the same system Phase-1 cancel-and-replan, with the diagnostic label `initiator = "idle_hold_release"`.

Terminal route completion is not a latch and is not part of this rule: when Phase 1 detects the terminal walk, the matching live path has already been cleared by normal stop-completion in the prior Phase 3, so Phase 1's direct `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` call has no inclusive cancel to write.

| Latch | Owner | Type | Canonical writer helper | Written by (phase + entity) | Consumed by (phase + entity) | Gating state | Multi-tick |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `path_cancelled` (`75cb`) | `RouteRun` | `bool` | `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (leaf — no fan-out) | Phase 3 — `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` invoked from `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` callback | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` route-deviation cleanup step | `controller.is_recovery_clean` | Yes — survives across ticks until gating state is satisfied |
| `phase_latches.path_cancel_request` (`c832`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `Optional[PendingPathCancelRequest]` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(initiator, reason=None)` (leaf — no fan-out) | Phase 1 (reconciliation), Phase 2 (command drain / ATC escalation), or API/UI handler (between ticks). All callers go through the helper. | Phase 3 — explicit-cancel step; calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` when an active path exists; skips dispatcher call and consumes latch when no active path | `TrainController.is_stopped() == True`. While the train is still rolling, the latch survives across ticks and is reconsidered on the next eligible Phase 3 pass; cancellation must not release authority/blocks ahead of a moving train. | Yes — survives across ticks while the train is rolling; consumed in the first Phase 3 pass after the train has fully stopped. Multiple writers within the rolling window overwrite the same single payload slot (most-recent payload wins); no extra blocking is needed. |
| `phase_latches.route_abort` (`bc5d`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `bool` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(reason, has_active_path=...)` — fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "operator_cancelled"` when `has_active_path` is true | API/UI/admin handler calls the helper. The helper performs the inclusive fan-out per the Writer-helper rule above. | Phase 3 — route-abort step; calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None` (the inclusive cancel has been consumed or was never written because no live path existed at write time). The abort step does not branch on active-path emptiness — that branching is owned by the `path_cancel_request` consumer. | Yes when an inclusive `path_cancel_request` was written: survives across ticks until that cancel has been consumed and `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None`. No when no inclusive cancel was written (no live path existed at write time): the gating predicate is trivially satisfied and the abort step runs in the same Phase 3 pass. |
| `phase_latches.power_off` (`f8d5`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `bool` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_power_off(reason, has_active_path=...)` — fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "power_off"` when `has_active_path` is true. The cross-field clear of `TrainController.ato.running` lives on the controller-local wrapper `_apply_power_off_request(...)`. | Phase 2 — operator-command drain calls `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`, which calls `_apply_power_off_request()` (acceptance gated on train stopped). | Phase 3 — power-off step; runs after the inclusive `phase_latches.path_cancel_request` has been consumed in the same Phase 3 pass | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None` (trivially satisfied because power-off is stopped-only at acceptance, so the inclusive cancel's `is_stopped()` gate is met on the same Phase 3 pass and the cancel runs in step `7b40` before this step `9a81`). | No — same-tick consumption. The power-off step performs only its own teardown (remove RouteRun → controller-local OFF cleanup); it does not branch on active-path emptiness. |
| `phase_latches.route_deactivate` (`2a31`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `bool` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(reason, has_active_path=...)` — fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `initiator = "revoked"` when `has_active_path` is true | API/UI/admin handler calls the helper per assigned train when `route.deactivate()` runs. | Phase 3 — route-deactivate step; sets `current_route_status.state = DEACTIVATED` and leaves the run as a historical `DEACTIVATED` row in the route registry | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None` (the inclusive cancel has been consumed). The deactivate step does not branch on active-path emptiness. | Yes — survives across ticks until the inclusive cancel has been consumed. |

**`SimulationController.update()` calls**

- **Step 0 — global block-occupancy detection (tick preamble, before the per-train loop).** `SimulationController.update(...)` first reads every block's current detector, debounces, attributes each confirmed entered/vacated block to a train, and snaps each affected train's span to the block seam, per **Power**, Block occupancy detection. This is a tick-level preamble outside any train's phase chain (like advancing `simulation_time`): it stashes each train's entered/vacated block slice, which that train's Phase 2 `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` folds into its Phase-3 progress report. Block entered/tail-vacate observations therefore originate here, from the detector; switch observations still originate from the train's calculated traversal in Phase 2.
- `self.route_executor.update(delta_seconds)` becomes a pure per-tick scheduling / Phase 1 preparation call. It advances route-executor bookkeeping time and prepares only the route-managed scheduling state that `SimulationController` consumes for this tick; it does not own the outer train loop and does not call `SimulationController._update_train(...)` itself.
- `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` remains the per-train Phase 2 execute step plus the immediate Phase 3 consume pass for that same train.
- `SimulationController` is the sole owner of executing the per-tick loop, while `RouteExecutor` owns the train ordering. `SimulationController.update(...)` advances time, calls `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` to refresh route-managed scheduling state, asks `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids)` for the ordered runnable-train list per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**, calls `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` as that train's complete Phase 1 route-executor tick immediately before that same train's `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` transaction, and then updates signals. `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids: Iterable[str]) -> List[str]` takes only the candidate train IDs; it does not take `delta_seconds` (time advancement happens in `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)`). The runnable list includes ALL trains (powered and powered-off). For a train that is `OFF` at transaction start, route Phase 1 is skipped but Phase 2 still runs (the operator-command drain so `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` can be accepted, `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` integration, and the `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` storage callback); if the controller remains `OFF` after the drain, Phase 3 is skipped for that transaction, and if the drain turns power on, the normal powered Phase 2 and Phase 3 flow runs in that same transaction while fresh route planning waits for the next eligible Phase 1 tick. For trains with no current route-managed Phase 1 work, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` is a no-op. `SimulationController` must not forward resource-entry or tail-vacate events itself.
- `SimulationController` owns one shared `PathDispatcher` instance for all trains. `TrainPhysics.tick(delta_seconds)` invokes `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` for that train on every tick, including ticks where the controller remains `OFF`; what that callback writes and appends — and why an `OFF` transaction appends no dispatcher/route progress reports — is owned by the **Physical progress / deviation procedure** and controller tick step `01f2`. The controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) is empty at the start of each transaction because the prior tick's Phase 3 cleared it. The Phase 3 pass for a powered train follows the canonical Phase-3 step order (`3e9b`–`4f6e`): drain the mailbox in order and clear it (the only place movement-derived deviation is committed), run the post-physics usable-authority stop check and call `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` on success (timestamps per **Time sampling**), then consume the four latches in canonical order; gating, latch-clear-on-no-op, and inclusive higher-level deferral rules are owned by the **Latch table** and the **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption** subsection above. Path completion is therefore committed before explicit admin cancel/OFF cleanup, while movement-derived deviation cancellation still wins inline during the drain. If a tick advanced the head across several blocks, those block entries arrive batched in that tick's final no-boundary report, ordered by `sequence_index` (per **Power**, Block occupancy detection); the drain fires one head-advance callback per entry, so the entries consume their waypoints in order, one callback at a time.
- After every train's transaction has emitted its `track_voltage` (Phase 2; see the **Phase Table**), `SimulationController` returns every block whose holder cleared this tick and is still unheld (no `occupied_by` and no `reserved_for_train`) to `0` `track_voltage` by draining the queue `BlockManager` built as those holders cleared (see **Power**, Block power-controller role). Then `self.network.update_signals_from_blocks()` runs after dispatcher feedback so signals reflect all block-state changes, including path completion.

**SimulationController runtime surface**

- `SimulationController` is the registered runtime surface shared by `TrainController`, `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and `TrainPhysics` whenever any simulation runtime is active. It is the canonical home for runtime services and is the only owner of the public `add_train(...)` API surface. When a `SimulationController` instance exists for a network, it must finish initializing and registering this surface before any `TrainController` instance is constructed.
- `SimulationController` is optional in editor mode. Pure map-editor sessions (geometry placement, connectivity wiring, static field edits that do not affect runtime occupancy or locks) run with no `SimulationController`, no `BlockManager` runtime, no dispatcher, and no controllers; entities that hold a reference to `SimulationController` MUST type it as `Optional[SimulationController]` and tolerate `None`. An editor session that wants live track/switch testing MAY instantiate a lightweight `SimulationController` (with `BlockManager` wired but no runtime route/dispatcher trains) so that switch state changes route through the canonical mutation workflow normally. The choice is binary at the editor session level — there is no partial wiring.
- The v1 surface exposes exactly the shared services and registries needed by those components: `network`, `block_manager`, `path_dispatcher`, `route_executor`, `notification_center`, the controller registry keyed by `train_id`, the physics registry keyed by `train_id`, `get_train(train_id)`, `get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, `get_or_create_physics(train_id)`, route-event delivery methods using the canonical `handle_` prefix (`SimulationController.handle_path_completion(...)`, `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(...)`) which delegate to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` and `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` respectively, and the public train-addition API `add_train(...)`. Callers that need the route's currently armed target call `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(train_id)` directly through the route-executor surface; there is no separate runtime-services projection for it. `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id) -> Optional[str]` remains the canonical dispatcher projection for the train's active path id, and `PathDispatcher.get_active_path(train_id) -> Optional[Path]` is the canonical accessor that returns the active `Path` record itself (composed as `paths_by_id[active_path_id_by_train(train_id)]` when a path is active, else `None`). `add_train(...)` is owned by `SimulationController` because the train-addition API is invoked from the frontend/client layer to introduce a new train into the running simulation; it constructs the `Train`, ensures the controller exists, and writes the initial `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` from placement input before any simulation tick. **Threading model**: All API/UI/admin requests that mutate runtime state (including `add_train(...)`, controller-command queueing, latch writes) run on the same single execution thread as the per-tick phase loop. The runtime processes API requests strictly between ticks; if a tick is in progress when a request arrives, the request is queued and processed only after the tick's post-tick signal update completes. (Multi-threaded execution is Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.) Because all API requests run between ticks, the system can also rely on always-recompute-live reads: API actions may have changed shared state, so consumers must recompute live (e.g. via `_find_next_task_target`) instead of trusting cached values from a prior tick. `add_train(...)` is therefore synchronous and all-or-nothing inside that single-threaded model: if any sub-step fails (validation, span derivation, controller registration), the partial state must be rolled back and the call must return failure rather than registering a partially wired train. A request whose `train_id` already exists must fail without mutating state.
- External operation surfaces exposed through routes/API/UI/admin code honor the same phase workflow as internal code. A request that wants to cancel/revoke a live path resolves the target train/controller, calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(initiator, reason=None)`, and returns success once that accepted/pending payload has been written. A request that wants to abort the whole route run calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` per the **Writer-helper rule** in the **Latch Table** preamble; route-run removal is driven by the abort latch. A request that sends an operator command returns success once the command has been queued. Any later semantic failure is emitted by the real command or dispatcher operation when the command queue or Phase-3 handoff is actually consumed, through `NotificationCenter`. The later per-train Phase 3 pass is the only place that calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clears authority, releases future resources, removes route runs, and invokes route callbacks. Request handlers must not mutate dispatcher path records, controller authority, route-run state, block reservations, or switch locks directly.
- When a `SimulationController` instance is created for a runtime session, it is the first runtime object initialized for that network run. `SimulationController.__init__` constructs/registers `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `ResourceCoordinator`, `PathDispatcher`, and `RouteExecutor`, wires `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `ResourceCoordinator`, `PathDispatcher`, and `RouteExecutor` to the same `SimulationController` reference so that cross-entity callbacks such as the `SwitchManager` → `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` notify resolve through `self.simulation_controller.block_manager` rather than requiring direct injected back-references, and only then allows `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` to create a `TrainController`. Any later-created or later-loaded entity must be registered with this same `SimulationController` before it is allowed to mutate cross-entity runtime state. If that surface is not registered yet, `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(...)` must fail rather than constructing a partially wired controller.
- Every `TrainController` stores only a reference to `SimulationController`, not separate dispatcher or route-executor references. Controller-owned release/removal commands do not call dispatcher or route teardown inline anymore; they only write the appropriate Phase 3 handoff (`pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, and/or `pending_power_off`). That same train's later Phase 3 consume pass then performs `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, any required `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` call, and controller-local cleanup in canonical order. Route-deviation cleanup itself is a Phase 1 step that runs on the next eligible tick after the train has reached the stopped recovery state.
- `PathDispatcher` and `RouteExecutor` also resolve controllers through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` and must not keep private controller registries or construct controllers directly.

**Canonical runtime procedures**

These named procedures are the canonical home for ordering-sensitive cross-component workflows. Later sections may restate local consequences, but they should cross-reference these procedures rather than inventing a second order.

- **Physical progress / deviation procedure**:
  1. `e3e6` — `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` emits one ordered `PhysicsSpanUpdate` per crossed resource boundary, plus one final no-boundary callback every tick so Phase 3 always has a deterministic consume point.
  2. `35d0` — `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` writes the new controller-owned span/speed, converts the tile delta into one typed `ControllerProgressReport`, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). It does not call `PathDispatcher` directly.
  3. `908c` — During that same train's Phase 3 consume pass, `SimulationController` drains the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) in FIFO order by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each report, then clears the mailbox.
  4. `83a2` — `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` handles entered resources before any path bookkeeping by calling `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, path_id, route)` for each one. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train uses through a switch tile (derived from the controller's `ResourceEvent.entry_edge` + `switch_exit_edge`); it is ignored for blocks. The coordinator routes block events to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)` and switch events to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`, which compares the incoming `route` against the current `Switch.physical_occupants`, and only when no conflicting route is held by a different train records `Switch.physical_occupants[route] = train_id`, consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock for that switch (the intrusion check defined in the **Switch entity** section: a physical occupant that holds no compatible lock for its route is a fatal intrusion, not a clean entry), and converts applicable same-path switch/flank locks into train-owned physical protection. Two trains using non-conflicting routes (per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`) coexist cleanly. Physical occupancy is the top-priority truth: if a train physically enters a resource and recording that entry would not overwrite another owner's live route or block claim, the resource state must record it even if the software authority state is already wrong.
  5. `018a` — If recording that physical entry would overwrite another train's current physical/reservation owner, `PathDispatcher` enters the canonical fatal physical-entry conflict procedure below immediately, leaves the prior owner intact, and stops processing the report.
  6. `389a` — If `report.deviation == true`, dispatcher commits the cancellation immediately, inline during local progress drain, by calling `cancel_path(path_id, initiator="deviation", ...)`. The original `deviation_kind` value (`"out_of_sequence"` or `"beyond_authorized_target"`) flows into the `path_cancellation` event payload defined in the **Cancellation** subsection. It does not run normal `Path.current_resource_index` advancement or `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` for that report. The canonical cancellation context is delivered upward only after the dispatcher cancel mutation has committed for that train on that same tick. The next eligible Phase 1 reruns ordinary covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from the train's current physical position. Both deviation flavors share the same recovery path.
  7. `640a` — If `report.deviation == false`, dispatcher applies ordinary active-path head progress and route head-advance callbacks. For each entered block advance, after `Path.current_resource_index` is updated, the route side compares that single reported block/direction with the current next task on the `RouteRun` directed walk. If that task is finished or passed, the callback records `task_runs[i].finished_at` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances with that write) and returns. If the same physics tick crossed another boundary, the next report in the progress mailbox discovers the next task via a fresh `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call. The callback must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, must not arm a future planning cursor, and must not consume more than one task. Fresh target resolution for a new authority waits for the next eligible Phase 1.
  8. `02bb` — Dispatcher processes `tail_vacated_resources` after all entered-resource handling above.
- **Active-path completion procedure**:
  1. `e564` — Stop-completion observation is post-physics in v1. `TrainController.tick(...)` maintains lookahead and stopped-only command handling, but it does not latch or emit path completion.
  2. `e4ad` — `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` moves the train for that tick, and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` writes the post-move span/speed plus any resource/deviation observations, appending them to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`).
  3. `4e0e` — Later in that same train's Phase 3 consume pass, `SimulationController` drains local progress reports first by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each report.
  4. `9d8b` — After local progress has been fully consumed, including any movement-derived deviation handling from those reports, if an active dispatcher path still exists for that train, `SimulationController` asks the controller's post-move state whether the train is now stopped at the usable end of that live authority. Plain English: this means "the train has already moved for this tick and is now fully stopped at the controller's usable authority stop boundary." Explicit latched admin teardown such as `pending_path_cancel_request` and `pending_power_off` is processed only after this normal completion chance.
  5. `8df5` — The usable-authority stop check is true only when all of the following are true: the controller still has live authority, depart has been accepted for the current authority (`controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`, set by any canonical transition into that state in the Controller state machine table — a successful `TrainController.depart()` call or the reactive speed-based evaluation observing motion under live authority), the selected cab/travel frame is heading in the active authority frame toward the current authority end, `TrainController.is_stopped()` is true, `TrainController.eb.active == False`, and the distance-0 lookahead's dominant restriction is the path-end stop (`atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"`; under discrete forward-Euler integration `atc_limit` sits at its floor near zero rather than landing exactly on `0`, so the reason code — not the raw value — is the operative test). The train may satisfy this check either because it physically progressed through the final `resource_sequence` entry or because the remaining projected authority distance is too short to grant additional positive clearance after applying the controller safety margin (it braked to rest at the safety-margin boundary short of a very short or zero-length final block). The check does not require physical head occupancy in the requested destination block, a route-authored centimeter stop target, or a second exact-position test.
  6. `f8c6` — If that post-physics controller check is false, Phase 3 does not attempt path completion for that train on that tick.
  7. `bc55` — If the post-physics controller check is true, Phase 3 calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` directly rather than routing completion back through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`. Timestamps that record this transition (`Path.last_status_updated_time`, the route-side stop's `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at`, the next directed-walk task's `started_at`, and any related notification `event_time`) are sampled by the writer at the moment of the write per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**.
  8. `790a` — `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` accepts completion only when the active path still exists, `Path.authority_end_block_id == Path.requested_destination_block_id`, and the terminal authority block entry's block-relative traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`. Dispatcher computes that terminal traversal direction from the final block `resource_sequence` entry's explicit directed frame and the block's canonical ordered tiles. For a single-tile terminal block there is no increasing/decreasing `ordered_tiles` axis to anchor `forward`/`reverse`, so the direction match is skipped there (the `authority_end_block_id == requested_destination_block_id` equality already pins arrival). Dispatcher does not require the train head to physically occupy the requested destination block; the controller safety margin / ATC stop boundary is the higher-priority stopping rule. By construction of the create/extend acquisition rules (the waypoint-target exclusion in **`create_path()` flow** and the matching rule in **Path extension**), the live authority's terminal block is never the resolved target block of a `task_kind == "waypoint"` task on the train's `RouteRun`, so dispatcher path completion always lands on a `stop` target.
  9. `09b0` — On accepted completion, `PathDispatcher` follows the canonical terminal-teardown order (defined here once; cancellation in `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` follows this same order with its own status value and callback): release not-yet-entered block reservations and switch/flank locks through `ResourceCoordinator.release_path_resources(path_id, block_ids, completed=True)` (which composes `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)` and `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(path_id)`), transition `Path.status` to `COMPLETED` (which by definition makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return `None` for this train and makes the controller's `received_path_plan` property return `None` on its next read), call `TrainController.on_path_complete()` so the controller transitions to the **`READY` state**, then call `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`.
  10. `29c5` — `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` records the matching stop as finished by setting `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = time.time()` via `_enter_task_arrival(...)` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position` with that write, and the next task's derived `started_at` automatically reflects this `finished_at`). The callback then clears the route-owned active-path correlation. The `stop` task and any following `dwell` task are independent rows. The completion callback does not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`; dwell entry is performed by the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` after this callback returns.
  11. `d843` — On the next eligible tick, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` runs its dwell-entry step: when the route's next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`, the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`. The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at` written in step `29c5`; the dwell-entry step does not need to write it. While the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (or the next task is `dwell` and the controller is about to enter dwell), Phase 1 must not create or extend authority. The same train's later Phase 1 dwell-complete step checks `RouteTask.should_depart(current_time, task_runs[walk_position].started_at)`; when the dwell timer is satisfied, `RouteExecutor` sets `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = current_time` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances with that write), calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()`, and continues into target resolution for the next task in the same Phase 1.
- **Power-off procedure**: The `pending_power_off` latch coordinates a four-step teardown across `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and `TrainController`. The dispatcher cancel for an in-flight live path is committed by Phase 3 step `7b40` (the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer that the inclusive-latch fan-out wrote), not by the `pending_power_off` consumer itself.
  1. `98d3` — `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` is processed in the tick-entry **operator-command processing slot** inside `TrainController.tick(...)`. It validates only the stopped-only shutdown precondition for acceptance; it is valid while `eb.active == True` as long as the train is stopped. On acceptance it immediately disengages any active ATO run by setting `ato.running = False` and calls `TrainController._apply_power_off_request()`, which sets `pending_power_off = True` and (when a live path exists at write time) writes the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "power_off"` per the **Writer-helper rule**. It does not cancel dispatcher authority, clear EB, or remove route state inline.
  2. `278a` — On the same train's later eligible Phase 3, step `7b40` (the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer) commits the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request(POWER_OFF)` through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, gated by `is_stopped() == True` per the **Latch table**. Because `power_off_cmd()` is stopped-only at acceptance, that gate is trivially satisfied on the same Phase 3 pass. The ordinary cancellation callback (`RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`) runs as part of that commit. If no live path was present at `_apply_power_off_request()` time, no inclusive cancel was written and step `7b40` is a no-op for this train.
  3. `534f` — After step `7b40` has run (or no-opped), Phase 3 reaches the `pending_power_off` consumer step `9a81`. That step calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` through `SimulationController.route_executor`. The power-off consumer never calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` itself.
  4. `886a` — Finally that same Phase 3 cleanup performs controller-local OFF cleanup: transition to `OFF`, clear the active cab selection, clear any EB latch, and set `pending_power_off = False`. Note: between `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` invoking `finish_dwell()` (which transitions the controller to `READY`) and this OFF cleanup step clearing `eb.active`, the controller is briefly in the transient combination `(state == READY, eb.active == True)` when the operator powered off while EB was latched and the train was dwelling. This Phase-3 cleanup window is one case of `(state == READY, eb.active == True)`; it is cleared in the same Phase-3 pass before any other consumer observes it. The other legal case is a powered-on train that had EB latched while `OFF`: `power_on_cmd()` carries `eb.active == True` into `READY` and the train rests there (held at a stand by EB) until the operator clears it with the ordinary `TrainController.eb.release()` from `READY`. In both cases `is_recovery_clean` correctly reads false per the **`ControllerCondition` set** canonical home; no consumer needs `is_recovery_clean` between the two power-off-cleanup writes.
- **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**:
  1. `cd5e` — `SimulationController` owns the unrecoverable failed-runtime flag and fatal diagnostic payload.
  2. `16e0` — `BlockManager` (for block conflicts) or `SwitchManager` (for switch conflicts) reports a typed `FatalPhysicalEntryConflict` to `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` when a physical entry would overwrite another train's `occupied_by`, another train's `reserved_for_train`, a switch route that conflicts with a route held by a different train in `Switch.physical_occupants` (per `Switch.routes_conflict(...)`), enter a disabled block/switch, or enter a switch while holding no compatible reservation for its route (the intrusion check). A physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) resource is **not** a fatal conflict — the resource is physically live per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home, and clean-entry rules apply. Both fatal reports flow through the same `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` entry point that `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` calls. The payload is intentionally small and branchable: `resource_type`, `resource_id`, `incoming_train_id`, `prior_owner_train_id: Optional[str]`, `conflict_kind: FatalConflictKind`, and active path IDs when known. `prior_owner_train_id` is populated for conflicting train ownership and is `None` for `conflict_kind == FatalConflictKind.DISABLED_RESOURCE_ENTRY`.
  2a. `6e4a` — **`FatalConflictKind` canonical home.** Define `FatalConflictKind` here once as the diagnostic enum for fatal physical-entry conflicts with v1 values:
    - `OCCUPIED_BY_OTHER_TRAIN` (`bc14`)
    - `RESERVED_FOR_OTHER_TRAIN` (`f3f2`)
    - `SWITCH_OCCUPIED_BY_OTHER_TRAIN` (`c0bf`)
    - `DISABLED_RESOURCE_ENTRY` (`b0f5`)
    - `MALFORMED_ROUTE_KEY` (`a37e`) — a switch physical-entry event arrived with `route is None`, which the dispatcher must never emit for a switch tile in the interior of a path. See the **`ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` switch handoff** prose above.
    - `SWITCH_INTRUSION` (`d51c`) — a train physically entered a switch while holding no compatible reservation in `Switch.locks` for the route it occupies (the intrusion check defined in the **Switch entity** section). The physical occupant is not the holder of a route owner / lock for that route.

    `FatalConflictKind` describes an unrecoverable physical-entry conflict and is consumed only by `enter_failed_runtime(...)` diagnostics; cancellation diagnostics are carried by the free-form `initiator` string on the `path_cancellation` notification (defined in the **Cancellation** subsection) rather than by a parallel enum.
  3. `b9a2` — `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`, not the reporting entity, is the only writer of `SimulationController.failed_runtime = True`. It records the fatal payload, emits a `fatal_runtime_failure` notification through `NotificationCenter`, aborts the current simulation transaction immediately, and leaves all controller state, path rows, route runs, block states, switch states, and physical occupancy exactly as they were at the moment of detection for inspection.
  4. `525b` — Fatal conflict is not an ordinary cancellation/recovery branch. `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`, or `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`, because those APIs are recoverable workflow callbacks and would incorrectly clear state or replan while the runtime is failed.
  5. `ee19` — `SimulationController.update(...)` refuses further simulation advancement while the failed-runtime flag is set. V1 defines no automatic software resume. The UI/admin "release runtime failure" button is only an acknowledgement/resume gate after the operator has manually removed the problematic train or otherwise repaired the conflicting resource state. That release action calls a runtime-owned helper such as `SimulationController.release_failed_runtime_after_manual_cleanup()`, verifies that the fatal resource no longer has the conflicting physical owner / disabled-entry condition reported in the stored fatal payload, clears `failed_runtime` and the fatal payload, and emits a resume notification. It must not cancel paths, remove route runs, clear controller authority, or mutate resource ownership itself; those cleanup actions happen only through the explicit manual/admin repair workflow before the release button is accepted.

**Path completion notification flow**

- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` should commit completion only when the post-physics controller stop check has already passed for that train and the active path's authority end has reached the path's requested target block and direction. Completion ordering and release rules are exactly the **Active-path completion procedure** (steps `09b0`–`29c5`), delivering the canonical completion payload from the **route path-event callback schema**.
- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` returns a `PathCompletionResult` with this exact shape:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `success` | `bool` | `True` only when dispatcher accepted and committed completion. `False` means no completion mutation was committed. |
  | `path_id` | `Optional[str]` | The active path ID that was completed or considered. `None` only when no active path existed for `train_id`. |
  | `new_status` | `Optional[PathStatus]` | `PathStatus.COMPLETED` on success; the unchanged current status such as `PathStatus.ACTIVE` on a failed predicate; `None` when no path row exists. |
  | `reason` | `Optional[str]` | `None` on success. Required diagnostic string on failure, such as `"no active path"`, `"active path missing"`, or `"authority end has not reached requested destination block/direction"`. |

  On `success=False`, `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` leaves the path, controller authority, route state, and resources unchanged except for optional diagnostics. `SimulationController` may log or surface the failure result, but it must not synthesize a fallback completion or cancellation from that failure.
- `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, `TrainController`, `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, and any other runtime manager/controller component access cross-component services exclusively through the single `self.simulation_controller` back-reference. (`Switch` is a pure data entity and holds no `simulation_controller` back-reference; its cross-resource `BlockManager` notify is owned by `SwitchManager` — see the **Switch entity** section.) There is no separate "runtime services interface" object; the access shape is direct named-attribute access on the `SimulationController` instance: `self.simulation_controller.block_manager`, `self.simulation_controller.path_dispatcher`, `self.simulation_controller.route_executor`, `self.simulation_controller.notification_center`, `self.simulation_controller.network`, `self.simulation_controller.get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, `self.simulation_controller.handle_path_completion(...)`, and `self.simulation_controller.handle_path_cancellation(...)`. `PathDispatcher` calls the route-event methods through this surface, not through a concrete `RouteExecutor` reference; `TrainController` writes latches and calls only controller/`simulation_controller` methods, not route planning helpers directly.
- Wiring pattern: `SimulationController` is initialized first and is the canonical shared runtime owner. It then constructs `PathDispatcher` and `RouteExecutor` and wires every runtime manager/controller (`BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `ResourceCoordinator`, `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and each `TrainController`) to itself by setting `entity.simulation_controller = self` so all cross-entity callbacks resolve through that single attribute. `Switch` is NOT wired this way — it holds no back-reference; `SwitchManager` owns the switch-side runtime wiring and the `BlockManager` notify. Components may keep that `simulation_controller` reference, but they must not keep direct concrete references to each other when the same access can go through `self.simulation_controller.<named attribute>`.
- `TrainController` instances are created on demand through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, which runs only after the **SimulationController runtime surface** above is fully registered. Every new `TrainController` is constructed with the same `simulation_controller` reference, and controller-owned release/removal commands (e.g. `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`) write the appropriate Phase 3 handoff latches through controller-owned state rather than reaching directly into `PathDispatcher` or `RouteExecutor`. This keeps `SimulationController` the single registry of shared runtime components and removes any temporal concern about `TrainController` being constructed before `PathDispatcher` / `RouteExecutor`.
- Path completion should only be recognized after the train has already moved for that tick, Phase 3 has already committed any resource entry/deviation/tail-vacate effects, the controller's post-move stop check says the train is fully stopped at the usable authority stop boundary, and the live authority reaches the requested destination block in the requested destination direction. The controller safety margin is a braking/diagnostic target used by lookahead and is higher priority than trying to force the head to the physical edge or physical interior of the requested destination block. No completion callback should run while the train is still braking or creeping toward that usable authority stop.
- After dispatcher-owned completion release, route-level waypoint and dwell logic should follow the **Active-path completion procedure**: the completion callback records route-owned arrival/progress, and the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` call decides whether the train enters dwell (`RUNNING_AT_STATION`) or remains in the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection).

**`SimulationController._update_train(...)` flow**

1. Get or create a `TrainController`.
2. Get or create a `TrainPhysics`.
3. On first pairing only, initialize any controller-owned bootstrap values that still come from persisted train data, then establish the runtime 1:1 `Train` <-> `TrainController` pairing for that `train_id`.
4. Tick controller.
5. Tick physics.
6. Sync only train-owned persistent/configuration state back to `Train`. Do not sync live position/span, speed, path authority, power/cab state, or operational state back to `Train`.
7. Do not add a separate simulation-owned dispatcher feedback pass. `TrainPhysics.tick()` should compute a `span_update` and call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`, and that controller method should apply the controller-owned span/speed update and append one ordered `ControllerProgressReport` with any typed tail-vacate observations to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). That same train's Phase 3 consume pass later drains the mailbox and delivers those reports to `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`.

**`SimulationController._update_train(...)` calls**

- The per-train calls are `get_or_create_controller(train_id)` (the public `SimulationController` accessor that owns the controller registry), `_get_or_create_physics(train)`, `controller.tick(delta_seconds)`, and `physics.tick(delta_seconds)`. `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` both reach the registry through the same public train-ID-based accessor rather than keeping private `_get_or_create_controller` helpers.
- A newly created controller may bind only to train-owned persistent/configuration data needed for that 1:1 pairing. It does NOT seed `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` itself; initial span placement is owned by the application train-addition path. The API `SimulationController.add_train(...)` flow must create or obtain the controller and write `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before any simulation tick. `Train` does not carry live runtime authority or live position/span fields, so this constructor path does not seed `received_path_plan` (it is a read-only property that resolves the dispatcher's active `Path` into the canonical projection whenever read; before any path is created it returns `None`). The derived reads `assigned_path_id`, `ato_path_id`, and `ato_destination_block_id` are never written directly on the controller — they only surface when `PathDispatcher` has an active path. Runtime authority is always route-backed in v1: `RouteExecutor` creates the path in Phase 1 through `PathDispatcher.create_path(train_id=..., ...)`, using either the train's ordinary assigned route or a dedicated one-off operator route for ad hoc/local movement. The controller's `received_path_plan` property automatically reflects that path on the next read; there is no separate `receive_path_plan(...)` setter. In v1 there is no normal standalone non-route dispatcher-authority mode. `RouteExecutor` should already have ensured the controller exists before any Phase 1 path creation; `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` in `SimulationController._update_train(...)` remains only a controller-registry accessor/fallback, not a separate non-route authority path.
- In v1 there is no late-controller recovery hook. Controller existence is a hard precondition for authority assignment: `RouteExecutor` or any other caller must ensure `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` has already established the 1:1 `train_id -> controller` binding before `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` runs (the controller's `received_path_plan` property needs the controller object to exist before any reader can call it). If persisted/bootstrap train data is needed to initialize that controller, `SimulationController` resolves the `Train` entity internally from `train_id`; callers still use the train-ID-based accessor. Controller bootstrap must not recreate authority from train-side runtime mirrors, and `PathDispatcher` does not expose `on_controller_available(train_id)` or any post-bind authority refresh — the property always returns the live dispatcher state.
- Consumers read live target-speed data from controller lookahead at distance 0 and live operational state from `TrainController.ops.state`; the sync step writes no live fields to `Train` (step 6 above).
- Initial power/cab runtime state comes from the v1 controller defaults and later explicit `TrainController` commands only; live power/cab state is never re-synced from `Train`.

**`PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` signature**

- `report: ControllerProgressReport` — payload schema defined immediately below.
- Returns `None`. Side effects only; ordering follows the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**.
- Fatal physical-entry conflicts escalate through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`; the call does not return a failure code.

**Dispatcher progress/completion flow**

**ControllerProgressReport schema**: The canonical field set for `ControllerProgressReport` is defined here. Every producer (`TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)`) and consumer (`PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`) refers back to this heading by name. Fields: `train_id` (`str`), `event_time` (`float`, system-time Unix seconds sampled at the write site), `entered_resources` (ordered list of resource-entry events), `tail_vacated_resources` (ordered list of tail-vacate events), `current_head_sequence_index` (`Optional[int]`, index of the train head in the active `resource_sequence`), `deviation` (`bool`), `deviation_kind` (`"out_of_sequence" | "beyond_authorized_target"` — these are the only two values in v1), `deviation_reason` (`Optional[str]`, diagnostic plain text only, must not be used for branching). The report does not carry `path_id`; `PathDispatcher` resolves the active path from `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`. Each resource event in `entered_resources` and `tail_vacated_resources` carries `sequence_index` (when known), `resource_type`, and `resource_id`; block tail-vacate events additionally carry `direction` (the vacated block's forward `exit_edge` `N`/`E`/`S`/`W` for the traversal by which the train left that block); switch events (both entry and tail-vacate) additionally carry `entry_edge` and `switch_exit_edge` (the traversal-frame edges `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` copies from the switch tile's `TileTraversal`), from which `PathDispatcher` derives the unordered `route` key — both are required non-`None` on switch events and are `None` on block events.

- `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` is the runtime progress API; the payload is the **ControllerProgressReport schema** above. When live authority exists and the controller cannot map the head to the active `resource_sequence`, the report must set `deviation = true`, `deviation_kind = "out_of_sequence"`, and `current_head_sequence_index = None`; dispatcher treats that report as deviation/cancellation input rather than ordinary active-path progress. For a single report, processing order is the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**: entered physical resources are recorded first, deviation cancellation is handled after clean physical entry, and tail-vacated resources are processed last.
- `PathDispatcher` uses `deviation` plus `deviation_kind` to decide whether to cancel the active path in Phase 3. On a deviation report, dispatcher calls `cancel_path(path_id, initiator="deviation", ...)`; the original `deviation_kind` value (`"out_of_sequence"` or `"beyond_authorized_target"`) is preserved on the `path_cancellation` notification payload (defined in the **Cancellation** subsection) for diagnostics. EB recovery timing is gated by controller state (Phase 1 cannot replan until the controller has reached `READY` after `eb.release()`); power-off teardown is driven by `pending_power_off` / the controller transitioning to `OFF`. Planning and routing logic do not read `initiator`.
- The structured deviation predicates are:

  | `deviation_kind` | Predicate |
  | --- | --- |
  | `"out_of_sequence"` | Live authority exists, but the train head cannot be mapped to the active `received_path_plan.resource_sequence`, or maps to a resource that is not the current/next legal head resource for that active path. |
  | `"beyond_authorized_target"` | The head is physically beyond `Path.authority_end_block_id` in the path's directed traversal frame. This is an overshoot of the authorized clearance, including a truncated best-effort authority whose track ahead was not yet ready. |

- `TrainController` owns progress interpretation and canonical span storage. It applies the `span_update` from `TrainPhysics`, converts ordered `added_tiles` and `removed_tiles` into entered/tail-vacated block and switch resource observations, and compares the updated `current_span` with `received_path_plan.resource_sequence` when live authority exists. Stop completion is evaluated separately later in that same train's Phase 3 consume pass from the controller's post-move state after same-transaction progress reports have already been drained. When no live authority exists, the report may still carry physical entry/release observations but `current_head_sequence_index` is `None` and no active-path progress can be inferred by the controller. `PathDispatcher` still owns `Path.current_resource_index`, path status, reservation/lock consumption, resource release, path cancellation/completion, and route callbacks.
- For entered resources, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` must first record clean physical entry exactly as defined in the **Physical progress / deviation procedure** above. Only after those physical writes succeed, and only when `report.deviation == false` and an active path still exists for `train_id`, may dispatcher resolve the relevant dispatcher-owned path record and update `Path.current_resource_index` from the reported `sequence_index` when the train head enters a resource in that live `Path.resource_sequence`.
- After dispatcher has recorded clean physical entry and updated `Path.current_resource_index` for the entered resource, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` must call the single route-facing head-advance callback `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(train_id, path_id, resource_event)` for the path's active route execution, if any. The `path_id` argument is the dispatcher-resolved active path ID. `resource_event` is a structured payload carrying `resource_type`, `resource_id`, and `traversal_direction: Optional`. For block resources, `resource_event.traversal_direction` is the block-relative `"forward"` / `"reverse"` traversal direction computed by the canonical block-direction helper from the entered `resource_sequence` block entry against `Block.ordered_tiles`. For switch resources it is `None`. `RouteExecutor` must not poll `TrainController.get_current_span()` or `TrainController.get_lookahead()` inside `RouteExecutor.update(...)` to discover head movement. `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` is a Phase-3 route-progress callback that consumes at most one task per call:
  - If `resource_type != "block"`, return; switches are no-ops on the route side.
  - Call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to live-resolve the next non-finished task on the directed walk. The helper is read-only and does not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target`.
  - If the resolved next task's `task_kind == "waypoint"` and its resolved-target block and direction match the callback's reported block and direction, the callback consumes that task: set `task_runs[i].finished_at = current_time` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `i` with that write); update `direction` if needed; return. The next report in the same local progress drain discovers the next task via the same live recompute.
  - If the resolved next task's `task_kind == "stop"`, the callback is a no-op on the route side regardless of whether the reported block matches: stops are served only by dispatcher-committed completion through `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` → `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` → `_enter_task_arrival(...)`. The head-advance callback must not record `finished_at` for a `stop` task.
  - If the reported block does not match the next task's target block, the callback is a no-op on the route side. Coverage of an intermediate task that the train physically passed through without entering is reconciled by the next Phase 1's covered-task index advance — Phase 3 does not run that reconciliation.
  - The callback must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, must not arm a future planning cursor, and must not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.

  Head-entry route progress is not gated by depart-acceptance: if the head enters a `waypoint` task's resolved block before departure acceptance, the callback still consumes that task.
- When the entered resource is a block, that same dispatcher-owned entry handoff calls `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry("block", block_id, train_id, path_id)`, which routes to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)` — the only normal runtime path that marks a block physically `OCCUPIED`. It consumes any same-train dispatcher reservation for that block because the resource has moved from future authority into train-owned physical occupancy.
- When the entered resource is a switch, the same dispatcher-owned entry handoff calls `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry("switch", switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)`, which routes to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)`; entry semantics, conflict cases, and flank-lock conversion are owned by the **SwitchManager** section and step `83a2` of the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train uses through the switch tile, derived by `PathDispatcher` from the controller's `ResourceEvent.entry_edge` + `switch_exit_edge` (in turn populated from the train's `TileTraversal`). The dispatcher must always pass a non-`None` route for switch physical-entry events: a switch in the middle of a path always has neighbouring tiles on both sides so `_derive_switch_route(...)` always resolves an entry and an exit edge. A `route is None` argument is a v1 contract violation; `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` reports it through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** with `conflict_kind = FatalConflictKind.MALFORMED_ROUTE_KEY` rather than writing any occupancy state.
- Physical occupancy updates still happen even when no `ACTIVE` path remains for that train. If `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` is already clear because the old authority was completed or cancelled, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` must still apply the resource-typed physical entry update via `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` and simply skip path-progress / completion bookkeeping because there is no active authority row left to update. Clean physical entry records the reported block or switch as occupied by this `train_id` and consumes same-train future claims. A conflicting physical entry is not a normal workflow: it enters the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** — no silent overwrite of the prior owner, no recoverable-cancellation treatment, no continued route or dispatcher bookkeeping; the simulation freezes for manual/admin repair and the runtime failure release action defined there.
- A live dispatcher-owned authority path stays `ACTIVE` until it is either `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED`; entering the final block/resource only updates progress and completion predicates. There is no other lifecycle state between them.
- Tail-vacate release remains resource-local and dispatcher-mediated. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` reports vacated resources in `tail_vacated_resources`, and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` releases each vacated resource by calling `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)` after it has processed any `entered_resources` in that same report. The coordinator routes the release to `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` for blocks and to `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)` for switches. `direction` is required for blocks (the vacated block's forward `exit_edge`) and ignored for switches. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train used through the switch tile (derived by `PathDispatcher` from the vacating `ResourceEvent.entry_edge` + `switch_exit_edge`); it is the canonical key into `Switch.physical_occupants` and is ignored for blocks. The dispatcher must always pass a non-`None` route for switch tail-vacate events for the same reason as physical entry above. When the supplied `route` matches a current `Switch.physical_occupants` entry owned by this `train_id`, that single entry is removed; when no remaining routes are owned by this train, train-owned physical protection for that switch is also cleared and the neighbour flank-release walk runs. When the supplied `route` does not match any `physical_occupants` entry but `train_id` still owns at least one entry on this switch (route key drifted from the original entry, e.g. controller emitted a stale `ResourceEvent`), the manager emits a `NotificationCenter` diagnostic naming the train, the switch, and the mismatched route, then scans `physical_occupants` for entries owned by `train_id`, removes the entry that geometrically matches (or, when no entry geometrically matches, removes the single entry owned by this train if there is exactly one), and runs the same flank-release walk. Active progress continues to resolve through `PathDispatcher`'s registries rather than from `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan`, but tail-release logic after authority release must resolve from the resource itself so it still works after there is no active dispatcher authority. The single `resource_type` string branch lives inside the coordinator method; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on it.
- `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` is not a deferred-release hook. Once a path is no longer `ACTIVE`, any later physical release is resource-local and triggered by tail-vacate observations (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches).

**Controller/train sync**

- `TrainController.tick(delta_seconds)` owns ATC, ATO, and manual-control runtime calculations; target speed and speed-limit reason live only in the canonical controller lookahead payload, never as standalone scalars (see **Design Direction**).
- `Train` should keep only persistent/configuration state in sync from the simulation loop.

**Tick-order dependency**

- For one train's transaction, Phase 1 must run before Phase 2 because the controller should read path tiles that already exist.
- For one train's transaction, Phase 2 must run before Phase 3 because the dispatcher should read positions that physics has just produced.
- Physics progress and route-level reactions are synchronous within the same per-train transaction: a boundary crossing reported through `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` is committed by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` and any route callback before the next train's transaction starts.

## Units

**Units contract**

- All deceleration math is expressed in a single unit system. `SpeedProfile.deceleration_rate`, `emergency_brake_rate`, `friction_rate`, and `acceleration_rate` are all in `km/h/s`, and `TrainPhysics` and the controller braking-distance helpers consume them directly in those units (converting to `cm/s` / `cm/s^2` only through the model-scale helpers when integrating distance). There is no second deceleration rate in other units to reconcile: braking is driven entirely by the `SpeedProfile` rates, so no per-layer unit reinterpretation occurs.
- One canonical stopped predicate is used everywhere: `TrainController.is_stopped()` with `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH = 0.1`, reading controller-owned `current_speed_kmh`. `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH` is the global "considered stopped" speed threshold; it is distinct from any ATC zero-speed limit. The "ATC limit" mentioned elsewhere in this document means the ATC-derived speed cap at distance 0 in the canonical lookahead — when that cap is `0` the ATC is granting no clearance distance. Controller-reported stop completion, `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`, `TrainController.commands.set_cab()`, `TrainController.commands.set_mode()`, and `TrainController.eb.release()` must all use this same `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH` helper instead of introducing layer-specific stop thresholds.

**Time sampling**

- v1 timestamps are sampled at the write site, never threaded as method or callback parameters. Whenever this document says `current_time` for a path/route/notification timestamp, it means `time.time()` sampled at the moment the writer commits the write. Every site that records a `last_status_updated_time`, `started_at`, `finished_at`, `last_error.time`, or notification `event_time` calls `time.time()` itself at the moment of the write; callers do not pass the timestamp in. When one writer needs to record both an event time on a notification and a status timestamp on a related entity in the same step, the writer samples `time.time()` once locally and uses that one value for both writes.

**Safety margin globals**

**Why this shape.** Two values, not one, because the right standoff distance from a track-end (where overrun has no platform interaction) is different from the right standoff distance at a platform stop (where overrun affects passenger boarding). One global per terminus type captures the operational distinction without forcing each route or block to author its own value. Putting both values inside `TrainController` lookahead computation gives one canonical projection consumer for ATC, ATO, and stop-completion to read; other layers consume the projected boundary rather than the raw margin number.

- The controller safety margin is two distinct global values, not a single tunable. Both are owned by `TrainController` lookahead computation and are not authored on routes, paths, or per-block fields.
  - `TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`: applied when the authority-end block is bounded by a track-end / dead-end / non-station authority terminus.
  - `PLATFORM_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`: applied when the authority-end block is the resolved terminal block of a platform stop.
- `TrainController` lookahead is the single computation site for these margins. When building the authority-boundary projection in `get_lookahead()`, the controller finds the terminal block entry for `authority_end_block_id`, projects the authority-end block's forward boundary in that directed terminal frame, and counts back from that edge by the selected safety margin to build the ATC/ATO speed curve. ATC is intentionally not trying to drive the train all the way to the physical edge of the cleared/authorized block. This projection is a braking and diagnostics calculation only; it is not route-authored stop-position data and is not a route completion tolerance. Other layers (dispatcher, route, physics, signals) must not re-derive or duplicate this margin; they consume the lookahead-projected boundary instead.

## Canonical Entity Index

**Entity list**

- `TrackSegment`
- `TrackResource` (shared identity base of `Block` and `Switch`)
- `Switch`
- `SwitchManager`
- `Block`
- `BlockManager`
- `ResourceCoordinator`
- `Path`
- `PathDispatcher`
- `Route`
- `RouteRun` (current-code implementation may still use the class name `RouteExecutionState`, but the canonical concept is the per-train route run)
- `Platform`
- `Station`
- `Train`
- `TrainController`
- `TrainPhysics`
- `SpeedProfile`

**Canonical shared contracts**

- `ControllerProgressReport` schema is defined once under the **ControllerProgressReport schema** heading in the **Dispatcher progress/completion flow** section. Every producer and consumer refers back to that heading by name.
- `Path` field schema is defined once in the Path section and every producer/consumer should refer back to it by name instead of reconstructing the field set piecemeal.
- `Path.path_tiles` tile-entry schema is defined once in the Path section and every controller/UI/physics geometry projection should refer back to it by name.
- `Path.resource_sequence` entry schema is defined once in the Path section and every controller/UI/physics projection should refer back to it by name.
- `Platform` / `Station` ownership and `RailwayNetwork` reverse-index contracts are defined once in the Platform And Station section and every route/planning/lookup caller should refer back to that section by name.
- `TrainController.ops.state` state-machine table is defined once in the TrainController section and every caller should use that same state set and transition meaning.
- `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` schema is defined once in the TrainController section and every producer/consumer should refer back to it by name.
- `RouteExecutor` path-event callback schema (`RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` / `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`) is defined once in the Route section and every callback chain should refer back to it by name.
- The hardware interface boundary (`OutputInterface`, `SwitchOutputInterface`, `InputInterface`, their simulated defaults, the logical-id → physical-channel rule, simulated fault injection, and the safe state) is defined once in the **Power** section under **Hardware interface boundary**; every hardware-facing site refers back to it by name.

## TrackResource

**Resource base contract**

- `TrackResource` is the shared base class of `Block` and `Switch`. It carries the common identity surface every dispatcher-facing caller needs: `resource_id` (the entity's primary ID, projected as a `@property` over `block_id` / `switch_id`), `resource_type` (the literal string `"block"` or `"switch"`), `name`, `occupied_by`, `disabled`, and `manual`. All other entity-specific fields stay on the concrete subclass.
- `TrackResource` declares no reserve/release/lock methods. Per the **BlockManager** and **SwitchManager** canonical homes, the manager layer is the sole mutator for reservation, occupancy, CAUTION, and lock fields; `Block` and `Switch` remain data containers in v1.
- `Path.resource_sequence[*].resolve(network)` returns the live `TrackResource` for an entry, or `None` if the resource is not registered. The entry remains the source of truth for path-local geometry (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`); the helper is purely a registry read so callers that want polymorphic access to the underlying entity do not duplicate the `network.blocks.get(...)` / `network.switches.get(...)` branch.

**`disabled` and `manual` availability flags**

This subsection is the canonical home for both flags. `Block`, `Switch`, **Platform And Station** (for logical platforms and stations, which are not `TrackResource` subclasses but follow the same rules), `PathDispatcher`, `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `ResourceCoordinator`, `RailwayPathfinder`, and `RouteExecutor` all refer back to this subsection by name rather than restating its rules. The flag name `manual` here refers only to the resource availability flag and is unrelated to the train controller's manual driving mode; in prose, always qualify as `Block.manual` / `Switch.manual` / `Platform.manual` / `Station.manual`, or as "the `manual` availability flag," to avoid confusion with `TrainController.control_mode == "manual"`.

- `disabled: bool = False` is the physical-absence flag. A disabled resource behaves like missing track / an open circuit: every automatic and operator-initiated code path treats it as unavailable. Pathfinding rejects any candidate that traverses it; `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` rejects it; `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` and manual/API switch-state commands fail against it; route target resolution rejects it as a `BlockTarget` / `PlatformTarget` / `StationTarget`; and a physical entry is the canonical unrecoverable fatal physical-entry conflict (`conflict_kind = FatalConflictKind.DISABLED_RESOURCE_ENTRY`, see the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**). `disabled` has no override — no operator command can bypass it.
- `manual: bool = False` is the virtual-disable flag. A manual resource is physically present and live, but every automatic system treats it as if it were disabled. The structural differences from `disabled` are:
  - **Operator override.** A `manual_override: bool = False` parameter is carried by `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, and `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`. When `manual_override = True` is set on the call, manual resources participate normally: pathfinding includes them, block reservation accepts them, switch lock acquisition accepts them, and switch-state commands proceed. When `manual_override = False` (or omitted), manual resources are filtered out exactly like disabled ones. The override flows through the entire acquisition chain — `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(...)`, `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`, `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`, `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` — so the eligibility decisions can see it. `manual_override` relaxes the `manual` filter and the `Block.allowed_travel_directions` filter; it never relaxes the `disabled` filter. When `manual_override = True`, the candidate `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` need not be present in `block.allowed_travel_directions` for pathfinding, reservation, or terminal-direction acceptance, and the block's own `allowed_travel_directions` set is not mutated.
  - **Physical entry is not a fatal conflict.** A physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) resource is treated by `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` / `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` as ordinary clean entry. `occupied_by` is set normally, flank-lock conversion happens normally, and no `FatalConflictKind` is reported. The `manual` flag filters future planning, not present occupancy.
  - **No topology notification.** Toggling `manual` on a switch does not change connectivity and does not require a `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(...)` notification (toggling `disabled` does, because connectivity does change).
- `manual_override = True` always originates from an explicit operator decision, and the operator surface is route-backed: an operator-initiated route (for example a Quick Route request) stores `Route.manual_override = True`, and `RouteExecutor` passes the assigned route's `manual_override` into every `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` call for that run, which forwards it through the acquisition chain. `RouteExecutor` never originates `manual_override = True` itself, and every automatic/scheduled route leaves `Route.manual_override = False`, so automatic plans never touch manual resources. Request handlers never call `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` directly (see **Layered responsibilities**); they carry the operator's override on the route they create.
- When both `disabled` and `manual` are true on the same resource, `disabled` wins everywhere: the resource is unconditionally off-limits and no `manual_override` reaches it. The two flags are independent booleans (both may be true at once); no invariant requires them to be mutually exclusive.
- Operator/admin code may toggle `manual` to either value at any time without preconditions, because `manual` is a future-planning filter and does not disturb already-held reservations, already-set `occupied_by`, or already-acquired locks. Operator/admin code may toggle `disabled = True` only when the resource has no physical occupancy, no live reservation, no live `CAUTION` (blocks only), no future locks (switches only), and no train-owned protection (switches only); the per-entity rules in the **Switch entity** and **Block** sections own those preconditions and `Platform.disabled` / `Station.disabled` toggles in **Platform And Station** are unconditional for the logical entities themselves. v1 has no force-cleanup workflow that bypasses these preconditions.

**Availability flag REST API surface**
