This subsection lists the v1 REST surface for reading and writing the `disabled` and `manual` flags on every entity that owns them, plus the `manual_override` operator parameter on path and switch-state commands. The endpoints reuse existing routes; only the request body and parameter list change.

| Endpoint | Body / parameters | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `PATCH /api/blocks/<block_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Toggles the two fields on the named block. Either field may be omitted to leave it unchanged. Setting `disabled = True` runs the **Block** admin-reject preconditions (occupancy / reservation / `CAUTION`) and returns HTTP 409 on rejection; setting `manual` to either value always succeeds. Returns the updated block payload including both fields. |
| `PATCH /api/switches/<switch_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Same shape as the block PATCH. Setting `disabled = True` runs the **Switch entity** admin-reject preconditions (occupancy / future locks / train-owned protection) and returns HTTP 409 on rejection; setting `manual` always succeeds. Toggling `disabled` also fires `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)`; toggling `manual` does not. |
| `PATCH /api/platform/<platform_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Toggles the two fields on the named platform. Both succeed unconditionally — the flags affect only future target resolution per **Platform And Station**. |
| `PATCH /api/station/<station_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Toggles the two fields on the named station. Both succeed unconditionally. |
| `PUT /api/switches/<switch_id>/state` | `{ "position": int, "manual_override": bool }` (`manual_override` defaults to `False`) | Operator-initiated switch position change. Routes through `SwitchManager.request_position_change(switch_id, position, manual_override=...)`. A manual switch fails the request through the `command_result` notification unless `manual_override = True`; a disabled switch always fails. |
| `POST /api/paths` | existing path-creation body extended with `"manual_override": bool` (defaults to `False`) | Quick Route: operator-initiated movement request. The handler constructs a `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route with a single `task_kind == "stop"` task for the requested destination (per the one-off reposition flow under **Concrete departure calls**), stores the body's `manual_override` on that route (`Route.manual_override`), assigns the train, and returns accepted/pending. The handler never calls `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` directly; Phase 1 creates the path and passes the route's `manual_override` down the acquisition chain. When `True`, manual blocks/switches participate in candidate search and acquisition. Disabled resources are never reached. |
| `PUT /api/paths/<path_id>/extend` | existing extend body extended with `"manual_override": bool` (defaults to `False`) | Operator retarget of the train's current movement. The handler runs the operator retargeting flow from **Route Edit And Reconciliation** (abort current run + assign a fresh `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route carrying the body's `manual_override`). It never calls `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` directly; route-driven extension toward the armed target is automatic in Phase 1. |

All four entity PATCH endpoints emit a single canonical `command_result` notification per the **Runtime notifications** contract — `kind = "command_result"`, payload `{command_id, command_name, <entity>_id, success, reason}` — regardless of whether the request mutated `disabled`, `manual`, or both. On 409 rejections the response body carries the reason in the same shape as other admin-reject contracts (no parallel error format).

GET endpoints (`GET /api/blocks/<id>`, `GET /api/switches/<id>`, `GET /api/platform/<id>`, `GET /api/station/<id>`) include both `disabled` and `manual` in their response payload so the editor UI can render the current values without a second round-trip.

**Entity-editor UI controls**

The UI design language for these toggles lives in [`docs/DESIGN_LANGUAGE.md`](docs/DESIGN_LANGUAGE.md), not in this document. The doc-level contract is: every entity editor panel (Block, Switch, Platform, Station) exposes a control that lets the operator read and write the entity's `disabled` and `manual` flags through the PATCH endpoints above. The Route editor exposes the per-field push-policy control for the allowlisted fields (`priority`, `acquisition_mode`); the RouteRun editor exposes per-run override write/clear for the same allowlisted fields. The visual vocabulary (segmented control, toggle, card composition) is owned by `DESIGN_LANGUAGE.md`; this document does not duplicate it.

## TrackSegment

**Path locking**

- Switch locking is owned entirely by the `Switch` entity (multi-owner `Switch.locks`); track-segment locking is not stored on `TrackSegment`.
- Temporary reservation direction lives at the block/path-plan level: `Block.reserved_direction` is the dispatcher-owned block reservation direction; `Path.resource_sequence[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge` plus nested `path_tiles[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge` carry the per-resource and per-tile traversal direction.
- `TrackSegment` keeps static geometry, classification, and `switch_id`. If the segment is a switch, callers resolve the active segment from `Switch.position` in `network.switches` via `switch_id`. `TrackSegment` does not store live switch state.

**Track-segment role and traversal**

- The retained `TrackSegment` schema in v1 is explicit:
  - persistent fields kept on `TrackSegment`: `x`, `y`, `track_bits`, `dead_end_ports`, `signal_ids`, `switch_id`, `length`, `curve_radius`, `wear_level`, `last_maintenance`, `maintenance_count`
  - computed read kept on `TrackSegment`: `track_type`
  - removed live runtime fields: `_switch_state`, `occupied_by`, `reserved_by`, `locked_by_path`, `locked_state`, `path_direction`
  - `TrackSegment` does not store station, platform, or block membership.
- Entity collection ownership in v1 is strictly hierarchical, and each level owns the IDs of the immediately lower level:
  - `Station.platform_ids: List[str]` is the canonical list of `Platform` IDs in that station group.
  - `Platform.ordered_blocks: List[str]` is the canonical ordered list of `Block` IDs in that platform.
  - `Block.ordered_tiles: List[BlockTileEntry]` is the canonical ordered list of `TrackSegment` tiles in that block, where each `BlockTileEntry` carries `(x, y, entry_edge)` — see the **Block** section for the full shape.
  - `TrackSegment` does not store any back-reference for station/platform/block membership.
  All four entities (`TrackSegment`, `Block`, `Platform`, `Station`) are registered with `RailwayNetwork`. Reverse lookups ("which platform owns this block?", "which block owns this tile?") are answered through `RailwayNetwork`-owned indices, not through fields stored on the lower-level entity.
- **Non-empty parent rule.** Every grouping entity in this chain always owns at least one child: a `Station` keeps at least one `Platform` in `platform_ids`, a `Platform` keeps at least one `Block` in `ordered_blocks`, and a `Block` keeps at least one tile in `ordered_tiles`. This holds at create time and edit time — a grouping entity is never created empty, and no edit may leave one empty. When an edit removes a grouping entity's last child — `remove_tile_from_block` / `remove_tiles_from_block` taking a block's last tile, a platform edit that removes the platform's last block, or `remove_platform_from_station_group` taking a station's last platform — the parent is deleted in that same operation instead of being left empty, routing through `delete_block` / `delete_platform` / `delete_station`. The auto-delete cascades upward: deleting a `Block` first removes it from any owning `Platform.ordered_blocks`, and if that emptied the platform the `Platform` is deleted too; deleting a `Platform` first removes it from any owning `Station.platform_ids`, and if that emptied the station the `Station` is deleted too. Deleting a grouping entity (the auto-delete here or an explicit `delete_*`) never deletes the tiles, blocks, or platforms it grouped — those lower-level resources keep existing on their own, just no longer attached. The last-child removal and the parent's deletion commit together: if the parent's own delete preconditions reject it, the child removal that triggered it is rejected as a whole, so an empty grouping entity is never stored.
- `TrackSegment` does not carry static or temporary traffic-direction policy in v1. Static allowed travel direction is block-owned, through `Block.allowed_travel_directions`; route/requested travel direction is route-owned, through `RouteExecutor` resolving `RouteTask.task_direction` into `ResolvedRouteTarget.requested_destination_direction`; temporary reservation direction is dispatcher-owned, through `Block.reserved_direction`; and finer-grained traversal frame data lives on `Path.resource_sequence[*]` and nested `path_tiles[*]`.
- Tile-to-platform / tile-to-block / tile-to-station lookups go through `RailwayNetwork`-owned reverse indices computed from `Block.ordered_tiles`, `Platform.ordered_blocks`, and `Station.platform_ids`. `station_id` is reserved for the logical multi-platform `Station` identifier; `platform_id` is reserved for the concrete `Platform` identifier; the tile-level entity stores neither.
- `TrackSegment` is not the authoritative store for live switch state or switch locks. Switch state lives on `Switch`; lock ownership is expressed through `Switch.locks` (for switches) and `Block.reserved_by_path` / `Block.reserved_for_train` (for blocks).
- If the segment classification says the tile is a switch, `TrackSegment.switch_id` should point to the authoritative `Switch` entity in the network entity database. Live switch state and switch lock ownership should live only on that `Switch` entity.
- Split planning-time traversal enumeration from live-state traversal reads.
- Live traversal helpers:
  - `TrackSegment.get_active_exits(entry_edge, resolver) -> List[str]`
  - `TrackSegment.get_active_entry_for_exit(exit_edge, resolver) -> Optional[str]`
  where `resolver` is the network or an equivalent switch-state resolver that can read `Switch.position` via `switch_id`.
- If the resolved owning `Switch` exists and `Switch.disabled == True`, the live traversal helpers return no usable traversal for that switch tile. `Switch.manual` does not affect live traversal here, because a manual switch is physically present and connected; the `manual` filter is applied only by the planning/acquisition layer per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- Planning-time helper:
  - `TrackSegment.enumerate_traversals(entry_edge) -> List[(exit_edge, required_switch_state)]`
  This helper enumerates every legal geometric traversal through the tile independent of the switch's current live state. For an ordinary non-switch tile, it yields the one legal `(exit_edge, None)` pair. For a switch tile, it yields the legal `(exit_edge, required_switch_state)` pairs that dispatcher path search may acquire.
- `TrackSegment.enumerate_traversals(...)` remains a pure geometric helper and does not inspect `Switch.disabled` or `Switch.manual`. Callers that resolve the owning switch through the network entity layer must drop all enumerated traversals for that switch tile when `Switch.disabled == True`, so pathfinding/wayfinding treats a disabled switch as absent connectivity. The `Switch.manual` filter is applied higher up at the pathfinder layer (see the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home and `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)`), where the `manual_override` parameter can keep or drop the traversal.
- `TrackSegment.get_active_exits(...)` and `TrackSegment.get_active_entry_for_exit(...)` map one edge through the currently active segment selected by `Switch.position`. `TrackSegment.enumerate_traversals(...)` is the planner-facing geometry contract and must not consult live switch alignment.
- For Y-shape switch geometry in v1, branch-to-branch traversal is invalid. `TrackSegment.enumerate_traversals(...)` must not yield branch-to-branch options.
- `PathDispatcher` path construction/extension and BFS switch derivation use `TrackSegment.enumerate_traversals(...)`. `TrainPhysics._advance_end()`, tail tracing, and other live movement readers use the active-state helpers. Switch traversal never reads track-stored live state.

**Track-segment invariants**

- Switch-integrity while a train spans a switch: `Switch.physical_occupants` (read diagnostically through `Switch.occupied_by`) plus the still-held `Switch.locks` entries prevent a switch from flipping out from under the spanning train. No global path lock is needed for this guarantee.
- Tail tracing still uses the live-state entry/exit helper, but it now resolves through the live `Switch.position` per the directed resolver contract in **Track-segment role and traversal** rather than through a separately persisted track-tile field.

## Switch Entity

**Switch entity**

- `Switch` is a `TrackResource` subclass; the shared identity surface (`resource_id`, `resource_type == "switch"`, `name`, `occupied_by`, `disabled`, `manual`) comes from the **TrackResource** canonical home. Switch-only fields below extend that base.
- `Switch` should become a first-class resource entity at the same level as a block.
- The retained `Switch` schema in v1 is explicit:
  - identity/location: `switch_id`, `x`, `y` (and the `name` / `occupied_by` / `disabled` / `manual` fields inherited from `TrackResource`)
  - retained physical/configuration fields from current code: `main_length_cm`, `diverge_length_cm`, `main_radius_cm`, `diverge_radius_cm`, `switch_type`, `notes`
  - new authoritative live/runtime fields: `position`, `locks`, `flank_protection`, `train_protection_claims`
  - `Switch` holds no `simulation_controller` back-reference and never notifies another entity itself. It is a pure data/primitive entity: `Switch.acquire_lock(...)`, `Switch.release_lock(...)`, and `Switch.apply_position_change(...)` mutate only switch-local state. The cross-resource `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(...)` notify that must follow a `Switch.position` write is owned by `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`, the single entry that pairs the write with the notify and the physical throw + continuity check (see below). The dispatcher-facing entry points for path-driven multi-switch acquisition / release / clean physical entry / tail-vacate live on **SwitchManager** (which calls these `Switch` primitives); `PathDispatcher` reaches `SwitchManager` through the **ResourceCoordinator** facade, and `SwitchManager` reaches `BlockManager` through its own `simulation_controller` reference.
- `Switch.disabled` and `Switch.manual` are the availability flags defined canonically in the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** subsection under **TrackResource**. The switch-specific behaviour those rules imply:
  - A disabled switch behaves like missing track / an open circuit. Live traversal helpers return no usable traversal through it (see the **Track-segment role and traversal** rule), `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` fails, manual/API switch-state commands fail, and pathfinding skips it. Because the switch is treated as physically absent, `TrainPhysics` should stop at the last reachable live edge; if a controller progress report nevertheless says the train entered that disabled switch, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` routes the entry to `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` → `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`, which must treat it as a fatal physical-entry conflict and report `conflict_kind = FatalConflictKind.DISABLED_RESOURCE_ENTRY` to `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`; it must not silently set `Switch.occupied_by`.
  - A manual switch (not also disabled) is physically present and live. Live traversal helpers still see it; pathfinding still skips it; `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` and manual/API switch-state commands still fail — unless the calling operator path carries `manual_override = True`, in which case those operations proceed normally. A controller-reported physical entry into a manual switch is **not** a fatal conflict; `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` treats it as ordinary clean entry, sets `Switch.occupied_by`, and performs the canonical flank-lock conversion.
  - Operator/admin code must reject setting `Switch.disabled = True` on a switch that is physically occupied, has future locks, or has train-owned protection; the operator/admin must remove the conflicting train, cancel the holding path, or release the train-owned protection through the ordinary runtime workflows before the disable request is accepted. Toggling `Switch.manual` to either value is always permitted at any time (no preconditions); it does not disturb existing locks or `occupied_by` and is purely a future-planning filter.
  - Toggling `Switch.disabled` changes connectivity and must notify `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` (or an equivalent topology-availability callback) so directional `CAUTION` reevaluation sees the open circuit immediately. Toggling `Switch.manual` does not change connectivity and does not require this notification.
- `position: int` is the authoritative physical switch alignment, with values `0 = MAIN`, `1 = DIVERGE`. `Switch.position` is the single authoritative alignment everywhere — routing, geometry, locks, and traversal all read it. It is the *commanded* alignment; the switch throw continuity check (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role) is what confirms the physical hardware actually reached it, and in simulation that check always confirms because the reading is derived from `Switch.position` itself. When traversal code needs the active switch segment, it should resolve that alignment from the `Switch` entity rather than keeping a second authoritative live copy on `TrackSegment`.
- `occupied_by: Optional[str]` is a derived read-only projection on `Switch` that returns `next(iter(self.physical_occupants.values()), None)` — any one current occupant, or `None` if no train is on the tile. The `Switch` override has no stored field and no writer (it overrides the `TrackResource` base slot on `Switch` only; `Block.occupied_by` remains a stored field with its own writers). The authoritative store is `physical_occupants: Dict[FrozenSet[str], str]`, a route-keyed map from the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train is using to its `train_id`. Each entry says "this train is physically traversing this route on the tile right now"; entries are written on switch-resource entry and cleared on tail-vacate. Two route entries may coexist when the routes do not physically conflict per `switch.routes_conflict(...)` — e.g. a scissors crossover in DIVERGE state holds both NW (one train) and SE (another train) simultaneously without collision because those two arcs are in opposite quadrants and share no point. Reservation/lock ownership remains separate. The map's value is a `train_id` (not a `route_run_id` or `path_id`): physical occupancy is a property of the train's body and persists even when the train has no active path or route run, so only `train_id` keys it reliably. Conflict predicate: two routes conflict iff they share any edge midpoint (their edge frozensets intersect) or both pass through tile center (NS and EW straights only). The only geometrically non-conflict pairs are `{NW, SE}` and `{NE, SW}`.
- Physical occupancy (`physical_occupants` / its `occupied_by` read) and reservation ownership (`Switch.locks`) answer different questions and are never merged: `physical_occupants` says which train is bodily on the tile right now; `Switch.locks` says which path/train has *reserved* the switch — and a lock holder need not be the train physically on the tile (a flank-protection lock is sourced from a *neighbour* switch's acquisition, `source_switch_id != None`). `Switch.locks` is the canonical "route owner"/reservation layer.
- **Intrusion check.** A train that is physically on the switch must hold a compatible reservation for the route it occupies. This is enforced at physical entry: `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)` consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock for that switch; if the entering train holds no lock granting `route`, the entry is a fatal intrusion — the manager reports it through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** with `conflict_kind = FatalConflictKind.SWITCH_INTRUSION` and writes no occupancy state. The check fires only on a *newly-entered* switch tile (the physical-entry event produced from `added_tiles`); a switch already in the train's seeded span at `create_path(...)` time is never re-entered and so never reaches this check (and was never newly locked, since seeded already-covered switches are not acquired for fresh locks). This is a general comparison of physical presence against reservation ownership, not a per-workflow branch; once a train has cleanly entered (its lock consumed), its continued presence is protected by `physical_occupants` plus `train_protection_claims`, not by a retained lock.
- **Switch occupancy vs reservation — the three stores at a glance.** These fields are distinct and must never be conflated: `physical_occupants` (and its `occupied_by` read) answer "who is bodily on the tile," while `Switch.locks` answers "who has reserved it." A lock holder need not be physically present, and a physical occupant must hold a lock (or it is an intrusion).

  | Field | Data format | What it represents / does | Written by | Read by (consumers) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `Switch.physical_occupants` | `Dict[FrozenSet[str], str]` — key is the unordered `route = frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})`, value is `train_id` | **Authoritative** physical occupancy: which train is bodily traversing which route on the tile right now. Route-keyed so two non-conflicting routes (e.g. `{NW, SE}`) can coexist. | `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` records `[route] = train_id` on clean entry; `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)` removes the matching entry on tail-vacate | The entry conflict gate (`Switch.routes_conflict(...)`), the intrusion check, the tail-vacate release procedure, the switch-integrity guard, and the `occupied_by` mirror refresh |
  | `Switch.occupied_by` | `Optional[str]` — a single `train_id` or `None` (overrides the `TrackResource` base slot on `Switch` only; `Block.occupied_by` stays stored) | **Non-authoritative** single-value diagnostic projection of `physical_occupants` (reflects any one current occupant). Never the gate for an entry/vacate decision. | Derived — has no writer; recomputed on read from `physical_occupants` as `next(iter(self.physical_occupants.values()), None)` | Diagnostics, UI / data-flow projection, and human-readable "is anyone on this switch" reads |
  | `Switch.locks` | `Dict` keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id)`; each claim stores `path_id`, `source_switch_id: Optional[str]`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, `route` (non-`None` for a main claim, `None` for a flank claim) | The **reservation / "route owner"** layer — future authority plus flank protection. A main claim (`source_switch_id is None`) is this path's own use of the switch; a flank claim (`source_switch_id != None`) is protection sourced from a *neighbour* switch's acquisition. A lock holder need not be physically on the tile. | `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` → `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)`; removed by `Switch.release_lock(...)` / `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(...)`; the main claim is *consumed* at head entry and flank claims are *converted* into `train_protection_claims` | `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` eligibility, the cross-train route check (`routes_conflict`), the intrusion check (entrant must hold a compatible lock), and `is_locked()` / `can_lock(state)` |

  (Related: `train_protection_claims` — keyed by `(source_switch_id, train_id)` — is the train-owned physical protection that a consumed flank claim becomes after head entry; it is cleared on the source switch's tail-vacate, not by a path release.)
- Future authority on a switch is held in the multi-owner `locks` collection, keyed canonically by `(path_id, source_switch_id)` where `source_switch_id` is `None` for a main-usage claim and the originating main switch ID for a flank-protection claim. Each future-authority lock claim stores exactly `path_id`, `source_switch_id: Optional[str]`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, and `route` — the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through this switch. A main-usage claim (`source_switch_id is None`) always carries a non-`None` `route` so the cross-train co-locking check in `acquire_lock(claim)` has the geometry it needs; a flank-protection claim (`source_switch_id is not None`) carries `route = None`. The convenience read `claim_role` (= `"main"` if `source_switch_id is None` else `"flank"`) may still be referenced by name in this document. Path locks do not carry a deferred-release flag in v1.
- `train_protection_claims` is the train-owned physical protection set used after the train head has entered a switch and consumed the path lock. Each claim stores exactly `train_id`, `source_switch_id`, and `locked_state`; it blocks conflicting switch state changes until the source switch tail-vacate event clears it.

**Return-contract principle for `BlockManager` / `Switch` mutators.** Every method returns either `bool` (single success/failure) or a structured `<Method>Result` object (multiple distinguishable failure modes that callers branch on). When the result's "kind" or "reason" field is purely diagnostic (no caller branches on it), it is a free-form `str`, not a new enum. New enums require a caller that actually branches on the values; otherwise they are diagnostic-only and belong as strings.

- `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` takes a structured `claim` payload carrying `path_id`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, optional `source_switch_id` (`None` for a main-usage claim, originating main switch ID for a flank-protection claim), the `route` the path uses through this switch (the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` from the canonical claim shape above — non-`None` for a main-usage claim, `None` for a flank-protection claim), and `manual_override: bool = False` (propagated from the calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` so manual switches are reachable only when the operator explicitly chose to override; see the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home). The input field name `locked_state` matches the stored claim field, so the same name is used everywhere — there is no input-vs-stored renaming inside the helper. `manual_override` is not stored on the resulting recorded lock entry; it is only consulted at the eligibility step inside `acquire_lock(...)`. It must first check `Switch.locks`, `Switch.occupied_by`, and `Switch.train_protection_claims`. Compatible future switch locks may be shared only when no switch throw is required and only within the cross-train rule defined two bullets below: if `Switch.position` already equals `claim.locked_state` and all existing future locks / train-owned protection are compatible with `claim.locked_state` and the new claim is not a cross-train primary collision, `acquire_lock(claim)` may record another future lock claim without moving the switch. If the current state differs, `acquire_lock(claim)` may throw the switch only when no physical occupancy or train-owned protection exists and no future lock requires another state. Physically occupied switches are not acquirable for future authority except for same-train already-covered span entries that are seeded into `Path.resource_sequence` and are not newly locked. Returns a plain `bool` at the entity level (`True` = recorded or idempotently present, `False` = conflict). The specific failure reason here is diagnostic-only and no caller branches on it, so per the **Return-contract principle** above the entity does not return a structured result. `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` wraps that `bool` into a `LockAcquireResult(success: bool, reason: Optional[str])` for manager-level callers, synthesizing the diagnostic `reason` (e.g., `"disabled switch"`, `"manual switch (no override)"`, `"locked state conflicts with existing claim"`, `"physical occupancy by other train"`, `"cross-train primary lock conflict"`, `"protection conflict"`).
- `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` must fail immediately when `Switch.disabled == True`. It must also fail when `Switch.manual == True` and `claim.manual_override == False`; when `Switch.manual == True` and `claim.manual_override == True`, the manual filter is bypassed and the remaining eligibility checks (locks, occupancy, protection) run normally.
- Every successful `Switch.position` mutation path, whether it came from dispatcher lock acquisition or from a manual/API switch-state command, must use the same canonical mutation workflow: validate availability per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home (always reject when `Switch.disabled == True`; reject when `Switch.manual == True` and the caller did not pass `manual_override = True`); validate that no future lock, physical occupancy, or train-owned protection requires a different state; write `Switch.position` (the commanded alignment); then notify `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)`; then, only when that write actually changed the alignment, drive the physical throw through `SwitchPowerController` and run the throw continuity check (both defined in the **Power** section, Switch power-controller role). The validate-and-write step is `Switch.apply_position_change(new_position)` (a pure data operation that does not notify any external entity, drive any motor, or run verification). The cross-resource notification to `BlockManager` is paired with that write inside `SwitchManager.request_position_change(switch_id, new_position, manual_override=False)`, which is the single public entry point for position-change requests; admin / API / UI handlers route through the manager so the notification is never skipped, and they pass `manual_override = True` only when the operator has explicitly chosen to override a manual switch. Dispatcher lock acquisition that must throw the switch routes its `Switch.position` write through the same `SwitchManager`-owned paired write+notify (the throw inside `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` calls `apply_position_change(...)` and the `BlockManager` notify together, the same pairing `request_position_change(...)` performs), so acquisition and manual/API commands both pair the write with the notify and with the `SwitchPowerController` throw + continuity check; nothing writes `Switch.position` without that pairing. `Switch` itself does not hold a back-reference to `SimulationController`; `SwitchManager` owns the runtime wiring and reaches `BlockManager` through its own `simulation_controller` reference. Pure geometric editor writes (placement, connectivity wiring, static field edits that do not affect occupancy or locks) do not invoke runtime methods and remain safe without any runtime surface.
- A non-dispatcher/manual/API switch-state command follows that same canonical workflow and may change `Switch.position` only when the availability check accepts the request and no lock, physical occupancy, or train-owned protection blocks the requested state. If any such claim exists, the state-change request must fail rather than bypassing the workflow. Such a manual/API command is a runtime mutation routed through `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)` and therefore requires the **SwitchManager**'s `simulation_controller` to be wired; it must raise in pure editor mode and is available only when the editor session has instantiated a runtime surface.
- Manual/API switch-state command result delivery follows the canonical `NotificationCenter` contract. The API/UI request handler returns accepted/pending once the request has been validated and forwarded to the targeted `Switch`. The actual command result — success or failure — surfaces through `NotificationCenter` as a `command_result` notification with `source = "Switch"`, `severity = "info"` on success or `"warning"` on failure, payload `{command_id, command_name, switch_id, success, reason}`. A throw whose continuity check did not confirm (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role) surfaces here with `success = False` and the continuity `reason`; `Switch.position` is not rolled back. This matches the operator-command notification contract under **Runtime notifications**; request handlers must not synthesize a synchronous error response that bypasses this surface.
- A disabled switch cannot be targeted by a manual/API switch-state command in v1; the request must fail through that same `command_result` notification rather than mutating the disabled switch. A manual switch can be targeted by a manual/API switch-state command only when the command carries `manual_override = True`; without the override, the request fails through the same `command_result` notification. The `manual_override = True` bypass never applies to a disabled switch. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the override contract.
- `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` should be idempotent only for an already-recorded claim on that same canonical claim key with the same `locked_state`, and succeed immediately in that case.
- `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` should allow multiple paths to hold future lock claims on the same switch when those claims all require the same physical `claim.locked_state` and the switch is already in that state. Cross-train primary co-locking (two different `train_id`s both holding `source_switch_id is None` claims) is gated by route geometry: both claims must carry a non-`None` `route` (the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through this switch), and the new claim is accepted iff its route does not physically conflict with any existing primary claim's route per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`. This is what lets a scissors crossover in DIVERGE state hold one path on NW and another on SE simultaneously. In v1, primary claims (`source_switch_id is None`) must always carry a non-`None` `route`; `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` rejects with `reason = "missing_route_on_primary_claim"` when any expanded primary request has `route is None`. Flank claims (`source_switch_id is not None`) do not carry a route and are not subject to the cross-train route check. Same-train multi-path co-locking is unaffected by the route check. A blocked cross-train claim falls through the ordinary `SWITCH_LOCKED` failure into `PathDispatcher`'s BEST_EFFORT truncation, which lands the live authority on the block before the contested switch; the holding train's tail-vacate clears its route entry and the blocked train extends past on a later tick.
- `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` must fail if any existing future lock or train-owned protection on that switch requires a different physical state, or if satisfying the request would require throwing a physically occupied switch.
- When the train head enters a switch, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` routes the entry through `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`. That manager consumes that path's main switch lock for the entered switch and converts any path-owned flank locks sourced from that main switch into `train_protection_claims`. From that point onward the occupied switch and its active flank protection are train-owned physical state, not path-owned cleanup.
- `release_lock(path_id, source_switch_id=None)` removes that future-authority claim immediately. It must not inspect train physical occupancy or set a pending flag; an occupied switch is protected by `Switch.occupied_by` and any `train_protection_claims`, not by a retained path lock. Returns `bool`: `True` if a matching claim was found and removed; `False` if no matching claim existed. Idempotent; no exception raised.
- Releasing or rolling back a switch lock claim does **not** restore a previous physical `Switch.position` in v1. `Switch.position` stays at the last successfully written alignment; a later compatible or conflicting path acquisition sets it again if needed.
- `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id, train_id)` performs neighbour-side tail-vacate cleanup. It removes only train-owned physical protection claims where `claim.train_id == train_id` and `claim.source_switch_id == source_switch_id`. This helper manages train-protection claims only; it does not read or mutate `physical_occupants` or `occupied_by`. It is called after the source switch's tail-vacate event has removed that train's `physical_occupants` entry and only when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train on the source switch; it is not a path-release trigger. Returns `bool`: `True` if at least one matching claim was removed; `False` if none. Idempotent.
- `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id, train_id)` does **not** inspect `physical_occupants` or `occupied_by`; the caller (`SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)`) is responsible for the ordering check — invoking it only once the releasing train owns no remaining `physical_occupants` entry on the source switch. The helper purely removes train-owned protection claims matching `(source_switch_id, train_id)`.
- Candidate `end_a` dead-heat tie-break source: `start_locator.physical_end` carries the `end_a` / `end_b` label; the caller passes that field as the dead-heat key, not a `GraphWalkCandidate` field.
- Tail-vacate switch release is keyed on the authoritative route-keyed `Switch.physical_occupants`, not on the single-value `Switch.occupied_by` projection (which surfaces only one of possibly several occupants). It removes the `physical_occupants` entry whose key equals the supplied `route` and whose value equals the releasing `train_id`. Only when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train does it release neighbour-side train protection sourced from that switch. If the supplied `route` matches no entry but the train still owns at least one entry on this switch, the route-drift fallback defined in the canonical tail-vacate procedure applies; if the train owns no entry at all, emit a `NotificationCenter` diagnostic such as `switch <switch_id> release for train <train_id> skipped: train holds no physical occupancy here`.
- `Switch.locks` is a multi-owner lock collection. Multiple compatible entries from the same train (different paths, flank claims sourced from different neighbour switches) always coexist. Primary claims (`source_switch_id is None`) from different trains coexist when their routes (the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` carried on the claim) do not physically conflict per `switch.routes_conflict(...)` — see the cross-train rule defined for `acquire_lock(claim)`. Physical occupancy is route-keyed (`Switch.physical_occupants`); two trains can hold non-conflicting routes on the same tile (e.g. NW + SE on a scissors crossover in DIVERGE state), and the `Switch.occupied_by` field is a non-authoritative mirror of any one current occupant for diagnostic reads.
- The switch entity carries `flank_protection: List[Tuple[str, int]]`. In v1 each tuple is exactly `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)`, where `blocking_state` is the neighbour state that would create the flank conflict if that neighbour were set toward this switch.

**Switch invariants**

- `is_locked()` and `can_lock(state)` should check the canonical `(path_id, source_switch_id)`-keyed future lock collection plus train-owned physical protection, not a single field. `can_lock(state)` means "is there a compatible lock/protection state for this switch right now," not "is there only one owner." Train-owned protection continues to block a conflicting acquisition until the later tail-vacate callback removes it.

## Flank Protection Computation

**Flank protection computation**

- `RailwayNetwork.recompute_flank_protection(switch_id)` is the single canonical owner that computes flank-protection relationships at the network level and stores them on each switch entity (`Switch.flank_protection`) instead of on individual paths. No other entity writes `Switch.flank_protection`.
- Use one canonical global setting `FLANK_PROTECTION_DISTANCE_CM = 5.0` when searching for nearby switches that require mutual locking.
- `RailwayNetwork.recompute_flank_protection(switch_id)` searches the network graph outward from the switch tile (within `FLANK_PROTECTION_DISTANCE_CM`), determines which neighbour states would create a flank conflict, and rebuilds that switch's `flank_protection` list of `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` entries. It is called for each affected switch at switch placement time.
- The same `RailwayNetwork.recompute_flank_protection(...)` runs again on every topology change, for each switch near the change: the editor add/delete-switch and connectivity-edit paths, and the `Switch.disabled` toggle path (the same topology-availability surface that fires `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)`; see the **Switch entity** disable rule). This keeps the stored `flank_protection` from going stale and corrupting acquisition.
- In v1, future flank protection uses the same `Switch.locks` compatibility rule as ordinary future switch use: multiple paths may hold compatible same-state lock claims, and no lock or protection set may demand conflicting physical states. Physical occupancy is route-keyed (`Switch.physical_occupants`): two trains on non-conflicting routes may coexist on the tile, while conflicting routes never do. Future flank-protection claims use the same lock-claim shape as main-usage claims (the canonical `(path_id, source_switch_id, train_id, locked_state, route)` claim under **Switch entity**); flank claims set `source_switch_id` to the main switch whose acquisition created that claim and carry `route = None`, while main-usage claims have `source_switch_id = None` and a non-`None` `route`. When the train head enters the source switch, those future flank claims are consumed into `train_protection_claims` until source-switch tail-vacate clears them. Stored flank-protection data remains `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)`; the acquisition-time `safe_state` is derived from it at the switch-first acquisition step, which is its single canonical definition site (see the route-point exemption under `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`).
- A switch that the same path traverses as a main-usage claim (a route point on that path) is never additionally flank-locked by that path. It is set and locked by its own main claim, which is its own flank protection. The producer still stores the `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` relationship for these switch pairs — the relationship is a property of the static topology, not of any one path — and the route-point exemption is applied at acquisition time, where path membership is known. A non-route-point neighbour that two of the path's route points would pin to opposite states cannot be flank-protected for that path: it is left unlocked when both route points are acquired in one call, or kept only as the earlier route point set it when `best_effort` splits the path across segments (the switch-triangle apex case). See the route-point exemption and the same-path conflict exemption under the switch-first acquisition step (`SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`).

## Platform And Station

**Platform and station model**

- `Platform` and `Station` are canonical first-class entities in v1. Unchanged local fields, helper internals, and serialization details may continue to follow current code unless this document overrides them; the changed contracts below are the canonical ownership and lookup rules.
- `Station.platform_ids` is the canonical ordered grouping list of concrete `Platform` IDs belonging to that logical station. Route planning that resolves a `StationTarget` task_location reads only through that list; it does not inspect tile-level station membership fields.
- `Platform.ordered_blocks` is the canonical ordered physical extent of that concrete platform. Route target resolution, platform-end selection, and platform availability checks all read platform membership from this ordered block list rather than from `TrackSegment.station_id`, `TrackSegment.block_section_id`, or ad hoc tile scans.
- A `Station` always owns at least one `Platform`, and a `Platform` always owns at least one `Block`. Removing the last one deletes the now-empty parent instead of leaving it empty, and that delete cascades upward. See the **Non-empty parent rule** under **TrackSegment** for the full create / edit / auto-delete contract.
- `RailwayNetwork` owns the required reverse indices: at minimum block -> platform, tile -> block, tile -> platform, and tile -> station-group lookups derived from the canonical `Station.platform_ids`, `Platform.ordered_blocks`, and `Block.ordered_tiles` ownership chain. Callers that need membership or reverse ownership resolve it through `RailwayNetwork`; lower-level entities do not keep back-reference membership fields in v1.
- Route resolution, platform availability, current-span coverage checks, and UI/export helpers that need platform/station membership use those `RailwayNetwork`-owned indices plus the canonical `Platform` / `Station` ownership fields. They do not consult tile-level membership.
- `RailwayNetwork.add_station / add_platform / delete_station / delete_platform / extend_station / extend_platform / get_station / get_platform / create_station_group / delete_station_group / add_platform_to_station_group / remove_platform_from_station_group / tile_to_platform_id / tile_to_station_id` are doc-canonical method names but are now implemented as forwarders to `StationRegistry`. The actual platform/station CRUD logic lives in `station_registry.py`. The `platforms`, `station_groups`, and `_tile_to_platform` registries remain stored on `RailwayNetwork` itself; `StationRegistry` reads and writes them via a back-reference. External callers (UI, scenario setup, tests) keep using the network surface unchanged.

**Platform and Station availability flags**

`Platform` and `Station` carry the same `disabled` and `manual` availability flags defined canonically in the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** subsection under **TrackResource**. They are not `TrackResource` subclasses (`Platform` and `Station` are logical entities with no physical occupancy, no directional locks, and no `occupied_by`), but their availability flags follow the same operator-override and automatic-filter rules.

- `Platform.disabled: bool = False` — the platform cannot be the resolved target of any route task. `StationTarget` candidate enumeration excludes the platform from `Station.platform_ids`-derived candidates; a route task with `PlatformTarget(this)` fails its resolution attempt. There is no operator override — a disabled platform is unconditionally off-target. The constituent blocks remain governed by their own `Block.disabled` / `Block.manual` flags; the platform-level flag does not propagate to its blocks. Per the existing "operator/admin edits to platform/block target-resolution inputs ... do not rewrite an already-successful live segment" rule under **Route And RouteExecutor**, toggling `Platform.disabled` mid-flight does not disturb any live segment that already resolved through this platform; it only affects the next time Phase 1 commits a new successful create/extend attempt.
- `Platform.manual: bool = False` — automatic route target resolution treats the platform as if disabled: `StationTarget` candidate enumeration excludes it; a route task with `PlatformTarget(this)` fails its resolution attempt unless the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True` (see the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home). An operator-initiated route carrying `Route.manual_override = True` (for example a Quick Route request) can still land authority on one of the platform's constituent blocks through Phase 1's create/extend with that flag; the dispatcher-level override is what gates the underlying call. The platform-level `manual` flag does not propagate to its blocks.
- `Station.disabled: bool = False` — no `StationTarget` task can resolve to this station; the station's `platform_ids` are excluded from the station-level candidate enumeration. Individual `Platform` records inside the station remain reachable via `PlatformTarget(...)` subject to each platform's own flags. There is no operator override at the station level.
- `Station.manual: bool = False` — every `StationTarget` auto-resolution treats this station as if disabled (all of its `platform_ids` are excluded from candidate enumeration) unless the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True`. Individual platforms inside it remain reachable through their own `PlatformTarget` rules subject to each platform's own flags; operator-initiated routes against the station's constituent blocks remain reachable via the route-carried `manual_override` forwarded to the dispatcher for the underlying blocks.
- Operator/admin code may toggle `Platform.disabled`, `Platform.manual`, `Station.disabled`, and `Station.manual` at any time without preconditions. The flags affect only future target resolution and do not disturb any in-flight authority that has already resolved through the platform/station. When both `disabled` and `manual` are true on the same entity, `disabled` wins (same combination rule as **TrackResource**).

## Block

**Block model**

- `Block` is a `TrackResource` subclass; the shared identity surface (`resource_id`, `resource_type == "block"`, `name`, `occupied_by`, `disabled`, `manual`) comes from the **TrackResource** canonical home. Block-only fields below extend that base.
- `state` is a computed read-only property derived from the following underlying primitive fields:
  - `occupied_by: Optional[str]` — physical train presence (non-None means a train is in the block).
  - `caution: Optional[CautionState]` — physical safety-margin record. `None` when not in caution. `CautionState` carries exactly `exit_edge: "N" | "E" | "S" | "W"` (the departing train's exit edge). The two-field unit is always written together by `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` and cleared together by `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` or `release_path_block_reservations(completed=True)`, so storing them as one Optional record makes the all-or-nothing rule structural rather than enforced by prose. Convenience reads `_is_caution` (= `caution is not None`) and `caution_exit_edge` (= `caution.exit_edge` or `None`) may still be referenced by name throughout this document for readability.
  - `reservation: Optional[BlockReservation]` — the one active path's dispatcher reservation on this block, or `None` when no path holds it. `BlockReservation` carries exactly `path_id: str` (which active path holds the reservation), `train_id: str` (the train the reservation is intended for; separate from `occupied_by` so that `BlockManager.occupy_blocks()`'s RESERVED-entry safety check stays effective after `occupied_by` is removed from the RESERVED state), and `exit_edge: "N" | "E" | "S" | "W"` (the direction reserved by the dispatcher through the block). The three fields are always written together by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` and cleared together by `Block.release_path_reservation()`/`occupy_blocks(...)`, so storing them as one Optional record makes the all-or-nothing rule structural rather than enforced by prose. Convenience reads `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_for_train`, and `reserved_direction` are derived from `reservation.path_id`, `reservation.train_id`, and `reservation.exit_edge` respectively (each `None` when `reservation is None`); they may still be referenced by name throughout this document for readability.
  - `disabled: bool = False` — physical-absence availability flag inherited from `TrackResource`. A disabled block cannot be used as a direct waypoint/stop target, cannot be reserved, cannot be physically entered without raising the canonical fatal physical-entry conflict, and pathfinding/wayfinding treats it as absent connectivity. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the shared rules.
  - `manual: bool = False` — virtual-disable availability flag inherited from `TrackResource`. Automatic systems treat a manual block exactly like a disabled block (skipped by pathfinding, rejected by reservation, rejected as a route target); a `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` call that carries `manual_override = True` (forwarded from an operator-initiated route's `Route.manual_override`) bypasses that filter and may target, traverse, and reserve manual blocks. A physical entry into a manual (but not disabled) block is ordinary clean entry, not a fatal conflict. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
  - `allowed_travel_directions: Set["forward", "reverse"]` — static block-level travel policy for this block. Defaults to both directions allowed. This is not a reservation field and is not stored on `TrackSegment`; `Block.reserved_direction` remains only the temporary dispatcher reservation direction for one path. Operator-initiated `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` calls that carry `manual_override = True` bypass this filter exactly like the `manual` flag — pathfinding, reservation, and terminal-direction acceptance ignore `allowed_travel_directions` when the override is set, and the field's stored value is not mutated. Automatic callers never set the override, so route-driven plans still honor it. A block restricted to a single direction is also what makes it exempt from the best-effort junction-fouling truncation exclusion (item 11): a one-way block can host no opposing traversal, so a truncated authority may stop on it right after a switch, while a two-way block off a switch is held back from (unless it is in `CAUTION`, which item 11 also exempts so a follower can close up behind a leader). See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- The computed `state` property should use priority order: OCCUPIED > CAUTION > RESERVED > FREE.
  - Return `OCCUPIED` if `occupied_by is not None`.
  - Return `CAUTION` if `caution is not None`.
  - Return `RESERVED` if `reserved_by_path is not None`.
  - Return `FREE` otherwise.
- The intended computed-state table is:
  - physical `occupied_by is not None` -> `OCCUPIED`
  - physically free + `caution is not None` -> `CAUTION`
  - physically free + not caution + `reserved_by_path is not None` -> `RESERVED`
  - physically free + not caution + not reserved -> `FREE`
- This makes composite physical+reservation states directly representable without contradictions:
  - OCCUPIED + `reserved_by_path` set (start-block scenario): `state` reads `OCCUPIED`; reservation is visible and clearable as a plain field write with no state check.
  - CAUTION + `reserved_by_path` set (path holds block through safety margin): `state` reads `CAUTION`; reservation is likewise clearable unconditionally.
- Treat `reserved_by_path` as the dispatcher's resource lock and `occupied_by` as physical train presence only.
- `Block.ordered_tiles` is the canonical block-local traversal order, and `Block.allowed_travel_directions` is the static allowed-direction policy. The list index is the canonical block-local traversal index. It is initialized from the tile list supplied to backend block-creation/edit logic, preserved through block edits, and never rewritten by runtime switching or path acquisition. Backend validation must verify the supplied list is already one connected switch-free walk in that exact order; it must not reorder, reverse, or silently renormalize the caller-supplied sequence. Block edits may only insert or remove member geometry at the ends while preserving relative order; middle insertions, reorders, or reversals are rejected by backend edit validation. Removing a block's last tile does not leave an empty block: it deletes the block per the **Non-empty parent rule** under **TrackSegment**. For a direct block target, `requested_destination_direction == "forward"` means movement in the increasing direction of `ordered_tiles`, `"reverse"` means the decreasing direction. Before `RouteExecutor` accepts the request, and before `PathDispatcher` accepts a request with `manual_override = False`, the concrete `"forward"` or `"reverse"` value must be present in the target block's `allowed_travel_directions`. `PathDispatcher` calls with `manual_override = True` skip this check per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- `Block.ordered_tiles[0]` is the canonical local index-0 tile; v1 does not add a second stored "index-0 tile" field. Each entry in `Block.ordered_tiles` carries `(x, y, entry_edge)`, where `entry_edge` is the external edge through which forward traversal enters that tile. The forward-entry anchor for the block is therefore `Block.ordered_tiles[0].entry_edge`; no separate field is stored. The forward exit edge is not stored on the entry because it is determined by connectivity to the next tile in the ordered list (or, for the terminal tile, by the block's external boundary toward the next block).
- The canonical local-direction anchor table is:

| Field | Type | Required | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `ordered_tiles` | ordered list of `(x, y, entry_edge)` entries | yes | Canonical local block order; `ordered_tiles[0]` is local index `0` and its `entry_edge` is the forward-entry anchor for the block |

- The canonical block-direction helper uses `Block.ordered_tiles` adjacency to derive traversal frames in multi-tile blocks and reads `Block.ordered_tiles[0].entry_edge` for single-tile blocks, same-tile ambiguity resolution, and validation.
- Because acquisition decisions need to check all blocks at once, `BlockManager` should be the sole mutator of Block runtime state (`occupied_by`, `_is_caution`, `reservation`); `Block` should remain a data container. Block geometry / topology mutation (`length_cm`, `speed_limit_kmh`, `ordered_tiles`, plus `track.block_section_id` writes during block create / extend / delete / split) lives in a separate `BlockGeometryManager` (file `block_geometry_manager.py`). `RailwayNetwork.create_block / create_block_with_tracks / extend_block / delete_block / assign_track_to_block / validate_block_continuity / remove_tile_from_block / remove_tiles_from_block` are doc-canonical method names but are now implemented as forwarders to `BlockGeometryManager`. The two managers have disjoint write surfaces: `BlockManager` for runtime state, `BlockGeometryManager` for geometry / topology.
- There is no admin-side direct-mutation surface for Block state. `Block.state` is a read-only computed `@property`, and v1 owns block state through `BlockManager` / `ResourceCoordinator`. Admin endpoints that need to drive block state for testing must drive it through real flows (place a train, create a path, etc.) so the canonical writers run. The `PATCH /api/blocks/<block_id>` endpoint rejects `state` mutations with HTTP 410.

**`CAUTION` vs reservation independence**

- `CAUTION` is an independent safety state indicating the block ahead (in the departing train's direction) is still occupied; it is orthogonal to path reservation.
- A block can be simultaneously `CAUTION` and `reserved_by_path` (path still holds it for a future or in-progress train), or `CAUTION` with no reservation (train has passed and path is done). With the computed-state model both combinations are natively representable — the `state` property returns `CAUTION` in both cases, and `reserved_by_path` can be cleared by a plain field write with no state guard.
- `CAUTION` by itself does not make a block unreservable. Its operational effect is that `TrainController` lookahead applies `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` when it samples the block. `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` must still allow a `CAUTION` block when the primitive reservation predicate below is satisfied.
- Runtime physical entry into a disabled block is not a normal recoverable workflow. `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` must report it through the fatal physical-entry conflict procedure with `conflict_kind = FatalConflictKind.DISABLED_RESOURCE_ENTRY`; it must not silently mark the disabled block occupied. Runtime physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) block is treated as ordinary clean entry — `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` records `occupied_by` normally; the `manual` flag is a future-planning filter, not a physical-entry gate. Operator/admin code must reject setting `Block.disabled = True` on a block that is physically occupied, reserved, or in `CAUTION`. v1 has no force-cleanup workflow that bypasses those claims; the operator/admin must remove the conflicting train, cancel the holding path, or wait for `CAUTION` to clear through the ordinary runtime workflows before the disable request is accepted. Toggling `Block.manual` to either value is always permitted at any time (no preconditions); it does not disturb existing reservations or `occupied_by` and is purely a future-planning filter. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the shared rules.
- The `OCCUPIED → CAUTION → FREE` lifecycle is driven purely by physical train movement through `BlockManager`; it does not depend on whether a path reservation exists.
- Path reservation (`reserved_by_path`) is consumed by the dispatcher/manager on train-head entry into that block, or released by the dispatcher on path completion/cancellation if the block was never entered. This is separate from the occupancy state machine.
- `CAUTION` is directional: `block.caution.exit_edge` records the compass edge through which the departing train left (the direction it was moving). The `CAUTION → FREE` decision — which forward neighbour is consulted, how the occupant's `travel_direction` is read, and when the protection chain is stale — is owned by `BlockManager._reevaluate_caution_blocks()`; see **`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)** under **BlockManager**.

**Block methods**

- Path reservation and physical occupancy are separate concerns with separate `BlockManager` entry points: reservation eligibility is owned by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` and physical entry by `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)`, both defined in the **BlockManager** section.
- `Block.reserve_for_path(path_id, train_id, direction)` is an internal helper called only by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`. It writes `block.reservation = BlockReservation(path_id=..., train_id=..., exit_edge=...)` (the derived `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_for_train`, and `reserved_direction` reads all become defined together). The `direction` argument may be a compass `exit_edge` (`"N" | "E" | "S" | "W"`) or a block-relative `"forward"` / `"reverse"`; the block converts via `Block.ordered_tiles` adjacency (multi-tile) or `Block.ordered_tiles[0].entry_edge` (single-tile) and stores the compass `exit_edge` in `block.reservation.exit_edge`. The reservation never overwrites `occupied_by` (which stays physical-only).
- `Block.release_path_reservation()` is an internal helper called only by `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)`. It clears `block.reservation` (so the derived `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_direction`, and `reserved_for_train` reads all become `None` together) with no state guard. It must NOT touch `block.caution` (that field is the physical-lifecycle owner's, `BlockManager`).

**Block invariants**

- Physical entry is written only by `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(block_ids, train_id)` (full contract in the **BlockManager** section). On clean entry the three primitive fields move together: `occupied_by = train_id`, `caution = None`, and any same-train `reservation = None` (the derived convenience reads `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_direction`, and `reserved_for_train` all become `None` together). `OCCUPIED` overrides `CAUTION` on clean entry; stale caution metadata must not survive an intervening clean entry.

## BlockManager

**Block-manager model**

- `BlockManager` is the database-style lock manager for block resources and the single serialization point for atomic block acquisition, whether the dispatcher is attempting a full-route all-or-nothing reservation or a best-effort prefix reservation.
- `BlockManager` holds both the owning `RailwayNetwork` entity layer and an `Optional[SimulationController]` runtime back-reference. It uses the network for block/switch registries and topology reads; it uses the runtime surface only to report unrecoverable fatal physical-entry conflicts through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`. `SimulationController`, not `BlockManager`, owns the failed-runtime flag, runtime freeze, stored fatal payload, and release-after-manual-cleanup gate. Fatal conflict handling itself does not perform route/controller/path teardown; any later train removal or resource repair is an explicit manual/admin cleanup workflow.
- `occupy_blocks(block_ids, train_id)` is the atomic occupy helper and the sole physical-entry writer (no separate single-block method; single-block calls pass a one-item list). Clean entry sets `block.occupied_by = train_id`, clears `block.caution = None`, and consumes any same-train reservation by clearing `block.reservation`. If a target block is already occupied by another train or reserved for another train, it reports the canonical unrecoverable hard-failure path by sending a typed `FatalPhysicalEntryConflict` to `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`; it must not silently overwrite a prior owner. Returns `OccupyResult(outcome: str, reason: Optional[str])` where `outcome` is one of the free-form strings `"clean_entry"`, `"idempotent_noop"` (block already occupied by this same `train_id`), or `"fatal_conflict"`. When `outcome == "fatal_conflict"` the method has already invoked `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` as a side effect; the caller checks the outcome and stops processing. The method always returns; no exception or short-circuit.
- Define `ReservedBlockTraversal` as the canonical value type for `directions` in `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`. It carries exactly `exit_edge` (`"N" | "E" | "S" | "W"`) and `block_direction` (`"forward" | "reverse"`). `block_direction` is derived canonically from the block's immutable internal tile order: if the directed in-block traversal advances to higher indices in `Block.ordered_tiles`, it is `"forward"`; if it advances to lower indices, it is `"reverse"`; if the traversal stays within one ordered tile, use the chosen entry's explicit directed frame for that tile rather than train-end labels.
- `reserve_blocks_for_path(block_ids, path_id, train_id, directions)` reserves a path's blocks atomically.
- `directions` in `reserve_blocks_for_path(block_ids, path_id, train_id, directions)` should be a mapping keyed by `block_id`, where each value is one `ReservedBlockTraversal`. `PathDispatcher` owns building that mapping from the chosen path geometry before calling `BlockManager` and must use the same canonical block-direction helper used by route callbacks. `ReservedBlockTraversal.exit_edge` is written to `block.reservation.exit_edge` (read as `Block.reserved_direction`); `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` is used only for `Block.allowed_travel_directions` validation.
- `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` accepts a `manual_override: bool = False` parameter propagated from the calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home, and must first check eligibility for every future block in the candidate block set before writing any fields. Blocks already physically occupied by the train may appear in `Path.resource_sequence` for geometry/progress, but they are not included in the future reservation candidate set. A candidate future block is eligible when `block.disabled == False`, `block.occupied_by is None`, `block.reserved_by_path is None or block.reserved_by_path == path_id`, either `manual_override == True` or the candidate `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` is present in `block.allowed_travel_directions`, and either `block.manual == False` or `manual_override == True`. `block.caution is not None` is not an eligibility failure by itself; `CAUTION` affects the following train through `TrainController.CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH`, not by blocking an otherwise legal reservation. If any block in that candidate set fails eligibility the entire call is rejected and no state changes are made. `PathDispatcher` owns choosing whether that candidate set is the full requested route (`all_or_nothing`) or the longest best-effort prefix it wants to attempt atomically.
- `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` writes the reservation atomically by calling `Block.reserve_for_path(path_id, train_id, reserved_exit_edge)` with the mapping value's `exit_edge` for each future block; the helper sets `block.reservation` in one step (the derived `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_for_train`, and `reserved_direction` reads all become defined together). It must not create path-owned reservations for blocks the train already physically occupies; those resources are train-owned physical occupancy and are represented only in `resource_sequence` / `current_resource_index` for path progress. Returns `ReserveResult(success: bool, failed_block_id: Optional[str], reason: Optional[str])`. On success all reservations are written atomically; `failed_block_id = None`. On failure no field is mutated, `failed_block_id` is the first block that failed eligibility, and `reason` is a free-form diagnostic string.
- `release_path_block_reservations(block_ids, path_id, completed=False)` releases not-yet-entered reservation ownership during completion and cancellation without modifying physical occupancy state. This manager method must clear only blocks that both belong to that path's dispatcher-owned future reservation set and currently satisfy `block.reserved_by_path == path_id`; a mismatched block is ignored and must emit a `NotificationCenter` diagnostic such as `release <block_id> but it's not reserved for <path_id>` rather than being treated as a hard release failure. Because `Block.release_path_reservation()` is a plain field clear with no state guard, this method can call it for each matching owned block regardless of whether the block is currently `CAUTION` or `RESERVED`; normal head-entry consumption means occupied resources should no longer be path-owned future reservations. When `completed=True` (path completed with train fully stopped at terminal), it additionally force-clears `block.caution = None` on any still-CAUTION matching owned blocks. When `completed=False` (path cancelled mid-flight), physical CAUTION state is left untouched — the train is still moving and the safety margin must be preserved. Returns `bool`: `True` if at least one matching reservation was released; `False` if none. Head-entry consumption of a block reservation happens through the physical-entry path, not this release path. This is the single reservation-release entry point, for routine path completion/cancellation release and manual/admin reservation cleanup alike (cleanup callers pass the affected block IDs and the matching `path_id`); it never touches `occupied_by` or `caution` outside the `completed=True` exception, and manual/admin cleanup must not bypass it.
- `on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` is the canonical tail-vacate entry and the only normal train-vacated physical transition entry point. Its write set, no-op cases, and cascade are defined in **`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)** below. Returns `bool`: `True` on a clean tail-vacate; `False` on the no-op cases. No exception raised.
- `BlockManager` should read block/switch registries from the owning `RailwayNetwork` entity layer rather than maintaining its own copied block/switch registries. `RailwayNetwork` remains the canonical home of the block registry, switch registry, and topology graph/traversal helpers. `BlockManager` reads `network.blocks`, `network.switches`, and one shared network-topology adjacency helper from there; it must not rescan unrelated entity lists or invent a second topology cache of its own.

**`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)**

- `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` reports normal tail-vacated block resources through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, which routes the release through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(...)`; the coordinator is the only normal runtime caller that invokes `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` for block resources. `TrainPhysics` does not call `BlockManager` directly.
- `BlockManager` does not independently decide that a block resource should be released; that instruction comes from `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` after a detector-confirmed tail-vacate observation (debounced no-current; see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). For normal runtime release, `train_id` must still match the current physical owner. If `block_id` is not present in the `BlockManager` registry (e.g. during early bootstrap or after teardown), `on_block_tail_vacated()` is a no-op that returns `False` without emitting a diagnostic. If the block exists but `block.occupied_by != train_id`, `on_block_tail_vacated()` must leave the block unchanged and emit a `NotificationCenter` diagnostic such as `block <block_id> release for train <train_id> skipped: currently occupied by <other_train_id>`.
- `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` is one-shot: the detector-confirmed tail-vacate fires the single tick the detector confirms the block clear (after the off-debounce window). If `block.occupied_by != train_id`, the diagnostic emits exactly once for that `(train_id, block_id)` and is not retriggered, because the detector reports the clear only once — the block leaves the confirmed-occupied set on that tick and is not re-reported.
- `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` is safe to call once `BlockManager` is constructed, even before any blocks/switches have been registered. Empty-registry calls are a no-op.
- `direction` on `on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` is the vacated block's forward `exit_edge` (`N`/`E`/`S`/`W`) for the traversal by which the train left that block.
- If `block.occupied_by == train_id`, `on_block_tail_vacated()` clears `block.occupied_by` and sets `block.caution = CautionState(exit_edge=direction)`. With the computed-state model the block's `state` property now returns `CAUTION`.
- After updating the vacated block, `BlockManager` should call `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` to cascade any blocks that can already advance to `FREE`.
- `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` iterates all blocks currently in `CAUTION`. In the normal reevaluation path, a `CAUTION` block advances to `FREE` unless the forward protection chain is both reachable AND ends in an `OCCUPIED` block whose train is still moving away in the protect direction. `BlockManager` owns this walk, but it performs it through the shared `RailwayNetwork` topology graph / neighbour-traversal helper rather than by scanning the full entity lists. "Forward block" is defined precisely: start from the vacated block's exit edge given by `block.caution.exit_edge`, inspect the neighbouring resource in network connectivity, and keep following live tile connectivity through any intervening switch tile using the current `Switch.position` until the next block resource is reached. That next reachable block resource is the only block consulted for the `CAUTION -> FREE` decision; blocks behind the vacated block or off the currently selected switch branch are ignored. When that block is `OCCUPIED`, `BlockManager` compares the occupant train's motion against the protection chain in the chain's own frame at the occupant's block: the same connectivity walk that found the forward block also yields the edge through which the chain enters that block (for a straight adjacent block this is simply the side facing the `CAUTION` block; through switch tiles and curves it is the entry edge the walk actually used). `BlockManager` reads the occupant train's current `travel_direction` through its `SimulationController` back-reference (via `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id).travel_direction`) and clears `CAUTION` only when that `travel_direction` points back out through that chain-entry edge — the train has flipped its cab and is heading back toward the `CAUTION` block. Any other resolved `travel_direction` means the train is still moving away, so `CAUTION` is kept. This shape prevents a following train (entering from behind) from keeping a caution block alive, lets a later switch throw invalidate the old protected chain, and lets a same-train cab flip retire the now-stale safety margin on the side the train has just reversed away from.
- If `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` cannot resolve any reachable forward-adjacent block in `block.caution.exit_edge`, if live connectivity no longer reaches the block that was previously being protected, or if the `OCCUPIED` forward block holds a train whose current `travel_direction` points back out through the chain-entry edge of its block (the cab-flip case above), there is nothing ahead left to protect. In that case it should clear `block.caution = None` immediately, so the computed state becomes `RESERVED` if `reserved_by_path` is still set, otherwise `FREE`. When `BlockManager` has no `SimulationController` back-reference wired (early bootstrap, unit tests that exercise `BlockManager` in isolation), or when the occupant train's `travel_direction` cannot be resolved, the reevaluator preserves the existing `CAUTION` state — the clear requires positive evidence of reversal, not absence of evidence.
- When a CAUTION block clears, set `block.caution = None`. The block's computed `state` then re-evaluates to `RESERVED` if `reserved_by_path` is still set, or `FREE` if it is not.
- Reevaluation is triggered by manager-owned connectivity changes, not by a periodic scan: at minimum `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` must call `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`, and every successful `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` call must also trigger `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`. `Switch` does not independently clear block caution state; it only changes connectivity, and `BlockManager` remains the sole owner of the actual `CAUTION -> FREE` decision.
- `reserved_by_path` is NOT checked as a condition for `CAUTION → FREE`. The physical safety margin and the dispatcher reservation are independent: a block can become physically clear of `CAUTION` while still holding a `reserved_by_path` value. In that case the computed `state` becomes `RESERVED`, not `FREE`, until the dispatcher separately clears the reservation via `release_path_block_reservations()`.
