- Explicit exception: `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)` may force-clear `block.caution = None` during terminal completion release for owned blocks. That is a completion-time release rule, not part of the normal neighbour-based reevaluation path.

## SwitchManager

**Switch-manager model**

- `SwitchManager` is the database-style lock manager for switch resources and the single serialization point for atomic multi-switch lock acquisition, whether the dispatcher is attempting a full-route all-or-nothing acquisition or a best-effort prefix. It mirrors `BlockManager`'s contract on the switch side.
- `SwitchManager` holds the owning `RailwayNetwork` reference and an `Optional[SimulationController]` runtime back-reference. It uses the network for the switch registry; it uses the runtime surface only to report fatal physical-entry conflicts through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`. In editor mode (no `SimulationController`) the manager exists but its runtime entry points are not exercised.
- `SwitchLockRequest` is the canonical value type for one requested switch lock. It carries exactly `switch_id`, `locked_state`, `claim_role` (`"main" | "flank"`, matching the convenience read defined under **Switch entity**: `"main"` when `source_switch_id is None`, else `"flank"`), optional `source_switch_id` (`None` for a main claim; the originating switch_id for a flank claim), and `route: Optional[FrozenSet[str]]` — the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through the switch, required non-`None` when `claim_role == "main"` and `None` for flank requests. `PathDispatcher` populates `route` from the chosen candidate's switch step (`GraphWalkStep.entry_edge` / `exit_edge`); the manager copies it onto each `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` claim and rejects a primary request whose `route` is `None` per the cross-train co-locking rule under **Switch entity**.
- `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(requests, path_id, train_id, manual_override=False)` attempts every requested switch lock atomically. The `manual_override` parameter (default `False`) is propagated from the calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; the manager sets `claim.manual_override = manual_override` on every `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` call it issues so manual switches participate when (and only when) the operator explicitly overrode. On any failure it rolls back the locks recorded in this call and returns `LockAcquireResult(success: bool, failed_switch_id: Optional[str], reason: Optional[str], locked_switch_ids: List[str])`. Same all-or-nothing rule as `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`.
- `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(path_id)` releases every lock owned by `path_id` across every switch. `SwitchManager.release_path_locks_for_switches(switch_ids, path_id)` is the segment-rollback variant scoped to a passed switch_id list.
- `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)` is the canonical switch tail-vacate entry, called by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for switch tail-vacate events through the `ResourceCoordinator` facade. `route` is required (non-`None`) and is the canonical key into `Switch.physical_occupants`. Internally it calls the entity-level helper `Switch.on_train_tail_vacated(train_id, route)`, which removes the single `physical_occupants` entry matching `route` and — only when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train — clears train-owned protection and runs the neighbour flank-release walk per the **Switch entity** tail-vacate contract.
- `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)` is the canonical clean-physical-entry entry. `route` is required (non-`None`); a `route is None` argument is reported through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** with `conflict_kind = FatalConflictKind.MALFORMED_ROUTE_KEY`. It owns the flank-lock conversion: on clean entry it records `Switch.physical_occupants[route] = train_id`, consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock for that switch, and converts every required flank-protection claim on neighbours into train-owned `train_protection_claims` per the **Switch entity** flank-protection contract. Conflict cases are reported through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** and return `False`: a route that conflicts with a route held by a different train (per `Switch.routes_conflict(...)`), a disabled switch entry, and the intrusion case (the entering train holds no compatible lock for its route — see the intrusion check in the **Switch entity** section). Physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) switch is **not** a conflict case — it is treated as ordinary clean entry per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- The actual `Switch.position` write still goes through the canonical mutation workflow on `Switch` per the **Switch entity** section; `SwitchManager` calls into that workflow rather than mutating `Switch.position` directly.
- The physical throw emission and the throw continuity check are owned by `SwitchManager` as part of that same paired operation: after the `Switch.position` write + `BlockManager` notify, and only when the alignment actually changed, it drives the throw through `SwitchPowerController` and asks it to verify the throw. `SwitchManager` reaches `SwitchPowerController` through its `simulation_controller` reference (with a network fallback), the same way it reaches `BlockManager`; `SwitchPowerController` in turn reads the entry/branch block voltages through the block `BlockPowerController`. The check, its skip rules, and the unconfirmed-throw handling are defined canonically in the **Power** section (Switch power-controller role); `SwitchManager` does not restate them.

## ResourceCoordinator

**Resource-coordinator model**

- `ResourceCoordinator` is the unified facade `PathDispatcher` calls to reserve, release, and route physical-entry / tail-vacate events for an entire `PathSegment`. It owns no state — it is a pure composition layer over `BlockManager` and `SwitchManager`. The single remaining `resource_type` string branch lives inside `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(...)` and `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)`; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on `resource_type` for reserve / release / rollback / tail-vacate / physical-entry.
- `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(future_block_ids, block_traversals, switch_requests, path_id, train_id, manual_override=False)` runs the switch-lock phase first, then the block reservation phase. The parameters are `future_block_ids: List[str]`, `block_traversals: Dict[str, ReservedBlockTraversal]` (keyed by `block_id`, matching `reserve_blocks_for_path`'s `directions` contract in the **BlockManager** section), `switch_requests: List[SwitchLockRequest]`, `path_id: str`, `train_id: str`, and `manual_override: bool = False`; `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` pass them positionally. The `manual_override` field (default `False`) is propagated per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home and forwarded to `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` and `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` so manual resources are reachable only when the operator explicitly chose to override. On block-reservation failure it rolls back the just-acquired switch locks before returning. Returns `SegmentReserveResult(success: bool, failed_resource_type: Optional[str], failed_resource_id: Optional[str], reason: Optional[str], reserved_block_ids: List[str], locked_switch_ids: List[str])`.
- `ResourceCoordinator.rollback_segment(block_ids, switch_ids, path_id)` releases any partial holds placed on a segment that failed mid-flight. Used by `PathDispatcher._rollback_segment(...)`.
- `ResourceCoordinator.release_path_resources(path_id, block_ids, completed)` composes `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)` and `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(path_id)`. Returns `(released_block_ids, released_switch_ids)`.
- `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)` is the single entry called by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each `tail_vacated_resources` event. It routes to `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)` for blocks (using `direction`, ignoring `route`) and `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)` for switches (using `route`, ignoring `direction`).
- `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, path_id, route)` is the single entry called by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each `entered_resources` event. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` for switch events (required, non-`None`) and is ignored for blocks. It routes to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([resource_id], train_id)` for blocks and `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)` for switches; the helper `_convert_switch_locks_on_entry` no longer lives on `PathDispatcher`.

## Path And PathDispatcher

**Path model**

- The dispatcher path lifecycle has three states: `ACTIVE`, `COMPLETED`, and `CANCELLED`. The lifecycle value is stored on `Path` as `status: PathStatus`. `status` is initialized to `ACTIVE` on successful `create_path(...)` and is the field transitioned by completion and cancellation.
- The core identity / routing fields on `Path` are `path_id`, `status`, `requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, `train_id`, and `resource_sequence`. The reads `authority_end_block_id` and `path_tiles` are exposed as derived functions (`get_authority_end_block_id()`, `get_path_tiles()`) computed from `resource_sequence` on each call; see the **Path field schema** for the canonical contracts.
- `RouteExecutor` owns route scheduling priority through the current shared `Route.priority` once a run exists, and the run-phase scheduling tier through the derived `RouteRun.is_starting`. Dispatcher path records do not carry a second copied `priority` field or any phase-tier field in v1; scheduling stays route-owned rather than becoming path-owned metadata.
- `Path.get_path_tiles()` returns the ordered physical tile list for the authority in train tail -> current authority end order, computed live from `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles`. Every `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles` slice uses the canonical **path_tile entry schema** below; shared contracts must not use bare `(x, y)` tuples.
- Resource acquisition is synchronous inside `create_path()`. Terminal-approach behavior is represented by progress/completion predicates, not a separate live status.
- `resource_sequence` is an ordered list of path-step records interleaving blocks and switches in traversal order. The per-entry schema is defined canonically in the **resource_sequence entry schema** subsection immediately below.
- `RouteExecutor` platform-end resolution, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` start-side selection, and `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` suffix search must all use the same shared backend pathfinding home/helper. The implementation may extend the existing backend pathfinding code in `PathDispatcher.find_paths()` / `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_paths(...)` or extract that code into a `RailwayPathfinder` class, but it must not create a second disconnected pathfinder with different comparison or direction rules. That shared helper must be callable before any live `Path.resource_sequence` exists, must return the ordered candidate walk it used for comparison, and must be the single source for candidate-length comparison, direction filtering, current-span end selection, and tie-breaking rather than letting different layers reimplement those rules independently.
- The dispatcher path-acquisition API surface is exactly `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` for a new authority and `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for growing an existing live authority. V1 must not introduce a third acquisition wrapper or route code path with a third acquisition contract.

**Path field schema**

This is the single canonical field set for the dispatcher-owned `Path` entity in v1.

**Why this shape.** `resource_sequence` is the single source of truth for path geometry; `authority_end_block_id` and `path_tiles` are derived functions over it so that any update to live authority is one mutation on one list, not multiple synchronized stores. `current_resource_index` is the head-progress anchor, separated from `requested_destination_*` because head progress and target identity are independent (the head can advance while the requested target is unchanged). Keeping `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` on the dispatcher record (and out of the controller projection) lets only `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` enforce target-match completion.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `path_id` | `str` | Unique dispatcher path identifier |
| `status` | `PathStatus` | One of `ACTIVE`, `COMPLETED`, `CANCELLED` |
| `train_id` | `str` | Assigned train |
| `path_tiles` | `function (read-only): get_path_tiles() -> List[path_tile]` | Function that returns the full ordered train-tail -> authority-end geometry by extracting and concatenating `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles` in `sequence_index` order on each call. Not a stored field; not a cache; no writers; no rebuild step. Shared contracts that previously read `Path.path_tiles` call `Path.get_path_tiles()` instead. |
| `resource_sequence` | `List[resource_sequence entry]` | Ordered interleaved block/switch resources. This is the single source of truth for path geometry. |
| `current_resource_index` | `int` | `sequence_index` of the current path-progress anchor resource: head-based once a true head exists, otherwise initialized to the authority-side resource at the physical train end closer to the path end |
| `requested_destination_block_id` | `str` | Caller's current requested block target carried by this live path |
| `requested_destination_direction` | `str` | Resolved block-relative traversal constraint at the requested target (`"forward"` or `"reverse"` only); used for compatibility/filtering, not exact terminal-frame geometry |
| `authority_end_block_id` | `function (read-only): get_authority_end_block_id() -> str` | Function that returns `resource_sequence[-1].resource_id` on each call (the **Path invariants** rule below requires the terminal `resource_sequence` entry to always be a `block` entry). Not a stored field; not a cache; no writers. Updates to live authority are made on `resource_sequence` itself, and the function reflects the new terminal block automatically. Shared contracts that previously read `Path.authority_end_block_id` call `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` instead. |
| `last_status_updated_time` | `float` | System-time Unix timestamp when `status` last changed. Sampled by the writer at the moment it commits the `Path.status` transition (see **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**): `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` calls `time.time()` when it writes `Path.status = ACTIVE`; `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` calls `time.time()` when it writes `Path.status = COMPLETED`; `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` calls `time.time()` when it writes `Path.status = CANCELLED`. When the same writer also emits a notification with an `event_time` field for that transition, it samples `time.time()` once and uses that one value for both the status timestamp and the notification field. |

- `Path` is the entity name for the dispatcher-owned resource-sequence authority record itself. Shared contracts must refer back to this table instead of rediscovering the field set from scattered prose.

**Path status state machine**

This is the single canonical state-machine contract for `Path.status` in v1. `status` is the lifecycle enum. Shared contracts must not invent extra path lifecycle states or path-owned pending cleanup flags.

| From `status` | Trigger | To `status` | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (none) | `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` succeeds | `ACTIVE` | Path record is inserted into `paths_by_id` and indexed as the train's active authority. Writes `last_status_updated_time` to the synchronous create-time system-time Unix timestamp. |
| `ACTIVE` | `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` is called from the post-physics Phase 3 completion check and the active path's live authority reaches the path's requested target block and direction | `COMPLETED` | The **Active-path completion procedure** owns ordering. Dispatcher commits the post-physics controller stop check plus the path-owned requested-target check; it does not infer completion from precise train position or require physical head occupancy in the requested block. |
| `ACTIVE` | `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, initiator=None, reason=None)` | `CANCELLED` | Not-yet-entered authority resources are released synchronously; controller authority is cleared; the `Path.status` transition to `CANCELLED` makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return `None` (or skip to a different `ACTIVE` path) by definition; `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` is notified. |
| `COMPLETED` | any | `COMPLETED` | Terminal. `cancel_path(...)` on a `COMPLETED` path fails with reason `"already completed"`. |
| `CANCELLED` | any | `CANCELLED` | Terminal. `cancel_path(...)` on a `CANCELLED` path fails with reason `"already cancelled"`. |

- `status` is set once per lifecycle transition by the originating synchronous call. No later stopped cleanup pass re-transitions status or writes a pending field.
- Physical `occupied_by` (block or switch) is outside this state machine. It is cleared only by tail-vacate observations committed through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` — detector-confirmed for blocks (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), controller-reported traversal for switches — and never by `status` transitions.
- A train may have at most one `ACTIVE` path at a time; retained `COMPLETED` / `CANCELLED` rows do not block a fresh `create_path(...)`.
- In a stored live path, `resource_sequence[-1]` must always be a `block` entry. A v1 path may contain switch resources internally, but its live authority end is always expressed by `authority_end_block_id` on a block resource, never on a terminal switch.

**path_tile entry schema**

This is the single canonical definition of one tile step inside `Path.path_tiles` and inside each `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles` slice.

- Each `path_tile` entry carries exactly:
  - `x`
  - `y`
  - `entry_edge`
  - `exit_edge`
  - `resource_sequence_index` — the parent `Path.resource_sequence[*].sequence_index`.
- `entry_edge` and `exit_edge` are required on every tile entry; shared contracts must not infer them later from neighboring coordinates.

**resource_sequence entry schema**

This is the single canonical definition of a `resource_sequence` entry. Every site that describes the `received_path_plan` projection, dispatcher/UI authority projections, the `to_dict()` exports, or any other projection of authority geometry refers back to this schema by name rather than restating it.

**Why this shape.** Each entry carries both the resource identity (`resource_type`, `resource_id`) and the directed traversal frame (`entry_edge`, `exit_edge`, plus the nested `path_tiles` slice). Storing the directed frame explicitly — rather than letting consumers infer it from tile coordinates — means every traversal is unambiguous even where a tile could be walked in either direction, and downstream lookahead/braking math has the geometry it needs without re-walking the tile graph. Storing `entry_edge` and `exit_edge` redundantly at both the resource and tile level lets resource-level consumers read the frame without unpacking the tile slice while still keeping per-tile detail available when needed.

- Each `resource_sequence` entry carries exactly:
  - `sequence_index` — position of the entry in the path's train-tail -> authority-end traversal order.
  - `resource_type` — `"block"` or `"switch"`.
  - `resource_id` — stable network resource ID for the block or switch.
  - `path_tiles` — the contiguous ordered slice of canonical `path_tile` entries belonging to that resource, in train-tail -> authority-end traversal order. Every nested tile entry in this slice has `resource_sequence_index == sequence_index`.
  - `entry_edge` — the directed traversal-frame entry edge for that resource in train-tail -> authority-end traversal order.
  - `exit_edge` — the directed traversal-frame exit edge for that resource in train-tail -> authority-end traversal order.
- No other fields are required in v1.
- Ordered tiles alone are not enough because a single tile can be traversed in either direction and the stop frame changes with the chosen entry side; `entry_edge` and `exit_edge` are required fields on every entry.
- `entry_edge` must equal the first nested `path_tiles[0].entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` must equal the last nested `path_tiles[-1].exit_edge`; they are stored redundantly so resource-level consumers can read the traversal frame without unpacking the whole tile slice.
- For switch resources, the required switch state is not stored as a fixed field on the entry. It is derived at acquisition time from the planner-facing `TrackSegment.enumerate_traversals(entry_edge)` contract on the switch tile, using the `(entry_edge, exit_edge)` pair selected for that resource's `path_tiles` slice. This derivation replaces storing `switch_positions` as a separate dict on the path.
- These per-resource tile slices plus explicit resource/tile entry-exit edges are the geometry contract for controller/UI consumers.
- Replace `current_block_index` with `current_resource_index`, which should track the `sequence_index` of the current path-progress anchor resource, not the first matching raw resource ID. Once a cab is selected or live progress updates establish a true head, this index is strictly head-based. Before that, it is initialized to the authority-side resource at the physical train end that is closer to the path end under the same comparison used by automatic cab selection.
- Flank protection lives on the switch entity (acquired automatically when the switch is locked); `Path` does not store its own flank-protection list.
- `Path` carries the requested target fields `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`. `requested_destination_direction` is the caller's already-resolved block-relative traversal constraint for the requested target and is always `"forward"` or `"reverse"` by the time dispatcher stores it. It is used to filter compatible terminal candidates, to choose a legal terminal traversal frame, and as part of the dispatcher completion predicate; no stop-centimeter field is stored on the path.
- `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` returns the live authority end block whose forward boundary the controller brakes toward using its safety-margin policy. The function is defined in the **Path field schema**; it has no setter and no stored backing field.
- The live terminal frame always comes from explicit directed geometry on the relevant terminal `resource_sequence` block entry (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`); the spec must not rely on readers inferring direction from tile coordinates alone or from `requested_destination_direction` alone.
- When the live authority already reaches the requested destination, `authority_end_block_id == requested_destination_block_id` and the terminal block entry's computed block-relative traversal direction equals `requested_destination_direction`. The direction half does not apply when the terminal block is a single-tile block, which has no `ordered_tiles` order to anchor block-relative direction against; the dispatcher completion gate skips it for that case (see the direction-skip rule under **Active-path completion procedure**). When the live authority is a best-effort truncated prefix, the requested target fields remain unchanged while `authority_end_block_id` describes the temporary current authority boundary.
- `Path` does not store a path-owned pending cleanup field. Physical `occupied_by` state and train-owned switch protection change only through resource entry/tail-vacate observations committed by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` — detector-confirmed for blocks (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), controller-reported traversal for switches.
- `PathDispatcher` should treat each `Path` record as the authoritative dispatcher-owned database entry, keyed by `path_id` in `paths_by_id[path_id]`, with `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` deriving the one active authority path for that train. The controller-facing authority is only a read-only projection of that current dispatcher record.
- Dispatcher-owned path records should remain available while active and, after termination, as retained `CANCELLED` or `COMPLETED` records for history/inspection. In v1, retain that history indefinitely rather than aging it out. Update `last_status_updated_time` on every `status` transition for recordkeeping, but authority release must not depend on any controller-side copy surviving.
- `PathDispatcher`'s retained path database should be the authoritative historical store for every path it has created. After `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` has been cleared, controller progress reports resolve active authority only against the new active authority, while delayed release or occupancy reporting for a block or switch that remains under the train resolves from the resource itself (`BlockManager` state for blocks, `Switch.occupied_by` / `Switch.train_protection_claims` for switches). Retained history rows do not by themselves block creation once the old path is no longer active (the same-train rejection while an `ACTIVE` path exists is owned by step 1 of the **`create_path()` flow**); only ordinary live resource conflicts remain.
- `train_id` is mandatory for every dispatcher authority path, including ordinary route-driven paths and one-off operator routes. Frontend/API callers supply it; anonymous authority paths are out of scope. Future authority on reserved resources is tracked through `Block.reserved_by_path` (written by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`) and `Switch.locks` (keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id)`, where main claims use `source_switch_id = None`, and each claim stores `path_id`, `source_switch_id`, `train_id`, and `locked_state`).

**Path API migration**

- The canonical write interfaces are `PathDispatcher.create_path(requested_destination_block_id, train_id, requested_destination_direction, acquisition_mode=..., manual_override=False)`, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(path_id, requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction, acquisition_mode=..., manual_override=False)`, and `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, initiator=None, reason=None)`. `extend_path(...)` is the same request contract as `create_path(...)` plus the leading `path_id` that identifies which live path is being grown in place. `manual_override` is the operator-override flag defined in the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; default `False`, and `True` only when the requesting run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True` (operator-initiated routes such as Quick Route). `RouteExecutor` forwards that route-stored flag; request handlers never call these interfaces directly. `initiator` and free-form `reason` are diagnostic in v1, populated by the writer for the `path_cancellation` notification; callers must not branch on either when deciding planning or routing behavior. Create/extend branching uses the structured `result_kind` / `failure_kind` fields in the create/extend result object instead of parsing `reason`.
- The canonical replacement read interfaces are the controller-facing `received_path_plan` projection for active authority geometry, `TrainController.get_current_span()` for current physical span, `TrainController.get_lookahead()` for live speed/limit diagnostics, and a dispatcher-owned progress projection `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)` (full field set defined under **Data Flow To UI**) when UI/debug consumers need live `current_resource_index`. That projection is resolved from dispatcher-owned path registries (for example `active_path_id_by_train` plus `paths_by_id`) and returns the currently active path bound to that `train_id`; it does not read train/controller mirror fields.
- Route-managed planning remains point-to-point per call. `RouteExecutor` never asks dispatcher/pathfinder to solve a whole multi-waypoint route in one search. Each `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)` request targets exactly one currently resolved waypoint block at a time, and longer waypoint chains are formed by concatenating repeated successful point-to-point segments onto the same live path.

**`create_path()` flow**

1. Before candidate search or any side effect, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` must reject the request if `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` already returns an `ACTIVE` path, returning `success=False` with `failure_kind = train_already_has_active_path` per the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**. A train can have only one active authority plan at a time. This failure happens with no path ID allocation, switch acquisition, block reservation, controller mutation, switch-state mutation, or `paths_by_id` record insertion. Callers must use `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` to grow that same live path, or wait until the old path has transitioned out of `ACTIVE` before calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` again.
2. Candidate search should enumerate direction-compatible resource sequences from the train's current occupied span to `requested_destination_block_id`, reject any candidate that uses repeated resources, reject any candidate that traverses a disabled block or disabled switch, reject any candidate that traverses a manual block or manual switch when `manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home), reject any candidate whose traversal violates a block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions` when `manual_override == False` (per the same canonical home), and reject any candidate that reaches the terminal block in a way that does not satisfy `requested_destination_direction`. The `requested_destination_block_id` itself is subject to the same availability rules: a disabled target block produces no viable candidate; a manual target block produces no viable candidate when `manual_override == False`. On a single `create_path(...)` call, dispatcher then selects exactly one candidate using the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**, applied across the remaining start-side choices, and applies `acquisition_mode` to that one candidate. There is no fall-through to a second candidate within the same call. Higher-level alternative-target attempts are owned by `RouteExecutor`: in one eligible Phase 1 pass it may try additional distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` targets, but it must not call `create_path(...)` twice for the same resolved pair in that pass. After every distinct same-tick target has failed without authority creation or growth, planning waits until the next eligible tick.
3. Dispatcher must build and store the exact directed terminal frame for the requested target (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`) as part of the terminal `resource_sequence` block entry. Dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal block traversal. When the requested target was resolved from a multi-block platform, `RouteExecutor` must already have resolved one concrete terminal block for that run before calling dispatcher, and the terminal frame uses that already-resolved terminal block directly; dispatcher does not choose between platform blocks itself.
4. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` should take `requested_destination_block_id`, `train_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, `acquisition_mode`, and `manual_override`; it should not require a caller-supplied `start_block_id`, a stop-centimeter value, or a second opaque waypoint/platform stop-context payload. Dispatcher derives the planning start side from `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id).get_current_span()` plus controller cab state by calling the same shared network-graph walk query that `RouteExecutor` uses. If a cab is selected, the only start-side candidate is the current head end implied by that cab. If no cab is selected, compare both physical ends of the train against the already resolved terminal block and use the end whose successful graph walk is shorter. If one physical end is currently on a switch resource, map that end to the one occupied block inward from that end of the current span before running the candidate comparison; if no such inward occupied block exists for that end, that end is not a valid start-side candidate. Use the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**, applied across the remaining start-side choices. Dispatcher must not introduce any other layer-local ad hoc tie-break. Depending on the caller, that shared graph query may return only distance metrics or the ordered tile/block walk used to build `resource_sequence`.
5. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` should also take an explicit `acquisition_mode` parameter of type `PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode`, an enum owned by `PathDispatcher` with v1 values `all_or_nothing` and `best_effort`.
6. Candidate search must become direction-aware. The shared ordered-walk entry point (named `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...)` in this document if the implementation extracts a class from the existing dispatcher search) must reject candidates whose traversal uses a disabled block or disabled switch, reject candidates whose traversal uses a manual block or manual switch when `manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home), reject candidates whose traversal violates `Block.allowed_travel_directions` when `manual_override == False` (per the same canonical home), and reject candidates whose terminal traversal does not satisfy `requested_destination_direction`. For a direct `block_id` target, `requested_destination_direction` is interpreted against that block's immutable internal traversal order: `"forward"` means the increasing direction of that block-local order and `"reverse"` means the decreasing direction. `RouteExecutor` must resolve any waypoint-authored `"either"` into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` before calling `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`; dispatcher never receives `"either"` in the v1 path contract. If dispatcher still cannot build one unique terminal frame for the chosen move, path creation must fail rather than guessing.
7. Stop-position centimeter inputs are not part of `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` in v1. The controller computes the braking target from the authority boundary and its canonical safety margin.
8. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` does not build same-block repositioning authority. A request whose `requested_destination_block_id` is already inside the train's current occupied span fails with `failure_kind = invalid_request` and a `reason` such as `"destination block already covered by the train's span"`: a covered stop destination is finished by Phase 1's covered-task index advance (the covered-stop rule — train stopped, no active path, destination block inside the span), so no dispatcher authority exists for it. Argument naming is consistent throughout: `requested_destination_block_id` is the caller's current requested target passed into `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)`, and `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` returns the current live-authority end derived from `Path.resource_sequence`.
9. Every stored live authority therefore crosses at least one resource boundary beyond the train's covered span: the forward suffix contains at least one not-yet-covered resource, and the terminal frame always comes from a real forward traversal. Dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal block traversal; it never synthesizes an in-place frame from the train's own covered tiles.
10. In `all_or_nothing` mode, if the full chosen candidate path cannot be acquired, roll back and return failure; no live path is created. Dispatcher does not fall through to a second candidate inside the same call.
11. In `best_effort` mode, if the full chosen candidate cannot be acquired, `PathDispatcher` should truncate the authority to the furthest forward block-terminated resource prefix it can acquire on that candidate and still create one live path. Dispatcher does not fall through to a second candidate inside the same call; if that candidate cannot produce any storable live `resource_sequence` because the train has no usable occupied-span seed, because no block-terminated prefix can be acquired, or because of the head-on-switch failure below, the call fails and `RouteExecutor` may then try a different distinct resolved target later in that same eligible Phase 1 pass or on a later eligible tick, but not the same resolved pair twice in one pass. That truncated live path keeps the same `path_id`, preserves the requested target fields, and stores a `resource_sequence` whose final block is the last block in the current authority (the derived `authority_end_block_id` reflects this automatically). The controller does not need a special "you are already at your authority end" branch: it enters the ordinary running flow, and ATC/lookahead handles the geometry using the current authority boundary plus safety margin. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` never returns `result_kind = blocked_no_growth` in v1. A successful create has created a brand-new path record with at least one block resource in its live `resource_sequence`; if the live authority reaches the requested target, return `result_kind = reached_requested_target`, otherwise return `result_kind = truncated_grew`. If no block-terminated live authority can be stored, return `success=False` with a structured `failure_kind` rather than storing a no-growth create result.
    - Special case: if the furthest acquirable prefix ends on a `switch` resource, dispatcher must truncate again to the previous `block` resource before storing the live path, so the stored live authority is always block-terminated.
    - The stored live authority's terminal block must not be the resolved target block of a `task_kind == "waypoint"` task on the requesting `RouteRun`'s current directed walk. If the block-terminated truncation point would land on a waypoint task's resolved target block, dispatcher must extend the storable prefix at least one block past it before storing the live authority. If no further forward block beyond the waypoint target can be acquired on this attempt, the create call fails with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` and a `reason` such as `"cannot truncate authority at a waypoint target"`. This keeps the rule that waypoints are consumed by head-entry on a still-live authority, never by dispatcher path completion.
    - The stored live authority's terminal block must not be a block that a stopped train ahead needs in order to leave (the reversal-trap exclusion). If the first block past the truncation point is occupied by a train that holds no live authority of its own, and that train's next route move would leave that block by coming back through the candidate terminal block (a reverse / turn-back), dispatcher must truncate further to the previous block-terminated prefix before storing the live authority. Parking the follower on that terminal block would trap the two trains nose-to-nose: the follower holds it to go in while the occupant needs it to come out, and neither can move. If no shorter block-terminated prefix can be acquired, the create call fails with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` and a `reason` such as `"cannot hold best-effort authority where it would trap a reversing train ahead"`. The follower waits one block back and grows its authority once the occupant has left. The check reads only the occupant's immediate exit block, so an occupant that leaves forward — an ordinary platform pass-through, or a move that continues out the far side (for example a loop that runs on through a tunnel rather than reversing) — never restricts the follower. The occupant's next move is read through `RouteExecutor.find_next_move_target(train_id)` (dispatcher reaches the route executor through `simulation_controller`); a moving occupant that already holds a live path is exempt, since it clears forward under its own authority. `RouteExecutor.find_next_move_target(...)` (canonical definition site) resolves the block of the occupant's next non-`dwell` task, following `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` wrap and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` end reflection the same way `_find_next_task_target(...)` does. **Route-definition validation** guarantees at least one `task_kind == "stop"` task, so a next non-`dwell` task always exists on a valid walk; if the walk somehow contains no non-`dwell` task at all, `find_next_move_target(...)` raises a fatal diagnostic rather than returning a misleading `None`.
    - The stored live authority's terminal block must not be a two-way block that the path enters straight off a switch (the junction-fouling exclusion). A block is two-way when its `Block.allowed_travel_directions` allows both `forward` and `reverse`; a one-way block (restricted to a single direction) is exempt, since no opposing traversal can be granted on it. When the furthest acquirable block-terminated prefix would stop the train on a two-way block sitting straight off a switch, dispatcher must truncate further to the previous block-terminated prefix, before that switch. Holding one block back keeps the switch clear: a train parked on the block past the switch fouls the junction, where an opposing move could still be routed through that switch and meet it head-on, while a train held before the switch leaves the junction free for either train to use. A one-way terminal block needs no hold-back, so trains may pack tight right up to it — for example along a one-way corridor, where the single permitted direction makes a head-on impossible. A two-way terminal block that is in `CAUTION` is exempt too: a caution block is one a train has just passed forward through this junction, so the corridor through the switch is already committed to one-directional following use. Letting the follower close up onto it at the caution speed cap is the close-up that `CAUTION` exists to enable (see the `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` speed-cap home); the leader still occupies the block ahead in the caution's protect direction, so no opposing move can be routed up to the caution block, and once the follower physically enters it the block is `OCCUPIED`, so no opposing grant onto it is possible either. Apart from this caution carve-out the exclusion is occupancy-independent: for a non-caution two-way block off a switch it holds the authority back from the junction before any opposing train has arrived, so there is no window in which both trains have already committed past the switch (the reversal-trap exclusion above instead keys on a specific stopped occupant, so it cannot hold back until that occupant is already in place). If no shorter block-terminated prefix can be acquired, the create call fails with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` and a `reason` such as `"cannot hold best-effort authority fouling a switch into a two-way block"`. The exclusion constrains only truncation short of the requested target; when the live authority reaches the requested target itself, its terminal may be any block, switch-adjacent or not.
    - If the train's covered span itself is empty so no non-empty live `resource_sequence` can be formed for the new path, `PathDispatcher.create_path(..., acquisition_mode=best_effort)` must fail.
    - Exception: if the train head is currently on a switch resource and no forward block beyond that switch can be acquired, `PathDispatcher.create_path(..., acquisition_mode=best_effort)` must fail with `failure_kind = head_on_switch_no_block_authority` rather than storing a head-on-switch holding authority. A natural-language failure reason such as `"cannot create best-effort authority while head is on a switch"` is sufficient in v1.
12. Allocate the new `path_id` before switch acquisition so `Switch.locks` and flank-protection claims can be keyed by that same path ID. Once the chosen acquisition succeeds, create the dispatcher-owned path record directly in `ACTIVE` state; the controller observes the new movement-authority projection on its next `received_path_plan` read. There is no intermediate persisted lifecycle status.
13. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` seeds `resource_sequence` from the train's already-occupied resource span. Start-side selection uses the canonical rule already defined in the previous step: cab-selected -> head-side occupied block; no cab -> both end blocks compared by the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**. The stored sequence is `[already-occupied resources from the opposite span end through the chosen start side] + [forward candidate from start side to requested target]`. The already-occupied backward portion is not re-reserved; only the forward suffix is subject to `all_or_nothing` vs `best_effort` acquisition. `current_resource_index` is initialized to the head resource (cab selected) or to the authority-side resource at the closer span end (no cab). On each seeded entry, the side toward the path direction is `exit_edge` and the opposite side is `entry_edge`. Covered-task normalization may prevent route-side calls for already-covered `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks. For `task_kind == "stop"`, the covered-task advance step immediately satisfies the stop when the train is already stopped, no active path exists, and the task's destination block is inside the train's current span (the covered-stop case — e.g. the route was created while the train was already at the destination platform). While the train is rolling or a live path exists, stops are not finished by coverage; `create_path(...)` is still required to produce movement authority.
14. Build `resource_sequence` in traversal order so both blocks and switches are tracked in one sequence.
15. Store the resolved requested target (`requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`) on the `Path`. The current authorized target read `authority_end_block_id` is derived from the stored `resource_sequence`'s terminal block entry, so storing the sequence is enough.
16. The active-path guard has already run before acquisition. A retained `CANCELLED` / `COMPLETED` row or retained history row does not by itself block `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`; only ordinary resource conflicts still apply.
17. Store the new dispatcher-owned path record in `paths_by_id[path_id]` with `Path.status = ACTIVE` (which by definition makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return this `path_id`).
18. Do not copy reserved-block state onto `Train`; future reservations remain dispatcher-owned on resources and in the controller-facing authority projection until head-entry consumes them.
19. The movement-authority projection must preserve an explicit traversal frame for every block and switch entry; readers must not try to recover that frame later from tile coordinates alone.

**`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**

- `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` and `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` return a small result object with exactly `success: bool`, `path_id: Optional[str]`, `resource_sequence: Optional[List[resource_sequence entry]]`, `requested_destination_block_id: Optional[str]`, `requested_destination_direction: Optional[str]`, `authority_end_block_id: Optional[str]`, `result_kind: Optional[PathAcquisitionResultKind]`, `failure_kind: Optional[PathAcquisitionFailureKind]`, and `reason: Optional[str]`.
- `PathAcquisitionResultKind` has v1 values:
  - `reached_requested_target` (`321a`): the live authority reaches the requested destination block/direction for this attempt.
  - `truncated_grew` (`33b1`): `best_effort` acquired at least one new forward resource but the live authority is still short of the requested target.
  - `blocked_no_growth` (`b003`): `best_effort` proved the current live path is still a valid prefix toward the requested target but could not acquire the next forward resource because of live occupancy/reservation/lock conflict on this tick; valid only for `extend_path(...)` against an existing live path. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` never returns this result kind.
- `PathAcquisitionFailureKind` has v1 values:
  - `no_viable_path` (`062a`): no topology-compatible, direction-compatible suffix exists to the requested target under the availability rules (disabled resources always excluded; manual resources excluded when `manual_override == False`; `Block.allowed_travel_directions` enforced when `manual_override == False`, per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home) and the repeated-resource rule.
  - `blocked_before_target` (`d6e3`): a viable candidate exists but the requested authority could not be acquired to the requested target because of live occupancy/reservation/lock conflict on one or more required resources. In `all_or_nothing`, this means the full candidate could not be acquired due to that conflict. In `best_effort` create, this means no block-terminated live authority could be stored for the chosen candidate — either because of that conflict, or because every acquirable block-terminated prefix is rejected by a truncation-terminal exclusion (the waypoint-target, reversal-trap, or junction-fouling exclusions of item 11) so no storable authority short of the target remains. Direction problems use `invalid_reverse`; topology/connectivity problems use `no_viable_path`; `extend_path(...)` precondition problems use `missing_or_inactive_path`.
  - `invalid_reverse` (`4d0e`): `extend_path(...)` would reverse at the current authority end.
  - `invalid_request` (`065a`): required request state is missing, ambiguous, or unusable, such as a `requested_destination_block_id` that is already inside the train's current occupied span (covered destinations are finished by Phase 1's covered-task index advance, never by dispatcher authority).
  - `head_on_switch_no_block_authority` (`0f9b`): `best_effort` could not form a block-terminated authority because the train head is on a switch and no forward block beyond that switch can be acquired.
  - `missing_or_inactive_path` (`d76f`): `extend_path(...)` was called with a missing or non-`ACTIVE` path.
  - `train_already_has_active_path` (`a91c`): `create_path(...)` was called for a `train_id` that already has an `ACTIVE` path. Callers must use `extend_path(...)` to grow that same live path, or wait until the old path has transitioned out of `ACTIVE` before retrying `create_path(...)`.
- On success, `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, `authority_end_block_id`, and `result_kind` are required; `failure_kind = None`; `reason = None`; and `resource_sequence` is the full current live `resource_sequence` after that create/extend operation completes. `RouteExecutor` may inspect `result_kind` and geometry to see whether the armed pending-waypoint target is already contained in the live path, whether best-effort authority grew, or whether the live path remains a valid blocked prefix. It does not advance any route waypoint index from this return payload alone.
- Success means that `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` created a new path record with a non-empty block-terminated live `resource_sequence`, or that `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` either appended at least one new resource to the existing live `resource_sequence` or returned `result_kind = blocked_no_growth` for an existing live path that remains a valid prefix toward the caller's requested target but is temporarily blocked by live occupancy/reservation/lock conflict. A create-path call never succeeds as `blocked_no_growth`; if it cannot store any block-terminated live authority for the chosen candidate, it fails. An extend-path call may succeed without appending only in the explicit `blocked_no_growth` case. Structural failures such as no legal forward suffix, invalid reverse, or missing/inactive path still return `success=False`.
- On failure, `success = False`, `path_id = None`, `resource_sequence = None`, all target fields are `None`, `result_kind = None`, `failure_kind` is required, and `reason` is required.
- `reason` is human-readable diagnostic text for create/extend failures. Callers may display or log it, but they must not branch on exact string equality in v1; control flow branches only on `success`, `result_kind`, and `failure_kind`.

**`cancel_path()` return contract**

- `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, initiator=None, reason=None)` returns a small result object with exactly `success: bool`, `path_id: Optional[str]`, `new_status: Optional[PathStatus]`, and `reason: Optional[str]`. `initiator` is an `Optional[str]` free-form label (e.g., `"eb"`, `"operator_cancelled"`, `"power_off"`, `"deviation"`, `"revoked"`) that flows directly into the `path_cancellation` notification payload defined in the **Cancellation** subsection; v1 has no enum for it, and no planning or routing logic branches on it.
- On success, `success = True`, `path_id` is required, `new_status = CANCELLED`, and `reason` is optional plain-text diagnostic context.
- On failure, `success = False`, `path_id = None`, `new_status = None`, and `reason` is required.
- Failure cases are explicit in v1:
  - missing path -> failure with a plain-text reason such as `"path not found"`
  - already `COMPLETED` path -> failure with reason `"already completed"`
  - already `CANCELLED` path -> failure with reason `"already cancelled"`
- When the path is currently `ACTIVE`, `cancel_path(...)` performs the normal cancellation flow described below and returns success. Callers may display or log `reason`, but they must never branch on exact reason text.

**Resource acquisition**

- The caller's `manual_override` flag (default `False`) flows through the entire acquisition chain: `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` pass it to `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(...)`, which forwards it to `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` (used by block-eligibility checks) and `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` (which sets `claim.manual_override` on every `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` call it issues). Pathfinder and candidate-search calls invoked from inside `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` pass the same flag into the `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` request so manual resources are included or excluded consistently with the acquisition step. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the full override contract.
- Acquire switches first, then blocks, so switch conflicts are discovered before any blocks are reserved. In `best_effort`, dispatcher still uses this switch-first probe to decide the storable prefix: walk the chosen candidate's future switch resources in path order, acquiring main and flank claims until the first switch/flank acquisition failure. If a switch acquisition fails, roll back any switch claims already acquired for that probe, truncate the candidate to the previous block before that failed switch, and then attempt the block reservations for that block-terminated prefix. If every switch in the candidate probe succeeds but block reservation later fails, use the first failing block as the truncation point, roll back, and retry the block-terminated prefix before that block. The final stored prefix is then acquired atomically using the same switch-first, blocks-second order. If that final prefix contains no storable block authority for `create_path(...)`, the create fails; for `extend_path(...)`, no appended prefix may still become `blocked_no_growth` when the existing path remains a valid blocked prefix.
- For each switch in path order, call `switch.acquire_lock(claim)` with a `claim` carrying `path_id`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, and `source_switch_id=None` (the `None` source marks the claim as a main-usage claim), and immediately acquire any `switch.flank_protection` locks. A `flank_protection` entry is skipped when its `neighbour_switch_id` is itself a main-usage switch in the same acquisition request set (a route point this path traverses): that neighbour is already set and locked by its own main claim, so issuing an opposing flank claim on it would make the path self-conflict and `Switch.acquire_lock` would reject the route's own primary. This route-point exemption is the canonical home for the rule. Two switches in series on one route — for example a depot-throat pair where the path runs out of one switch straight onto the lead through the next — each appear in the other's `flank_protection` list, so this exemption is what lets a route through such a throat acquire its switches at all. A `flank_protection` entry is also skipped when two of this path's route points would lock the same `neighbour_switch_id` to opposite `safe_state` values — the switch-triangle case, where that neighbour sits at the apex with one route point on each of its two diverging legs (for example a depot-lead crossover apex whose legs reach the mainline switch on one side and the N-track switch on the other). No single position of that neighbour aims its branch away from both route points, so it cannot flank-protect the path; locking it to either state would reject one route point's own primary. The two route points need not be acquired in the same call: in `best_effort` the switch-first probe truncates a path into separately-acquired segments, so the earlier route point may already be committed, with its flank claim recorded on the apex — as a future flank lock keyed `(path_id, earlier_route_point)` before the train reaches that route point, or as a `train_protection_claim` owned by this train once the train has entered it. The exemption therefore skips the apex whenever the opposite-state demand is already held there by this same path or train, not only when both route points appear in the current call. This same-path conflict exemption then leaves the apex protected only as the path's earlier route point already set it (and unlocked when both route points are in one call), rather than failing the segment — the path's own block reservations and route-point switch locks still keep a second train from intruding through it. Like the route-point exemption it is applied at acquisition time, where path membership — including route points committed by this path's earlier segments — is known. Each remaining (non-exempt) `flank_protection` entry `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` is acquired by calling `neighbour_switch.acquire_lock(claim)` with `source_switch_id=switch.switch_id` (the originating main switch ID marks the claim as a flank-protection claim) and `locked_state=safe_state`, where `safe_state` is the neighbour's state whose active segment does **not** point toward the primary switch's track — i.e. the state that keeps the neighbour's diverging path aimed away, preventing a head-on approach. `safe_state` is computable from switch geometry: it is the neighbour state whose selected branch exit edge is not the edge facing the primary switch. In v1, because switches are binary, `safe_state` is simply the opposite of the neighbour's `blocking_state` recorded at placement time. Every acquired flank claim stores `source_switch_id = switch.switch_id` so later switch head-entry can convert only the flank claims sourced from that main switch into train-owned protection.
- Acquire blocks atomically through `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`.
- Resource acquisition writes `Block.reserved_direction` through `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` for every reserved block. Tile-level traversal direction is carried by `resource_sequence[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge` and nested `path_tiles[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge`; there is no `TrackSegment.path_direction` field.
- Distinguish retained geometry, static allowed travel, and temporary reservation direction: v1 does not define a track-level `traffic_direction_policy` field. Static allowed travel direction lives on `Block.allowed_travel_directions`; `Block.reserved_direction` is only the dispatcher-owned temporary reservation direction for one path; and the path plan geometry carries the finer-grained per-tile traversal frame inside that block.
- On failure, roll back in reverse acquisition order, including switch locks and block reservations. There is no separate tile-level `path_direction` assignment to clear.
- That rollback releases the acquired claims/reservations only; it does not restore an earlier physical `Switch.position`. The live switch alignment remains at the last successfully written value until some later switch command or path acquisition changes it again.
- Completion and cancellation release blocks through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)`, not direct `block.release()` calls.
- In v1, block reservations remain single-train/single-path resources even though `Switch.locks` can have multiple compatible owners. `Block.reserved_direction` therefore has at most one active value per reserved block and should be cleared when that block reservation is released.
- `create_path(..., acquisition_mode: PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode)` and `extend_path(..., acquisition_mode: PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode)` must share the same `PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode` enum, with two v1 behaviors:
  - `AcquisitionMode.all_or_nothing`: dispatcher acquires only the full requested authority or returns failure after full rollback
  - `AcquisitionMode.best_effort`: dispatcher may truncate to the furthest forward acquirable resource prefix, but the acquisition of that chosen prefix is still atomic
- In `best_effort` mode, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` must either create a block-terminated live path record or fail; it never reports no-growth success. `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` may report no-growth success only as `success=True/result_kind=blocked_no_growth`, and only when the existing live path remains a valid prefix toward the caller's current requested target while the next forward resource is temporarily blocked by live occupancy/reservation/lock conflict. That result updates the path's stored requested target contract; the controller projection reflects the same-path update on the next read. Any other extend attempt that appends no resource fails and leaves the existing path unchanged.
- The special head-on-switch case remains a failure because v1 does not allow storing a head-on-switch live authority end.
- In all acquisition modes, the stored live authority end must be block-terminated. A same-tick acquisition result that reaches a switch but no following block is not a valid stored live authority in v1; dispatcher must roll that trailing switch/flank acquisition back before storing the path.

**Controller-mediated progress**

- `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` should compute the next `TrainSpan`, next `current_speed_kmh`, and the ordered tile-level delta for that movement, then call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` at least once every tick. `TrainPhysics` does not call dispatcher progress, completion, or release APIs directly and does not own the stored span.
- `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` should atomically write `span_update.new_span` and `span_update.current_speed_kmh` into controller-owned storage, then build the entered/vacated resource observations from their two sources: block entered/tail-vacated observations are taken from the Step-0 detector slice stashed for this train (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection — the detector is a black box to this layer; the simulated `InputInterface` derives its current map from the calculated spans, but the controller consumes only the confirmed result), while switch entered/tail-vacated observations are detected by converting `span_update.added_tiles` and `span_update.removed_tiles` through network tile/resource lookup, `received_path_plan.resource_sequence` when authority exists, and current traversal context. It then appends one ordered `ControllerProgressReport` to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). Stop completion is not reported from this method; it is evaluated separately later in that same train's Phase 3 consume pass from the post-move controller state.
- The controller progress report payload follows the canonical **ControllerProgressReport schema** defined in the **Dispatcher progress/completion flow** section. `current_head_sequence_index` is the index of the train head's current position in `received_path_plan.resource_sequence`, or `None` when no active resource-sequence mapping is available. When live authority exists and the controller cannot map the head to that active `resource_sequence`, the report must set `deviation = true`, `deviation_kind = "out_of_sequence"`, and `current_head_sequence_index = None`; dispatcher treats that report as deviation/cancellation input rather than as ordinary active-path progress. For a single report, dispatcher processing order is entered resources first, then deviation cancellation if `report.deviation == true`, then tail-vacated resources.
- Phase 3 is the only normal consumer of these reports. It drains the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) in order, calls `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each one, and clears the mailbox. Within that same drain, each route head-advance callback compares its single reported block advance with the current next waypoint; if that callback consumes a waypoint, the following report in the mailbox is what may discover and test the next waypoint. After local progress reports have been consumed and any movement-derived deviation cancellation from them has already run, if an active path still exists for that train, `SimulationController` runs the post-physics usable-authority stop check against the controller's current state and, on success, calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` (timestamps per **Time sampling**). Explicit latched teardown such as `pending_path_cancel_request` or `pending_power_off` is consumed only after that normal completion chance.
- The controller report carries `deviation_kind` as a structured diagnostic for the dispatcher / route callback chain, but v1 route progression does not branch on it. Once the active path has been cancelled in Phase 3, the next eligible Phase 1 always returns to the ordinary covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` flow from the train's current physical position; already-covered pass-through handling and stop handling follow the normal task-kind rules.
- When physical movement crosses multiple resource boundaries within one simulation tick, `TrainPhysics` must fire multiple sequential `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` calls — one per resource boundary crossed, in physical traversal order — rather than batching them into a single multi-resource report. The per-boundary reports carry the switch observations (calculated traversal, this tick); the detector-confirmed block observations are folded into that tick's final no-boundary report, ordered by `sequence_index` when the entries map into the active `resource_sequence` and by physical entry order otherwise (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). Consumers must therefore tolerate the one-tick detector lag between block and switch observations; the monotonic `Path.current_resource_index` rule below absorbs the skew. `TrainPhysics` computes each incremental `span_update` up to the next boundary, calls `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` for that boundary, which appends that step's report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), then computes the next boundary update and repeats. The boundary `event_time` on each returned `ControllerProgressReport` is sampled by `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` itself via `time.time()` at the write site (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**); `TrainPhysics` does not pass a timestamp in. After the last boundary in that tick, or on a tick with no boundary crossing, `TrainPhysics` must still emit one final callback for that tick. When no resource boundary was crossed, that final update carries `added_tiles=[]` and `removed_tiles=[]`; when nothing changed physically, it may be an explicit no-change callback. This guarantees that Phase 3 always runs, that latches such as `pending_power_off` have a deterministic consume point, and that downstream side effects (lock consumption, tail-vacate release, route head-advance callbacks, switch state notifications) are later committed in the same physical order as the local progress reports. Within any single `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` call, `entered_resources` and `tail_vacated_resources` therefore typically carry one entry each at most; if a single call legitimately needs to carry more (for example simultaneous head entry and tail vacate at the same boundary), events within each list must still be ordered by physical traversal / vacate order, and dispatcher still processes the entered list before the vacated list.
- Entry ownership is resource-typed and dispatcher-mediated; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on `resource_type` — it routes every entry through `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, path_id, route)`:
  - on block entry, the coordinator routes to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)`; this is the only normal runtime path that writes `Block.occupied_by`
  - on clean switch entry, the coordinator routes to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)`, which records `Switch.physical_occupants[route] = train_id`, consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock, and converts applicable flank locks into train-owned protection. Two trains using non-conflicting routes coexist (per `Switch.routes_conflict(...)`); the hard-failure case is an incoming `route` that conflicts with a route held by a different train, or the intrusion case where the entering train holds no compatible lock for its route (see the **Switch entity** intrusion check). A conflict must not overwrite the prior owner
- Dispatcher progress should update `Path.current_resource_index` using the reported path-step `sequence_index`, not by searching for the first matching raw `resource_id`. `Path.current_resource_index` is head-based progress only; it advances when the train head enters a new resource and does not wait for the later tail-based release of earlier resources. Because switch observations (calculated, same tick) and block observations (detector-confirmed, one tick later) interleave with that skew, `current_resource_index` is monotonic: an entry whose `sequence_index` is at or behind the current value leaves it unchanged (`current_resource_index = max(current, reported sequence_index)`).
- A live path should remain `ACTIVE` after creation, through intermediate resources, and after entering the final block/resource. Entering the terminal resource should not change lifecycle state by itself.
- Only Phase 3 may attempt dispatcher path completion. After local progress reports have been consumed and any movement-derived deviation handling from those reports has already run, if an active path still exists for that train, `SimulationController` runs the post-physics usable-authority stop check against the controller's current state. That check is the predicate defined in step `8df5` of the **Active-path completion procedure**. If it is true, Phase 3 calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` (timestamps per **Time sampling**). `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` still owns the path-level acceptance check: the path completes only when the active `Path.authority_end_block_id == Path.requested_destination_block_id` and the terminal authority block entry's computed block-relative traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`. The direction half of this check is skipped when the terminal authority block is a single-tile block (`len(Block.ordered_tiles) == 1`): a one-tile block has no increasing/decreasing `ordered_tiles` order to anchor block-relative `forward` / `reverse` against, so the terminal-direction derivation and the block's own compass-exit convention need not agree, and the `authority_end_block_id` equality plus the post-physics usable-authority stop check already pin physical arrival. When the direction check applies (multi-tile terminal block) and fails, the path remains live and route-side extension/retry logic continues. The single canonical depart-acceptance signal is `controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`, set by any canonical transition into that state listed in the Controller state machine table (either a successful `TrainController.depart()` call or the reactive speed-based evaluation observing motion under live authority). Manual driving input may command motion under live authority before Phase 1's `depart()` call lands; ATC still protects the train. The reactive transition into `RUNNING_MOVING` is treated as equally legitimate to depart-acceptance: stop-completion eligibility, `ato.running` setting (when `control_mode == "ato"` and `received_path_plan is not None`), and other readers all key on `state == RUNNING_MOVING` regardless of which transition fired. Head-entry route progress, including `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption through `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`, is not state-gated. Explicit latched admin teardown such as `pending_path_cancel_request` and `pending_power_off` is consumed only after this normal completion chance.
- A distance-0 ATC limit at its floor is not by itself stop completion. No-cab, no-authority, EB, obstruction/conflict, wrong-direction cab, and truncated best-effort authority all drive the distance-0 limit to zero without being stop completion; only `atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"` (with the controller stopped and the other `8df5` conditions) is.
- For a stop waypoint whose live authority already reaches its requested target block and direction, a post-physics controller stop at the safety-margin-adjusted usable authority stop completes the path normally. The head may be short of the physical requested block when the safety margin/ATC boundary requires that; ATC remains the higher-priority rule and there is no "go back and try again" prompt for a within-block or before-block safety-margin stop.
- EB activation records `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "eb"` when a live path exists, and that request is committed by the same train's Phase 3 consume pass through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. While EB is latched, the post-physics usable-authority stop check must fail; after EB release any route continuation uses a fresh path request.
- Controller-reported deviation produces `path_cancellation` with `initiator = "deviation"`; the structural distinction between wrong-resource (`"out_of_sequence"`) and beyond-authorized-target (`"beyond_authorized_target"`) flows on the event payload's `deviation_kind` field defined in the **Cancellation** subsection. Cancellation wins over any same-tick completion attempt. Recovery follows the canonical recovery table; both deviation flavors share the same recovery shell. A `task_kind == "waypoint"` target the train has entered, passed, or later covers counts as served and does not produce a separate overshot-waypoint deviation.
- A best-effort truncated path must not complete just because the train has stopped at the current authority boundary while the current authorized target is still short of the requested target. In that case the same path remains `ACTIVE`, the controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` (stopped under zero ATC clearance against the still-live authority), and `RouteExecutor` keeps trying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on that same `path_id` until the idle-hold release threshold: when the authority neither grows nor sees the head advance for `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS` consecutive Phase-1 ticks, Phase 1 releases the truncated authority and re-plans after a cooldown so the held reservations/locks return to higher-priority runs — see the **idle-hold maintenance and release** rule in Planning Kernel step `66aa`.
- Completed paths should release only not-yet-entered block reservations through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)`, because reservations for already-entered blocks were consumed on head entry. That reservation release may force-clear terminal `CAUTION` only for matching still-reserved owned blocks, but it must not force-clear any physical `OCCUPIED` block early. On this `ACTIVE -> COMPLETED` transition, dispatcher follows the **Active-path completion procedure** ordering. The retained record may remain in `paths_by_id` for history, but physical release of a switch or block still under the train resolves from the resource itself. When a later tail-vacate resource event arrives through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, it clears `Switch.occupied_by` / block occupancy and any train-owned switch protection through the resource-specific release path. `RouteExecutor` treats the dispatcher callback as authoritative completion and does not re-check safety-margin projection itself.
- When creating or extending a path, resolve and store the requested target contract on the dispatcher-owned `Path` record first, then update `Path.path_id` and `Path.resource_sequence` on that same record. `Path.authority_end_block_id` is a derived read of `resource_sequence[-1].resource_id` and updates automatically. The controller's `received_path_plan` property reads these live on the next access; there is no separate controller write step:
  - if the live authority already reaches the requested target, the derived `authority_end_block_id == Path.requested_destination_block_id` and the terminal block entry's computed block-relative traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`
  - if the live authority is still truncated, the derived `authority_end_block_id` is the current temporary end of `resource_sequence`; the requested target fields remain on the `Path` record only
- Drive resource release from tail-vacate observations (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches). `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` routes every vacate through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)` (the single `resource_type` branch lives inside the coordinator; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on it):
  - vacated blocks go through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)`
  - vacated switches go through `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)`.
