The physical state is cleared first — the matching `Switch.physical_occupants[route]` entry owned by this train is removed — and only then, when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train on this switch, the manager clears train-owned protection sourced from that switch via `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id=switch_id, train_id=train_id)` on each protected neighbour. The physical-vacate handler never decides which lock to drop; the manager does, keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id)`, so a switch that holds both a main lock and a flank lock for the same route releases the correct one (per the **Switch entity** tail-vacate contract)
- All normal block release goes through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` so `OCCUPIED -> CAUTION -> FREE` stays a single-manager state machine. Manual/admin reservation cleanup uses the same `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)` entry point as routine completion/cancellation release.

**Path extension**

- The controller has no lookahead cache to invalidate (lookahead is computed live each read; see the lookahead section). Path updates therefore have no per-controller invalidation step.
- A change to `Block.allowed_travel_directions` (static config edit) does **not** invalidate any already-active path's reservations; the live path's `reserved_direction` was set at acquisition time and remains valid for the life of that path. The new direction policy takes effect only at the next `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` call. Operator/admin code must not edit `allowed_travel_directions` to indirectly cancel a live path; cancellation goes through `pending_path_cancel_request`.
- Truncated best-effort authority where the train physically stops at the temporary boundary while no resolved target is armed (the one-tick gap between task-completion and the next Phase 1 target arm) is permissible: the controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` against the still-live truncated authority with zero ATC clearance, and the next Phase 1 may extend authority once the next target resolves.
- Extension is forward-only. **`RouteExecutor` must call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` only with the same `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair as the currently armed `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`, where `walk_position` is the index returned by the latest `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call for the live planning chain.** Any other target is a route-side invariant violation and must be expressed via cancel-and-replan, never via extend with a different requested target.
- When the path model switches to `resource_sequence`, append new tiles and new resources while skipping duplicated connection/start entries.
- Reject any extension that would cause a resource to appear twice in the combined path.
- `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` should take the same argument contract as `create_path(...)`, except that it identifies the existing live path by `path_id`: `path_id`, `requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, `acquisition_mode`, and `manual_override` (mirroring `create_path(...)`'s full signature; `manual_override` flows through the acquisition chain exactly as for create). The live path identified by `path_id` is the fixed already-acquired prefix; `extend_path(...)` grows that existing path forward from its current authority end toward the caller-supplied requested target for this extend attempt. Dispatcher does not run a separate shared-walk prefix-compatibility test. If the requested extension cannot continue forward from the existing live path without revisiting an already-held resource or otherwise violating the ordinary path rules, `extend_path(...)` fails and leaves the existing path unchanged.
- The shared request contract between `extend_path(...)` and `create_path(...)` is the same, but `extend_path(...)` adds extra constraints: it requires a live `path_id`, and the newly appended suffix must continue forward from that live path's current authority-end edge in the same travel direction the existing prefix was built in. The caller-supplied `requested_destination_direction` applies only to the new terminal block `requested_destination_block_id`; it is not a license to reverse at the current authority end. A `requested_destination_direction` that would require the train to reverse 180 degrees at the current authority end (for example, the requested new terminal block lies behind the current authority-end edge on the existing prefix's travel frame, or reaching it would require exiting the authority-end block backward out of its current `exit_edge`) is invalid for `extend_path(...)` and must be rejected with `success = False`, `failure_kind = invalid_reverse`, and a diagnostic `reason` such as `"extend would reverse at authority end"`. The existing live path must remain unchanged in that rejection. Callers that actually need the train to turn back at the current terminal must cancel the existing path and issue a fresh `create_path(...)` from the train's current span instead.
- `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` must return `success=False` with `failure_kind = missing_or_inactive_path` and a required `reason` when `path_id` does not exist or when the referenced path is not currently `ACTIVE`.
- In `all_or_nothing` mode, `extend_path(...)` should either acquire the full requested suffix to `requested_destination_block_id` or leave the existing path unchanged. Any outcome that cannot reach the requested target is `success=False`; use `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` when the suffix exists but cannot be fully acquired because of live occupancy/reservation/lock conflict, and `failure_kind = no_viable_path` when no legal suffix exists.
- In `best_effort` mode, `extend_path(...)` should keep the same `path_id` and append the furthest forward acquirable suffix it can add to the current path. The `resource_sequence` therefore grows in place on the same live path instead of creating a new path. When at least one resource is appended, update the stored requested target fields on that same `Path` to the caller-supplied contract for that attempt, even if the live authority remains truncated short of it, and return `result_kind = truncated_grew` or `reached_requested_target` as appropriate. The same waypoint-target exclusion as on `create_path(...)` applies to the appended suffix: the live authority's new terminal block after the extend must not be the resolved target block of a `task_kind == "waypoint"` task on the requesting `RouteRun`'s current directed walk. If the block-terminated truncation point would land on a waypoint target block, dispatcher must extend the storable suffix at least one block past it before committing the extend; if no further forward block beyond the waypoint target can be acquired on this attempt, the existing path is left unchanged and the call fails with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` and a `reason` such as `"cannot truncate authority at a waypoint target"`. The same reversal-trap exclusion as on `create_path(...)` also applies to the appended suffix: the live authority's new terminal block after the extend must not be a block a stopped train ahead needs to reverse back out through. If growth would land there, dispatcher does not append that block; if that leaves no resource to append while the existing path stays a valid prefix blocked only by that occupied block, the extend returns `blocked_no_growth` as for any other live-occupancy block on the next forward resource. The same junction-fouling exclusion as on `create_path(...)` also applies to the appended suffix: the live authority's new terminal block after the extend must not be a two-way block entered straight off a switch (one-way terminal blocks, and two-way blocks in `CAUTION`, are exempt — see the item 11 canonical rule). If growth would land there, dispatcher does not append that block, holding the authority one block back before the switch so the junction stays clear. If no resource can be appended but the dispatcher can prove the existing path is still a legal prefix to the requested target and the next resource is blocked only by live occupancy/reservation/lock conflict, return `success=True` with `result_kind = blocked_no_growth`, update the existing path's stored requested target fields to the caller-supplied contract for that attempt, leave `resource_sequence` unchanged (the derived `authority_end_block_id` is therefore unchanged too), and let `RouteExecutor` retry later. The controller's `received_path_plan` property continues to return the same `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, and `authority_end_block_id` projection on its next read; refreshed requested-target fields remain dispatcher-internal. If no resource can be appended because no legal forward suffix exists, return `success=False` with `failure_kind = no_viable_path` and leave the existing path unchanged.
- While the path is still truncated short of the caller's current requested target, the stored `resource_sequence` terminates on the current last acquired block; the derived `authority_end_block_id` reflects that terminal automatically.
- Every successful `extend_path(...)` updates the dispatcher's `Path` record in place. `truncated_grew` / `reached_requested_target` carry the grown `resource_sequence`; `blocked_no_growth` carries the unchanged geometry but updates the requested-target fields. The controller's `received_path_plan` property reads these updates live on the next access. When the live authority finally reaches the requested target, the derived `authority_end_block_id` equals `Path.requested_destination_block_id`.
- In `best_effort` mode, a no-growth extend result is therefore branchable without parsing `reason`: `success=True/result_kind=blocked_no_growth` means "valid prefix, temporarily blocked; keep the live path and retry later," while `success=False/failure_kind=no_viable_path` or `invalid_reverse` means "the existing live path cannot continue to this requested target; RouteExecutor must latch cancellation before any fresh create-path attempt."

**Cancellation**

- `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, initiator=None, reason=None)` is the single canonical cancellation entry point in v1. `initiator` is a free-form label that flows directly into the `path_cancellation` notification payload for logs/UI; it must not be used as a condition by any planning or routing logic. `reason` is plain-text diagnostic context for logs/UI only and likewise must not be used for condition branching. Recovery branching is driven by controller state, runtime latches (`eb.active`, `pending_power_off`, `received_path_plan`), current span coverage, and the normal waypoint-kind rules.
- Release owned blocks through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)` instead of direct `block.release()` calls. In the desired v1 model, cancellation release clears future reservation ownership only; it never clears physical occupancy.
- Path cancellation or revocation effects are owned entirely by `PathDispatcher`, and the cancellation commit happens only in Phase 3. `RouteExecutor` or `TrainController` may request cancellation only by recording controller-owned Phase-3 handoff state such as `pending_path_cancel_request`; they must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clear authority, or release resources directly from Phase 1 or Phase 2. If Phase 1 route logic decides that the current live path must be revoked, it records or refreshes that cancel request and then stops planning for that train for that tick; later eligible Phase 1 passes follow the same latch-only rule while the incompatible live path still exists. Only after the same train's later Phase 3 consumes that request, `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` completes, and the ordinary cancellation callback clears route-side live-path correlation may a later eligible Phase 1 resume ordinary create/extend planning. When Phase 3 later commits the cancellation through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, dispatcher transitions `Path.status` to `CANCELLED`, which by definition makes the controller's `received_path_plan` property return `None` on its next read.
- Power-off uses this same dispatcher cancellation workflow through the inclusive-latch fan-out: see the **Power-off procedure** for the canonical four-step sequence (`98d3` → `278a` → `534f` → `886a`).
- On the `ACTIVE -> CANCELLED` transition, `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` runs synchronously and must finish lower-layer teardown before it makes the path externally non-active. Cancellation follows the canonical terminal-teardown order defined in step `09b0` of the **Active-path completion procedure**, with `Path.status` transitioning to `CANCELLED` (which by definition removes that path from `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` and makes the controller's `received_path_plan` property return `None` on its next read). Only after that ordered teardown is complete may `cancel_path(...)` return success and deliver the route-side cancellation callback. Cancellation clears future reservation/authority only; it never clears `Block.occupied_by`, `Block.caution`, `Switch.occupied_by`, or train-owned switch protection. A block reservation may be cleared even when the train still physically occupies that block because reservation ownership and physical occupancy are independent, but in normal flow same-train reservations for entered blocks have already been consumed on head entry. A same-train new path is allowed once the old path is no longer `ACTIVE`, subject to ordinary live resource conflicts and train-owned physical protection.
- Future switch/flank release for both `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` and `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` is computed by scanning the whole `Path.resource_sequence`, not by retaining a separate path-level flank list and not by stopping at `Path.current_resource_index`. For each switch resource in the sequence, `PathDispatcher` attempts to release any still-future main switch lock claim owned by that `path_id` (keyed `(path_id, source_switch_id=None)`); that release is idempotent and no-ops when head entry already consumed the claim into `Switch.occupied_by` / train-owned protection. For each same switch resource, `PathDispatcher` also inspects the switch's current flank-protection configuration and releases matching still-future flank lock claims owned by that `path_id` on each protected neighbour switch, keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id=<main switch id>)`. This scan clears only path-owned future lock claims. It must not clear `Switch.occupied_by` or `Switch.train_protection_claims`; those remain physical/train-owned and are released only by later controller-reported switch tail-vacate.
- Reservation / physical state ownership in v1 should be read literally:
  - dispatcher-owned future reservation state: `Block.reserved_by_path`, `Block.reserved_for_train`, `Block.reserved_direction`, `Switch.locks`, flank-protection lock ownership for resources the train has not entered yet
  - block physical state owned by `BlockManager` and mutated only through `BlockManager` entry/tail-vacate APIs invoked by `PathDispatcher` on detector-confirmed observations (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection): `Block.occupied_by`, `Block.caution`
  - dispatcher-mediated switch physical state, written by `PathDispatcher` on controller-reported resource-entry / tail-vacate observations rather than directly by `TrainPhysics`: `Switch.physical_occupants` and `Switch.train_protection_claims` (the `Switch.occupied_by` read is a derived projection of `physical_occupants`, not a separately written store)
  - a block or switch has at most one physical `occupied_by` train at a time (single-train physical ownership). A single train may hold multiple path records layered over that physical ownership; path membership is tracked separately by `PathDispatcher` on `Path` records, not by duplicating `occupied_by` per path.
  - cancellation / completion release clears only future block reservations and future switch locks that have not already been consumed by head entry
  - only physical entry / tail-vacate observations committed through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` may clear or set `BlockManager`-owned block physical state or dispatcher-mediated switch physical state (block observations are detector-confirmed per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; switch observations are controller-reported traversal)
  - once a resource is physically occupied, later tail-vacate clears physical occupancy and train-owned protection rather than path-owned residue
- After cancellation transitions the dispatcher-owned path out of `ACTIVE`, the controller follows the no-authority braking path defined in step `826a` of the **Controller tick order**: the canonical lookahead becomes the no-authority empty payload, ATC sees overspeed at distance 0 (when the train is still rolling), and EB is engaged per the **canonical EB activation effects**. The operator clears EB through `eb.release()` once stopped, which transitions the controller to `READY`. Physics keeps integrating live network state during the EB brake; cancellation does not unlock a physically occupied switch.
- After cancelling a live path, the runtime should deliver the canonical cancellation context through `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, reason=None)` (which delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`) only after `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` has finished its own lower-layer mutation for that train. The diagnostic `initiator` and original `deviation_kind` (when the cancellation was deviation-driven) are surfaced only on the `path_cancellation` notification; they do not cross the dispatcher/route callback boundary. This notification is mandatory, but the free-form `reason` string is informational only. `RouteExecutor` should inspect whether that cancelled path actually affects its current waypoint / assigned route state, must not treat the old path as finished, and must defer all next-target selection / fresh authority assignment until the next eligible Phase 1 after the stopped no-authority or post-EB recovery point.
- **`path_cancellation` notification.** This is the canonical structured event surface for cancellation diagnostics. `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` emits one `path_cancellation` notification through `NotificationCenter` as the last step of the canonical cancellation flow, after the dispatcher mutation has committed and the route-side `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` callback has been invoked. UI/log consumers use this event as the canonical surface for cancellation diagnostics. The notification carries:
  - `notification_id` — assigned by `NotificationCenter` per the canonical notification rule
  - `event_time` — system-time Unix seconds; sampled by `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` via `time.time()` at the moment it emits this notification (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). The same sampled value is the one the writer also stores into `Path.last_status_updated_time` for the `ACTIVE -> CANCELLED` transition.
  - `source` — `"PathDispatcher"`
  - `severity` — derived from the `initiator` actually committed by Phase 3: `"info"` for `"operator_cancelled"` / `"power_off"` / `"revoked"`; `"warning"` for `"deviation"`; `"error"` for `"eb"`; `"info"` when `initiator` is `None` or any other string. Because `initiator` is purely diagnostic (most recent same-tick payload wins), the recorded severity may not match the originating writer's intent in race cases.
  - `train_id`, `path_id`
  - `payload`:
    - `initiator: str` — required; free-form label populated by the writer (`"eb"`, `"operator_cancelled"`, `"power_off"`, `"deviation"`, `"revoked"`, or any other writer-supplied string for new origins)
    - `deviation_kind: Optional["out_of_sequence" | "beyond_authorized_target"]` — required when `initiator == "deviation"`; `None` otherwise
    - `reason: Optional[str]` — plain-text diagnostic context
**Path invariants**

- In v1 the dispatcher path lifecycle is exactly `ACTIVE` / `COMPLETED` / `CANCELLED` (see the **Path status state machine**): acquisition is synchronous inside `create_path()`, so a live path has the single state `ACTIVE`. Whether the train has started moving, entered the terminal resource, or is stopped at the stop point is represented by controller state, `current_resource_index`, and explicit completion predicates, not by separate path lifecycle values.
- `resource_sequence` replaces both `block_sequence` and `switch_positions`, so existing callers iterating those fields need updating.
- `current_resource_index` should track `sequence_index`, not the first occurrence of a raw resource ID.
- For v1, repeated resources in a single path are invalid and should be rejected during both `create_path()` and `extend_path()`.
- Switch resources contribute `0 cm` to forward-distance accumulation. Any helper that sums per-resource physical length should therefore add `0` for `resource_type == "switch"` and continue the cumulative axis across the surrounding block geometry.
- `FLANK_PROTECTION_DISTANCE_CM` should be a global config, not a per-path setting.
- In v1, switch locks are a multi-owner compatibility set: multiple paths may hold compatible same-state lock claims on the same switch, but conflicting required states are not allowed and physical occupancy stays single-owner.
- `PathDispatcher` does not run a behind-train release sweep; block release is fully owned by `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` triggered from physics.

## Route And RouteExecutor

**Route data**

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `route_id` | `str` | Unique route identifier |
| `name` | `str` | Display name |
| `tasks` | `List[RouteTask]` | Ordered route definition — see **RouteTask data** |
| `mode` | `RouteMode` | One of `ONE_OFF`, `CIRCULAR`, `ROUND_TRIP` |
| `assigned_train_ids` | `List[str]` | Stored list of train IDs assigned to the route. Canonical mutators are `Route.assign_train(train_id)` (appends if not already present) and `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` (removes if present); both are idempotent. The field is written from these canonical entry points only — see **Route assignment writers** below. |
| `is_active` | `bool` | Route execution enabled |
| `started_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp when activated |
| `created_at` | `float` | System-time Unix timestamp when the Route record was constructed. Sampled by `Route.__post_init__` via `time.time()` at the write site (see **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). Written exactly once at construction; never mutated by edit, assignment, activation, deactivation, or any other handler. |
| `last_edited_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp of the most recent route-definition edit. `None` for a freshly-constructed Route that has never been edited after creation. Written only by the canonical route-definition edit writers — see **Route edit timestamps** below. Not touched by `Route.assign_train` / `Route.unassign_train` (assignment) or `Route.activate` / `Route.deactivate` (lifecycle). |
| `priority` | `int` | Route-executor scheduling priority, `0..9` where `9` is highest and `0` is lowest (default `5`) |
| `acquisition_mode` | `PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode` | Default route path-acquisition policy (default `AcquisitionMode.best_effort`) |
| `manual_override` | `bool` | Operator override for `manual` resources (default `False`). Set `True` only on operator-initiated routes (the Quick Route tool exposes the option when the operator explicitly chooses to use manual resources). `RouteExecutor` passes the assigned route's `manual_override` into every `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` call for that run, which forwards it through the acquisition chain and pathfinder per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` applies the same flag to its `manual` target filtering. Edits take effect on the next create/extend attempt with no run teardown. |
| `start_mode` | `RouteStartMode` | Default for which stop a fresh run begins at: `FIRST` (default), `NEAREST`, or `SPECIFIC`. Consulted once when Phase 1 allocates a fresh run; not a live override. See **Run start-mode seeding**. |
| `start_task_index` | `Optional[int]` | Stop index used only when `start_mode == SPECIFIC` (the index into `Route.tasks` of the stop to begin at). `None` otherwise. See **Run start-mode seeding**. |

**Route assignment writers**

`Route.assigned_train_ids` is mutated only through these canonical writers:

- `Route.assign_train(train_id)` and `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` are the two helper methods; both are idempotent.
- HTTP/UI/admin entry points that ultimately call those helpers (existing v1 routes; their exact paths and payload shapes are unchanged from current code unless this document overrides them):
  - `POST /api/train-routes` accepts an `assigned_train_ids` list in its body and calls `Route.assign_train(train_id)` for each.
  - `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>` diffs the existing list against the requested list and calls `Route.assign_train(train_id)` / `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` per change.
  - `PUT /api/trains/<train_id>/assign-route` calls `Route.assign_train(train_id)` (with optional unassign of any previous route).
- Internal teardown writers that call `Route.unassign_train(train_id)`:
  - `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` (called by Phase 3 abort, power-off, and deactivate consumers).
  - `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` (called by Phase 1 terminal-walk completion).
- Operator retargeting / route swap handlers also call `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on the old route and `Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the new route as part of the atomic two-step retarget flow defined in **Route Edit And Reconciliation**.

`RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` (derived read) is kept in sync with `Route.assigned_train_ids` (stored) by construction: the only sites that flip a `RouteRun.state` out of `RUNNING` are also the sites that call `unassign_train(...)` on the matching `Route`. New assignments writing `Route.assign_train(train_id)` create a fresh `RUNNING` `RouteRun` only at the next eligible Phase 1 RouteRun (re)allocation step, so a brief window between the assign call and Phase 1 may show the train in `Route.assigned_train_ids` with no `RUNNING` `RouteRun` — that is the canonical signal Phase 1 step `9c4f` keys on to allocate a fresh run. A per-assignment start-mode input supplied with the assign call rides this window: it is stashed on `RouteExecutor._pending_start_overrides[train_id]` and consumed by that step `9c4f` allocation (see **Run start-mode seeding**).

**Route edit timestamps**

`Route.created_at` is sampled exactly once via `time.time()` inside `Route.__post_init__`. No other writer touches it.

`Route.last_edited_at` is sampled via `time.time()` only by edit handlers that mutate the route definition. The set of fields whose mutation bumps `last_edited_at` is exactly: `name`, `tasks`, `mode`, `priority`, `acquisition_mode`, `manual_override`. Mutations to `is_active`, `started_at`, and `assigned_train_ids` do NOT bump `last_edited_at` — those are lifecycle / assignment events and are timestamped through `Route.started_at` (activation) and the per-train `RouteRun` registry respectively.

The canonical route-definition edit entry points are:

- `POST /api/train-routes` — when the new Route is created with at least one of the five edit fields supplied in the request body, the handler writes `last_edited_at = time.time()` once at the end of the handler before persisting. A bare create with no edit fields leaves `last_edited_at = None`.
- `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>` — the handler writes `last_edited_at = time.time()` once at the end of the handler if and only if at least one of the five edit fields was actually written on this request. A request that touches only `assigned_train_ids` does not bump `last_edited_at`.

`Route.priority`, `Route.acquisition_mode`, and `Route.manual_override` edits bump `last_edited_at` even though they do not trigger the structural-edit teardown described in **Route Edit And Reconciliation**: `last_edited_at` records the time of the last definition write, not the time of the last structural change. These two fields are also the v1 RouteRun override allowlist — see **RouteRun overrides** for the per-field push semantics that distribute later edits to existing runs. UI/log consumers that need to distinguish structural from non-structural edits cannot do so from `last_edited_at` alone in v1; they observe the teardown side effects instead.

**Canonical ownership split**

- `Route` is the shared plan definition only: ordered tasks plus route-level policy such as mode and acquisition mode. It does not own per-train mutable execution cursors.
- A route may be assigned to multiple trains in v1, but each train may execute at most one route task at a time. Each assigned train has at most one `RUNNING` `RouteRun` at any moment; the live read is `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` (computed by searching the registry for the `RUNNING` `RouteRun`). Historical `RouteRun` rows for the same train (state `FINISHED` or `DEACTIVATED`) are preserved indefinitely in the route registry as a parallel history; only the active-state predicate is single-instance. The run's `route_id` identifies which shared `Route` definition that train is currently following. That `RouteRun` is the per-train runtime instance of the shared plan and owns only per-train mutable execution state: task cursor, current traversal direction, per-task timing and resolved-target state (`task_runs`), route-version reconciliation state, and current route status. Shared route execution state across trains is not allowed.
- `Route` is live shared execution input in v1, not a snapshot source that is copied into each run. Active runs read the current shared `Route` through `RouteRun.route_id`, and later structural route edits trigger immediate teardown of every assigned train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` followed by fresh `RouteRun` allocation; see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**.
- In helper prose below, when an active-run helper still shows a `route` parameter, read that as the current shared `Route` resolved from `RouteRun.route_id` unless the paragraph explicitly says a new dedicated route assignment is being created.
- Because multi-train assignment is supported, `RouteRun` is the only canonical runtime progress store. `Route` carries no runtime progress fields; consumers read per-train progress from each `RouteRun` (a route-level progress summary is out of scope — see the **Out of Scope** table).
- Per-train route/path errors belong to `RouteRun.last_error` (an `Optional[ErrorRecord]` carrying both `text` and `time`); `Route` does not keep a shared last-writer-wins error summary in v1.
- Departure is not a route-level policy in v1. Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly for every successful create/extend on the first eligible Phase 1 pass; the ATO-vs-manual behavioral difference is owned inside that call per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**. The driver in manual mode sees that depart has been accepted (state == `RUNNING_MOVING`) and starts moving the train; there is no separate operator-confirmation button.
- `acquisition_mode` at route level is the policy for how this route wants dispatcher authority to be acquired (`all_or_nothing` or `best_effort`), with default `AcquisitionMode.best_effort` when the route definition omits it. `RouteExecutor` reads the current shared `Route.acquisition_mode` for both `create_path(...)` and later `extend_path(...)` retries on the same planning chain.
- `priority` at route level is the canonical route-executor scheduling priority, with default `5`. Priority values are integers in `0..9`, where `9` is highest and `0` is lowest. `RouteExecutor` sorts runnable route runs by the current shared `Route.priority`; later shared-route edits may therefore legitimately reshuffle later tick order for active runs. Non-route and one-off/manual train work uses effective priority `5` when `SimulationController` merges it into the per-tick runnable-train order.
- `ResolvedRouteTarget` carries exactly `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`. `requested_destination_direction` in this structure is always the block-relative direction for `requested_destination_block_id`. `Platform` has no direction field, no platform-frame direction, and no direction-conversion helper in v1; `PlatformTarget` and `StationTarget` resolution chooses a concrete platform-end block, then derives the resolved direction from `Block.ordered_tiles`, validates it against `Block.allowed_travel_directions`, and stores that block-relative result. There is no persistent per-task resolved-target cache outside `task_runs`: every new route-side path attempt resolves a transient candidate live from the current shared `Route`, current platform availability, train span, and task policy via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`. That helper is read-only and returns one candidate per call. Phase 1's authority-acquisition step is the only canonical writer of `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`, after a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that candidate. For a `StationTarget` task, `_find_next_task_target` returns one candidate per call; Phase 1 retries with successive candidates by re-invoking the helper after a failed `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` attempt within the same tick. A `PlatformTarget` or `BlockTarget` task has only one candidate per tick. If a path request succeeds, the path record's `requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, and `authority_end_block_id` become the dispatcher-owned stored target contract for that successful live segment, while `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target` records the candidate actually accepted for that segment. Operator/admin edits to platform/block target-resolution inputs (for example platform block membership/order, `Block.disabled` / `Block.manual` toggles, `Platform.disabled` / `Platform.manual` / `Station.disabled` / `Station.manual` toggles, or `Block.allowed_travel_directions` changes) do not rewrite an already-successful live segment; they take effect the next time Phase 1 commits a new successful create/extend attempt.

**Canonical Next-Target Selection**

- **`ResolvedNextTaskProjection` schema.** The return type of `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=None)` is `Optional[ResolvedNextTaskProjection]`. The optional `exclude` argument is a caller-owned set of already-attempted `ResolvedRouteTarget` values for the current planning problem; the helper itself stores no per-tick state. `None` means no next task on the current directed walk (terminal). Otherwise the projection carries:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `walk_position` | `int` | Walk-position index in `RouteRun.task_runs` of the next non-finished walk-position on the directed walk. The underlying `RouteTask` is `Route.tasks[task_runs[walk_position].task_index]`. |
  | `task_kind` | `"waypoint" \| "stop" \| "dwell"` | The task's kind, copied from the underlying `RouteTask.task_kind` for caller convenience. |
  | `direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | The route walk direction at this task (may have flipped at a `ROUND_TRIP` reflection). |
  | `resolved_target` | `Optional[ResolvedRouteTarget]` | The resolved block + direction for `waypoint` and `stop` tasks. `None` for `dwell` tasks (which carry no dispatcher target) and for tasks whose target cannot be resolved in the current attempt (e.g. all station candidates have been tried, or the target is missing / unavailable). |
  | `failure_kind` | `Optional[TargetResolutionFailureKind]` | Why resolution produced no target; set exactly when `resolved_target is None` for a non-`dwell` task. `missing_target` when the `task_location` ID (block, platform, or station) is not present in the network — a **broken** task. `target_unavailable` when the target is present but cannot resolve to a usable block this tick (`disabled` / `manual`, or a `StationTarget`'s platforms are all filtered or held). `None` for `dwell` tasks and whenever `resolved_target` is present. Drives the broken-vs-unreachable split in **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |

  The helper is **read-only**: it does not write `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` and has no other side effect on `RouteRun` state. The canonical writer of `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` is Phase 1's authority-acquisition step, after a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task. All other callers (head-advance callback, dwell-complete step, UI projection) consume the helper's return value only.

  `TargetResolutionFailureKind` has v1 values `missing_target` (the `task_location` ID — block, platform, or station — is not present in the network) and `target_unavailable` (the target is present but cannot resolve to a usable block this tick: it is `disabled` / `manual`, or a `StationTarget`'s platforms are all filtered or held by other trains). It classifies target *resolution* failures only; a target that resolves but whose authority cannot be acquired is classified by `PathAcquisitionFailureKind` instead and is always treated as unreachable, never broken. See **Unreachable and broken task handling**.

- **Index-driven, live-recompute model.** The "armed next task" is reflected by `current_waypoint_index` and `direction`, not by a separate flag. The "armed target" is whatever `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns when called. Whenever any phase needs the current next-task projection — Phase 1's planning step, Phase 1's dwell-complete check, the head-advance callback's next-task pointer, or a UI projection — call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`. The helper computes fresh from current shared `Route` + current physical progress + current span coverage. It does not arm authority, does not write `current_route_status.state`, does not advance `current_waypoint_index`, does not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target`, and does not call `PathDispatcher`.
- `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` is read-only with respect to `RouteRun` storage. For `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, the helper resolves the direction flip at route ends and returns the flipped `direction` in the projection; the caller commits that direction to `RouteRun.direction`.
- For a station task with multiple compatible platform-end candidates on the current tick, `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=...)` picks one candidate per call. The helper itself does not maintain any per-tick state: the caller (Phase 1's per-tick retry loop) owns the `exclude` set of already-attempted resolved targets for this planning problem and passes it on each call. The helper enumerates the current candidate ordering, skips any candidate present in `exclude`, and returns the next untried one (or `None` when exhausted). For a `PlatformTarget` or `BlockTarget` task, only one candidate exists per tick. Non-Phase-1 callers (Phase 1 dwell-complete step, head-advance callback) pass `exclude=None` and receive the first candidate under the canonical ordering; that candidate is deterministic for the same shared `Route` + physical-progress + platform-availability inputs.
- If `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns no next task on the current directed walk, the current walk is terminal: Phase 1 calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside `tick_train_phase1(...)`, which sets `current_route_status.state = FINISHED` (`RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` automatically returns `None`) and (for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` routes with no remaining assigned trains) calls `route.deactivate()` for any other still-assigned trains. The `RouteRun` is preserved as a historical `FINISHED` row in the route registry. Phase 1 then returns for that tick. By the time Phase 1 reaches this step, the matching live path has already been cleared by stop-completion in the prior Phase 3 pass, so no dispatcher cancel is needed. For `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, `_find_next_task_target` never returns `None` (it wraps or reflects), so this terminal-walk branch never fires for those modes. When every task on a `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` walk is broken, the run does not finish — it holds in the no-reachable-task stall instead (see **Unreachable and broken task handling**).
- If a next task exists but no legal concrete platform/block target can be resolved under the task's targeting policy on this attempt, the helper returns a `ResolvedNextTaskProjection` whose `walk_position`, `task_kind`, and `direction` describe that walk-position and whose `resolved_target` is `None`. The helper returns overall `None` only for the terminal-walk condition (the `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` terminal case defined in the terminal-walk bullet above). If no live authority exists and Phase 1 has exhausted all same-tick candidates, Phase 1 stops for that train for that tick. If live authority exists, leave the live path and route status unchanged unless a later `extend_path(...)` result proves structural incompatibility.
- Callers must not rewrite `direction` or `task_runs[i].resolved_target` through raw increment/decrement index arithmetic or by indexing `Route.tasks` outside the helper. Next-task selection comes from `_find_next_task_target(...)`; committed progress advances through the head-advance callback recording `finished_at`, through Phase 1 dwell-complete step recording `finished_at`, and through Phase 1's covered-task index advance for already-passed `waypoint` tasks and same-block stopped-at-destination `stop` tasks.
- `Route.mode` is consumed inside `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`:
  - `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`: if no next task exists, return `None` (terminal).
  - `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`: wrap from the last task to the first task.
  - `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`: reflect at the route ends and flip `direction` together with the next task.
- The canonical `ROUND_TRIP` truth table for a three-waypoint route `[A, B, C]` is:
  - `714a`: `(-1, forward) -> (0, forward)`
  - `a91b`: `(0, forward) -> (1, forward)`
  - `1f41`: `(1, forward) -> (2, forward)`
  - `c733`: `(2, forward) -> (1, reverse)`
  - `79a9`: `(1, reverse) -> (0, reverse)`
  - `7db6`: `(0, reverse) -> (1, forward)`

**RouteTask data**

Retained current-code enums used by route data:

- `RouteMode`: `ONE_OFF`, `CIRCULAR`, `ROUND_TRIP`

`RouteTask` is the unified route-task schema in v1. Every entry in `Route.tasks` is a `RouteTask`.

| Field | Type | Applies | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `task_kind` | `"waypoint" \| "stop" \| "dwell"` | all | Required task behavior discriminator. `waypoint` is a pass-through traversal (no stop, no dwell). `stop` is a platform stop that enters dispatcher-committed completion. `dwell` is a hold at a location after a stop. |
| `task_location` | `BlockTarget \| PlatformTarget \| StationTarget` (tagged union) | all | The concrete target for this task. `BlockTarget(block_id: str)` for direct block targets, `PlatformTarget(platform_id: str)` for fixed concrete platforms, `StationTarget(station_id: str)` for logical stations resolved at runtime. The tagged union enforces that exactly one targeting mode is present. |
| `task_time` | `Optional[float]` | all | Semantics depend on `task_kind`. For `waypoint`/`stop`: expected travel time in seconds from the previous task's location to this task's location; used to classify arrival as early/on-time/late when the `RouteTaskRun` timing data is available; omit when no timing classification is needed. For `dwell`: how long in seconds to remain at the location; omit to allow departure immediately (equivalent to `dwell_time_sec = 0` in the old model). |
| `task_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse" \| "either"]` | all | Route-level block-relative traversal preference. `RouteExecutor` resolves `"either"` to a concrete direction before any dispatcher call; the dispatcher never receives `"either"`. Defaults to `"either"` when omitted. |
| `target_time` | `Optional[float]` | all | Optional absolute system-time Unix timestamp giving the *expected* time for this task: expected arrival time for `waypoint` / `stop`, and expected dwell **start** time for `dwell`. For `waypoint` / `stop` it is informational (schedule-adherence / ETA classification) and gates nothing. For `dwell` it feeds the scheduled-departure hold in `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` (see **Task time semantics**): the dwell holds until `task_time` after the later of actual start and `target_time`, so a train never departs before its scheduled departure. |

Route-task authoring rules:

- A typical platform stop with a hold is authored as two consecutive tasks: `stop(task_location=PlatformTarget(platform_X), task_time=expected_travel_seconds)` followed by `dwell(task_location=PlatformTarget(platform_X), task_time=hold_seconds)`. The `stop` enters dispatcher-committed completion; the `dwell` holds the train in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` until the dwell timer elapses.
- A terminal stop with no hold is authored as a single `stop` task with no following `dwell` task (or with `task_time` omitted on the implied dwell). There is no `dwell_time_sec = 0` field equivalent; a missing `dwell` task is the correct encoding.
- A pass-through is a single `waypoint` task. It resolves to a concrete `ResolvedRouteTarget` for authority planning, is completed by head entry into the resolved block, and does not invoke dwell logic or `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
- `task_kind` is required. Missing, null, or unrecognized `task_kind` is a validation error at task creation time; `RouteExecutor` must never infer a default kind.
- A terminal task must not be `waypoint`. If the train must stop and reverse immediately with no dwell, the terminal task is `stop`.
- For target resolution and path acquisition, `waypoint` and `stop` tasks use the same resolution chain and the same dispatcher target contract. Both resolve to a `ResolvedRouteTarget` for authority planning. The behavioral difference: a `stop` arrival is dispatcher-committed completion from the post-physics controller stop check, and the route's `RouteTaskRun` records `finished_at` at stop completion; a `waypoint` is completed by head entry with no stop, no dwell, and no `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
- A `dwell` task does not create or require new dispatcher authority. While a `dwell` task is the current active task, Phase 1 does not create or extend authority. The `dwell` task is complete when its `task_time` has elapsed since `task_runs[walk_position].started_at` (the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at`, i.e. the moment the stop completed), at which point Phase 1 dwell-complete step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` sets `finished_at`, calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()`, and leaves the next task to a later Phase 1.
- Station/platform distinction is explicit:
  - `Station` is a logical grouping of platforms used by route planning and platform selection.
  - `Platform` is the concrete physical stop. When a waypoint's task_location is a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget`, dispatcher path requests, block IDs, and directions are always derived from one resolved concrete platform, never from a station group directly.
  - The current attempt may resolve one concrete `platform_id` when resolution goes through a platform. For a `BlockTarget` waypoint, no platform resolution is needed.
- `Platform.ordered_blocks` is the canonical ordered list of block IDs in the platform, used to identify the two platform-end blocks and preserve edit stability. `Platform` carries no direction, no platform-local forward/reverse semantics, no per-block alignment mapping, and no user-facing directional option. It is initialized from the block order supplied to backend platform-creation/edit logic; backend validation must verify the supplied list is one connected switch-free walk in that exact order. Platform edits may only insert or remove linked blocks at the ends while preserving relative order; middle insertions, reorders, or reversals are rejected. Removing a platform's last block deletes the platform rather than leaving it empty, per the **Non-empty parent rule** under **TrackSegment**.
- `Platform` carries no direction or platform-side helper. If a platform should serve only one direction, the user must configure the underlying end block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions` so only that traversal is allowed.
- `requested_destination_direction` is the resolved traversal constraint at the requested target. Task authors express the route-level preference as `forward`, `reverse`, or `either` on `RouteTask.task_direction`, but those values are block-relative preferences, not platform-relative preferences. By the time `RouteExecutor` builds `ResolvedRouteTarget`, it must have resolved that preference into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for the chosen `requested_destination_block_id`. For a task that resolves through a concrete platform, the candidate comparison defined in the platform resolution subsection below evaluates terminal traversals through the platform end block(s); `task_direction == "forward"` or `"reverse"` filters out candidates whose terminal traversal does not match that authored block-relative direction or is not allowed by `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. `task_direction == "either"` applies no route-authored direction filter, but every candidate must still satisfy `Block.allowed_travel_directions`, and `RouteExecutor` still stores the chosen block-relative concrete direction. When the task_location is a `BlockTarget`, `"forward"` / `"reverse"` are interpreted directly in that block's immutable internal traversal order, and `"either"` means `RouteExecutor` picks whichever block-relative direction is reachable under the same current-span comparison and allowed by `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. Dispatcher never receives `"either"` in the v1 path contract; route resolution must always eliminate it before calling `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`. These are deliberately two distinct types: `RouteTask.task_direction` is the route-author input (may be `"either"`); `ResolvedRouteTarget.requested_destination_direction` is the strict subtype `"forward" | "reverse"` passed to dispatcher. Implementations must reflect this difference in their static types so that handing an unresolved `RouteTask.task_direction` to `PathDispatcher.create_path()` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path()` is a type error rather than a runtime branch.
- Define the exact dispatcher terminal frame separately. The terminal frame is the explicit directed geometry used for controller braking and diagnostics: `path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` for the relevant terminal block. When a live path exists, dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal `resource_sequence` block entry. Boundary/safety-margin projection must never infer that frame from `requested_destination_direction` alone.
- Resolve waypoint targets through one canonical chain: `station -> platform -> block`. If the task_location is a `PlatformTarget`, resolution starts at `platform`. If it is a `BlockTarget`, resolution skips station/platform lookup and targets that block directly.
- When a task_location is a `StationTarget` rather than a `PlatformTarget`, `RouteExecutor` must resolve one concrete platform before path creation. In v1, platform availability is evaluated relative to the train for this route attempt. A platform is unavailable when any linked block is held by another train's physical occupancy or another train/path's live reservation. `CAUTION` by itself does not make a platform unavailable: a caution block may still be reserved/acquired when ordinary reservation rules allow it, and the caution state is consumed by `TrainController` lookahead as a lower ATC speed cap. The current train's own physical occupancy or current live authority must not make that platform unavailable to itself; same-train coverage is handled by Phase 1's covered-task index advance and same-train path conflicts are handled by the shared pathfinder/acquisition rules. Deterministic tie-breaking is required and follows the canonical candidate-order rule below.
- Platform/station target selection in v1 is:
  - if the task_location is a `PlatformTarget`, resolve exactly that platform for this tick. Pick its single best compatible platform-end candidate by the canonical graph comparison. If that create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, wait until the next eligible tick rather than trying another platform or another platform end in the same Phase 1 pass.
  - if the task_location is a `StationTarget`, build the complete ordered transient candidate list of all compatible available platform-end candidates across every platform in `Station.platform_ids`, sorted by the canonical graph comparison (`tile_count`, then `walk_length_cm`, then `candidate_rank`, then final `end_a` dead-heat only across current-span end choices). In that same Phase 1 pass, `RouteExecutor` may try each distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair from that station candidate list at most once, stopping as soon as one create/extend attempt creates or grows authority.
  - if the task_location is a `BlockTarget`, resolve exactly that block/direction for this tick. If the create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, wait until the next eligible tick rather than inventing alternatives.
- A waypoint that wants "try any platform in this station" should use a `StationTarget`; a waypoint that uses a `PlatformTarget` targets that one concrete platform.
- Station candidate enumeration preserves the route-specified `requested_direction` literally as a block-relative traversal preference. If the waypoint specified `forward` or `reverse`, candidate platform ends are compatible only when their terminal traversal satisfies that direction under the candidate block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. If the waypoint specified `either` (or omitted the field, which is treated as `either`), any `Block.allowed_travel_directions`-compatible terminal traversal may be used.
- Later retries follow the same route-side selection policy. A concrete platform retry keeps the same platform and recomputes its current best compatible end on the later tick. A station retry may re-enumerate all compatible available platform-end candidates for that station on the later tick. If no concrete platform/block candidate can be resolved for the retry under that policy, `RouteExecutor` fails target resolution for that retry and must not treat it as an `extend_path(...)`-side retarget problem.
- For covered normalization and subsequent task service on the current directed route walk, a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task with `task_kind == "waypoint"` counts as satisfied when the train covers any compatible concrete platform in that task's allowed platform set; a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task with `task_kind == "stop"` counts as satisfied only after the stop has been served by dispatcher-committed stop completion and dwell exit. When either satisfaction rule applies, `RouteExecutor` uses the actually covered or served compatible block/platform only to decide satisfaction and to report diagnostics; it must not force resolution to some other platform, must not write a persistent served-platform choice for later route-control decisions, and must not depend on a persistent per-task cache to remember that choice later.
- For each concrete platform being considered, `RouteExecutor` must gather the terminal candidate block at each end of that platform by calling the same shared network-graph walk query that `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` uses. For a multi-block platform, the candidate end blocks are the first and last entries of `Platform.ordered_blocks`; for a single-block platform, both platform ends collapse to the same block, so only that one block participates. If a cab is selected, only the current head-side start locator participates in this stop-planning comparison. If no cab is selected, both physical train ends may participate, and a switch-side end is first mapped inward to the occupied block at that end of the current span; if no such inward occupied block exists, that end is dropped from the comparison set. Each candidate graph-query result carries the terminal block-relative direction derived from the canonical block-direction helper through `Block.ordered_tiles`; that direction must pass the task direction filter and `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. For a `PlatformTarget` task, pick the shortest successful candidate using the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**, applied across the remaining current-span end choices. For a `StationTarget` task, keep the full sorted list of compatible successful platform-end candidates as transient same-Phase-1 retry state, and try each distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair at most once in that pass. The chosen or currently attempted block becomes `requested_destination_block_id`, and the chosen block-relative direction becomes `requested_destination_direction`. This is how task-authored `"either"` is converted into one concrete `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for `PlatformTarget` and `StationTarget` tasks. If no candidate succeeds, fail the task attempt. The chosen/current candidate block is the dispatcher-facing target block for both `stop` and `waypoint` tasks that resolve through a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task_location.
- For a task-authored `task_direction == "forward"` or `"reverse"`, the same candidate comparison is used, but pairs whose resulting block-relative direction does not match the authored direction are dropped from the comparison before selection. For `task_direction == "either"`, no route-authored direction filter is applied and the shortest allowed candidate wins.
- After resolution, `RouteExecutor` derives the per-attempt `ResolvedRouteTarget` (`requested_destination_block_id`, block-relative `requested_destination_direction`) for the current task and passes only that transient route-owned request contract into `PathDispatcher`. There is no platform-frame direction to convert. Phase 1's authority-acquisition step, after a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task, writes the actually-attempted candidate into `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`, where `walk_position` is the index returned by the same Phase 1 pass's `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` projection. `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` itself never writes that field. Callback-time code may call the helper to recompute live without disturbing `task_runs[i].resolved_target`; the stored value remains whatever Phase 1's last successful create/extend wrote and is the authoritative match key for later stop-completion lookup. Later planning retries call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` again whenever Phase 1 needs a fresh resolve, and Phase 1's next successful create/extend overwrites the bookkeeping value.
- A task with `task_kind == "stop"` may therefore use a `task_location` that is:
  - a `PlatformTarget`, or
  - a `StationTarget`, in which case `RouteExecutor` resolves a concrete `platform_id` for the current attempt before path creation, or
  - a `BlockTarget`, in which case no platform resolution is required.
- Direction compatibility depends only on block-level `Block.allowed_travel_directions` plus current availability — there is no `required_platform_side` or platform-side direction helper.
- Route arrival for tasks with `task_kind == "stop"` uses explicit stop-policy data:
  - the resolved destination block for the task attempt
  - the block-relative traversal direction for that attempt
- Every per-attempt `ResolvedRouteTarget` carries an explicit route-owned traversal-constraint field, `requested_destination_direction`. Before path creation, `RouteExecutor` must resolve the task-authored `task_direction` into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for `requested_destination_block_id` and store that value on `ResolvedRouteTarget`. Dispatcher still derives the exact terminal frame from explicit terminal geometry, but it does so after route resolution has already eliminated `"either"` from the request contract.
- If the task_location is a `StationTarget` or `PlatformTarget`, `requested_destination_block_id` is resolved only after `RouteExecutor` has picked a concrete platform for that train's current attempt and then applied the canonical shorter-path platform-end selection rule above. If the task_location is a `BlockTarget`, that block becomes `requested_destination_block_id` directly, but a disabled block is never a valid direct target, and a manual block is a valid direct target only when the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True` (the forwarded override lets the dispatcher call accept it; with `manual_override = False` the resolution attempt fails). The same availability rule applies to platform/station resolution: `RouteExecutor` excludes any platform whose own `Platform.disabled` is `True` and every platform belonging to a station whose `Station.disabled` is `True`, and applies the same filter to `Platform.manual` / `Station.manual` unless the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True`. See the **Platform And Station** availability flags and the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home. In all cases, the stored `requested_destination_direction` is the block-relative direction for that resolved block; there is no raw platform-level direction token in the route definition or resolved runtime state.
- Do not define a separate stop-window tolerance or route-authored stop-position field in v1. The controller safety margin is used by controller lookahead and ATO braking to shape the train's stop, but route completion does not manually verify a centimeter tolerance around any route value.
- For tasks with `task_kind == "stop"`, task arrival is not simply "the train is in the task block", but it is also not gated by an exact-stop tolerance check in v1. A stop task enters dwell only after dispatcher completion caused by the post-physics controller stop check for the active authority that reaches the resolved target, and that stop task is marked finished only when the following dwell task exits (or immediately if no dwell task follows). `task_kind == "waypoint"` is reached by train-head entry / physical pass of its resolved block. Controller deviation cases produce `path_cancellation` with `initiator = "deviation"`; the structural distinction between out-of-sequence and beyond-authorized-target flows on the event payload's `deviation_kind` field.
- One shared helper, such as `RailwayNetwork.project_authority_boundary_delta(head_locator, authority_end, safety_margin_cm) -> delta_cm`, is the single home for boundary projection. `head_locator` is a `GraphLocator` carrying `tile`, `edge`, and `distance_cm` for the live train head. `authority_end` carries `block_id` and `terminal_frame: DirectedBlockFrame` (whose `path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` describe the directed terminal frame for the authority-end block). The helper uses tile lengths and traversal geometry to project the live train head and the safety-margin-adjusted authority boundary onto the same forward axis, then returns their signed separation in centimeters on that shared axis: positive means the train head is past the safety-margin boundary in the forward axis, negative means it is still short of it, and zero is on-target. In v1 this helper is for braking/diagnostic projection only; it is not the authoritative path-completion gate, and `RouteExecutor` must not compare live train position against it itself.

**Task time semantics**

- All stored route/task timestamps use system-time Unix seconds.
- `RouteTask.target_time` is the *expected* time for a task (expected arrival for `waypoint` / `stop`, expected dwell start for `dwell`). On `waypoint` / `stop` it is informational only and gates nothing. On a `dwell` task it sets the scheduled departure together with `task_time`: the scheduled departure is `target_time + task_time`, and the dwell never departs before it.
- `RouteTask.should_depart(now, dwell_started_at)` is evaluated only on a `dwell` row and uses only system-time Unix seconds in v1. It departs when `now >= max(dwell_started_at, target_time) + task_time`, treating `target_time` as `-∞` when absent and `task_time` as `0` when absent (`dwell_started_at` is the dwell row's derived `started_at`):
  - with both set: hold for `task_time` after the later of actual start and the scheduled start `target_time`, so a late arrival still serves the full hold and an early arrival waits until the scheduled departure
  - with only `task_time` set: depart when `now - dwell_started_at >= task_time`
  - with only `target_time` set: depart when `now >= target_time`
  - with neither set: depart immediately (`now >= dwell_started_at` always holds)
- A `stop` task is independent of its neighbors. It is completed at dispatcher-committed stop completion through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` (which writes the stop's `finished_at` and advances the derived route-progress cursor). A `stop`'s own `target_time` is its expected arrival time and is informational only — a bare `stop` with no following `dwell` never holds for a time; a time-gated platform hold must be authored as `stop` followed by a `dwell` carrying `task_time` and/or `target_time`. A following `dwell` task (when present) is a separate row with its own `started_at` / `finished_at` lifecycle. `should_depart(...)` is only evaluated against `dwell` rows.

**Route-definition validation**

- A valid route definition must contain at least one `task_kind == "stop"` task in total. Single-stop routes are valid (e.g. one-off reposition or single-destination delivery); the single stop is both the first and final stop.
- Consecutive stop tasks that resolve to the same concrete target are valid. Route validation must not reject or collapse them merely because their resolved `requested_destination_block_id` / `requested_destination_direction` pair is identical. At runtime the second stop is finished by Phase 1's covered-task index advance — the train is already stopped at that destination with no active path, so the covered-stop rule applies — and its following `dwell` row (when present) then runs the ordinary dwell-entry/dwell-exit steps.
- Every task must provide `task_kind` explicitly as `"waypoint"`, `"stop"`, or `"dwell"`. Missing `task_kind`, null `task_kind`, or any other value is a validation error at task creation/update time.
- A route made only of `waypoint` tasks is invalid.
- The stored `Route.tasks` list must end in `task_kind == "stop"` (or a `dwell` task immediately following a `stop`) before a `RouteRun` may be created. The route editor/API must validate that terminal stop requirement before sending the route to route-run activation. `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` changes how the stored task list is interpreted during execution, but it does not remove the requirement that the authored list itself ends at a stop or dwell. The terminal task sequence is part of the route: the route is not complete until any terminal `dwell` task's timer has elapsed, or the terminal `stop` task has completed when no `dwell` follows it. A terminal `stop` with no following `dwell` is the canonical "stop and end immediately" shape; a terminal `stop` followed by a `dwell` with `task_time > 0` or `target_time` is the canonical "end after holding at the platform" shape. Both are valid.
- A bulb loop or other turnaround that returns to the same stop must be modeled explicitly in the task list (for example `A(stop) -> bulb(waypoint) -> dwell(A) -> A(stop)`). `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` does not infer that geometry from one terminal stop.
- Every `task_kind == "stop"` task must identify a valid stop target through `task_location`: a `BlockTarget`, a `PlatformTarget`, or a `StationTarget` that can be resolved to a platform at runtime.
- `Route.priority` must be an integer in `0..9` (`9` highest, `0` lowest); out-of-range values are a route-definition validation error rejected at activation time.
- `RouteExecutor` should reject or refuse to activate any route definition that violates these rules rather than trying to infer missing stop semantics at runtime.

**Unreachable and broken task handling**

This subsection is the canonical home for what Phase 1 does when its authority-acquisition step (Phase 1 step `66aa`) cannot create or grow live authority for the current armed task on a tick. Every Phase 1 step that keys on this behavior refers back here by name. The handling depends on *why* the task could not be served, which splits into two causes:

- **Broken task (missing target).** `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns a sentinel projection (`resolved_target is None`) with `failure_kind == missing_target`: the task's `task_location` names a `BlockTarget` / `PlatformTarget` / `StationTarget` whose ID is no longer present in the network — for example a `StationTarget` whose station was deleted when its last `Platform` was reassigned to another station (see the **Non-empty parent rule** under **TrackSegment**). Waiting can never resolve a missing target, so a broken task is never waited on.
- **Unreachable task (target present, not usable now).** Either `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns a sentinel projection with `failure_kind == target_unavailable` (the target exists but is `disabled` / `manual`, or — for a `StationTarget` — every platform in `Station.platform_ids` is currently filtered or held by another train), or it returns a concrete `resolved_target` but `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` could not acquire authority for it on this tick (a live `PathAcquisitionFailureKind` such as `blocked_before_target` or `no_viable_path`). In both cases the target still exists, so the condition may clear on a later tick.
- The split is consulted only after the canonical same-tick retry loop (the `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=...)` retry defined in **`create_path()` flow** step 2) has tried every distinct candidate for the current planning problem without creating or growing live authority, and no live authority exists for the task. It is **not** consulted while live authority for the task still exists, while `extend_path(...)` returned `blocked_no_growth` (the path remains a valid prefix), or while planning has not yet run.
- **Unreachable task → wait.** Phase 1 leaves the task armed, records the diagnostic on `RouteTaskRun.last_error` and `RouteRun.last_error` (per the **`RouteTaskRun.last_error` canonical writer**), writes no `finished_at`, and does not move the route-progress cursor. The next eligible Phase 1 tick reruns `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` against current conditions. This is the only behavior for an unreachable task; v1 has no per-route "skip instead of wait" option, and a `disabled` / `manual` target is held rather than skipped because it can be re-enabled.
- **Broken task → skip toward the next valid task.** Phase 1 does not wait on a broken task. A task is **valid** when its `task_location` ID is present in the network (whether or not that target is currently available); a broken task is the only non-valid kind. Phase 1 first checks whether the directed walk still contains any valid task: for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` it scans the remaining tasks from the current `task_index` onward; for `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` it scans all of `Route.tasks`, because the walk cycles through them.
  - **If a valid task exists**, Phase 1 skips the broken task: it records the diagnostic on `task_runs[walk_position].last_error`, sets `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = current_time` (advancing the derived `current_waypoint_index` and triggering `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` per the canonical append rule), and emits a `NotificationCenter` diagnostic such as `task <walk_position> skipped: target <id> missing`. Consecutive broken tasks are skipped this way — at most one full `len(Route.tasks)` scan within a single Phase 1 pass — until a valid task is reached; the next eligible Phase 1 plans that valid task (or waits on it if it is unreachable). A skip writes `finished_at`, so a skipped task is never retried.
  - **If no valid task exists** (every task on the directed walk is broken):
    - For `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`, skipping the remaining tasks leaves the directed walk terminal, so the next eligible Phase 1 sees `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` return `None` and calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` per the terminal-walk rule; the run ends `FINISHED` having served nothing. Each broken-task skip already emits its own `NotificationCenter` diagnostic, so `finish_route_run` needs no special-casing for this case.
    - For `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, the walk never goes terminal, so Phase 1 must not keep skipping and appending. It instead sets `RouteRun.no_reachable_task = True` (a direct write — not a latch), records the diagnostic on `RouteRun.last_error`, emits a `NotificationCenter` diagnostic on the `False -> True` transition, and returns for that tick without arming authority. The train holds with no authority and sits idle, and the derived `display_state` projects `"STALLED"` (see **Route-run state**). The flag is cleared by the next Phase 1 pass that finds any valid task on the walk — for example after the missing station/platform is recreated — at which point ordinary planning resumes. `no_reachable_task` is never set for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`.
- **`dwell` walk-positions.** A `dwell` walk-position is broken-skipped only when its predecessor stop had no live authority and therefore no dwell entry; once dwell entry happened, the dwell-timer rules apply and the handling never interrupts an already-entered dwell.
- Terminal-walk handling is otherwise unchanged: `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returning `None` is the only thing that drives `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)`, and for the reasons above that happens only for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`.

**Route-run state**

This is the canonical field set for `RouteRun` in v1. The canonical store for every `RouteRun` is the per-route `RouteRegistryEntry.runs` map under `RouteExecutor.route_registry[route_id]`; a `RouteRun` is filed there by its `run_number` at creation time and stays there indefinitely as either a live `RUNNING` row or a historical `FINISHED` / `DEACTIVATED` row. A train may have many historical `RouteRun` rows in the registry over time; the live invariant is that at most one `RouteRun` per `train_id` has `current_route_status.state == RUNNING` at any moment. `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` is a derived read, not a stored dict: it scans the registry for the one `RouteRun` whose `train_id` matches and `current_route_status.state == RUNNING`, returning that one or `None`. The parallel history accessor `RouteExecutor.history_for_train(train_id)` returns every `RouteRun` (any state: `RUNNING`, `FINISHED`, `DEACTIVATED`) for that train across all routes, ordered by `run_number` ascending. Return type: `List[RouteRun]`. The accessor flattens across all `RouteRegistryEntry.runs` maps in the registry, selects only rows where `run.train_id == train_id`, and sorts the result by `run_number` ascending. Throughout the rest of this section the name `exec_state` is used as a short alias for the train's currently `RUNNING` `RouteRun` (i.e. the result of `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`); when that read returns `None`, no helper that names `exec_state` runs.

`RouteExecutor.route_registry` is in-memory only; it is not persisted across process restart (persistence is Out of Scope for v1 per the Out of Scope table).

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `run_number` | `int` | Composite run-instance number assigned by `RouteExecutor` when this `RouteRun` is created. `RouteExecutor` keeps a stable ordered route registry as an instance field `RouteExecutor.route_registry: Dict[str, RouteRegistryEntry]` keyed by `route_id`, where each `RouteRegistryEntry` stores `route_registry_index: int` (append-only, assigned in insertion order starting at `0`, never recycled or compacted on route removal), `run_count_for_route: int` (1-based count of runs created for that `route_id`), and `runs: Dict[int, RouteRun]` (every `RouteRun` ever created for this route, keyed by its `run_number`; `RUNNING` runs and historical `FINISHED` / `DEACTIVATED` runs are stored side by side). `COPY_COUNT_BASE = 1000000` in v1, and `run_number = route_registry_index * COPY_COUNT_BASE + run_count_for_route`. The per-route counter increments on every new run for that `route_id`. A route that is removed and later re-added receives a new `route_registry_index`. `run_number` is assigned at `RouteRun` creation and is never recomputed. A run's `route_id` is established at run creation and never mutated, so `run_number` always identifies the same route registry entry. It is diagnostic-only and must not be parsed for current `route_id`. |
| `train_id` | `str` | The train this run belongs to. Stored on the run itself; the registry indexes runs by `route_id` plus `run_number`, not by `train_id`. |
| `route_id` | `str` | The shared `Route` definition this run is executing. Established at run creation and never mutated, even if the shared route's content later changes. Operator retargeting and route swap do not rewrite `route_id`; they go through "abort current run + create new run" (see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**). |
| `started_at` | `float` | System-time Unix timestamp when this run instance started. Written exactly once at `RouteRun` creation as `current_time` and never mutated afterward. Used as the synthetic predecessor sentinel for `task_runs[0]` in any "elapsed since predecessor" computation. |
| `current_waypoint_index` | `Optional[int]` (read-only derived) | Physical-progress cursor: the largest walk-position index `i` in `task_runs` where `task_runs[i].finished_at is not None`, or `None` when no task has finished yet. Computed each access from `task_runs`; not a stored field; no writers — progress is recorded by `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, now)` (runtime), which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` as one step of advancing the walk position, and the cursor reflects that update on the next read. (At allocation, start-mode seeding may also pre-finish a skipped prefix; see **Run start-mode seeding**.) The walk-position order is the directed walk order (each `task_runs[i]` represents one position on the directed walk; new walk-positions are appended on completion of `task_runs[i-1]` per `Route.mode` — see `task_runs` row below). A dispatcher-completed stop arrival therefore advances this cursor when it calls `advance_walk_position(...)` for the stop; a following `dwell` row is advanced by its own later `advance_walk_position(...)` call. A run with start mode `FIRST` starts with no `task_runs[*].finished_at` set, so `current_waypoint_index` reads as `None` and the first planning target is `task_runs[0]`.
