With `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`, allocation pre-finishes the skipped prefix `[0, s)`, so `current_waypoint_index` reads `s - 1` and the first planning target is the seeded start position — see **Run start-mode seeding**. **Naming note:** this field indexes all task kinds (`waypoint`, `stop`, `dwell`), not only `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks. The name is historical. |
| `direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Effective traversal direction for the current directed route walk. Initialized to `"forward"`; reversible round-trip behavior comes from this field plus `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(...)`, which is the sole computer of its flipped value. This is the one and only direction field on `RouteRun`; all route-walk code reads and writes this field as the literal `"forward"` / `"reverse"` string. |
| `task_runs` | `List[RouteTaskRun]` | Per-run directed-walk table. Each entry represents one walk-position. The list grows over the run's lifetime: at run creation it is initialized with `len(Route.tasks)` entries (one per task in the initial walk; entry at walk-position `i` has `task_index = i`). Start-mode seeding may pre-finish a prefix of these entries at allocation so the run begins at a later stop (see **Run start-mode seeding**). Each completed walk-position is recorded by the canonical advance helper `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, now)`, which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and then calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` per the canonical append rule below. For `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, the unfinished suffix length stays at `len(Route.tasks)` indefinitely; for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` no append happens, so the unfinished suffix shrinks to zero after the terminal task. Each entry owns per-walk-position `started_at` / `finished_at` timing, resolved target bookkeeping, and per-task error context — see **RouteTaskRun schema** below. The synthetic predecessor for `task_runs[0]` is `RouteRun.started_at`. |
| `last_error` | `Optional[ErrorRecord]` | Most recent route/path error for this train's run (per-train; `Route` does not carry a shared last-writer-wins error summary in v1). `None` when no error has been recorded; otherwise an `ErrorRecord(text: str, time: float)` (system-time Unix seconds). The `text` and `time` fields are always written together, so the pair is one Optional record rather than two parallel Optional scalars. |
| `path_cancelled` | `bool` | Latch: set by `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` when the matching live path is cancelled; consumed by Phase 1 route-deviation cleanup when `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true. See the Latch Table in the Core Runtime Loop section for the full lifetime rule. |
| `no_reachable_task` | `bool` | Set by Phase 1 when every task on the current directed walk is **broken** (each task's `task_location` target ID is missing from the network) for a `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` run, so no authority can be planned and the train holds idle. Cleared by the next Phase 1 pass that finds any valid (present-target) task on the walk. Not a latch — it has no gating predicate and no cross-phase handoff, so it carries no Latch-Table entry and no writer helper; Phase 1 maintains it by direct write. Drives the derived `display_state == "STALLED"` projection. Always `False` for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` runs, which finish instead. Default `False`. See **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `current_route_status` | `RouteRunStatus` | Always-present route-owned status. Allocated with `state = "RUNNING"`. `state` is read by exactly one consumer for control flow: the `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` active-run derived read keys on `state == RUNNING` to identify the train's currently-active run. Phase 1, Phase 2, Phase 3, and route-callback bodies never read `state` for control flow — they key on `received_path_plan`, the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table), controller cleanup latches, and current span coverage. See the write-contract table below for writers and triggers. The canonical home for resolved targets is `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`; `current_route_status` does not carry a parallel resolved-target collection. |
| `overrides` | `Dict[str, Any]` | Sparse per-run overrides of a small allowlisted set of route-level operational fields. Keys are field names from the v1 override allowlist (`"priority"`, `"acquisition_mode"`); a key being absent means "use the shared `Route` value for that field." Default: empty dict. See **RouteRun overrides** below for the override allowlist, the effective-value read pattern, and the force-push / soft-push edit-distribution rules. |
| `start_mode` | `Optional[RouteStartMode]` | The resolved start mode this run was seeded with at allocation (`FIRST` / `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`). Written once by the start-mode seed during step `9c4f`; diagnostic only (surfaced read-only in the run-record payload). NOT a live override — start mode has no meaning after the run's starting walk-position is seeded. See **Run start-mode seeding**. |
| `start_walk_position` | `int` | The walk-position (list index in `task_runs`) this run was seeded to plan toward first — its first real movement target. `0` for start mode `FIRST`; otherwise the seed position `s` after start-mode seeding pre-finishes the prefix `[0, s)`, so `next_planning_index == s` immediately after seeding. Written once by the start-mode seed during step `9c4f`. Read only by the derived `is_starting`. |
| `is_starting` | `bool` (read-only derived) | `True` while this run is still completing its first real movement target — `next_planning_index <= start_walk_position` — and `False` once the train completes the task at `start_walk_position`. Monotonic: it flips to `False` exactly once and never back, because `next_planning_index` (the first unfinished walk-position — the lowest `i` with `task_runs[i].finished_at is None`) only advances over the run, including across `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` wrap-around (`current_waypoint_index` is unsuitable here — it is `task_index` space and cycles on wrap). Read by the runnable-train ordering as the primary phase tier (running-phase runs ordered before starting-phase runs) — see the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**. A run re-allocated after a structural edit is seeded afresh, so it reads `is_starting == True` again until it completes its new first target. |
| `idle_hold_ticks` | `int` | Count of consecutive Phase-1 ticks on which this run held a truncated best-effort live authority (`authority_end_block_id != requested_destination_block_id`) that neither grew nor saw the head advance. Maintained by the Phase-1 authority-acquisition step (`66aa`) by direct write: incremented on each such no-progress tick, reset to `0` on any authority growth, head advance, full-authority acquisition, or when no live path is held. Not a latch — it has no gating predicate and no cross-phase handoff, so it carries no Latch-Table entry and no writer helper. Drives the idle-hold release in step `66aa`. Default `0`. |
| `release_cooldown_ticks` | `int` | Post-release acquisition-skip countdown. Set to `IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS` by step `66aa` when it latches an idle-hold release; while `> 0`, step `66aa` decrements it by one and skips authority acquisition for this run so the just-released reservations and switch locks stay free for higher-priority runs to claim, then resumes normal planning at `0`. Not a latch; Phase 1 maintains it by direct write. Default `0`. |

- Operator/manual reposition is modeled through the ordinary one-off `Route` + `RouteRun` flow, with no special run-level flag. Such a route is created with `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` and follows the ordinary route-definition validation rules below. A one-off reposition can be authored as a single-task route whose only `task_kind == "stop"` task is the destination; with a single stop, start-mode seeding (see **Run start-mode seeding**) resolves to that task and `current_waypoint_index` initializes to `None`, so `_find_next_task_target` resolves that stop as the first executable movement task. A destination block the train's span already covers is finished by the covered-stop rule in Phase 1's covered-task index advance (no dispatcher authority is created); any other destination goes through the general Phase 1 / `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` flow.

`ErrorRecord` carries exactly:

- `text` — `str`, the diagnostic error message
- `time` — `float`, system-time Unix seconds when the error was recorded

It is the canonical paired-Optional shape used for both `RouteRun.last_error` and `RouteTaskRun.last_error` so that the message and its timestamp are always either both present or both absent.

**RouteRun overrides**

This subsection is the canonical home for `RouteRun.overrides`, the per-run effective-value read helpers, and the operator-driven push semantics that distribute later route edits to existing runs.

- **Override allowlist (v1).** Exactly two route-level fields may be overridden per run: `priority` and `acquisition_mode`. Every other route field — `mode`, `tasks`, `assigned_train_ids`, `is_active`, `created_at`, `last_edited_at`, `started_at`, `route_id`, `start_mode`, `start_task_index` — is shared and not overridable. (`start_mode` / `start_task_index` are not live overrides, but they do accept a one-shot per-assignment input consumed at allocation — see **Run start-mode seeding**.) Any attempt to write a key outside the allowlist is a validation error on the override-edit endpoint; storage rejects the request rather than silently ignoring the unknown key.
- **Storage shape.** `RouteRun.overrides` is a sparse `Dict[str, Any]` keyed by the allowlisted field name. A key being absent means "use the shared `Route` value for that field." A key being present means the value at that key is the effective per-run value, regardless of what the shared `Route` currently holds. The stored types match the route-level types exactly (`priority: int`, `acquisition_mode: AcquisitionMode`); validation runs at write time.
- **Effective-value read helpers.** `RouteRun` exposes one read helper per allowlisted field, each returning the per-run effective value: `RouteRun.effective_priority` and `RouteRun.effective_acquisition_mode`. Each helper resolves the current shared `Route` via `RouteExecutor.get_route(route_id)`, returns `self.overrides[<field>]` if that key is present, and otherwise returns the matching field from the shared `Route`. The helpers are read-only and recompute each access (no cached value); later operator edits to either the run-level override or the shared `Route` take effect on the next read. Every Phase 1 / Phase 3 / RouteExecutor consumer that needs one of these two fields reads through the corresponding helper rather than reading `Route` or `overrides` directly; the runnable-train ordering at `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(...)` reads `effective_priority` per assigned run (and `RouteRun.is_starting` for the phase tier, per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**), and the create/extend acquisition reads `effective_acquisition_mode`.
- **Override write entry point.** Operator/admin writes to `RouteRun.overrides` route through one canonical handler: `RouteExecutor.set_run_override(train_id, field, value)` sets one allowlisted field on the train's currently `RUNNING` `RouteRun`. The handler validates the field name is in the allowlist and the value's type/range matches the shared route's validation rule for that field; on success it writes `overrides[field] = value` and bumps a single same-run audit timestamp `overrides_updated_at` for diagnostics. A symmetric `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(train_id, field)` removes one key from `overrides`, restoring the shared-route value for that field; clearing a key that is not present is a no-op success. Both helpers are no-op success when the train has no `RUNNING` `RouteRun`. Historical (`FINISHED` / `DEACTIVATED`) runs reject override edits — their `overrides` snapshot is frozen as part of the historical record.
- **Push semantics.** When the operator edits a shared `Route` field (through `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>`), the edit may carry a per-field push policy on each changed allowlisted field, encoded as the `push` parameter on the route-edit payload: `push = "force"`, `push = "soft"`, or `push` omitted (default). The route-edit handler applies one rule per changed allowlisted field, against every currently `RUNNING` `RouteRun` for that route:
  - **`force`** — overwrite any existing `RouteRun.overrides[field]` with the new shared value. Equivalent to calling `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(train_id, field)` on every `RUNNING` run for that route after writing the shared field. The shared route now holds the truth and no per-run override remains on this field.
  - **`soft`** — leave any existing `RouteRun.overrides[field]` intact; runs without an override for this field automatically pick up the new shared value through the effective-value helpers. No per-run write happens.
  - **omitted / default** — equivalent to `soft`; the shared edit lands on the route definition and runs without an override start using the new value on the next read, but no override is cleared.
  - Push policies are per-field; one route edit may carry `priority: {value: 7, push: "force"}` and `acquisition_mode: {value: "all_or_nothing", push: "soft"}` in the same request body. Non-allowlisted fields (e.g. `tasks`) ignore the `push` key — they have no per-run override to push to.
  - Push policies apply only to the route's shared field write; if the same request edits `tasks` or other non-allowlisted fields, those follow the existing **Route Edit And Reconciliation** structural-edit teardown rules unchanged.
- **Historical runs.** Push policies never touch `FINISHED` or `DEACTIVATED` runs. Their `overrides` snapshot is part of the historical row and remains as written when the run was active.
- **API surface.** `PATCH /api/route-runs/<run_number>/overrides` body `{field: value}` (single field) or `{overrides: {field: value, ...}}` (batched) calls `RouteExecutor.set_run_override(train_id, field, value)` per entry. `DELETE /api/route-runs/<run_number>/overrides/<field>` calls `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(train_id, field)`. Both endpoints reject historical runs with HTTP 409. `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>` accepts per-field push policies under the existing edit body; the encoding is `{<field>: {value: ..., push: "force"|"soft"}}` for allowlisted fields and the bare `{<field>: value}` shape continues to work for non-allowlisted fields and for allowlisted fields when no per-field push policy is needed (defaults to `soft`).

This is the canonical field set for `RouteRun.current_route_status` in v1.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `state` | `"RUNNING" \| "FINISHED" \| "DEACTIVATED"` | Stored. The active-run derived read `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` keys on `state == RUNNING` to identify the train's currently-active run. No Phase 1, Phase 2, Phase 3, or route-callback body branches on `state` for control flow — they key on `path_cancelled`, controller state, and runtime latches per the **`ControllerCondition` set**. `RUNNING`: ordinary execution (incl. dwell and unarmed-between-legs). `FINISHED`: the route's task list ran to its terminal stop (and any following dwell) and Phase 1's terminal-walk path called `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)`. `DEACTIVATED`: the run was terminated before its task list finished. The four canonical writers of `DEACTIVATED` are: (i) admin route deactivation through `route.deactivate()` (consumed by Phase 3 `pending_route_deactivate`); (ii) operator-driven route abort or retarget/swap (consumed by Phase 3 `pending_route_abort`); (iii) power-off cleanup (consumed by Phase 3 `pending_power_off`); (iv) train removal. All three latch consumers call the same `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` helper. Both `FINISHED` and `DEACTIVATED` are terminal: the `RouteRun` stays in its `RouteRegistryEntry.runs` map forever as an ordinary historical row (parallel to retained `Path` history), and Phase 1 is a no-op for that run (no fresh planning, no covered-task advance). The `RouteRun` itself records nothing beyond the `DEACTIVATED` state; UI/log consumers that need to distinguish the originating cause read the canonical `path_cancellation` notification (when a live path was cancelled) and the `command_result` notification for the originating operator/admin command. |
| `display_state` | `"RUNNING" \| "DEVIATED" \| "STALLED" \| "FINISHED" \| "DEACTIVATED"` (read-only derived) | Derived UI projection computed each access, in priority order: returns `"DEVIATED"` whenever `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True`; else returns `"STALLED"` whenever `RouteRun.no_reachable_task == True` (the whole directed walk is broken — see **Unreachable and broken task handling**); otherwise returns `state`. Not a stored field; no writers. UI/log consumers render this value; runtime branching never keys on it. |

`RouteRun.current_route_status` has this v1 write contract:

| Writer | Trigger | State after write | Other required writes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Phase 1 (after a successful create/extend) (`a436`) | a run resolves a fresh next target via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and Phase 1's authority-acquisition step succeeds in `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that target | `RUNNING` | Phase 1 sets `direction` to the resolved direction and writes `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)`) to the candidate that the successful create/extend used. |
| `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (`b0fb`) | the matching live path is cancelled or revoked | no `state` write — `display_state` projection follows `path_cancelled` | set `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` (see Latch Table). The callback does not clear `task_runs[i].resolved_target`; whatever Phase 1's last successful create/extend wrote stays in place. The derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` follows automatically from `path_cancelled == True`. |
| Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step (`5b81`) | `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true | no `state` write — `display_state` projection follows `path_cancelled` | clear `path_cancelled`; the derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` clears with it; the next eligible Phase 1 plans normally |
| Phase 1 terminal-walk detect — direct `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` (`75fa`) | the dwell-complete or covered-task-advance step finishes the last task on the directed walk and a same-Phase-1 `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns `None` | `FINISHED` | leave the `RouteRun` in its `RouteRegistryEntry.runs` map as the historical `FINISHED` row (the active-state predicate flips so the derived `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` no longer returns it, and `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` returns `None`); if the route is `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` and now has no remaining assigned trains, also call `route.deactivate()` (which writes `pending_route_deactivate` for any other still-assigned trains and sets `Route.is_active = False`); then return for that tick. By the time this fires, the matching live path has already been cleared by stop-completion in the prior Phase 3 pass, so no dispatcher cancel is needed. |
| Phase 3 — `pending_route_abort` consumer (`a87c`) | the `pending_route_abort` latch is consumed (operator/admin route-abort, retarget, or swap) | `DEACTIVATED` | the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` (when written, `initiator = "operator_cancelled"`) must already have committed earlier in the same Phase 3 pass per the Latch table; the consumer then calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`. The helper writes `DEACTIVATED` and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. Clear `pending_route_abort`. |
| Phase 3 — `pending_power_off` consumer (`b7d2`) | the `pending_power_off` latch is consumed (operator-issued `power_off_cmd()`) | `DEACTIVATED` | the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` (when written, `initiator = "power_off"`) must already have committed earlier in the same Phase 3 pass per the Latch table; the consumer then calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`. The helper writes `DEACTIVATED` and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. Controller-local OFF cleanup (transition controller `state` to `OFF`, clear cab and EB latch, clear `pending_power_off`, clear `ato.running`) follows the helper return. |
| Phase 3 — `pending_route_deactivate` consumer (`72e2`) | the `pending_route_deactivate` latch is consumed (operator/admin or `route.deactivate()` ran while this run had not yet finished) | `DEACTIVATED` | the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` (when written, `initiator = "revoked"`) must already have committed earlier in the same Phase 3 pass; the consumer then calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`. The helper writes `DEACTIVATED`, unassigns the train, and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. When the structural-edit teardown recorded a held re-assignment for this train, the consumer re-applies it (`Route.assign_train(train_id)`) after that clear, per the **Shared-route edit teardown**. Clear `pending_route_deactivate`. |

`RouteExecutor` owns these direct status writes and the latch/diagnostic writes that feed the derived `display_state` projection. Other entities such as `BlockManager`, `PathDispatcher`, and switch/resource callbacks may trigger the route callback that causes a state change, but they do not write `RouteRun.current_route_status.state` directly. All cancellation kinds surface the same `display_state == "DEVIATED"` projection. UI consumers that need the cancellation kind subscribe to the canonical `path_cancellation` event on `NotificationCenter` (defined in the **Cancellation** subsection). The functional gate for deviation cleanup is the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table), not `current_route_status.state` or `display_state`. The active-run predicate is a separate derived read on `state == RUNNING` and is the only place `state` is read for control flow. The `"DEVIATED"` projection clears back to whatever `state` currently holds (typically `"RUNNING"`) automatically when the same train's Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step clears `path_cancelled` after the controller has reached `READY`; that cleanup does not arm a target or create authority. The separate `"STALLED"` projection follows `RouteRun.no_reachable_task` the same way and ranks below `"DEVIATED"`: Phase 1 sets the flag when the whole directed walk is broken and clears it once a valid task returns (see **Unreachable and broken task handling**). `FINISHED` and `DEACTIVATED` are terminal — Phase 1 is a no-op for runs in those states (no fresh planning, no covered-task advance), and the `RouteRun` is retained as a historical record rather than removed.

**RouteTaskRun schema**

`RouteTaskRun` is the per-run task-state entry. `RouteRun.task_runs` is a `List[RouteTaskRun]` parallel to `Route.tasks` — one entry per task index, allocated when the `RouteRun` is created and indexed by the same integer index as `Route.tasks`. `RouteTaskRun` is the canonical owner of per-task timing, resolved dispatcher target, and per-task error context.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `task_index` | `int` | Back-reference into `Route.tasks` for the underlying `RouteTask` this walk-position represents. With the growing-`task_runs` model, walk-position `i` (the index of this entry in `task_runs`) is no longer guaranteed to equal `task_index` — for `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, walk-positions and `task_index` decouple after the first wrap/reflection. Readers needing the underlying task definition use `Route.tasks[task_index]`. |
| `resolved_target` | `Optional[ResolvedRouteTarget]` | Block and direction that Phase 1's most recent successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for this walk-position used. Written exclusively by Phase 1's authority-acquisition step on success; never written by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` itself, never written by Phase 3 head-advance / stop-completion callbacks, never cleared by any callback. The "what is the next target?" answer for live decisions is recomputed by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and consumed in place. `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the authoritative match key for `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` lookup, so for `task_kind == "stop"` the stored value reflects the platform/block dispatcher actually completed against. `None` until Phase 1 successfully creates/extends authority for this walk-position at least once; `None` for `dwell` walk-positions (which carry no dispatcher target). |
| `started_at` | `Optional[float]` (read-only derived) | For walk-position `i == 0`, equals `RouteRun.started_at`. Otherwise equals `task_runs[i - 1].finished_at` (the directly-preceding walk-position's finish), or `None` if that predecessor has not finished yet. Walk-position order is the directed walk order, so `i - 1` is always the directed-walk predecessor regardless of `Route.mode`. Not a stored field; no writers; no first-write rule. Consumers (`RouteTask.should_depart(...)`, `delay_class` computation, dwell timer) read this property directly. |
| `finished_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix seconds when this walk-position ended. For `waypoint` walk-positions, set when head-entry consumes the task. For `stop` walk-positions, set when dispatcher-committed stop completion accepts the task. For `dwell` walk-positions, set when the dwell timer is satisfied and the controller transitions out. `None` until the walk-position ends. `finished_at` is written by `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, the canonical runtime advance helper (it writes `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` per the canonical append rule); the timestamp write is one step of advancing the walk position, not a separate trigger. The one other writer is allocation-time start-mode seeding, which pre-finishes skipped prefix positions when the run begins at a later stop (see **Run start-mode seeding**); it sets `finished_at = RouteRun.started_at`, leaves `delay_class` `None`, and does not append. |
| `delay_class` | `Optional["early" \| "on_time" \| "late"]` | Timing classification computed when `finished_at` is recorded, by comparing the underlying `Route.tasks[task_index].task_time` (expected travel time from the previous task's location to this task's location) with the actual elapsed time since the predecessor walk-position's `finished_at` (use `RouteRun.started_at` for walk-position `0`). The comparison uses a tunable on-time band `DELAY_CLASS_ON_TIME_TOLERANCE_SECONDS`: elapsed below `task_time - tol` is `early`, above `task_time + tol` is `late`, otherwise `on_time`. `None` when `task_time` is absent on the shared task or when the walk-position has not yet finished. Not computed for `dwell` walk-positions, and a walk-position pre-finished by start-mode seeding or skipped as a broken task keeps `delay_class = None`. |
| `last_error` | `Optional[ErrorRecord]` | Most recent per-task error recorded during a failed planning or completion attempt for this task. Diagnostic only. `None` when no error has been recorded; otherwise an `ErrorRecord(text: str, time: float)` (system-time Unix seconds). |

`ResolvedRouteTarget` is the resolved dispatcher request contract for one task attempt: `requested_destination_block_id: str` and `requested_destination_direction: "forward" | "reverse"`. It supersedes `ResolvedWaypointTarget`.

The "armed next task" is reflected by the directed-walk projection, not by a separate flag: it is the next `task_runs[i]` returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` for the current directed walk. There is no `_arm_next_target(...)` step. `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the historical record of what Phase 1's last successful create/extend used for that task, not a gate that says "this task is armed."

For predecessor timestamp lookup (used by `delay_class`, dwell timer, and any other "elapsed since predecessor" computation): the predecessor is the directly-preceding walk-position. For walk-position `0` on a newly created `RouteRun`, the predecessor is the synthetic route-start sentinel `RouteRun.started_at`. The derived `task_runs[i].started_at` returns `RouteRun.started_at` for `i == 0` and `task_runs[i - 1].finished_at` otherwise, so any "elapsed since predecessor" computation reads `task_runs[i].started_at` directly. That predecessor `finished_at` always exists by the time walk-position `i` becomes active, because tasks finish in walk-position order and each new walk-position is appended only after its predecessor's `finished_at` is written.

**`RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, now)`** is the canonical helper that advances one walk-position to completion during runtime, and is the only runtime writer of `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` (the one other writer is allocation-time start-mode seeding — see **Run start-mode seeding**). In one call it (1) writes `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = now`, (2) computes `task_runs[walk_position].delay_class`, and (3) calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` to grow `task_runs`. The timestamp write is one step inside advancing — it is not itself "the advance" and does not separately trigger anything. Every progress site routes through this helper: the head-advance callback, the stop-completion callback (via `RouteExecutor._enter_task_arrival(...)`), the Phase 1 dwell-complete step, and the Phase 1 covered-task index advance. The derived `current_waypoint_index` and each successor's derived `started_at` reflect the new `finished_at` on their next read.

**`RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`** is the internal append step that `advance_walk_position(...)` calls after the timestamp / `delay_class` writes commit. It selects the next `task_index` to append per `Route.mode`:

- `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`: do not append. After the terminal task finishes, `task_runs` stops growing.
- `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`: append a new `RouteTaskRun` whose `task_index` is the next-in-cycle index (i.e. `(task_runs[i].task_index + 1) mod len(Route.tasks)`).
- `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`: append a new `RouteTaskRun` whose `task_index` follows the canonical ROUND_TRIP truth table: forward direction past the last task reflects to the second-to-last task with reverse direction; reverse direction past the first task reflects to the second task with forward direction. The appended walk-position's direction is the reflected `direction` that `_find_next_task_target(...)` returns in its projection and that Phase 1's caller commits to `RouteRun.direction` (per the **`current_route_status` write contract**); `_find_next_task_target(...)` performs no `RouteRun` write. The appended `RouteTaskRun` itself carries no `direction` field — direction lives on the `RouteRun`.

The appended `RouteTaskRun` initializes with `finished_at = None`, `resolved_target = None`, `delay_class = None`, `last_error = None`. Its derived `started_at` reads `task_runs[i - 1].finished_at` (the value just written by the caller). The helper never re-reads or mutates older walk-positions.

- `RouteRun` is the canonical per-train runtime object for one assigned route. Current-code implementation may still name the class `RouteExecutionState`, but every changed contract in this section refers to that object as the train's `RouteRun`.
- `RouteRun` carries `current_waypoint_index`, `direction`, and `task_runs`.
- `RouteRun.current_route_status` is route-owned temporary per-run status, not a shared route-definition cache. `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` reads this object and the dispatcher-passed completion context. `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` also reads it. Both callbacks identify the current task by index; whenever they need a fresh `ResolvedRouteTarget`, they call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`.
- `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the historical record of Phase 1's last successful create/extend for task `i`. The only writer is Phase 1's authority-acquisition step, on a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task. `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` is read-only; head-advance, dwell-complete, and UI consumers consume the helper's return value and do not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target`. No callback ever clears it; it is overwritten only by the next successful create/extend that targets the same task.
- `RouteExecutor` owns a scheduling registry over these `RouteRun` entries. On each Phase 1 tick it iterates runnable runs by run phase then descending effective priority (running-phase runs before starting-phase runs per `RouteRun.is_starting`), per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**, rather than arbitrary per-train order. Among equal phase tier and priority, stable existing iteration order is acceptable in v1.
- `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` is the route-owned removal entry point used by Phase 3 abort, power-off, and deactivate consumers (per the **`current_route_status` write contract**). All three Phase-3 latch consumers call it with the same single argument; the originating cause is recorded only on the `path_cancellation` and `command_result` notifications, not on any helper parameter or any field on the `RouteRun`. Natural terminal completion uses Phase 1's direct `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` call instead and writes `FINISHED`. When `remove_train_route_run(...)` is invoked, it first looks up the train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` via `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`. If no `RUNNING` run exists (e.g. a powered-off train without a route, or a train whose run was already torn down by an earlier consumer in the same Phase 3 pass), the call is a no-op success and returns immediately. Otherwise it performs the active-run teardown sequence:
  - if a live dispatcher path still exists for this train (`active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` is set), the caller must already have cancelled it through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` before invoking this method (the canonical caller is the same Phase-3 cleanup pass that already runs the inclusive-cancel step, so the latch-cancel-then-callback flow handles this naturally),
  - if the controller is currently in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (the `RouteRun` is dwelling), the method calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` to exit dwell into the **`READY` state** before clearing route-run state,
  - it calls `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on the run's `route_id` (idempotent if the train has already been unassigned by an earlier handler step) and flips the run's active-state predicate by setting `current_route_status.state = DEACTIVATED`, so the derived `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` read no longer returns it. (A structural-edit teardown re-assigns the train to the edited route *after* this unassign: the Phase-3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer applies its held `_pending_reassign` entry once the clear is done — see the **Shared-route edit teardown**. The abort and power-off consumers record no held re-assignment, so the train stays unassigned.) Historical rows from finish/deactivate that already exist in the registry are NOT touched. The `RouteRun` itself is NOT removed from the registry; only the active-state predicate flips, and the run remains as a historical row (visible to `RouteExecutor.history_for_train(train_id)`).

  An explicit operator/admin "abort route" request — including operator retargeting and explicit route swap — is delivered through the controller-owned Phase-3 latch `pending_route_abort` per the inclusive-latch rule defined in the **Latch Table** preamble. That same Phase 3 cleanup calls `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` only when the abort latch is consumed after the dispatcher cancel step (or the dispatcher-cancel-skipped no-active-path branch) has run. Aborting a dwelling train therefore takes that same path: Phase 3 cancels any live path (or skips the dispatcher cancel when none exists), then `remove_train_route_run(...)` exits dwell via `finish_dwell()` and flips the run's active-state predicate.

- **`RouteRun.last_error` and `RouteTaskRun.last_error` clearing rule.** Both fields are overwrite-only diagnostic records and are never cleared during a `RUNNING` run; later writers replace the prior `ErrorRecord` with the most recent failure context. Both initialize to `None` at run creation. They are not cleared by a successful create/extend, by stop completion, or by route-deviation cleanup. Run-terminal writes (`RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)`, `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`) leave whatever record was last written in place as part of the historical row.
- **`RouteTaskRun.last_error` canonical writer.** Phase 1 step `66aa` records per-task error context on `task_runs[walk_position].last_error` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)` for that attempt) on a failed `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task. The value is one `ErrorRecord(text=..., time=time.time())`. Same-task subsequent failures overwrite the field. The same step also writes `task_runs[walk_position].last_error` when `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns a sentinel projection (a `ResolvedNextTaskProjection` whose `resolved_target is None`) — whether `failure_kind == missing_target` (the `task_location` ID is not present in the network) or `failure_kind == target_unavailable` (every candidate block / platform is `disabled`, or is `manual` while the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = False`, or a `StationTarget`'s platforms are all filtered out for the same reasons); `walk_position` is the projection's walk-position and `text` names the reason (e.g. `"Station <station_id> is not in the network"`, `"Block <block_id> is set to manual"`, `"all platforms in station <station_id> are disabled or manual"`). Without this resolver-failure write, a broken-task skip (per **Unreachable and broken task handling**) advancing past the task would leave `task_runs[walk_position].last_error` empty. The only other write to these fields is the clear-on-success: when the `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` for the task succeeds, the same Phase 1 step `66aa` resets that task's `task_runs[walk_position].last_error` and the run-level `RouteRun.last_error` back to `None`, so a stale earlier failure does not linger once the train has acquired authority. Phase 1 step `66aa` also writes `RouteRun.last_error` on a failure (so the run-level "most recent error" reflects the latest task-level error). `TrainController.to_dict()` projects the live `RouteRun.last_error` as `path_error`, but only while the train currently has no path-backed authority (`path_backed_run_active` is `False`); the UI consumes `path_error` for the train-panel no-path banner and the Quick Route no-path toast.
- Do not add a synchronous `RouteExecutor.continue_route_run_after_eb(...)` hook. EB release is handled through latches: the EB activation has already written `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "eb"`, which Phase 3 commits and which sets `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` via the cancellation callback. The same train's later Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step consumes `path_cancelled` once the controller has reached `READY` with no live path. The next `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` tick reruns the ordinary planning kernel from the current physical position: covered-task index advance for already-passed `waypoint` tasks, `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to resolve the next task, and a brand-new `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`. This flow does not reuse, complete, or preserve the cancelled pre-EB path chain.
- The derived `current_waypoint_index` reads `None` when no task has finished yet (no `task_runs[i].finished_at` is set). There is no "synthetic current-location start stop" branch: a one-off reposition route is authored as a single-task `stop` route. With start mode `FIRST`, `current_waypoint_index is None` means "no task has been completed yet" and `Route.tasks[0]` is the first planning target; `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC` seed a later start position instead (see **Run start-mode seeding**).
- `current_waypoint_index` is the route-progress cursor: derived from `task_runs[i].finished_at`, it always marks the last task the train has actually finished. There is no separate stored planning cursor in v1; whenever any phase needs the next-task index, it calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh.
- The initial `RouteRun.direction` is `"forward"` through the stored route order. The route plan remains an ordered list in v1. Reversible round-trip behavior comes only from `RouteRun.direction` flipping between `"forward"` and `"reverse"` inside `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, not from building a second linked or reversed route structure.
- When a new target is resolved, `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns the next-task projection and resolved target candidate. Phase 1 sets `direction` to the resolved direction, and after a successful create/extend writes `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the helper's returned index) to the accepted candidate. Route planning must not look beyond the resolved task in one pathfinder request.
- A failed `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` without growing authority while a live path still exists, and a `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` for the matching live path, both leave physical progress unchanged. There is no separate planning-cursor rollback; later Phase 1 calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from the current shared route, current physical progress, and the current live authority end or no-authority stopped position as applicable, and that helper resolves the next task fresh. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` failure follows the same rule: the next Phase 1 simply re-invokes `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`.
- `RouteExecutor` reads the live path id via `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`. A single live path may remain active across multiple task segments. After `waypoint` consumption, `dwell` completion, or `stop` completion, `task_runs[i].finished_at` is recorded for the consumed task (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to that index automatically), and only a later progress callback or later Phase 1 may discover the next task on the same `path_id` when authority is still live. A single `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback never consumes more than one task.
- UI consumers wanting "why does the train have no live authority?" read `RouteRun.last_error` (the most recent path/route failure recorded by Phase 1) plus the controller's live `lookahead` diagnostics. (Watchdogs and alert notifications are Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.)
- Do not add a separate controller operational sub-state for cancellation recovery in v1. When a live path is cancelled, `PathDispatcher` still clears controller authority and the controller enters the no-authority EB protocol per step `826a` (lookahead becomes the no-authority empty payload; ATC overspeed at distance 0 escalates to EB). The deferred recovery gate is the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table); `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` sets it and records diagnostic context immediately in Phase 3, but does not choose a new target or create fresh authority there. Once the train reaches the stopped no-authority state (after EB has brought it to rest and `eb.release()` has cleared EB to `READY`), the same train's Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step consumes `path_cancelled`. A later eligible Phase 1 then re-enters ordinary planning: covered-task index advance for already-passed `waypoint` tasks, then `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to resolve the next task.
- Phase 1's covered-task index advance: at the start of each Phase 1 tick, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` reads the train's current span via `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id).get_current_span()` and walks the current directed route from `current_waypoint_index` (the derived read of "last finished task"). For each next non-finished task on the directed walk: if `task_kind == "waypoint"` and the task's resolved target block (resolved live for this purpose) is already inside the current span, set `task_runs[i].finished_at = current_time` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances with that write) and continue. If the next task is `task_kind == "stop"`, the covered-task advance finishes it (`task_runs[i].finished_at = current_time`) only in the covered-stop case — the train is stopped, no active path exists, and the task's destination block is inside the train's current span (e.g. a route authored while the train already sits at the destination platform, which has no path for the dispatcher to complete); while the train is rolling or a live path exists, block coverage never finishes a stop and it is served only by dispatcher-committed completion. If the next task is `task_kind == "dwell"`, return without advancing — dwell is handled by the Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`. There is no separate `_advance_past_covered_waypoints(...)` helper or `_skip_waypoint(...)` helper in v1; the index advance is just a small loop inside `tick_train_phase1`.

**Canonical Route-Progress Events**

- `current_waypoint_index` advances automatically (it is a derived read) when any of these route-owned events writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` in v1:
  - head-based `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption in `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`, at most one task per head-advance callback
  - `task_kind == "dwell"` completion after the dwell timer elapses following a dispatcher-committed stop arrival
  - Phase 1's covered-task index advance for already-covered `waypoint` tasks (including post-cancellation reconciliation) and covered stopped-at-destination `stop` tasks (train stopped, no active path, destination block inside the current span)
  - route edit / reconciliation that explicitly declares one or more pending tasks skipped
- Head-based `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption is a route-owned in-motion progress event, not a dispatcher stop-completion event. The canonical trigger chain is `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` → `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` → `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` → `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`. The callback contract is fully defined once in the **Dispatcher progress/completion flow** section; consult that section. Summary: each callback consumes at most one task. If the reported block matches the next non-finished task's resolved target, the callback records `task_runs[i].finished_at` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances with that write) and returns. Non-matching block reports are no-ops on the route side; intermediate tasks the train physically passed are reconciled by the next Phase 1's covered-task index advance.
- Already-covered `waypoint` normalization is a Phase 1 step: it records `task_runs[i].finished_at = current_time` (so the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances) for any next-on-walk `waypoint` task whose resolved target block is already inside the train's current span. `task_kind == "stop"` tasks are not skipped this way: they are served only by dispatcher-committed stop completion. Phase 1 may run this index advance on multiple consecutive tasks in one pass before requesting authority. Route edits, operator skip requests, and post-cancellation covered-task reconciliation use this same index-advance mechanism. There is no separate `_skip_waypoint(...)` helper.
- There is no stored planning cursor in v1. The "next task to plan against" is always the index returned by the latest `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call. `current_waypoint_index` (derived from `task_runs[i].finished_at`) is the only stored route-progress cursor, and it advances only through physical consumption (head entry, stop completion, dwell exit, or covered-task index advance, all of which write `task_runs[i].finished_at`). A successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` writes `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` for the helper-returned `walk_position` but does not by itself advance `current_waypoint_index`.

**Route-executor state reads**

- `RouteExecutor` should read live operational state from `TrainController`, not from mirrored runtime fields on `Train`.
- Departure, retry, arrival, dwell, and waiting decisions should use controller state as the operational source of truth.
- `Train` may still expose persisted or UI-facing state, but route-control decisions should not depend on it being a live mirror.
- Manual driving is `control_mode == manual`, not a separate operational state. A route-managed manual train still uses ordinary `READY` / `RUNNING_*` states and dispatcher authority; the difference from ATO is how movement begins and is driven, not whether `RouteExecutor` owns the waypoint plan. In particular, manual `READY` must not block route-side path creation. It changes only what `depart()` means after authority exists.

**Planning Kernel**

- Phase 1 is a state-machine: each canonical step has explicit preconditions and is a no-op when those preconditions are not met. The kernel never creates fresh authority while the train has not yet reached a stopped recovery point, while any teardown/recovery latch is still dirty, while the controller is `OFF`, or while the `RouteRun` is pending/inside a dwell step.
- Eligibility precondition for the planning kernel's authority-acquisition step (step `66aa`) and the re-resolve step (step `592a` when it would lead to acquisition): `RouteExecutor` may run those steps only when one of the following is true:
  - the train has live authority (`PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id) is not None`) AND `controller.active_conditions ∩ {PATH_CANCEL_PENDING, ROUTE_ABORT_PENDING, ROUTE_DEACTIVATE_PENDING} == ∅`, in which case only step `66aa`'s `extend_path(...)` branch is allowed. The latch exclusion prevents Phase 1 from extending a live path that Phase 3 is about to cancel; or
  - `RouteExecutor.is_clean_for_create(train_id)` is true per the **`ControllerCondition` set** canonical home, in which case the create branch is allowed.
- Steps `5fc7`–`3a37` (deviation cleanup, dwell-complete, helper lookup, covered-task advance, dwell-entry) run unconditionally on every Phase 1 entry — they make no `PathDispatcher` mutation and only mutate `RouteRun` state per their own gating predicates. When `pending_route_deactivate` is set, these steps still run unconditionally. The interactions with Phase 3's deactivation teardown are safe by construction: (a) if step `70c3` calls `finish_dwell()` on the deactivation tick, Phase 1 runs before Phase 3 and the controller has already exited `RUNNING_AT_STATION` by the time Phase 3's deactivate consumer runs `remove_train_route_run(...)`; that helper checks `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION` before calling `finish_dwell()` and correctly skips it; (b) if step `3a37` calls `enter_station_dwell()` on the deactivation tick, the same-tick Phase 3 deactivate consumer calls `finish_dwell()` through `remove_train_route_run(...)` before archiving the run. Do not add a guard to skip steps `70c3` or `3a37` when `pending_route_deactivate` is set; doing so would leave the controller stuck in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` when the deactivation teardown relies on the Phase-1-first ordering.
- The derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` projection is not itself a gate; it clears automatically when `path_cancelled` is consumed by the same Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step (step `5fc7`).
- If the controller is `OFF`, Phase 1 is skipped. If eligibility preconditions are otherwise unmet (still braking, dirty handoff), Phase 1 returns and waits for a later tick.
- For the EB case, the same eligibility rule applies: while `eb.active == True`, `controller.is_recovery_clean` is false per the **`ControllerCondition` set** canonical home and Phase 1 produces no authority. After `eb.release()` returns the controller to `READY`, the next eligible Phase 1 runs the planning kernel normally.
- One Phase 1 pass owns the planning-side `RouteRun` mutation in this canonical order (the only `RouteRun` writes outside Phase 1 are the `task_runs[i].finished_at` stamps `RouteExecutor` makes in its Phase-3 head-advance and stop-completion callbacks). `RouteExecutor` is the canonical owner of every step; `SimulationController` invokes the kernel through `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` and `RouteExecutor` reaches the controller through its top-level `SimulationController` reference (`self.simulation_controller.get_or_create_controller(train_id)`). Steps:
  0. `9c4f` — **RouteRun (re)allocation.** Look up the train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` (`RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`). If one exists, continue to step `5fc7`. Otherwise, scan the route registry for any `Route` with `train_id ∈ Route.assigned_train_ids` and `Route.is_active == True`; if none is found, Phase 1 returns (no work). If one is found, allocate a new `RouteRun` against that `route_id`: write `route_id`, sample `RouteRun.started_at = time.time()`, set `direction = "forward"`, allocate `task_runs` with `len(Route.tasks)` entries (one per task with `task_index = 0..N-1`, all other fields `None`), set `current_route_status.state = "RUNNING"`, then seed the starting walk-position per **Run start-mode seeding** (for `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC` this pre-finishes the skipped prefix so the run begins at the chosen stop; `FIRST` leaves the run at `task_runs[0]`). The newly-allocated run is the canonical home of all subsequent Phase 1 mutation for that train this tick. Continue to step `5fc7`.
  1. `5fc7` — **Route-deviation cleanup** (multi-tick latch). When `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` AND `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, clear `path_cancelled` (the derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` projection clears with it).
  2. `70c3` — **Dwell-complete timer check.** When `TrainController.ops.state == RUNNING_AT_STATION` and `RouteTask.should_depart(current_time, task_runs[walk_position].started_at)` is satisfied: call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, current_time)` (which writes the dwell row's `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position` and the next task's derived `started_at` reflects this `finished_at`); then call `TrainController.finish_dwell()` (resolved through the top-level reference); then continue into step `c481`.
  3. `c481` — **Look up the next non-finished task** on the directed walk by calling `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`. If the projection's `task_kind == "dwell"`, skip to step `3a37` (dwell-entry); otherwise continue to step `a5c6` with the result discarded — every step that needs the resolved target re-invokes the helper after any cursor change.
  4. `a5c6` — **Covered-task index advance.** While the next non-finished task is `task_kind == "waypoint"` and its (live-resolved) target block is inside the current span, call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances and the next task's derived `started_at` reflects this `finished_at`). Stop when the next task is `task_kind == "stop"` — except finish it in the covered-stop case (train stopped, no active path, and the task's destination block inside the current span); while the train is rolling or a live path exists, block coverage never finishes a stop — or `task_kind == "dwell"` (continue to step `3a37`) or no further task fits coverage.
  5. `3a37` — **Dwell-entry transition.** When the next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`, the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true: call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`. The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor's `finished_at`; this step does not need to write it. Return for that tick — no path creation while dwell is pending or active. (If the controller is already `RUNNING_AT_STATION` at step `3a37`, also return.)
  6. `592a` — **Re-resolve the next target.** Call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh now that any cursor-changing step has run. Use the returned projection's `walk_position`, `direction`, and `resolved_target` as the candidate for this authority attempt, and set `direction` from that projection. Do not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target` yet.
  7. `66aa` — **Authority acquisition.** Call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that one resolved target only, passing `manual_override` from the run's route (`Route.manual_override` resolved through `RouteRun.route_id`). On a successful create/extend, write `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the helper-returned index) to the candidate that the dispatcher actually targeted (this is the only canonical writer of that field in v1). When this Phase 1 has live authority for the current target, also call `TrainController.depart()` directly for the current authority. On a failed create/extend, record per-task error context on `task_runs[walk_position].last_error` and run-level error on `RouteRun.last_error` per the canonical writer rule. **Idle-hold maintenance and release (best-effort hoarding guard).** This step is the canonical home for releasing a stalled best-effort hold, maintaining `RouteRun.idle_hold_ticks` and `RouteRun.release_cooldown_ticks`. While `release_cooldown_ticks > 0`, this step does no acquisition: it decrements `release_cooldown_ticks` by one and returns for that train this tick, so the just-released reservations and switch locks stay free for higher-priority runs through the cooldown window. Otherwise, after the create/extend attempt, if the live authority is a truncated best-effort prefix (`authority_end_block_id != requested_destination_block_id`) that neither grew this tick (the `extend_path(...)` outcome was `blocked_no_growth`, not `truncated_grew` / `reached_requested_target`) nor saw the head advance, increment `idle_hold_ticks`; any growth, head advance, full-authority acquisition, or absence of a live path resets it to `0`. When `idle_hold_ticks` reaches `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS`, release the held truncated authority the system-replan way: call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(initiator="idle_hold_release")` per the **Writer-helper rule**, stop planning for that train this tick, set `release_cooldown_ticks = IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS`, and reset `idle_hold_ticks = 0`. Phase 3 then commits that cancel (the train is already stopped under zero ATC clearance), freeing the not-yet-entered reservations and switch locks; the ordinary post-cancel replan reruns from the train's real position on a later eligible Phase 1 tick once the cooldown has elapsed. A full authority (`authority_end_block_id == requested_destination_block_id`) is never truncated, so `idle_hold_ticks` stays `0` and this release never fires for a train that already holds authority to its real target. `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS` (the consecutive-no-progress tick budget) and `IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS` (the post-release acquisition-skip window) are tunable v1 constants.
- A single Phase 1 pass may loop locally through these steps. For one planning problem, Phase 1 may try multiple distinct same-tick resolved targets only when the current task is a `StationTarget` task: Phase 1 owns a local `tried` set of already-attempted resolved targets for this planning problem and calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=tried)` repeatedly, appending each returned `resolved_target` to `tried` after a failed `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` attempt. The helper itself stores no per-tick state; Phase 1 has all the information needed to decide which candidates have been attempted. The helper returns the next compatible candidate each time (or `None` when exhausted). A `PlatformTarget` task, `BlockTarget` task, or explicit local-reposition task has only one resolved pair for that tick; if that create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, the run waits until a later eligible tick rather than trying alternatives in the same pass. If every same-tick candidate is exhausted without authority creation or growth and no live path exists for that run, `RouteExecutor` stops Phase 1 for that train for that tick. If a live path still exists and `extend_path(...)` returns `success=True/result_kind=blocked_no_growth`, keep `current_route_status.state = RUNNING`, preserve the live path, and retry on later eligible ticks — subject to the **idle-hold maintenance and release** rule in step `66aa`, which releases a truncated authority that makes no progress for `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS` consecutive ticks so it stops hoarding resources a higher-priority run could use. If `extend_path(...)` returns `success=False/failure_kind=no_viable_path`, `invalid_reverse`, or another non-temporary structural failure for that live path, `RouteExecutor` must latch cancellation and wait for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel/recovery flow before any fresh create-path attempt.
- `RouteExecutor` never asks dispatcher/pathfinder to solve a whole multi-task route in one search. Longer authorities are formed only by repeated point-to-point segments appended onto the same live path identified by `path_id`.
- In normal route-managed execution, v1 does not concatenate beyond the currently armed task on the same tick. Route planning arms exactly one task at a time. If the current armed task is `task_kind == "waypoint"`, that task is still consumed only by the head-entry callback chain; after that physical consumption, the next Phase 1 tick may resolve the next task and extend or recreate authority from the train's then-current physical state.
- A waypoint-terminated authority before that next planning tick is a degraded but valid v1 authority boundary. If the train later brakes there, that is a safety stop caused by lack of further authority, not a planned stop for the `waypoint` task itself.
- `task_kind == "stop"` versus `task_kind == "waypoint"` changes only what happens when the resolved target is reached: `stop` enters dwell (or the `dwell` task that follows it) through dispatcher-committed completion from the post-physics controller stop check, while `waypoint` advances immediately without dwell. It does not change the dispatcher path API shape.

**Run start-mode seeding**

This subsection is the canonical home for which stop a fresh `RouteRun` begins at. It is consulted once, when Phase 1 step `9c4f` allocates a fresh run, and seeds the run's starting walk-position.

- **Start mode.** `RouteStartMode` has three values:
  - `FIRST` (default) — start at `task_runs[0]`. The covered-task index advance then walks past any already-covered `waypoint` tasks exactly as before; stops are never skipped by coverage. This is the v1 behavior when no start mode is set.
  - `NEAREST` — seed the run at the best `task_kind == "stop"` task for the train to begin at. A stop is eligible only when the train has a viable path to its resolved block in the block-relative direction that stop's task requires — the same directed graph-walk the stop-planning rule under **RouteTask data** runs to plan a real move there, which already filters on the task's `task_direction` and on `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. So a stop the tracks merely touch, but that can only be reached against its task's direction or against block travel-direction, is not eligible, and the ranking falls through to the next viable stop. Eligibility and track distance both come from that one shared directed graph-walk query (the **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload** comparison, run with the stop's required direction), so this subsection does not restate the metric or the direction filter. Among the eligible stops, rank each by four things in order, each one breaking ties left by the ones before it:
    1. **No reversal first.** Prefer a stop the train can drive straight on from. A stop is a *reverse stop* when, after arriving there, the train's next route move would have to leave it back through the same block the train arrived through — it has to turn around in place. A drive-on stop is preferred even when a reverse stop is a little closer. (The next move is the first `stop` / `waypoint` task after this one, skipping `dwell` tasks, wrapping for `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`, and reflecting at `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` ends — the same "next move" `RouteExecutor.find_next_move_target(...)` resolves for the **reversal-trap exclusion** under best-effort `create_path()`.)
    2. **Free first.** Prefer a stop no other train has taken. A stop is *taken* when its resolved block is either occupied by another train or is the live next-move target of another train already running this route. Reading other runs' current next move is what spreads a yard-full of trains across distinct stops instead of all of them picking the same nearest one; because it reads each run's *current* move, a train that has already driven past its own seed no longer holds a claim on it.
    3. **Nearest.** Prefer the shorter track-distance walk.
    4. **Lowest task index** breaks any remaining tie.

    If no stop has a viable directed path, fall back to FIRST.
  - `SPECIFIC` — start at the stop named by the effective `start_task_index`. The index must be in range and name a `task_kind == "stop"` task; otherwise fall back to FIRST.
- **Where it is set.** The route-level default is `Route.start_mode` / `Route.start_task_index`. A single assignment may override it with a per-assignment input (`start_mode` / `start_task_index` on `PUT /api/trains/<id>/assign-route`), stashed on `RouteExecutor._pending_start_overrides[train_id]` and consumed by the next fresh allocation. The effective value is: the per-assignment input if present, else the route default, else `FIRST`. The per-assignment input is **one-shot** — it is cleared at the top of every assign / retarget / unassign and removed when the fresh run consumes it, so it never leaks onto a different route's run and a re-allocation after a structural edit falls back to the route default. It is NOT a live `RouteRun` override (it is not in the override allowlist): start mode has no meaning once the run's starting walk-position is seeded, so it is consulted only at allocation.
- **Seeding mechanism.** At allocation (step `9c4f`), after `task_runs` is initialized with `len(Route.tasks)` entries, let `s` be the chosen start task index. For `FIRST` (and any fallback to FIRST), `s = 0` and nothing is seeded. For `s > 0`, the run is allocated with the skipped prefix already finished: `task_runs[i].finished_at = RouteRun.started_at` for `i` in `[0, s)`, with `delay_class` left `None` (those positions were not travelled). `next_planning_index` then lands on `s` and `current_waypoint_index` reads `s - 1`. This prefix-finish is an allocation-time initialization, distinct from the runtime `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` progress writer, and it does not append walk-positions. Because the initial walk holds only the forward leg (`task_index == walk_position` for `0..N-1`) and `s ≤ len(Route.tasks) - 1`, the apex is never pre-finished and `RouteRun.direction` stays `"forward"`; ROUND_TRIP reflection is handled normally by `_append_next_walk_task()` when the genuine apex later finishes. The resolved mode is recorded on `RouteRun.start_mode` for diagnostics, and the chosen start index `s` on `RouteRun.start_walk_position` (the seeded first planning target that the derived `RouteRun.is_starting` phase tier compares the progress cursor against).

**Route Edit And Reconciliation**

- Active runs reference the current shared `Route` through `RouteRun.route_id`. Any edit to `Route.tasks` that changes the waypoint set, ordering, or task-kind composition triggers immediate teardown of every assigned train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` for that `route_id`, followed by fresh `RouteRun` allocation against the edited `Route` on the next eligible Phase 1. There is no in-place reconciliation in v1 and no version stamp on the run. The fresh run picks its starting walk-position per **Run start-mode seeding**: because the per-assignment start input is one-shot (already consumed by the original allocation), a re-allocation after a structural edit uses the route-level `Route.start_mode` default. `Route.priority`, `Route.acquisition_mode`, and `Route.manual_override` edits are the exception — they take effect live without a teardown because they do not change the walk structure. Allowlisted-field edits may carry per-field `push = "force"` / `push = "soft"` policies that distribute the new value across existing `RouteRun.overrides` per the **RouteRun overrides** subsection. All edits in this paragraph (structural and non-structural) bump `Route.last_edited_at` per the **Route edit timestamps** subsection.
- A `RouteRun`'s `route_id` is established at run creation and never mutated.
- **Shared-route edit teardown.** When the shared-route edit handler accepts a structural edit, it walks `Route.assigned_train_ids` and per assigned train calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(...)`, which sets `pending_route_deactivate = True` and writes the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request(REVOKED)` when a live path exists. Each train's later eligible Phase 3 commits the inclusive cancel and the deactivate teardown through the standard latch consumption flow: the deactivate consumer calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`, which writes `current_route_status.state = DEACTIVATED` on the old run and (when `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `finish_dwell()` first. The historical row stays in the registry. Because `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` unassigns the train from the run's `route_id` (the edited route) as part of that clear, a same-route re-assignment cannot be applied up front — it would be undone by that unassign. The new assignment is therefore **held until the clear is done, then applied**: immediately after writing the deactivate latches, the handler records a one-shot pending re-assignment for each train (`RouteExecutor._pending_reassign[train_id] = route_id`, the edited route) instead of re-adding to `Route.assigned_train_ids` up front. The same train's Phase-3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer, **after** `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` has torn the old run down and unassigned the train, applies the held re-assignment by calling `Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the recorded route and clearing the stash. The next eligible Phase 1 then sees the train assigned to the edited route with no `RUNNING` `RouteRun` and allocates a fresh `RouteRun` against the edited `Route.tasks` per Phase 1's RouteRun (re)allocation step. (Operator retargeting / route swap differs: it assigns the train to a *different* route up front — see the next bullet — so its assignment is not undone by the old run's same-route unassign and needs no held re-assignment. `_pending_reassign` is consumed only by the `pending_route_deactivate` consumer; the abort and power-off consumers, and admin `route.deactivate()`, never record it, so they tear down without re-assigning.)
- The fresh `RouteRun` starts with `direction == "forward"` and `task_runs` initialized from the edited `Route.tasks`, then seeds its starting walk-position per **Run start-mode seeding** (re-allocation after a structural edit uses the route-level `Route.start_mode` default, since the per-assignment input is one-shot). For start mode `FIRST` this leaves `current_waypoint_index == None`; `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC` pre-finish a skipped prefix. Phase 1 then runs the ordinary planning kernel: the covered-task index advance walks past any `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks the train already covers, and `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns the first non-finished walk-position for the next planning step. Stops are not skipped by coverage, so the fresh run lands on the first non-covered stop or waypoint at or after its seeded start position automatically.
- Operator retargeting and explicit route swap follow the same teardown workflow with one variation: the API/UI/admin handler for the retarget/swap request atomically (a) calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` on the targeted train (which fans out to inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request(OPERATOR_CANCELLED)` when a live path exists) and (b) updates assignment — `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on the old route and `Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the new (or newly-created one-off `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`) route. The handler returns success immediately. The same train's later Phase 3 consumes the inclusive cancel and then the abort latch through `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`; the next eligible Phase 1 sees the train assigned to the new route with no `RUNNING` `RouteRun` and allocates a fresh `RouteRun` against the new `route_id`.

**Concrete `RouteExecutor` tick calls**

- `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` refreshes route-managed scheduling inputs for this tick. `delta_seconds` is the per-tick movement-window length (the gap between consecutive ticks at the simulation's current playback rate); it is used by physics integration and by callers that need a per-tick delta, but `RouteExecutor` does NOT maintain a separate `simulation_time` accumulator. All stored route/run timestamps use system-time Unix seconds (`time.time()`); there is no v1 `RouteExecutor.simulation_time` field. `RouteExecutor` owns the final runnable-train ordering through `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids)`: every candidate train resolves to one phase tier (running-phase vs starting-phase per `RouteRun.is_starting`; trains without a current `RouteRun` are running-tier) and one effective priority (route-managed trains use the current shared `Route.priority`; trains without a current `RouteRun` use effective priority `5`), and the merged set is sorted first by phase tier (running before starting) then by effective priority descending (`9` highest, `0` lowest), with stable existing iteration order as the only tie-break, per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**. Route-managed and non-route trains at the same phase tier and effective priority interleave in stable iteration order; route management does not bump priority within a tier. `SimulationController.update(...)` owns executing the returned train IDs, but it must not apply a second priority sort. `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` prepares the route-managed Phase 1 work that `SimulationController` will invoke by train; it must not itself call `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` for every train in a world-wide pass. Trains without a current `RouteRun` still remain in the `SimulationController` loop and simply have no route-managed Phase 1 work for that tick. Stored route/run timestamps such as `Route.started_at`, `RouteRun.task_runs[i].started_at`, `RouteRun.task_runs[i].finished_at`, `RouteRun.last_error.time` (when present), and `RouteTask.target_time` use system-time Unix seconds.
- `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` is the canonical public Phase-1 surface and is the entire Phase 1 route-executor tick for one train. `SimulationController.update(...)` calls it immediately before that same train's `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` transaction. Dwell handling, covered-task index advance, live target resolution, station candidate iteration, and `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` attempts all live here. `tick_train_phase1` creates or reuses the train's `RouteRun` (current-code class may still be named `RouteExecutionState`). If the controller is `OFF`, Phase 1 route work is skipped and power-on is handled by the Phase-2 command slot. If the next non-finished task on the directed walk has `task_kind == "dwell"`, dwell is the active route step: Phase 1 returns for that tick (Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` handles the transition into `RUNNING_AT_STATION`).
- Before any create/extend decision, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and receive a legal `ResolvedNextTaskProjection` for the current shared route and current physical progress. The returned `resolved_target` is the transient request candidate for this attempt. If the helper cannot currently resolve a legal concrete platform/block target under the task's fixed/flexible platform policy, the retry fails at target resolution and `RouteExecutor` must not call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that tick.
- Before any create/extend decision, the current attempt's resolved target is the route-owned request contract for the `walk_position` returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`: the task target block and the route-owned `requested_destination_direction` constraint. It must not resolve or pass any stop-position centimeter field. Dispatcher and controller use the chosen terminal frame plus controller safety margin for braking/boundary projection.
- Route-managed path building is always segment-by-segment along the current directed route walk. On each create/extend attempt, the resolved target represents exactly one resolved task target, never a bundled multi-task search request. After one segment is successfully created or appended, route planning stops for that train on that tick; if the armed target is `task_kind == "waypoint"`, the next task is not armed until that `waypoint` is physically consumed and the next planning tick runs.
- `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` should ensure the train's `TrainController` exists before it reads controller state or calls `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`. A route-managed train already has a controller by the time authority is assigned in Phase 1. `SimulationController` owns only the 1:1 binding between `train_id` and `TrainController`; it does not construct or write authority projections.
- Before resolving a new task or retrying the current one, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` runs the covered-task index advance (a small loop inside the method) so the route does not keep targeting a task the train already physically covers.
- The operational state checks inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` should read controller state rather than `train.state`.
- If controller state is `OFF`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` is not invoked for normal Phase 1 planning. A powered-off train must not create, extend, or rely on dispatcher authority, and the system must not auto-power-on it. `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` is accepted only through the Phase-2 command slot; route planning resumes on a later tick after the controller is powered and in an eligible state.
