The canonical power-off release/removal order follows the standard phase workflow per the **Power-off procedure**.
- If the next route task is `task_kind == "dwell"`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must treat dwell as the active route step before any departure/path planning and return for that tick. The Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` transitions the controller to `RUNNING_AT_STATION` automatically when its preconditions are met (`controller.is_recovery_clean` is true and the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set). Phase 1 must not advance past the dwell task or request authority until the Phase 1 dwell-complete step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` marks the `dwell` task finished.
- If controller state is `READY` and there is no pending/active dwell task, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` should call `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(train, route, exec_state)`. Path creation/retry is allowed from `READY` regardless of cab selection. The behavioral difference between ATO and manual is only departure execution after authority exists: ATO may auto-select/start, while manual mode leaves actual movement to the driver.
- If controller state is `RUNNING_MOVING`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` should not poll for `stop` task arrival; normal stop arrival comes only from dispatcher-committed path completion after the post-physics controller stop check has passed. `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption is also not polled inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`; it is driven by the head-advance callback chain `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` -> `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` -> `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` -> `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`. Each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback compares one reported block advance with the current route task pointer and may consume at most that one task; it must not arm the next planning target or create/extend authority. `waypoint` consumption is head-based route progress only; the later physical release of earlier path resources still waits for tail-vacate callbacks. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` in `RUNNING_MOVING` is therefore limited to best-effort `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` retries toward the currently armed target; it does not re-implement head-entry detection.
- If controller state is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` should run its Phase 1 dwell-complete step (which may call a helper such as `RouteExecutor._check_dwell_complete(train, route, exec_state)`) before any covered-task or planning work; if dwell does not complete on this pass, the run is dwelling/on hold and the rest of Phase 1 returns without new path or departure planning. The dwell-complete helper is owned by Phase 1 only and is never invoked from Phase 3 or from `RouteExecutor.update(...)`.
- If a still-live path's `authority_end_block_id` is still short of the route-owned `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target.requested_destination_block_id` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` for the live planning chain), `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` should keep retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on that same path using the stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` as the armed requested target. If reconciliation would produce a different `walk_position` or different requested target for the live path, Phase 1 must latch cancel-and-replan instead of extending the live path with a different pair. It must not request a brand-new path while `received_path_plan` is still present.
- More generally, dynamic best-effort extension is allowed in any controller state except `OFF`. The common zero-clearance retry case is when ATC is holding the train at the live-authority boundary, but if a still-live path remains short of its requested target while the train is still moving or otherwise stopped under authority, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` may proactively retry `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on later ticks without waiting for a state change, as long as it does not create a second live path.
- `RouteExecutor` does not call any stop-position resolver and does not pass stop centimeters into dispatcher. Route callers pass only the resolved block/direction request contract.
- `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)` is the route executor's normal "make progress toward the current task" entry point. The same function is used for the initial departure attempt and for retries after `PathDispatcher` has cancelled/revoked the previous path once the train has reached a fresh stopped no-authority state. If the cancellation stop escalated into EB, that retry point occurs only after `TrainController.eb.release()` has returned the controller to `READY` according to cab selection; active-path completion still comes back from `PathDispatcher`, not from `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` polling for end-of-path.
- `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly whenever live authority exists for the current route target, regardless of `control_mode`. There is no route-level `departure_mode` policy and no `departure_requested` latch in v1; the ATO-vs-manual behavioral difference is owned inside `TrainController.depart()` itself per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.
- After EB release, the EB-interrupted path has already been cancelled by the EB activation flow. `RouteExecutor` must wait for EB release, then make a fresh `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)` decision from the current route state; it must not try to reuse that cancelled path. Once fresh authority exists, the next eligible Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly per the same rule.

- **Departure-to-waypoint flow**

- The canonical step ordering and gating for one Phase 1 pass live in the **Planning Kernel** subsection (steps `9c4f`–`66aa`). This subsection only states the manual-vs-ATO behaviour at depart-acceptance time and the per-controller-state guards on `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)`; it does not redefine the kernel's step order, eligibility precondition, latch checks, or per-step writes. Where this subsection's prose appears to differ from the kernel, the kernel wins.
- `RouteExecutor` owns task targeting; `PathDispatcher` owns authority acquisition and terminal authority geometry; `TrainController` owns stop/braking behavior from that authority geometry using the controller safety margin. The departure flow is: resolve task block/direction, request or extend dispatcher authority as needed, and call `TrainController.depart()` directly once live authority exists.
- The stop, same-block, and dwell-entry rules in this subsection apply only to tasks with `task_kind == "stop"`. Tasks with `task_kind == "waypoint"` do not call arrival/dwell helpers; they constrain route geometry only.
- A `task_kind == "waypoint"` task uses the same block/direction resolution rules and the same point-to-point create/extend contract as a `stop` task. The difference is route behavior only: when that task's resolved target is reached, `RouteExecutor` advances immediately without dwell and leaves the next target to the next planning tick.
- A longer multi-task route is implemented as repeated point-to-point segments across later planning ticks. A pathfinder call never receives "go through all remaining tasks"; after one task target has been resolved and physical route progress actually consumes that target, a later `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` request is made from the then-current live authority end to the next resolved task. Physical route progress still waits for `waypoint` head entry or `stop` dispatcher completion.
- `RouteExecutor` owns task-chain progression on the route side. `PathDispatcher` grows authority geometry; it does not own task progression. A `waypoint` task is consumed only when the train head first enters that task's resolved block. Live `resource_sequence` coverage may be inspected for diagnostics or extension planning, but coverage alone does not advance `current_waypoint_index` and does not retarget dispatcher authority away from the currently armed task. Tail-based resource release is a separate physical-lifecycle concern owned by tail-vacate observations (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches) and dispatcher-mediated resource release.
- For a train departing from rest under route control, `RouteExecutor` does not try to grow authority past the currently armed task inside the same departure call. Departure uses the current live authority only; later ticks may extend or recreate authority after physical route progress advances and the next task becomes the armed target.
- There is a route-side already-covered skip in v1 only for `task_kind == "waypoint"` targets. If the resolved target block for the current `waypoint` task is already physically covered by the train's current span, `RouteExecutor` treats that task as already missed/skipped, does not request a same-block `create_path(...)`, and immediately advances route planning toward the next task on the current directed route walk. For `task_kind == "stop"`, coverage finishes the task only in the covered-stop case (train stopped, no active path, destination block inside the current span — recorded by Phase 1's covered-task index advance); while the train is rolling or a live path exists, a stop is served only by dispatcher-committed stop completion. Operator/manual reposition uses the ordinary one-off route flow as a `stop` task and follows this same rule.
- `RouteExecutor` does not resolve or compare stop-position targets at all. For any `stop` task that still needs authority, it passes only `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`. Dispatcher stores authority geometry and the controller owns safety-margin braking from that geometry. The route/task resolution step must still provide `requested_destination_direction` as a route-level constraint; dispatcher must not guess it from same-block geometry, and must not treat it as the exact terminal frame by itself.
- `RouteExecutor` uses `Route.acquisition_mode` for both `create_path(...)` and any later `extend_path(...)` retries while it is still pursuing that same planning chain. `AcquisitionMode.best_effort` is therefore a route policy choice, not a one-off branch invented mid-chain.
- For a `waypoint` task, the create/extend chain is still block-based: the current per-attempt resolved target still carries `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` just like a `stop` task. The difference is not the path API shape; the difference is that once the train head enters that task's resolved block, `RouteExecutor` consumes the `waypoint` immediately, advances the route cursor, and leaves the next create/extend step to a later planning tick.
- The canonical create-vs-extend decision for `RouteExecutor` is driven by `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` presence, not by any route-side re-evaluation of whether the current waypoint is reachable on an empty map. For the currently armed waypoint:
  - if `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan is None`, the train has no live authority, so `RouteExecutor` must call `PathDispatcher.create_path(train_id=..., ...)`
  - if `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan is not None`, the train is already on a live path, so `RouteExecutor` must call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(path_id=controller.path_projection.received_path_plan.path_id, ...)` on that same `path_id` rather than synthesizing a second path
  Because `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan` is derived from the dispatcher-owned active path, this rule is well-defined in v1: a cancelled/completed path transitions out of `ACTIVE`, so the next controller read returns no live plan and the next planning tick naturally falls into the `create_path(...)` branch. A pass-through waypoint at which the train happens to be stopped with no live plan is therefore a `create_path(...)` case, not an `extend_path(...)` case.
- When live authority exists for this train (`PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id) is not None`), `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)` must treat that live authority as the current route path for this attempt and must not request a second `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` on top of it. `RouteExecutor` reads the live path id from `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`. If the next route decision wants a subsequent waypoint target on the current directed route walk, `RouteExecutor` calls `PathDispatcher.extend_path(active_path_id, ...)`. If `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` returns `success=True/result_kind=blocked_no_growth`, the existing live path is still a valid prefix and `RouteExecutor` keeps it, stops Phase 1 for that train, and retries later. If `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` returns `success=False` with a structural `failure_kind` such as `no_viable_path` or `invalid_reverse`, dispatcher cannot continue the existing live path from its current authority end toward that requested target; `RouteExecutor` must latch `pending_path_cancel_request` for that same live path, stop Phase 1 for that train, and wait for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel → callback → route deviation cleanup flow. Later eligible Phase 1 passes keep using that latch-only behavior while the incompatible live path still exists. Only after that cancellation flow has completed may a later eligible Phase 1 request a fresh path.
- If `PathDispatcher.create_path(..., acquisition_mode=best_effort)` returns a live path whose current authorized target is still short of the route's current planning target, that is not a failure. It is a degraded but valid live authority: let the controller treat it as normal movement authority toward the current authorized end, and let `RouteExecutor` keep retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on the same `path_id` toward that same pending waypoint, re-resolving its current request contract on each retry, on later ticks in any controller state except `OFF`. A best-effort create request counts as success whenever it creates one new path record with a non-empty block-terminated live `resource_sequence`; a best-effort extend request counts as success when it grows authority or returns `result_kind=blocked_no_growth` because the live path is still a valid prefix but temporarily blocked by live resource ownership.
- Once such a live path exists, `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly when this target is otherwise route-ready. Any immediate zero-clearance hold is controller-owned and comes from lookahead/ATC against that live authority rather than from a special route-side suppression rule.
- On successful path, do not write train-side path cache fields from `RouteExecutor`. `PathDispatcher` owns the authoritative path-progress view; any controller/UI-facing path tiles or path-progress reads should be read as a projection of dispatcher state rather than as a train mirror.
- Do not set destination-block fields directly on `Train` or `TrainController` from `RouteExecutor`; controller authority comes only from the live `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan` projection.
- `PathDispatcher` owns the `Path` record underlying `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan`; `controller.assigned_path_id` should be only a derived read of `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan.path_id`, so `RouteExecutor` does not write that field.
- On successful path creation, authority growth, or `blocked_no_growth` target refresh, `RouteExecutor` should update route-owned state for the immediate live task carried by that path: set/keep `current_route_status.state = RUNNING`, set `direction` from the accepted projection, and write `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)` for this attempt) to the accepted candidate per the **`current_route_status` write contract**. Later planning retries against the same live path call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` again and use the returned `walk_position` plus its stored `resolved_target`. If reconciliation would change the target while authority is live, Phase 1 uses cancel-and-replan rather than rewriting the live target under the existing path. Returned `resource_sequence` geometry alone does not advance `current_waypoint_index`, which remains physical route progress, and v1 does not arm a second waypoint target on the same tick.
- After any successful `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` that returns a grown `resource_sequence`, `RouteExecutor` keeps the same currently armed waypoint/stop because `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` keeps returning the same `walk_position` until physical progress consumes that task. If the grown path is still short of that target, later retries continue toward the stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`. If the grown path now reaches that target, the next call to `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` still returns that same `walk_position` until physical route progress consumes the waypoint through head-entry callback, dispatcher-committed stop completion, or explicit skip. The result payload by itself does not advance physical route progress; `current_waypoint_index` still advances only when the train actually reaches/consumes that waypoint.
- `TrainController.depart()` is the controller's departure-acceptance command once a valid path plan exists. `RouteExecutor` calls it directly on every eligible Phase 1 pass with live authority, regardless of `control_mode`. ATO vs manual effects on acceptance are owned per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.
- When `TrainController.depart()` returns `success == False`, the caller must treat that as a failed departure attempt, not as movement. The controller leaves itself in its current stopped state, records or emits the diagnostic `reason` through `NotificationCenter`, and the next planning tick re-evaluates from current controller state and `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan` presence. If authority was cleared before the call, the normal create-path branch handles recovery on that later tick; if EB or power/state made departure invalid, the existing EB/power/stopped-state rules decide when retry is allowed.

**Concrete departure calls**

- Every `RouteExecutor` access to a train's controller goes through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train.train_id)` (the shared public accessor) rather than a private `_get_controller` helper or any direct dictionary lookup.
- If the resolved target block for a `task_kind == "waypoint"` task is already physically covered by the train's current span, `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)` must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` for that `waypoint` task. Phase 1's covered-task index advance has already advanced past it by recording `task_runs[i].finished_at` (which makes the derived `current_waypoint_index` advance) before the depart helper is reached, so the helper continues planning toward the next task on the current directed route walk. If the resolved target block for a `task_kind == "stop"` task is already physically covered, `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)` must still treat the stop as the current target and may request same-block stop authority; the stop is not finished until the normal post-physics usable-authority stop check and dispatcher completion have run.
- For any `stop` task that still needs authority, `RouteExecutor` does not compute or compare a centimeter-level local stopping target. It passes only the task's `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` into `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`.
- Explicit same-block/zero-movement authorities are route-backed in v1 through the ordinary one-off `Route` flow with no special run-level flag. A direct operator/API local-reposition request constructs a `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route with a single `task_kind == "stop"` task whose `task_location` is a `BlockTarget`, `PlatformTarget`, or `StationTarget` for the destination. Because a single-task reposition route has only one stop, start-mode seeding (see **Run start-mode seeding**) resolves to that task and `Route.tasks[0]` is the first executable target. `RouteTask.task_direction` for the destination follows the same task-authoring rule as any other route; the operator/API construction step is responsible for resolving "current cab/running direction" into a concrete `"forward"` / `"reverse"` value before the route is stored, because dispatcher never receives `"either"`. If that direction cannot be derived at construction time, the operator/API request fails before any route is stored. The run is executed through the ordinary Phase 1 / `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` flow. If the destination stop block is already covered when planning runs, the run still targets that stop and uses same-block stop authority or immediate usable-authority stop completion under the ordinary stop predicate; it does not skip the destination merely because the block is covered. These authorities carry no separate target-position field; the controller uses the standard safety-margin stop logic.
- When the current attempt uses `AcquisitionMode.best_effort`, compare the live path's current authorized target against the live path's stored requested target each tick (`Path.authority_end_block_id` versus `Path.requested_destination_block_id`). If the live path is still short of that requested target, keep calling `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on that same `path_id` using the newly re-resolved request contract for the same pending waypoint; do not synthesize a new path.
- If `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)` sees current live authority already present on the controller (`TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan is not None`), do not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` again. First perform any eligible `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` retry for the currently armed target; after that create/extend decision is complete for this Phase 1 pass, call `TrainController.depart()` directly for that current live authority. ATO vs manual effects are owned per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.
- `RouteExecutor` does not write `current_path`, `path_index`, `train.assigned_path_id`, `controller.ato_path_id`, or any destination field. `PathDispatcher` owns path-progress; `controller.assigned_path_id` is derived from the live `received_path_plan` projection.
- Route-driven movement requires a dispatcher path plan and reaches departure acceptance only through a direct `TrainController.depart()` call by `RouteExecutor`. The controller infers "no path authority" from an empty `received_path_plan`.
- If initial path creation fails before departure in `all_or_nothing` mode, `RouteExecutor` should record that route/path error on `exec_state.last_error` as one `ErrorRecord(text=..., time=current_time)` and keep the train in `READY`; the train never had authority, so no special controller waiting command is needed.
- In `best_effort` mode, only "no authority at all could be created" is a creation failure. A truncated but live path is not a failure path; the train may run under that current authority and later wait at the current authority boundary while `RouteExecutor` keeps retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on the same `path_id`.
- On cancel/revoke, `PathDispatcher` transitions `Path.status` to `CANCELLED` (the controller's `received_path_plan` property then returns `None` on its next read), the controller follows the no-authority EB protocol defined in step `826a`, and recovery follows the canonical recovery table. Phase 1 is gated by `controller.is_recovery_clean`; it never reads the cancellation `initiator`. If the next planning attempt fails, fall through to the ordinary pre-departure route/path error path — no recovery-only state machine.

**Arrival detection**

- The post-physics controller stop check owns stop-complete observation and `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` owns the completion commit; dispatcher-committed completion is the authoritative arrival trigger for a `stop` task. There is no separate platform-position arrival gate.
- `RouteExecutor._enter_task_arrival(exec_state, walk_position)` is the route-owned arrival-recording helper. `walk_position` is the walk-position in `task_runs` of the `stop` walk-position that just completed. The helper samples `time.time()` at the write site (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**) and sets `exec_state.task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` to that sampled value (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position` with that write, and the next task's derived `started_at` automatically reflects this `finished_at`). It does not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`; that transition happens in the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-entry step.
- `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` is the controller-side dwell-entry transition called by `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (Phase 1). The controller does not detect dwell entry on its own; it never reads `RouteRun`. The transition is valid only from the dispatcher-completed `READY` state (`READY`). The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at`, which `_enter_task_arrival(...)` recorded before this controller call runs.
- On the next eligible tick after `_enter_task_arrival(...)` records the stop's `finished_at`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` performs the dwell-entry step: when `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, the route's next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`, and the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`. The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of that predecessor `finished_at`; the dwell-entry step does not need to write it. The train does not plan a new target while dwell is pending or active.
- The dwell-complete check is a Phase 1 step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`, invoked before covered-task index advance. It runs only while controller state is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. It calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to live-resolve `walk_position` (the walk-position of the next non-finished task on the directed walk), then calls `RouteTask.should_depart(current_time_sec, exec_state.task_runs[walk_position].started_at)`, where both timestamps are system-time Unix seconds.
- Stop arrival sets `finished_at` for that stop directly in `_enter_task_arrival(...)`, which makes the derived `current_waypoint_index` advance. The stop task is independent of its neighbors: a following `dwell` task (when present) is a separate row with its own `started_at` / `finished_at` lifecycle; a `stop` with no following `dwell` simply has no dwell-entry / dwell-complete steps to run, and the next eligible Phase 1 either plans the next task or calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` for terminal completion.
- A route is complete only after the terminal task sequence (stop + any following dwell) has finished. Route completion handling never fires before the terminal dwell (if any) exits.
- If dwell/timing says depart, the Phase 1 dwell-complete step sets `exec_state.task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = current_time` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position` with that write, and the next task's derived `started_at` automatically reflects this `finished_at`), sets `exec_state.direction` from the route walk, and calls `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train.train_id).finish_dwell()`. The same Phase 1 then continues into target resolution for the next task via a fresh `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call.
- If no task remains after the completed stop+dwell pair, that Phase 1 dwell-complete step calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside `tick_train_phase1(...)` and returns for that tick. `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` performs the route-completion teardown (set `current_route_status.state = FINISHED`, possibly trigger `route.deactivate()` for one-off routes with no remaining assigned trains) per the **`current_route_status` write contract**. `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` automatically returns `None`. Dwell exit (`finish_dwell()`) has already run before this call; the `RouteRun` is preserved with `state = FINISHED` indefinitely as a historical record.
- `route.deactivate()` is the admin-driven entry point for switching off a shared `Route` while one or more assigned `RouteRun`s have not yet finished. It sets `Route.is_active = False` and walks every assigned train, writing `pending_route_deactivate` on each train's controller per the inclusive-latch rule defined in the **Latch Table** preamble. `route.deactivate()` is idempotent; calling it on an already-inactive route with no assigned trains is a no-op. The actual per-train teardown happens in each train's later Phase 3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer, which calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` after the inclusive cancel has committed; the helper writes `state = DEACTIVATED` and (when applicable) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. There is no separate `RouteRun.deactivate()` method, and `route.deactivate()` does not delete any `RouteRun` — the runs are kept as historical `DEACTIVATED` records.
- `finish_dwell()` is a **dwell-exit state-transition command only**, named `finish_dwell` rather than `depart_from_station` because it does not start movement. It transitions the controller out of `RUNNING_AT_STATION` to the **`READY` state**. The normal caller is `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-complete step. `RouteExecutor` is still responsible for deciding whether more route work remains, clearing route assignment when complete, and removing the execution-state entry; those are route-level bookkeeping, not controller state.
  `TrainController.finish_dwell()` must not touch `received_path_plan` (the property is derived from the dispatcher-owned active path; authority for the completed target already transitioned out of `ACTIVE` in `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` before dwell began), must not call `TrainController.depart()` / `TrainController.ato.go()`, and must not initiate physics movement. The next target's route-side restart happens in the same Phase 1 (after dwell exit) or on a later tick through `RouteExecutor._try_depart_to_waypoint(...)` -> `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` and then `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly once live authority exists. ATO vs manual effects are owned per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.

**Dispatcher completion handling**

**route path-event callback schema**

- This is the single canonical callback contract for `PathDispatcher -> RouteExecutor` path-terminal notifications.
- The canonical callback signatures are exactly:
  - `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(train_id, path_id, completion_target)`
  - `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, reason=None)`
  Parameters are listed positionally above; callers may pass them by name. No other parameter is accepted in v1. Neither callback carries a timestamp parameter — the callback writer samples `time.time()` at the write site for any `task_runs[i].finished_at`, `task_runs[i].started_at` (when first-written here), or `last_error.time` it records, per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**. `completion_target` is a structured payload carrying `authority_end_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`.
- The callback payloads are exactly:
  - completion context: `train_id`, `path_id`, `completion_target` (carrying `authority_end_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`)
  - cancellation context: `train_id`, `path_id`, `reason`
  These callbacks are invoked synchronously by `PathDispatcher` immediately after committing the path-status transition, so the callback's `path_id` always identifies the just-terminated path. There is no path-id correlation step.
- In that completion context:
  - `completion_target.requested_destination_direction` is the active path's stored block-relative requested target direction (`"forward"` or `"reverse"`) at completion time
  - the callback does not carry a route-owned target block. `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` resolves the matching route task by iterating the remaining task indexes in directed-walk order from the current route progress and selecting the first non-finished `task_kind == "stop"` task whose stored `resolved_target.requested_destination_block_id` equals `completion_target.authority_end_block_id` and whose stored `resolved_target.requested_destination_direction` equals `completion_target.requested_destination_direction`. For a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task whose live recompute could pick a different platform under the canonical helper ordering, the comparison is anchored by `task_runs[i].resolved_target` (the most recent platform actually targeted for this stop), not by a fresh `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call: the train physically completed at the platform whose block was stored, and that stored value is the authoritative match. The dispatcher payload carries only path-completion facts.
- In that cancellation context:
  - `reason` is optional plain-text diagnostic context only and must not be used for branching. UI/log consumers that need the originating kind subscribe to the `path_cancellation` event on `NotificationCenter` (its `initiator` field carries the writer-supplied label). v1 route-side recovery does not branch on the originating kind. EB recovery timing is gated by controller state (Phase 1 cannot run until `eb.release()` has returned the controller to `READY`); power-off teardown is driven by `pending_power_off` and the controller transitioning to `OFF`; every other case shares the same recovery shell.
- The single v1 recovery table for cancellation / mismatch cases is:

  | Case | Detection point | Immediate effect | Next eligible Phase 1 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | any cancellation (movement-derived deviation, or explicit operator cancel / revoke / EB) (`7fa0`) | `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` | preserve only confirmed physical progress, set `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` (the derived `current_route_status.display_state == "DEVIATED"` projection follows automatically) | rerun covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from current physical position once `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true; the next task's behavior follows normal task-kind rules |
  | live-path target re-resolution invalidates a live path — Phase 1's `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` would yield a different `walk_position` / requested target than the live path holds (`162a`) | Phase 1 latch + Phase 3 cancel / replan flow | let the cancel callback set `path_cancelled` per the cancellation row of the **`current_route_status` write contract** | rerun ordinary planning under the same `route_id` (the `RouteRun` is kept, never deactivated); later Phase 1 calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh |
  | EB interruption (`eddc`) | EB cancellation plus release flow | clear live path | the `RUNNING_MOVING` → stop → `eb.release()` chain naturally returns the controller to `READY`, at which point Phase 1 replans normally |
  | power off (`dc91`) | power-off cleanup flow | cancel live path if any, remove `RouteRun`, perform OFF cleanup | the `RouteRun` no longer exists and the controller is `OFF`; Phase 1 has nothing to do |

- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` should follow the **Active-path completion procedure** when the post-physics controller stop check has already passed and the active path requested-target check succeeds: release not-yet-entered block reservations, release not-yet-entered switch/flank locks, transition the `Path` to `COMPLETED` (which by definition makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return `None` for this train and makes the controller's `received_path_plan` property return `None`), call `TrainController.on_path_complete()`, and then call `SimulationController.handle_path_completion(train_id, path_id, completion_target)` where `completion_target` carries `authority_end_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` (the `SimulationController` surface delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` per the canonical route-event delivery contract in the SimulationController section).
- `handle_path_completion()` should treat dispatcher completion as authoritative and should not perform a second exact-stop or live-controller-state check. The callback is invoked synchronously by `PathDispatcher` immediately after dispatcher transitions `Path.status` to `COMPLETED` and clears authority, so the callback's `path_id` always identifies the just-terminated path. There is no path-id correlation step.
- Every live authority path has a matching `RouteRun` in v1 (including one-off operator routes). The callback can rely on `RouteRun` existing.
- The callback does not write `Train` fields — authority residue lives on dispatcher records and controller-facing projections.
- `PathDispatcher` clears controller authority by transitioning `Path.status` to `COMPLETED` before `controller.on_path_complete()` and before calling `handle_path_completion()`. The controller's `received_path_plan` property then returns `None` on its next read, and the derived reads `controller.assigned_path_id`, `controller.ato_path_id`, and `controller.ato_destination_block_id` disappear automatically.
- `TrainController.on_path_complete()` owns the post-path stop transition and lands in the **`READY` state**; `handle_path_completion()` does not call any `transition_to_standby()`-style helper.
- Controller destination-block/path release should remain dispatcher-owned: dispatcher transitions `Path.status` (the controller's `received_path_plan` property automatically reads the new state); `TrainController.on_path_complete()` reacts to that completion while `RouteExecutor` records the completed stop. `RouteExecutor` remains responsible for entering dwell (`RUNNING_AT_STATION`) on the next eligible Phase 1 and for marking the dwell task finished in the same Phase 1 dwell-complete step.
- `handle_path_completion()` must not need to query live train/controller path state after dispatcher release. It should use the completion context passed in by `PathDispatcher` plus route-executor-owned state only.
- `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` must not read `PathDispatcher.paths_by_id[path_id]` or any other dispatcher path record. It uses only the completion context and `exec_state.task_runs`.
- Iterate the remaining task indexes in directed-walk order from current route progress and pick the first non-finished `task_kind == "stop"` task whose stored `task_runs[i].resolved_target.requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` match the callback's `authority_end_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`. The callback does NOT call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` — `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the authoritative record of which platform/block this stop targeted, and a station task that previously resolved to platform B must not silently match against a different platform A on completion. On a match, call `RouteExecutor._enter_task_arrival(exec_state, walk_position=walk_position)`, which records `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, the canonical advance helper (the helper samples `time.time()` at the write site per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). The next eligible Phase 1 dwell-entry step handles the transition into `RUNNING_AT_STATION` when the route's next task is `dwell`. The callback always matches a `stop` task: by construction of the create/extend acquisition rules (the waypoint-target exclusion in **`create_path()` flow** and the matching rule in **Path extension**), the live authority's terminal block is never a `task_kind == "waypoint"` target, so dispatcher path completion cannot land on a waypoint task. If no non-finished `stop` task in the remaining directed walk has a matching `resolved_target`, that is a contract violation; raise a fatal diagnostic through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` rather than silently advancing or retrying.
- `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, reason=None)` is the cancellation-side counterpart. The lower-to-upper ordering is canonical: `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` finishes its own path/resource/controller mutation first, and only after that commit may the canonical cancellation context be handed upward to this route callback. Movement-driven cancellations detected after physics are initiated and committed in Phase 3 while the controller-owned progress mailbox is being drained. Explicit authority cancellations and EB-triggered cancellations are latched earlier through `pending_path_cancel_request`; the same train's later Phase 3 consume pass calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` only after the normal local-progress and completion opportunity has already been processed. Explicit route abort is a separate `pending_route_abort` latch consumed after authority cancellation. Phase 1 live-path target re-resolution that invalidates a live path uses the latch-cancel-and-replan workflow (revoke, keep the same `RouteRun`) defined in the recovery table above; a structural `Route.tasks` edit instead tears the run down per the **Shared-route edit teardown**. In all cases, the callback is bookkeeping only: it must not choose a fresh target or create fresh authority. The callback writes: record route/path error context on `exec_state.last_error` as one `ErrorRecord(text=..., time=...)` when relevant, and set `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` (the derived `current_route_status.display_state == "DEVIATED"` projection follows automatically). UI/log consumers that need the originating kind read it from the canonical `path_cancellation` event on `NotificationCenter` (defined in the **Cancellation** subsection). When the train later reaches the stopped recovery state, the same train's later Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step consumes `path_cancelled`. A later Phase 1 planning attempt reruns ordinary covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from the train's current physical position and creates a brand-new path. The callback must NOT advance `current_waypoint_index`, synthesize arrival, choose the next task, or create authority while the train is still braking.
- Every cancellation source (movement-derived deviation, operator cancel, revoke, EB, power-off) shares the same `handle_path_cancellation(...)` body. There is no per-source branch inside the callback. The recovery timing differences observed at the system level emerge from runtime gates that exist independently of any enum:
  - the Phase 1 eligibility precondition only allows replanning when the controller is in `READY`, which naturally defers EB recovery until `eb.release()` has run;
  - the power-off Phase 3 cleanup separately calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`, which flips the run to its historical `DEACTIVATED` row so no later Phase 1 has anything to replan; `handle_path_cancellation(...)` still records the same diagnostic state, but the run is unassigned moments later by the same Phase 3 cleanup.
  Planning and routing logic rely on these runtime gates (controller state, latches, presence of `RouteRun`, current span coverage, and task-kind rules).
- Live-path target re-resolution uses this same temporary-status-clear model only after the current live path has already left `ACTIVE`. When Phase 1's live recompute (`_find_next_task_target(train_id)`) would yield a different `walk_position` or requested target than the live path holds, the current live path must be treated as potentially stale because the system cannot prove the already-acquired authority is still correct. Phase 1 therefore latches `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "revoked"` (system/non-operator-driven), stops planning for that train, and waits for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel/callback/route-deviation-cleanup flow. This keeps the same `RouteRun`; it is NOT a structural `Route.tasks` edit, which always tears the run down to `DEACTIVATED` via `set_route_deactivate(...)` per the **Shared-route edit teardown**. Operator-driven retargeting and explicit route swap take a different path entirely (abort current run + create new run, see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**); the inclusive cancel for that workflow uses `initiator = "operator_cancelled"` per the **Latch Table** preamble. Only after the live path is no longer active may `RouteExecutor` clear or rewrite the affected route-owned temporary target state and restart ordinary planning from the train's current position under the reconciled route.
- Completion handling may observe a train still physically occupying the terminal block or switch footprint. That is not a contradiction: `RouteExecutor` treats dispatcher completion as authoritative arrival, while physical resource release still waits for the later tail-vacate callbacks.

## Train Entity

**Train ownership**

- `Train` is the persistent/configuration entity. Persistent/configuration fields kept on `Train` in v1 are identity, consist (including length), `max_speed`, and other non-live train-configuration data already on the entity. Route assignment is not among them: consumers ask `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)`, which prefers the live `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id).route_id` (the derived RUNNING-`RouteRun` read, not a stored dict — see **Route-run state**) and falls back to scanning `Route.assigned_train_ids` in the route registry. The active route is structurally encoded by the `RouteRun` registry, so no per-train sync is needed.
- All live runtime state lives on `TrainController`: `current_span` (live physical span, including end locators and ordered occupied tiles), `current_speed_kmh`, operational `state`, power/cab, ATO/path projections, and operational diagnostics. `TrainPhysics` computes movement and sends `span_update` deltas to the controller; the controller applies those updates to its own storage. Live target speed and speed-limit reason are reads from controller lookahead at distance 0; there is no `manual_target_speed`, `train.state`, `train.assigned_path_id`, `train.ato_destination_block_id`, `train.current_path`, `train.path_index`, or `train.reserved_blocks` field.
- Initial placement of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` is owned by `SimulationController.add_train(...)`. It constructs the train, builds its initial physical span from placement input (reusing the existing repo placement-to-position derivation that produces precise `end_a` / `end_b` locators plus `occupied_tiles`), obtains the controller, and writes the span directly into `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before any simulation tick runs. `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` does NOT seed the span — it only ensures the controller object exists and is registered.
- Persistence-loading code also calls `add_train(...)` with the saved placement payload for each restored train; there is no parallel `seed_train_span(...)` helper.
- After initial placement, `TrainPhysics` is the only normal producer of movement `span_update` values and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` is the only normal writer for `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`. Live movement updates never write back to `Train`.


## TrainController

`TrainController` is a multi-module train computer that mirrors a real-world cab and on-board control system. It is one entity per train, but internally it is composed of 10 discrete sub-modules organized into three layers — input, compute, output. Code that touches one sub-module should not need to load the others.

**Sub-modules and where to find them in this chapter.**

*Input layer — data feeds into the controller:*

- **Operator command surface** — queue and drain order plus the setter / queue methods themselves: `controller.commands.set_cab(...)`, `controller.commands.set_mode(...)`, `controller.commands.set_manual_mode_type(...)`, `controller.commands.set_handle(...)`, `controller.commands.set_manual_power_percent(...)`, `controller.commands.power_on_cmd()`, `controller.commands.power_off_cmd()`, `controller.commands.enqueue_command(...)`, plus `controller.eb.activate()` and `controller.eb.release()`. Lives in `train_controller/commands.py` (class `OperatorCommands`); the operator-command queue and drain order are stored on this sub-module and accessed externally as `controller.commands.command_inbox` and `controller.commands.drain_order`. The cab and mode selections it sets live on the top-level controller (`controller.cab`, `controller.control_mode`) per the **Controller retained field schema**; this sub-module does not store its own copy. The setter / queue methods orchestrate writes across multiple sub-modules (eb, ato, manual, route_hooks, physics_sync, ops) through the controller back-reference. See **Power/cab/mode command contracts** and **Driver/operator inputs** below.
- **Path plan projection** — live read-only projection of the dispatcher-owned `Path` into `received_path_plan`. Lives in `train_controller/path_projection.py` (class `PathProjection`). No owned fields — every read recomputes from `path_dispatcher.get_active_path(train_id)` (or other live sources) and auto-flips to `None` when the dispatcher transitions the path away from `ACTIVE`. Accessed externally as `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan`, `controller.path_projection.assigned_path_id`, `controller.path_projection.ato_path_id`, `controller.path_projection.ato_destination_block_id`, `controller.path_projection.path_backed_run_active`, and `controller.path_projection.get_live_path_data()`. See **received_path_plan schema**, **Dispatcher/route-executor inputs**, and **route/dispatcher command contracts** below.
- **Physics sync** — `current_span` and `current_speed_kmh` storage; `on_physics_updated()` handler; `ControllerProgressReport` generation. Lives in `train_controller/physics_sync.py` (class `PhysicsSync`); accessed externally as `controller.physics_sync.current_span`, `controller.physics_sync.current_speed_kmh`, and `controller.physics_sync.queued_progress_reports` (the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox: `on_physics_updated(...)` appends `ControllerProgressReport`s to it in Phase 2, and `SimulationController` drains and clears it in Phase 3 of the same tick — a within-tick transient, never retained across ticks). The orchestrator `controller.on_physics_updated(...)` stays flat on the façade because `TrainPhysics` invokes it directly and it drives cross-sub-module work (reactive state transitions, progress-report build). See **TrainSpan schema** and **Read accessors** below.
- **Route hooks** — external entry points called by other components: `enter_station_dwell()`, `finish_dwell()` (from `RouteExecutor`); `on_path_complete()` (from `PathDispatcher`). Lives in `train_controller/route_hooks.py` (class `RouteHooks`); owns the four Phase-3 retained latches bundled under `PhaseLatches`. Accessed externally as `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches` (and the `set_*` writer helpers on that aggregate per the **Latch table**). The orchestrator entry-point methods (`enter_station_dwell`, `finish_dwell`, `on_path_complete`) stay flat on the façade because they are direct external callbacks; they delegate to this sub-module when latch writes are needed. See **route/dispatcher command contracts** and **Completion ownership and timing** below.

*Compute layer — the on-board computers:*

- **Operational state machine** — `state` field and transitions (`OFF` / `READY` / `RUNNING_MOVING` / `RUNNING_AT_STATION`). Lives in `train_controller/ops.py` (class `OperationalStateMachine`); accessed externally as `controller.ops.state`, `controller.ops.handle_position`, and `controller.ops.handle_reason` (diagnostic). State-transition methods, the canonical stopped predicate `is_stopped()`, and related predicates (`is_recovery_clean`, `active_conditions`, `power_on`) stay on the façade because they orchestrate writes across multiple sub-modules. See **Controller state machine**, **Controller tick order**, and the **Allowed `(state, cab)` pairs** table.
- **ATC Computer** — live `lookahead` computation, ATC speed-limit application, overspeed-to-EB escalation. Lives in `train_controller/atc.py` (class `ATCComputer`); accessed externally as `controller.atc.safety_margin_cm` (tunable), `controller.atc.signal_lookahead_blocks` (tunable scan depth), plus the recompute helpers `controller.atc.clearance_ahead()`, `controller.atc.speed_limit_now()`, `controller.atc.speed_limit_with_code()`, `controller.atc.is_caution_ahead()`, `controller.atc.is_approaching_yellow_signal()`, `controller.atc.is_signal_red_for_block(block_id)`, and `controller.atc.effective_safety_margin()`. The lookahead build itself is owned by `LookaheadBuilder` in `lookahead_builder.py`. See **Lookahead is a pure derived value**, **canonical lookahead payload**, **ATC speed limit**, and **Speed curves** below.
- **ATO Computer** — handle-selection / curve-tracking / braking-notch logic, `ato.running` lifecycle, and depart-in-ATO acceptance. Lives in `train_controller/ato.py` (class `ATOComputer`); accessed externally as `controller.ato.running` (run-active flag), `controller.ato.info` (destination snapshot), `controller.ato.debug` (per-tick debug payload), `controller.ato.go()` / `controller.ato.stop()` (run lifecycle), `controller.ato.compute_handle(delta_seconds)` (per-tick handle selection), `controller.ato.compute_info()`, `controller.ato.sample_curve_at(curve, distance_cm, key)`, `controller.ato.simulate_forward(speed, handle, distance_cm, dt)`, `controller.ato.calculate_power_notch(...)`, and `controller.ato.calculate_braking_notch_for_speed(...)`. The module-level `rate_limit_handle(...)` helper lives in the same file. See **`ato.running` meaning and lifecycle** and **ATO handle calculation** below.
- **EB Workflow** — `eb.active`, EB activation effects, release contract, dwell exception. Lives in `train_controller/eb.py`; accessed as `controller.eb.active` (latch), `controller.eb.activate()` (operator-issued EB), and `controller.eb.release()`. The canonical home is the **EB workflow state machine** table below in this section; controller-side tick handling appears in **Controller tick order** below.
- **Manual driving** — `simulation` and `power_percent` submode behavior; `handle_position`, `manual.manual_power_percent`. Lives in `train_controller/manual.py` (class `ManualDriving`); accessed externally as `controller.manual.manual_mode_type` and `controller.manual.manual_power_percent`. The operator-facing setters `set_manual_mode_type(...)` / `set_manual_power_percent(...)` live on the operator command surface and write through to these fields. See **Manual mode and movement-authority boundary** below.

*Output layer — what the computer writes:*

- **Notification emission** — `command_result` per drained command and diagnostic notifications via `NotificationCenter`, which is the only runtime notification owner and the only sink/delivery surface for frontend/API consumers (see the **Runtime notifications** subsection in the Core Runtime Loop section). The per-controller buffer in `train_controller/notifications.py` is internal-only storage: controller-internal writers call `self.notifications.emit(msg)`, and that buffer feeds `NotificationCenter`. The frontend never reads `controller.notifications` directly; there is no per-train notification endpoint, and read/ack/retention mechanics belong to `NotificationCenter`.

The Phase-3 latches owned by `TrainController` live nested under `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches: PhaseLatches` (fields: `path_cancel_request`, `route_abort`, `power_off`, `route_deactivate`) and are documented in the **Latch table** in the Core Runtime Loop section. `TrainController` owns the in-memory storage; the Latch Table is the single canonical home for writers, helpers, consumers, gating predicates, and lifecycle.

**Module boundaries.** These rules consolidate the "only X writes Y" constraints that apply across the chapter:

1. The Operational state machine is the only writer of `state`.
2. The EB Workflow is the only writer of `eb.active`.
3. The ATC Computer is the only computation site for `lookahead`.
4. Phase-3 latches are written only through the writer-helpers listed in the **Latch table** preamble; no sub-module assigns latch fields directly.
5. The Manual driving sub-module is the only writer of `handle_position` and `manual.manual_power_percent`. The EB Workflow may also write `handle_position = EB` as part of the canonical EB activation effects; that exception is named in the EB workflow state machine.
6. The Operator command surface is the only entry point for external operator commands. No external caller invokes ATC, ATO, EB, or manual driving sub-modules directly.

**Data flow per tick.**

- *Input:* the Operator command surface drains queued commands at the start of `TrainController.tick(...)`; the Path plan projection refreshes on every read of `received_path_plan`; Physics sync absorbs `PhysicsSpanUpdate` from `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`; Route hooks fire when `RouteExecutor` or `PathDispatcher` calls them.
- *Compute:* the Operational state machine evaluates reactive transitions; the ATC Computer computes `lookahead`; the ATO Computer (in ATO mode) or Manual driving (in manual mode) produces the active command; the EB Workflow can override the result at any step.
- *Output:* Notification emission flows to `NotificationCenter`; Phase-3 latches are written when accepted commands or in-tick escalations need deferred dispatcher cancellation; controller-state writes land on the canonical retained fields.

**Controller state**

- `TrainController` owns live power/cab state: `state` (canonical operational state, including `OFF`), active cab selection, and the live direction selection implied by that cab. The convenience read `power_on` is derived from `state != "OFF"`; it has no separate writer. These are not re-synced from `Train` on every tick.
- `TrainController` owns the train's live physical span and speed. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` computes movement and emits `PhysicsSpanUpdate`; `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` is the normal writer that applies the new span/speed to controller-owned storage. `Train` itself does not store live position/span.
- `manual.manual_mode_type` holds the manual submode: `simulation` or `power_percent`.
- `TrainController.travel_direction` is a **read-only derived value** computed each time it is read from `(self.cab, self.current_span)`. There is no stored field and there are no writers. The mapping rule: the cab's physical end (`end_a` or `end_b` per `TrainSpan`) is the head, and `travel_direction` is the network-frame direction pointing from the opposite physical end through the cab end and onward into the next span resource ahead. When no next span resource exists ahead of the cab end (e.g. the cab end is at a track-end / dead-end / disabled-resource boundary, or the consist sits on a single tile with no live forward connectivity), `travel_direction` is the network-frame direction outward through the cab edge of the cab end's tile. This rule depends only on consist-and-cab geometry, not on `received_path_plan`. When `self.cab is None`, the value is `None`. Successful writes to `cab` (`TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`, `TrainController.depart()` auto-cab-selection) only write `cab`; they do not "atomically update" `travel_direction` because there is no field to update — the next read of `travel_direction` recomputes from the new cab.
- `manual.manual_power_percent` is the `power_percent`-mode manual control input: an integer `0..100` voltage-style command rather than a signed traction/brake command.
- `phase_latches.power_off: bool` is the stopped-only shutdown latch — row `f8d5` in the **Latch table**, which owns its writer, consumer, and gating rules. It is the canonical handoff that moves power-off from command acceptance into the standard per-train phase workflow.
- `TrainController` owns the per-train Phase-3 retained latch / flag aggregate in v1. The aggregate is `phase_latches: PhaseLatches`, with the four fields `path_cancel_request`, `route_abort`, `power_off`, and `route_deactivate`. `ControllerProgressReport` values are not retained controller fields; Phase 2 appends them to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), which Phase 3 drains and clears before the train transaction ends. The retained-field schema below lists `phase_latches` as a single field; the four nested fields are documented inside the **Latch table**. Dwell entry is NOT a flag — it is a state-machine transition driven by route state and controller state inside `TrainController.tick(...)`.
- Define the `READY` trigger explicitly: `READY` is the controller's state when the train is powered on and stopped, regardless of cab selection. A controller enters `READY` from `OFF` when power is turned on. Selecting or deselecting a cab while stopped keeps the controller in `READY`. Route authority may still be assigned while a cab is unselected, and `RouteExecutor` must not treat the absence of a cab as a blocker for `create_path(...)`. `TrainController.depart()` accepts authority per its canonical contract; auto-cab selection on success is part of that contract and is not restated here. Manual input may move the train even before `depart()` has been accepted (controller still in `READY`), but only when `received_path_plan is not None` so the canonical lookahead in step `22a9` exposes a path-bound ATC limit at distance 0; without a live path the no-authority empty payload sets `atc_limit = 0` at distance 0 and ATC holds the train at rest until a route assigns authority. Pre-depart manual movement cannot satisfy path stop-completion until `controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`. That state is reached by EITHER a successful `TrainController.depart()` acceptance OR the reactive speed-based `READY → RUNNING_MOVING` transition inside `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` (see the **Reactive speed-based state evaluation** bullets and the `RUNNING_MOVING` state row); both are equally valid. Such pre-depart manual movement may still produce head-entry route progress such as `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption; only stop-completion is gated on `state == RUNNING_MOVING`.- Manual driving is `control_mode == manual`; there is no separate `MANUAL` operational state.
- `assigned_path_id: Optional[str]` is a computed read-only property derived from `received_path_plan.path_id`; it is the controller-facing runtime read and is not stored or written independently.
- `received_path_plan` is the controller's read-only live movement-authority projection over the active dispatcher-owned path.
- `received_path_plan` is not stored controller state and has no writer. `PathDispatcher` owns the underlying `Path`; `TrainController` reads the projection and must not mutate path ownership or lifecycle.
- `TrainController` is also the canonical query and storage home for the train's current occupied span. The live span is produced by `TrainPhysics` and written into `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`; block/switch resources still carry their own physical `occupied_by` ownership, but tile-level ends and ordered occupied tiles live on the controller span rather than on `Train`. `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` ask the controller for one cab-independent physical span read instead of deriving it independently from `Train`. That span read must expose the ordered occupied span in physical order (`occupied_blocks` and `occupied_tiles`) plus the two explicit physical end locators needed for later ordering comparisons. The occupancy list is the block-level ownership view; the end locators are the tile-level precise geometry view. It does not depend on a currently selected cab.

**Controller retained field schema**

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `state` | controller state enum | Canonical operational state (`OFF`, `READY`, `RUNNING_MOVING`, `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) |
| `power_on` | `bool` (read-only derived: `state != "OFF"`) | Convenience read for "is the controller powered on?" Computed each access from `state`; not a stored field; no writers. Power transitions are owned by the canonical `state` machine, not by a parallel boolean. |
| `cab` | `Optional[str]` | Active cab selection (`"A"`, `"B"`, or `None`) |
| `control_mode` | `"ato" \| "manual"` | High-level driving mode selector |
| `manual.manual_mode_type` | `"simulation" \| "power_percent"` | Manual-mode submode selector. Construction default is `simulation`. Mutated only through `TrainController.commands.set_manual_mode_type(...)`. |
| `travel_direction` | `Optional[str]` (read-only property) | Read-only derived value computed each read from `(self.cab, self.current_span)`. Not a stored field; no writers. See the `travel_direction` derivation rule above. |
| `handle_position` | controller handle enum/value | Active handle command for ATO and manual `simulation` mode |
| `manual.manual_power_percent` | `int` | Active manual driver command for manual `power_percent` mode (`0..100`) |
| `eb.active` | `bool` | Emergency-brake latch |
| `ato.running` | `bool` | Whether an ATO run is presently active (the train has been commanded to run under ATO and has not yet been halted, dwelled, EB'd, or otherwise stopped). This is a run-active flag, not a mode flag; the mode flag is `control_mode`. |
| `phase_latches` | `PhaseLatches` | Aggregate of the four Phase-3 latches the controller owns: `path_cancel_request: Optional[PendingPathCancelRequest]`, `route_abort: bool`, `power_off: bool`, `route_deactivate: bool`. Writers go through `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_*(...)`; consumers in Phase 3 call `consume_*()`. The full lifecycle for each field — gating predicates, multi-tick survival, fan-out — lives in the **Latch table** in the Core Runtime Loop section. |
| `received_path_plan` | `Optional[ReceivedPathPlan]` (read-only property) | Live read of the controller's active movement authority. Computed each time it is read by projecting `self.simulation_controller.path_dispatcher.get_active_path(self.train_id)` into the canonical **received_path_plan schema**. Not a stored field; there is no `receive_path_plan(...)` setter. The projection exposes only controller-needed authority fields such as `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, and `authority_end_block_id`; dispatcher-only requested-target and lifecycle fields remain on `Path`. `None` when the train has no `ACTIVE` path. |
| `physics_sync.current_span` | `TrainSpan` | Controller-owned live physical span, including ordered occupied resources/blocks/tiles and both physical end locators. Lives on the `PhysicsSync` sub-module. |
| `physics_sync.current_speed_kmh` | `float` | Controller-owned live physical speed written by `TrainPhysics`. Lives on the `PhysicsSync` sub-module. |
| `lookahead` | canonical lookahead payload (read-only property) | Computed each time it is read from `(current_span, cab, received_path_plan, network_state, signals, current_speed_kmh, eb.active)`. Pure derived value; not a stored field; no cache; no invalidation rules. See **canonical lookahead payload** below for the schema returned by each read. |

- The table above is the canonical retained controller field set for v1. If a field is not in this table or in a later canonical payload schema, it should not remain as an independently written live controller field.

- **`ControllerCondition` set** (canonical home; referenced elsewhere by name). `ControllerCondition` is the canonical enum of currently-active controller-state conditions used to express recovery and create-eligibility predicates. v1 values:

  - `HAS_AUTHORITY` — `received_path_plan is not None`
  - `EB_LATCHED` — `eb.active == True`
  - `POWER_OFF_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.power_off == True`
  - `PATH_CANCEL_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.path_cancel_request is not None`
  - `ROUTE_ABORT_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.route_abort == True`
  - `ROUTE_DEACTIVATE_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.route_deactivate == True`

  `TrainController.active_conditions` is a derived `FrozenSet[ControllerCondition]` computed each read from the controller's stored fields per the mapping above. Not a stored field; no writers.

  `TrainController.is_recovery_clean` (derived `bool`): `state == READY` AND `active_conditions` contains none of `{HAS_AUTHORITY, EB_LATCHED, POWER_OFF_PENDING}`. This is the canonical "controller has reached the stopped recovery state" predicate. Other sites cross-reference it by name and must not restate the conjuncts.

  `RouteExecutor.is_clean_for_create(train_id)` (derived `bool`): `controller.is_recovery_clean` AND `active_conditions` contains none of `{PATH_CANCEL_PENDING, ROUTE_ABORT_PENDING, ROUTE_DEACTIVATE_PENDING}` AND `run.path_cancelled is False`. This is the canonical "Phase 1 may run the create branch" predicate.

  Adding a new latch in a future revision means adding one `ControllerCondition` value plus one mapping line — every existing predicate site automatically picks up the new condition.

- **Allowed `(state, cab)` pairs.** The relationship between `state` and `cab` is structurally constrained but enforced by the `set_cab(...)` and `power_off_cmd()` writers. The legal combinations are:

  | `state` | `cab` |
  | --- | --- |
  | `OFF` | `None` |
  | `READY` | `None` or `"A"` or `"B"` |
  | `RUNNING_MOVING` | `"A"` or `"B"` |
  | `RUNNING_AT_STATION` | `"A"` or `"B"` |

  Any other combination is a contract violation. Readers needing cab membership read `cab` directly; the state alone does not encode it.

**Lookahead is a pure derived value**

`TrainController.lookahead` (and any equivalent accessor such as `TrainController.get_lookahead()`) is computed each time it is read from `(current_span, cab, received_path_plan, network_state, signals, current_speed_kmh, eb.active)`. `received_path_plan` supplies the ordered authorized corridor; speed limits, block/switch state, signal-derived diagnostics, and physical position are resolved from the authoritative network/controller state rather than copied into the controller authority payload. There is no stored field, no per-tick cache, and no separate "pre-physics" / "post-physics" copies. Layers that read lookahead receive the live result. (Lookahead caching is Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.)

**Controller state machine**

**`READY` state** (referenced elsewhere by name): the controller's `READY` state means powered, stopped, not dwelling, regardless of cab selection. Other sites must cross-reference `state == READY` and must not invent additional substate names for it.

| State | Meaning | Canonical entry triggers | Canonical exits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `OFF` (`414b`) | Power off. `TrainController.eb.activate()` is accepted while `OFF` and latches `eb.active = True` (per the EB workflow); that latch survives the `OFF → READY` transition and is cleared only by a later `TrainController.eb.release()` from `READY`. | Phase-3 power-off cleanup consuming `TrainController.pending_power_off` from a stopped powered state; startup default | `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()`, `set_mode(...)`, or `set_cab(...)` while in manual mode (any of these power `OFF → READY`; `power_on_cmd()` carries any latched `eb.active` into `READY` without clearing it) |
| `READY` (`601a`) | Powered, stopped, not dwelling. Cab may or may not be selected. | `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()`; `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)` while stopped (manual mode only); post-path `TrainController.on_path_complete()`; successful no-authority stop after cancellation/revocation; `TrainController.eb.release()` from any powered state other than `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (release from `RUNNING_AT_STATION` clears `eb.active` and stays in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`); `TrainController.finish_dwell()`; reactive speed-based evaluation inside `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` when the just-written speed satisfies `TrainController.is_stopped()` and the train has no live authority (no-authority braking completion path, both ATO and manual). A driver in manual mode who voluntarily stops under positive clearance with live authority remains in `RUNNING_MOVING` per the next row. See the **Reactive speed-based state evaluation** bullets below for the full predicate set. | `TrainController.depart()` → `RUNNING_MOVING`; reactive speed-based evaluation inside `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` when the just-written speed satisfies `not TrainController.is_stopped()` (see below); `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`; `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` → writes `pending_power_off` (no synchronous exit); the Phase-3 power-off cleanup consumer performs the `READY → OFF` transition |
| `RUNNING_MOVING` (`185a`) | Depart accepted for the current authority. The train may be physically moving, or stopped at zero ATC clearance under the same live authority while waiting for clearance / authority extension. `RUNNING_MOVING` is the canonical "depart-accepted" indicator used by stop-completion eligibility and other readers. | `TrainController.depart()` acceptance; reactive speed-based evaluation inside `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` when the controller observes `not TrainController.is_stopped()` while in `READY` (rare manual edge case: a driver pre-rolls before depart was issued; once depart is accepted the controller transitions). | `READY` on post-path completion or no-authority stop; `RUNNING_AT_STATION` only through route-owned dwell entry after confirmed arrival; reactive speed-based evaluation to `READY` inside `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` when a train reaches the canonical stopped predicate without live authority and `eb.active == False` (while EB is latched the controller stays here and only `eb.release()` drives `READY`); stopped-only operator commands accepted while `is_stopped()` is true: `set_cab(...)` (manual mode only) → `READY`, `set_mode(...)` → `READY`, `power_off_cmd()` → writes `pending_power_off`, with Phase-3 power-off cleanup exiting through `READY` to `OFF` |
| `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (`90c4`) | Route-managed dwell at a confirmed stop task | `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-entry step calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` when the next non-finished task on the directed walk has `task_kind == "dwell"`. The controller does not detect dwell entry on its own; it never reads `RouteRun`. | `TrainController.finish_dwell()` (called by `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-complete step) → `READY`. EB during dwell does NOT exit `RUNNING_AT_STATION`: `TrainController.eb.activate()` is accepted and sets `eb.active = True`, but state remains `RUNNING_AT_STATION` and the dwell timer keeps running. `TrainController.eb.release()` from this state clears `eb.active = False` and stays in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)` (manual mode only) and `TrainController.commands.set_mode(...)` from `RUNNING_AT_STATION` apply their side effects (clear `ato.running`) but state stays `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. `power_off_cmd()` → writes `pending_power_off`; Phase-3 power-off cleanup calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` which calls `finish_dwell()` to exit dwell before OFF teardown. |

- **Reactive speed-based state evaluation**: `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` evaluates state-machine transitions synchronously after writing the new `current_speed_kmh` and before building the `ControllerProgressReport`. The transition fires in the same tick that physics produces the speed change, so callers do not have to wait one tick to observe it. All speed comparisons here key on the canonical `TrainController.is_stopped()` predicate (per the **Units contract**), never on raw `current_speed_kmh` arithmetic. The rules are:
  - If `state == READY` and `not TrainController.is_stopped()`: transition to `RUNNING_MOVING`. This is one of two canonical state-machine transitions into `RUNNING_MOVING` (the other is a successful `TrainController.depart()` call); both are equally legitimate first-class transitions. After this transition, if `control_mode == "ato"` and `received_path_plan is not None`, call `TrainController.ato.go()` — the canonical helper that sets `ato.running = True` per the **`ato.running` meaning and lifecycle** section; both the `depart()` path and this reactive path route through it so neither duplicates the precondition check. This rule covers the case where a manual driver pre-rolls under live authority before Phase 1's `depart()` lands, the case where ATC clearance returns under live authority and physics resumes motion before the next Phase 1 tick, and any other path that legitimately produces motion from `READY`.
  - If `state == RUNNING_MOVING` and `TrainController.is_stopped()` and the train has no live authority and `TrainController.eb.active == False`: transition to `READY`. This is the no-authority braking completion path. While live authority still exists and the train is stopped, the controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING`; clearance may return and movement may resume without a fresh `depart()` call. While EB is latched (`eb.active == True`) the controller also stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` / `RUNNING_AT_STATION`; only `TrainController.eb.release()` drives the return to `READY` (per the EB workflow), so the reactive path must not fire while EB is active.
  - `RUNNING_MOVING` with `TrainController.is_stopped() == True` under live authority where clearance is positive (driver simply stopped the train manually) stays in `RUNNING_MOVING`; the canonical stopped predicate alone does not exit `RUNNING_MOVING` when the driver has voluntarily stopped under positive clearance.
  - `RUNNING_MOVING` with `is_stopped() == True` where the ATC limit is zero under live authority — a best-effort truncated path whose `authority_end_block_id` is short of the route-owned armed stop target, and `extend_path(...)` returned `blocked_no_growth` — also stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` (see tick step `90d3`). `is_stopped() == True` alone does not exit `RUNNING_MOVING` while live authority exists, regardless of whether the stop is voluntary (positive clearance) or ATC-forced (zero clearance). In both cases, clearance may return when the path extends or conditions change, and movement may resume without a fresh `depart()` call.
- The state machine above is the single canonical operational-state contract. Shared logic must not introduce additional live route/runtime state enums for path completion or cancellation recovery in v1.
- **`RUNNING_AT_STATION` invariant**: `received_path_plan is None` whenever `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`. Path completion (`PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)`) transitions the `Path` to `COMPLETED` in Phase 3; only after that transition does Phase 1's dwell-entry step (`3a37`) fire and call `enter_station_dwell()`.
