No code path may call `enter_station_dwell()` while a `Path` with `status == ACTIVE` still exists for this train. This invariant is the structural basis for the EB-during-dwell skip of `pending_path_cancel_request` (row `0a8e` of the EB workflow table): because no live path exists during dwell, no path cancellation latch needs to be written.
- Stopped-only operator commands (`power_off_cmd()`, `set_cab(...)` in manual mode, and `set_mode(...)`) may be issued from any physically stopped controller state, including `OFF`. `set_mode(...)` (any mode) and `set_cab(...)` (manual mode only) issued while `OFF` power the controller up as a side effect — they land it in `READY` (the same `OFF → READY` transition `power_on_cmd()` performs; see the `OFF` state-table row) while also applying the cab/mode change, because selecting a cab or mode is a way of bringing a stopped train under control. `power_off_cmd()` while `OFF` is accepted as a no-op (the train is already off). In v1 these commands disengage any active ATO run and land in `READY` (or `OFF` after pending power-off cleanup); they do not cause automatic movement just because clearance later returns or dwell later finishes.
- Stopped recovery commands transition to `READY` (regardless of cab); powered off remains `OFF`.

**`ato.running` meaning and lifecycle**

- `ato.running` means "an ATO run is currently in progress": the train has been commanded to move under ATO via `TrainController.depart()` / `TrainController.ato.go()`, and that run has not yet been ended by arrival-at-dwell, path completion, authority clear, EB, or any stopped-state command (power / cab / mode). It is a **run-active** flag, not a **mode** flag. The mode flag is `control_mode` (`ato` vs `manual`), which says "what kind of driving the controller is configured for"; `ato.running` says "is an ATO run currently driving the train." A train in ATO mode is not necessarily `ato.running`: before the first `depart()`, between runs, during dwell, and after any run-ending event, `control_mode == ato` but `ato.running == False`. When the train is stopped with zero ATC clearance but the same live authority still exists (i.e. still in `RUNNING_MOVING` with `is_stopped() == True`), the run has not ended — `ato.running` stays `True` so the controller auto-resumes when clearance returns. A train in manual mode is never `ato.running`.
- `ato.running = True` is set by the canonical state-machine transition into `RUNNING_MOVING` whenever `control_mode == "ato"` and `received_path_plan is not None` at the moment of transition. This applies to both transition triggers: a successful `TrainController.depart()` acceptance (which calls `TrainController.ato.go()`) AND the reactive speed-based evaluation entering `RUNNING_MOVING` from `READY`. Both transitions converge on the same write; `ato.go()` remains the canonical helper that performs the write so callers do not need to duplicate the precondition check. No other command, tick handler, or callback flips this flag to `True`. In particular, `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_mode(...)`, `TrainController.eb.release()`, `TrainController.on_path_complete()`, `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`, and `TrainController.finish_dwell()` never set `ato.running = True`.
- `ato.running` has exactly one writer: `TrainController`. Neither `RouteExecutor` nor `PathDispatcher` nor any train-side mirror writes this flag directly; train-running state is not a route executor's concern. `ato.running = False` is set by `TrainController` on every canonical run-ending event: successful start of dwell (`enter_station_dwell()`), controller path completion (`on_path_complete()`), authority clear (observed inside `TrainController.tick(...)` when `received_path_plan is None` while `ato.running` was previously `True`), EB activation (inside the controller's EB entry path), `power_off_cmd()` immediately on acceptance before later OFF cleanup, `set_cab(...)`, and `set_mode(...)`. Resuming ATO after any of these events requires another `depart()` / `ato.go()` acceptance.
- A zero-ATC-clearance hold under live authority does not by itself clear `ato.running`. The controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` with `is_stopped() == True`, and the flag stays `True` so that when the distance-0 ATC limit becomes positive again the controller resumes forward motion without a fresh departure decision. Only the run-ending events listed above (path completion, dwell entry, EB, authority clear, power/cab/mode changes) toggle `ato.running` back to `False`.
- Manual mode never sets `ato.running`. In manual, `TrainController.depart()` marks the current authority ready to go and does not call `ato.go()`; `ato.running` stays `False` throughout manual driving.

**Power/cab/mode command contracts**

- `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` is the dedicated `OFF -> powered` transition (`set_mode(...)` always, and `set_cab(...)` in manual mode, also power `OFF → READY` as a side effect — see their command contracts below). It is valid only when power is currently off and is processed in the train's Phase-2 operator-command slot. When a train starts its transaction `OFF`, `SimulationController` skips that train's route Phase 1 because planning cannot run before command drain. The Phase-2 command slot still runs so power-on can be accepted. If `power_on_cmd()` succeeds, the controller is no longer `OFF`, so the transaction continues through the remaining controller logic, `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`, and the normal Phase 3 feedback/cleanup pass; only fresh route planning waits for a later Phase 1 tick. The controller transitions to `READY` regardless of cab selection. `power_on_cmd()` does not touch `eb.active`: if EB was latched while the train was `OFF`, the controller lands in `READY` with `eb.active == True` (held at a stand by EB) and the operator clears it with the ordinary `TrainController.eb.release()` from `READY`.
- `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` is valid only while the train is stopped, and it remains valid when `eb.active == True`. In the phase model it is a stopped-only controller command that validates acceptance during the tick-entry command drain inside `TrainController.tick(...)`, immediately disengages any active ATO run by setting `ato.running = False`, and calls `TrainController._apply_power_off_request()` (which sets `pending_power_off = True` and — when a live path exists at write time — writes the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "power_off"` per the **Writer-helper rule**). It does not cancel dispatcher authority, clear EB, or remove route execution inline. The actual route/dispatcher teardown follows the **Power-off procedure**. The controller turning off does not stop `TrainPhysics`; world physics continues to run.
- `TrainController.commands.set_cab(cab)` is an operator manual-driving command, valid only while `control_mode == "manual"` and the train is stopped (`is_stopped() == True`), which includes `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (dwell is a stopped state). In ATO the controller aligns the cab itself during `TrainController.depart()` (see the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**), so an operator-issued `set_cab` in ATO mode is rejected with a diagnostic such as `"cab selection is only available in manual mode"`; that ATO auto-selection is the system path, distinct from this operator command, and is not gated by this rule. If the requested cab equals the current cab (including same-letter no-op or `None == None`), `set_cab(...)` is a no-op success: it does NOT change controller state and emits no notification beyond the ordinary `command_result` acknowledgement. Otherwise it does not create motion or clear any currently assigned dispatcher authority; because the command is manual-only there is no active ATO run for an operator `set_cab` to disengage (`ato.running` is always `False` in manual mode per the **`ato.running` meaning and lifecycle**), and changing cab invalidates any prior manual depart-acceptance. State-transition rule: when called from `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, `set_cab(None)` is rejected (`success=False` with diagnostic such as `"cannot deselect cab while dwelling"`) because `(RUNNING_AT_STATION, None)` is not a legal `(state, cab)` pair per the **Allowed `(state, cab)` pairs** table; setting a different non-`None` cab letter is accepted and the state stays `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (dwell is preserved; the side effects above still apply). When called from `READY`, the state stays `READY` (only `cab` changes). When called from `OFF` (the controller already being in manual mode), the command powers the controller up to `READY` and applies the cab (selecting a cab brings a stopped train under control). When called from `RUNNING_MOVING`, `set_cab(...)` is rejected (`success=False`, e.g. `"Cannot switch cab while moving"`) only while the train is actually moving (`is_stopped() == False`); when the train is held stopped at the live-authority boundary (`RUNNING_MOVING` with `is_stopped() == True`, e.g. a zero-clearance hold), the `is_stopped()` precondition is satisfied and `set_cab(...)` is accepted and transitions the controller to `READY` per the **Controller state machine** table. If the selected cab points away from the current live authority, the controller must not rewrite that authority. Instead it rebuilds lookahead in the selected travel frame; if that frame has no forward authority, the distance-0 ATC limit becomes `0`, so ATC prevents wrong-direction movement under the mismatched cab choice. A fresh `TrainController.depart()` acceptance is required to re-arm departure.
- `TrainController.commands.set_mode(mode)` is valid only while the train is stopped (`is_stopped() == True`), which includes `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. It switches between `ato` and `manual`, disengages any active ATO run (`ato.running = False`), and does not create or clear dispatcher authority. `manual` is a control mode, not a separate operational state. State-transition rule: when called from `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, the state stays `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (dwell is preserved). When called from `READY`, the state stays `READY`. When called from `RUNNING_MOVING` (possible only while `is_stopped() == True`, since the command is rejected while the train is moving), the state transitions to `READY` per the **Controller state machine** table. When called from `OFF`, the command powers the controller up to `READY`.
- `TrainController.commands.set_manual_mode_type(mode_type)` is the canonical setter for `manual.manual_mode_type`. It is valid only while the train is stopped, powered, and `control_mode == manual`. Side effects: when entering `power_percent`, reset `handle_position` to neutral; when entering `simulation`, reset `manual_power_percent = 0`. It preserves cab selection and `received_path_plan`, and does not start movement. The construction default for `manual.manual_mode_type` is `simulation`. The frontend/API and editor/UI must expose this setter through the same operator-input route as `set_handle()` / `set_manual_power_percent()`.
- `TrainController.commands.set_handle()` accepts only when `control_mode == manual`, `manual_mode_type == simulation`, `state != "OFF"`, and a cab is selected; otherwise it returns `success=False`. `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent(percent)` accepts under those same driver-input conditions except `manual.manual_mode_type` must be `power_percent`. It clamps `percent` to `0..100`, writes `manual.manual_power_percent`, preserves `received_path_plan`, and does not start movement by itself. Both commands are still accepted while `eb.active == True`: the operator may move the handle or set any power level, but EB overrides motion (physics emergency-brakes regardless of the stored input) and neither command clears `eb.active` — EB is released only by `TrainController.eb.release()`. `TrainController.commands.set_handle()` is rejected (`success=False`) when `manual_mode_type == power_percent`. `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent()` is rejected (`success=False`) when `manual_mode_type == simulation` or when any of the shared acceptance preconditions fail.
- There is no separate `TrainController.set_travel_direction(...)` command in v1. Manual direction selection is only by cab selection through `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`.
- `TrainController.eb.activate()` is the canonical operator-issued EB command and intentionally has no other precondition: emergency means it must be available at all times. Its only acceptance gate is `eb.active == False` (idempotent rejection of a duplicate activation while EB is already latched). It is valid in any motion state, any operational state including `OFF`, with or without a cab, with or without a live path. On acceptance it performs the **canonical EB activation effects** defined in the **EB workflow state machine**. Exception while `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`: the controller is dwelling and has no live path (`received_path_plan is None`), so no `pending_path_cancel_request` is written (per row `0a8e` of the EB workflow table); state remains `RUNNING_AT_STATION`; the dwell timer keeps running and is not disturbed.
- `TrainController.eb.release()` accepts when `state != "OFF"`, `eb.active == True`, `TrainController.is_stopped() == True`, the controller's own `pending_path_cancel_request` is `None` (the EB cancel latch has been consumed), and the controller's own `received_path_plan` is `None` (the pre-EB authority has transitioned out of `ACTIVE` in `PathDispatcher`). Otherwise it returns `success=False` and leaves controller state unchanged. This gate prevents same-command-drain EB activation/release from setting recovery latches before Phase 3 has committed the EB cancellation. The check uses only controller-owned fields; no dispatcher read is needed. On acceptance from `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION` (EB had been raised during dwell, no live path was ever cancelled), state stays `RUNNING_AT_STATION` and dwell timing is unaffected. On acceptance from any other powered state, follow the standard release behavior described in the EB workflow state machine.

**EB workflow state machine**

This is the single canonical step-by-step contract for EB activation, release, and post-EB restart in v1. Shared contracts must not invent separate EB-recovery state flags on `TrainController` or `RouteExecutor` beyond the ones listed below.

**Canonical EB activation effects** (referenced elsewhere by name): Sets `eb.active = True`, forces handle to `EB`, clears `ato.running = False`, and records `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "eb"` if a live path exists. Other sites must cross-reference this paragraph by name and must not restate the effect list.

| Step | Trigger | Actor | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 (`4c64`). EB activation | ATC overspeed escalation (including the no-authority overspeed branch in step `826a` where `received_path_plan` has gone `None` while the train is still rolling) or operator-issued `TrainController.eb.activate()` | `TrainController.tick()` (Phase 2) for all EB paths: operator-issued EB is accepted during the tick-entry command drain, while ATC / no-authority EB is raised later in the same tick call | Performs the **canonical EB activation effects** above, then enters the early-exit tick loop. The same train's later Phase 3 consume pass commits that cancellation through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` once the train has stopped (per the gating in the Latch Table); if the active path was already cleared earlier in the same Phase 3 pass, the dispatcher cancel mutation is skipped per the **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption: stopped-train gating and no-active-path skip** rule and the latch is still consumed (controller-local EB state stays owned by the EB workflow). Ordinary tick work is skipped until the train stops and EB is released. |
| 1a (`0a8e`). EB activation while dwelling | `TrainController.eb.activate()` is accepted while `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION` | `TrainController.tick()` (Phase 2) | Sets `eb.active = True`, forces handle to `EB`, and does NOT write `pending_path_cancel_request` (no live path during dwell). State stays `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. Dwell timer (`task_runs[walk_position].started_at` with `task_kind == "dwell"`) is unaffected; dwell continues to count toward `RouteTask.should_depart(...)`. No deviation/cancellation effects fire. |
| 2 (`e67d`). Rolling stop under EB | Physics integrates EB deceleration | `TrainPhysics.tick()` | Train decelerates according to `SpeedProfile.emergency_brake_rate`; `current_span` and `current_speed_kmh` continue to update normally. No additional authority completion/cancellation side effects fire during rolling beyond the earlier EB-cancellation request, but physics remains authoritative: `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` continues emitting span updates, and the same train's Phase 3 consume pass continues committing physical entry and tail-vacate resource updates while EB is latched. |
| 3 (`9f74`). Full stop under EB | `TrainController.is_stopped()` returns `True` | `TrainController.tick()` | Controller remains in the EB early-exit loop with `eb.active == True`; no state transition yet. Dispatcher path completion is explicitly suppressed while `eb.active == True`. |
| 4 (`ad04`). EB release | `TrainController.eb.release()` called while stopped and powered after the EB cancellation latch has been consumed (controller's own `pending_path_cancel_request` is `None`) and dispatcher has transitioned the pre-EB active path out of `ACTIVE` (controller's own `received_path_plan is None`) | operator | Clears `eb.active = False`; transitions controller to `READY`. If the EB cancellation is still pending or the old live path still exists, the release command fails through `NotificationCenter` and does not transition state. `TrainController.eb.release()` does not call `RouteExecutor`, does not inspect or complete the cancelled pre-EB path, and does not clear route-owned recovery state itself. |
| 4a (`1bbc`). EB release from dwell | `TrainController.eb.release()` called while `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`. The acceptance gate (`pending_path_cancel_request is None`, `received_path_plan is None`, `is_stopped() == True`) is naturally satisfied because no live path was cancelled by the dwell EB and the dwelling controller has speed `0`. | operator | Clears `eb.active = False`; state stays `RUNNING_AT_STATION`; dwell timer is unaffected. No EB-specific restart/replan is needed for this branch — row `29a0` does not apply to the dwell EB cycle. |
| 5 (`29a0`). EB restart/replan | `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` (set by the EB cancellation callback) AND `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true on the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` | `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (Phase 1) | The Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step clears `RouteRun.path_cancelled` when a route run exists, so the derived diagnostic status returns to `RUNNING`. The same Phase 1 then continues into the ordinary planning kernel: covered-task index advance → `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` → fresh `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` → `TrainController.depart()` per the canonical depart contract. |

- `eb.active` is controller-owned, but EB activation must request dispatcher cancellation through `pending_path_cancel_request` immediately when a live path exists. The actual cancellation side effects still run only inside `PathDispatcher` during the same train's later Phase 3 consume pass; `TrainController` never releases resources itself.
- EB recovery uses the same recovery latches as any other cancellation: `RouteRun.path_cancelled` for route-owned recovery cleanup. No EB-specific route hook or auto-depart flag exists in v1.
- `eb.active` while `OFF`: `TrainController.eb.activate()` is accepted in `OFF` and latches `eb.active = True` with no live path to cancel. The latch survives `OFF → READY`: `power_on_cmd()` does not clear it, so a train powered on with EB latched rests in `(state == READY, eb.active == True)`, held at a stand by EB, until the operator issues the ordinary `TrainController.eb.release()` from `READY` (row `ad04`). There is no separate OFF-state EB clear path; `eb.release()` is rejected while `OFF` per its `state != "OFF"` gate.

**received_path_plan schema**

- This is the single canonical schema for the controller's active path-plan payload. Every producer/consumer should refer back to this subsection by name instead of restating the field list elsewhere.
- **Why this shape.** The controller's job is to brake to the live authority boundary using its safety margin and to compute speed restrictions live from the network and the authorized corridor. That work needs the corridor geometry (`resource_sequence`) and the boundary (`authority_end_block_id`); it does not need the dispatcher-side requested-target fields, which are a `PathDispatcher` completion-enforcement concern. Keeping the projection narrow lets the controller stay decoupled from dispatcher-side completion logic and lets `received_path_plan` regenerate as a pure read with no caching.
- `received_path_plan` carries exactly `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, and `authority_end_block_id` in v1. It is the controller's only active path-plan payload. `resource_sequence` uses the canonical **resource_sequence entry schema**, and each nested `path_tiles` slice uses the canonical **path_tile entry schema**, so the controller can extend lookahead along the actual geometry without inferring terminal-frame direction from tile coordinates alone. The requested target (`requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`) is stored on the dispatcher-owned `Path` record but is not included in `received_path_plan`; the controller does not see those fields. Completion enforcement against the requested target is internal to `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)`.
- When the live authority already reaches the dispatcher-stored requested target, `authority_end_block_id` in `received_path_plan` equals `Path.requested_destination_block_id`; when the path is a best-effort truncated authority, `authority_end_block_id` is the temporary current boundary.
- `received_path_plan` does not carry a separate cab or head-side field. The ordered `resource_sequence` is the combined already-occupied physical span plus forward path authority in tail-to-authority order. "Tail" means the physical end of the current span farther from `authority_end_block_id`; "authority side" means the physical end closer to the destination and pointing into the path authority. When `TrainController.depart()` runs in ATO with no cab selected, it derives the cab by comparing `TrainSpan.end_a` and `TrainSpan.end_b` against that ordered seeded span and chooses the cab whose physical end is on the authority side, per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract** (manual depart never auto-selects — it requires a pre-selected cab and rejects when `cab is None`). `end_a` is not a general preferred end in this model; it is only the final dead-heat tie-break when every authority-side comparison between the two physical ends remains exactly tied.
- Treat `assigned_path_id`, `ato_path_id`, and `ato_destination_block_id` as computed reads derived from `received_path_plan`, not as independently written runtime fields. The alias mapping is explicit in v1:
  - `assigned_path_id := received_path_plan.path_id`
  - `ato_path_id := received_path_plan.path_id`
  - `ato_destination_block_id := received_path_plan.authority_end_block_id`
  When `PathDispatcher` clears authority by transitioning `Path.status` to `COMPLETED`/`CANCELLED`, the controller's `received_path_plan` property returns `None` on its next read and all three disappear automatically.
- `lookahead` is a canonical pure derived value computed live on every read; it is the single canonical series used by ATC, ATO, UI, and monitor consumers.
- Target speed and ATC limit are reads from lookahead at distance 0; the controller does not store standalone `target_speed` or `effective_limit` scalars. The top-level `speed_limit_reason` field remains in the exported v1 payload shape for current UI/monitor consumers; its canonical source is the distance-0 lookahead speed-curve metadata.

**canonical lookahead payload**

- `TrainController.get_lookahead()` is the single canonical exported lookahead payload for ATC, ATO, UI, and monitor consumers. Every producer/consumer should refer back to this subsection by name instead of restating the field list elsewhere.
- **Why this shape.** Lookahead is the single read surface that fuses authority geometry, network state, signals, and current motion into one ordered forward projection. ATC, ATO, monitor UI, and stop-completion all sample from the same payload, so there is one place to add or change a forward-projected datapoint. Speed-curve metadata (including `atc_limit_reason_code`) lives on the same payload so that "why is the train holding at zero?" is answerable from one read instead of three. The empty/no-cab/no-train form preserves the same shape so consumers do not need to branch on payload variant.
- In v1, `get_lookahead()` carries exactly:
  - top level: `train_id`, `head_tile`, `head_distance_cm`, `travel_direction`, `cab`, `speed_limit_reason`, `lookahead`
  - nested `lookahead`: `blocks`, `signals`, `switches`, `stations`, `clearance_cm`, `safety_margin_cm`, `target_stop_cm`, `authority_boundary_delta_cm`, `speed_curves`
- `head_tile` is the current train head tile coordinate or `None` when unavailable. `head_distance_cm`, `clearance_cm`, `safety_margin_cm`, `target_stop_cm`, and `authority_boundary_delta_cm` are centimeters. `target_stop_cm` is the braking-target distance from the head (`clearance_cm` minus the effective `safety_margin_cm`, floored at 0; equal to `authority_boundary_delta_cm`). `travel_direction` is the train's current forward travel direction in the same frame used by movement/traversal. `cab` is the currently selected cab or `None`.
- `blocks`, `signals`, `switches`, and `stations` are ordered forward projections for UI/debug consumers. They are informational read models only; control decisions come from the same payload's clearance and speed-curve data.
- `lookahead.stations` keeps the current compatibility payload name in v1, but each entry refers to a concrete physical platform ahead on the track, not to a logical multi-platform station group.
- Each `lookahead.blocks` entry carries exactly:
  - `id`
  - `name`
  - `state`
  - `start_cm`
  - `end_cm`
  - `length_cm`
  - `tile_count`
  - `speed_limit_kmh`
- Each `lookahead.signals` entry carries exactly:
  - `id`
  - `name`
  - `state`
  - `distance_cm`
- Each `lookahead.switches` entry carries exactly:
  - `x`
  - `y`
  - `name`
  - `position`
  - `distance_cm`
  - `locked`
  where `position` is `"main"` or `"diverging"` for the live switch alignment at that tile, and `locked` is the exported boolean monitor view of whether that switch currently has any future lock claim or train-owned protection claim that blocks conflicting acquisition.
- Each `lookahead.stations` entry carries exactly:
  - `platform_id`
  - `station_id`
  - `name`
  - `distance_cm`
  - `length_cm`
  - `tile_count`
  - `top_speed_kmh`
- `platform_id` in this compatibility payload is the concrete physical platform identifier on the track.
- `station_id` in this compatibility payload is the logical station-group identifier that owns that platform, or `None` when no station group exists. `station_id` must not be reused as the physical platform identifier anywhere.
- Each `lookahead.speed_curves` entry carries exactly:
  - `distance_cm`
  - `atc_limit`
  - `operational_limit`
  - optional `atc_limit_reason_code`
  - optional `atc_limit_reason_detail`
  The limit fields are in `km/h`.
- The canonical `atc_limit_reason_code` enumeration is defined here once. Every consumer (`is_stopped()` predicate, post-physics usable-authority stop check, no-authority braking diagnostic, monitor/UI display) cross-references this set by name and must not use arbitrary strings or redefine the values elsewhere:
  - `"authority_stop"` (`f169`) — zero-speed restriction at the safety-margin-adjusted end of live authority; the sole code that satisfies the usable-authority stop check for path completion
  - `"caution"` (`39ed`) — approaching or inside a caution zone (block in `CAUTION` state or forward block occupied)
  - `"obstruction"` (`05e9`) — an in-path resource conflict that blocks forward progress below safe clearance distance
  - `"speed_limit"` (`5c1d`) — a posted block speed limit (`Block.speed_limit_kmh`) below the train's current maximum: a permitted-speed restriction ahead, distinct from `obstruction` (which is an in-path resource conflict, not a posted limit)
  - `"no_authority"` (`7f1a`) — no live path authority; `atc_limit = 0` at distance 0 regardless of block geometry, so ATC holds a stopped train at rest and escalates a rolling train to EB
  - `"no_cab"` (`2ce5`) — no cab selected; ATC limit is zero regardless of authority
  - `"wrong_direction_cab"` (`a54f`) — selected cab points away from live authority direction; ATC limit is zero
  - `"eb"` (`c3ea`) — emergency brake latched; ATC limit is zero
  - `"no_train"` (`7b09`) — the train or network object is not yet wired, so the empty distance-0 anchor still reports a structured reason code rather than a free-form string
- `atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"` is the canonical reason code for the zero-speed restriction at the active stop/end-of-authority boundary. The controller stop-completion predicate must key on this code, not on arbitrary reason strings.
- `authority_boundary_delta_cm` is the live signed separation from the train head to the safety-margin-adjusted authority boundary in the same forward distance axis as the `speed_curves[*].distance_cm` samples and the authority-boundary projection helper. With a live path it is derived from `authority_end_block_id` plus the terminal frame; without a live path it is `0.0` because no authority boundary exists. It is diagnostic/control lookahead data, not a route-authored stop-position field.
- Consumers asking "what is the target speed right now?" sample `lookahead.speed_curves` at distance `0`: `operational_limit` at distance `0` is the controller target speed, and `atc_limit` plus its reason metadata at distance `0` is the controller ATC limit/reason. The top-level `speed_limit_reason` field remains part of the exported v1 payload shape for compatibility with current monitor/UI consumers.
- The empty/no-cab/no-train form must preserve this same top-level and nested key shape, with empty lists and zero distances instead of returning a different payload shape. If a compatibility producer cannot compute one of the nested boundary/safety fields on an empty payload, it should still emit the key with `0.0` rather than omitting it.

**Controller tick order**

1. `3ec8` — If no train, return immediately.
2. `01f2` — Drain queued operator commands as the first substep of `TrainController.tick(...)`. After that drain, if `state == "OFF"`, keep the controller in `OFF` and return from the controller tick's powered decision work; `SimulationController` still runs `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` for storage/physics continuity, but that callback must append no dispatcher/route progress reports to the mailbox and Phase 3 is skipped for that still-off transaction. If `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` succeeded during the drain, the controller is no longer `OFF`, so `TrainController.tick(...)` continues into normal powered controller logic, `SimulationController` runs `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`, and Phase 3 runs normally for that same transaction. Route Phase 1 remains skipped only for the transaction that began while the train was `OFF`; route planning resumes on the next eligible tick.
3. `d483` — If emergency brake is active:
  - Re-apply the **canonical EB activation effects** defined in the **EB workflow state machine** if they have not yet been applied this tick (idempotent re-write of handle = `EB`, `ato.running = False`, and the `pending_path_cancel_request(EB)` payload when a live path exists). Then early-exit the tick while EB remains latched.
  - Stay in the early-exit loop until the train has stopped.
  - Require explicit `eb.release()` to clear `eb.active`.
  - While `eb.active == True`, `TrainController.tick()` does not perform post-EB restart logic. `TrainController.eb.release()` owns the transition to the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection); that release step does not clear route-owned recovery state. The earlier EB activation already wrote `pending_path_cancel_request(EB)`, which Phase 3 cancels and which sets `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` via the cancellation callback. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (Phase 1) owns the route-deviation cleanup step and observes `path_cancelled` once the controller has reached the `READY` state.
4. `061a` — If no cab is selected, ATC limit is forced to zero and `lookahead` returns the canonical empty payload on every read.
5. `22a9` — Otherwise `lookahead` returns the canonical payload for the current state on every read:
  - With a received path plan, lookahead extends along the path to the current `authority_end_block_id` boundary adjusted by the controller safety margin.
  - Without a path plan, `lookahead` is the canonical no-authority empty payload (same top-level and nested shape as the no-cab case). `speed_curves` carries a single anchor at `distance_cm = 0.0` with `atc_limit = 0.0`, `operational_limit = 0.0`, and `atc_limit_reason_code = no_authority`. `blocks`, `signals`, `switches`, and `stations` are empty lists. `clearance_cm = 0.0` and `authority_boundary_delta_cm = 0.0`. The controller does not project forward through the train's current occupied block when no live path exists; movement authority is binary on `received_path_plan` per the **`received_path_plan` schema** rule that there is no second authority source.
6. `c4c2` — Build ATC state from `lookahead`, including structured reason metadata.
7. `914f` — Apply ATC overspeed logic.
8. `0a4e` — Calculate ATO info.
9. `8d18` — In ATO mode, if `ato.running`, calculate handle with `_ato_calculate_handle()`.
10. `924d` — In manual mode, write a controller-owned command and let physics convert it to speed:
  - In `simulation` submode, store the driver's handle notch in `handle_position` and allow ATC to override with braking.
  - In `power_percent` submode, store the driver's percentage power in `manual.manual_power_percent` as an integer `0..100` and let physics map it through `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` when no braking override is active. This value is positive only and represents absolute applied track/motor voltage and its calibrated immediate running speed rather than signed traction vs brake effort. `manual.manual_power_percent` is set-and-forget: once written by a driver `set_manual_power_percent(...)` command or overwritten to `0` by a controller braking override, it stays at that value across ticks until the driver issues a new `set_manual_power_percent(...)`. The controller does not store a separate "driver-commanded" copy and does not auto-restore a prior driver level after a braking override clears; if ATC/service braking/EB forced `manual_power_percent = 0`, the train remains at `0` until the driver explicitly commands a non-zero value again.
  - At any given tick there is only one effective propulsion/brake command for the active mode. In ATO and manual `simulation` mode, the controller/physics speed solution is authoritative and `power_percent` / `track_voltage` are derived from that operating point. In manual `power_percent` mode, `manual.manual_power_percent` is authoritative only while no safety braking override is active; it maps to track voltage and the calibrated absolute speed for that same movement update, so the normal acceleration/deceleration-rate fields do not shape that manual-power command. Raising it immediately commands the higher calibrated voltage/speed point, lowering it immediately commands the lower calibrated voltage/speed point, and holding it steady holds the same commanded power/speed point. In manual `power_percent` mode there is no fallback to handle-based braking: if ATC, service braking, or EB intervenes, the controller clamps the manual target speed down to the ATC limit at distance 0 and forces `manual_power_percent = 0` for that tick (with EB additionally engaging `eb.active`). ATC outranks the manual command. A positive ATC cap therefore holds the train at that cap for the tick; a zero cap (EB, no cab, or no/at-end authority) yields the calibrated zero-speed/zero-voltage outcome from `power_speed_curve` — a full stop. `set_handle()` is unavailable while `manual_mode_type == power_percent`, so `handle_position` is not the braking path. There are no negative power-percent values.
11. `44e9` — Stop computing standalone `effective_limit` and `target_speed` scalars; consumers should read controller lookahead at distance 0 instead.
12. `89ba` — `TrainController.tick()` does not latch path completion. It builds the current state-machine outputs by reading `lookahead` (which recomputes live from current span/speed/path), but it must not mutate `Path` records, release resources, clear authority, or call route callbacks itself. The paired `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` callback only updates `current_span` / `current_speed_kmh`; subsequent `lookahead` reads automatically reflect the new values.
13. `32dd` — Let waiting decisions come from controller lookahead plus the route-owned armed target read live from `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`, where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` for the live planning chain (the canonical writer of `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` is the Phase 1 `create_path` / `extend_path` step), and let path completion commits come only from the later Phase 3 consume pass. During Phase 3, after earlier local progress reports have been consumed and any movement-derived deviation handling from them has already run, `SimulationController` runs the post-physics usable-authority stop check against the controller's current state only (the predicate defined in step `8df5`); the route-owned armed target is not an input to that check — the requested-target match is owned by `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)`. On success, it calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` (timestamps per **Time sampling**). Any explicit latched teardown such as `pending_path_cancel_request` is handled only after that normal completion opportunity. `TrainController.tick()` itself does not commit completion.
14. `90d3` — A best-effort truncated path stopping at its current authority boundary is not completion when that boundary is short of the route-owned armed stop target. The controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` with `is_stopped() == True` (depart was accepted; ATC is holding the train at zero); the same `received_path_plan.path_id` remains live while `RouteExecutor` keeps retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on it.
15. `826a` — If the active dispatcher path transitions out of `ACTIVE` while the train is moving, treat that as path-authority loss. The essential event is "authority removed": `PathDispatcher` changes the dispatcher-owned `Path.status`, then on the next `TrainController.tick()` after that change — which is the next tick's Phase 2 controller tick because cancellation now commits in Phase 3 after the current tick's controller/physics work — the controller reads `received_path_plan is None` and the canonical lookahead becomes the no-authority empty payload defined in step `22a9` (`atc_limit = 0` at distance 0, `atc_limit_reason_code = no_authority`). Any current_speed_kmh above zero is therefore overspeed at distance 0. The ATC overspeed branch in step `914f` escalates to EB per the **canonical EB activation effects** in the **EB workflow state machine**. EB brings the train to a stop. Once `is_stopped()` is true and the EB cancellation latch has been consumed, the operator clears EB through `TrainController.eb.release()`, which transitions the controller to the **`READY` state** per row `ad04` of the EB workflow. There is no service-brake-to-block-boundary path: a train that has lost its authority has no authority to brake against, so EB is the only stopping protocol. This is the cancellation/revocation path and it subsumes the generic ATO not-running branch for no-authority rolling trains.
16. `71ee` — After `TrainController.eb.release()` from any state other than `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, transition to the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection). EB activation has already requested cancellation of any live path through `pending_path_cancel_request(EB)`, so restart requires fresh path creation by the next eligible Phase 1, which then calls `TrainController.depart()` directly per the canonical depart contract. The controller does not self-resume. `eb.release()` from `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (the dwell EB cycle, see EB workflow row `1bbc`) clears `eb.active` only and stays in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`.
**ATC speed limit**

- ATC restriction selection does not read signal state. CAUTION restriction comes from a single controller-owned global cap, `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH`, applied when `TrainController` samples a block whose computed `Block.state` is `CAUTION`; signals are display state derived from blocks, not an ATC input.
- In manual mode with a received path plan, include `_calculate_authority_boundary_limit()` so the effective ATC limit enforces the current authority end correctly: brake to the `authority_end_block_id` boundary in the current live authority frame using the controller safety margin.
- Treat ATC reason reporting as structured metadata on the lookahead/curve payload, not only as a free-form controller string.

**ATO handle calculation**

- `_ato_calculate_handle()` reads the canonical controller `lookahead` property (itself a pure derived value computed live); it does not build a lookahead of its own.
- If the sampled operational speed curve at distance `0` is already at or below the stop threshold, calculate a direct braking notch immediately.
- Replace the current multi-handle forward simulation wording with a two-point sampling approach:
  - sample the operational speed curve at distance `0`
  - sample one future point at a delta-scaled horizon such as `speed_cm_s * self._tick_delta * LOOKAHEAD_TICKS`
  - combine those samples with the active ATC and block caps to choose power, coast, or brake
- Once the two-point horizon is in place, remove the old fixed-horizon wording and any special near-stop fast-path that only exists to compensate for the old horizon logic.

**Speed curves**

- Build a unified restriction set for the full clearance/authority area and take the minimum of all relevant curves and local limits at each sample point.
- Carry source metadata on each `lookahead.speed_curves` datapoint, such as `atc_limit_reason_code` and `atc_limit_reason_detail`.
- Preserve metadata for which restriction category produced the current minimum ATC limit so monitor/debug UI can explain the displayed number.

**Driver/operator inputs**

- `TrainController.commands.set_handle()` is the active manual-driving command for `simulation` submode manual control. The command may be accepted while manual mode is active, `manual_mode_type == simulation`, the train is powered, and a cab is selected; the effective command is still clamped by ATC lookahead — and fully overridden by EB while `eb.active` — before physics receives it, and the command never clears `eb.active`. `set_handle()` is rejected (`success=False`) when `manual_mode_type == power_percent`.
- `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent()` is the active driver input for `power_percent` submode manual control; the matching API endpoint/client method and train/editor UI control deliver operator input to the controller-owned field through the same route as other manual commands. It accepts under the same manual-input conditions as `set_handle()` except `manual.manual_mode_type` must be `power_percent`: manual mode active, train powered, and cab selected. Like handle input, the command may be accepted before depart has been accepted (and while `eb.active`), but ATC and EB remain authoritative and can override by forcing `manual_power_percent = 0` for that tick. The command clamps to `0..100`, writes `manual.manual_power_percent`, and does not start movement by itself. `set_manual_power_percent()` is rejected (`success=False`) when `manual_mode_type == simulation` or when any of the shared acceptance preconditions fail.
- `TrainController.depart()` is called directly by `RouteExecutor` whenever live authority exists for the current target, including one-off operator routes created from direct UI/API movement requests. No other component should synthesize departure acceptance by other means.

**Dispatcher/route-executor inputs**

- There is no `receive_path_plan(path_plan)` setter, cache refresh, or atomic replacement API in v1. `received_path_plan` is a read-only property that resolves the active dispatcher-owned path into the canonical **received_path_plan schema** on each read; updates to the dispatcher's `Path` registry surface automatically without any controller-side write.
- `received_path_plan` must follow the canonical **received_path_plan schema**. Its nested authority geometry must follow the canonical **resource_sequence entry schema** and **path_tile entry schema**; the controller must not be asked to recover terminal-frame direction later from tile coordinates alone.
- In v1, `received_path_plan` carries the authorized corridor and current authority boundary, not dispatcher-only requested-target fields. The live stop the controller should brake to on this tick is derived from `authority_end_block_id`, the terminal frame in `resource_sequence`, and the controller safety margin.
- `received_path_plan` should not carry a second lifecycle status enum. For the controller, movement authority is binary: `received_path_plan is not None` means there is live authority, and `received_path_plan is None` means there is not.
- `on_physics_updated(span_update)` is called by `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` after it computes the next span/speed and tile delta. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` applies the update to controller-owned `current_span` / `current_speed_kmh`, then builds the canonical progress report from the tile delta, the live `lookahead` (computed from the just-updated span/speed), and `received_path_plan.resource_sequence` when live authority exists, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). The appended `ControllerProgressReport.event_time` is sampled by `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` itself via `time.time()` at the moment the report is built (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**); the caller does not pass it in. `SimulationController` drains and clears that mailbox in Phase 3 by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` in order. This method owns span storage and progress interpretation only; it does not mutate dispatcher path records or route state directly.
- `on_path_complete()` is called by `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` when the post-physics controller stop check has passed, dispatcher verifies that the active path reaches its requested target block and direction, and dispatcher commits completion. `TrainController.on_path_complete()` should transition the controller into the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection). `received_path_plan` is a read-only projection over the dispatcher's `Path` registry; once dispatcher transitions `Path.status` to `COMPLETED` (immediately before this callback), the property automatically returns `None`. `TrainController.on_path_complete()` must not enter station dwell by itself; the next tick's Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` enters dwell automatically when the route's next task is `dwell`. `TrainController.on_path_complete()` is not called while EB is still latched; EB activation cancels the path, and post-EB continuation always uses fresh authority.
- `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` is the dwell-entry transition called by the Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`. There is no `dwell_entry_requested` flag in v1; `RouteExecutor` invokes it directly when `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true and the route's next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`. The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at`; the dwell-entry step does not need to write it. The controller call transitions to `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. The controller does not detect dwell entry on its own and does not read `RouteRun`.
- Missing path authority is represented entirely by `received_path_plan is None`. The controller has no `wait_for_path(reason)` command.
- `TrainController.depart()`, `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`, and `TrainController.finish_dwell()` are controller acceptance/transition commands. The closed list of `TrainController.finish_dwell()` callers in v1 is: (a) the Phase 1 dwell-complete step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (the canonical dwell-exit caller); (b) `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` when invoked by any of the Phase 3 abort/power-off/deactivate consumers with the controller currently in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. Both are covered by the **Same-transaction commit-callback exception** in the **Phase handoff rule**. `RouteExecutor` calls `depart()` directly; `enter_station_dwell()` is called only by the Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`. `TrainController.finish_dwell()` is the end-of-dwell state transition; it lands in the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection), does not clear `received_path_plan`, and does not start movement. Cab selection is preserved across route completion.
- Manual trains use the same Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` as ATO. The difference is departure behavior only: in manual mode `TrainController.depart()` records that the current authority has been accepted and the train is ready to go, but it does not itself start movement or call `TrainController.ato.go()`.
- `PathDispatcher` calls `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, reason=None)` only after the dispatcher cancel mutation has committed. The callback records diagnostic context and clears live-path correlation; recovery follows the canonical recovery table. `reason` is diagnostic only; the originating-kind label is on the `path_cancellation` notification's `initiator` field.
- `RouteExecutor` should not set controller path or destination fields directly; controller authority is read live by the controller through its `received_path_plan` projection over the dispatcher's `Path` registry.
- `TrainController.eb.release()` should only clear EB when the train is already stopped and powered, no EB cancellation latch remains pending on the controller's own `pending_path_cancel_request`, and the controller's own `received_path_plan` is `None` (the pre-EB path has been cancelled and transitioned out of `ACTIVE` by `PathDispatcher`). It transitions the controller to the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection). Because the earlier Phase 3 EB-cancellation commit has already cancelled any live path, `TrainController.eb.release()` never evaluates a stale path completion predicate. It does not call `RouteExecutor`, does not inspect route target state directly, and does not clear route-owned task-run state or pending cancellation diagnostics itself. The same train's Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step clears route-owned cancellation diagnostics once the controller is observed in the `READY` state, and the next eligible Phase 1 only performs covered-task index advance plus fresh planning.
- After any EB stop, the old active path must not be reused. EB activation requests cancellation of the active path by writing `pending_path_cancel_request` with `initiator = "eb"`; the same train's later Phase 3 consume pass commits that request through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. `TrainController.eb.release()` only clears the EB latch and transitions to the `READY` state. After fresh authority is created, the next eligible Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly per the canonical depart contract. There is no station/terminal exception to retain or complete the pre-EB path; normal route covered-task index advance and replanning handle the train's stopped position after EB release.

**route/dispatcher command contracts**

- `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` is a read-only property that resolves the dispatcher's currently active `Path` for this train into the canonical **received_path_plan schema** (or `None`) on each read. There is no setter and no atomic-replacement entry point. `PathDispatcher` updates its own `Path` record in `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)`, and the controller observes those updates on its next read. When the property transitions from non-`None` to `None` (because `Path.status` is no longer `ACTIVE`), the controller's authority-clear bookkeeping (notably clearing `ato.running` because authority clear is a run-ending event) runs at the start of the next `TrainController.tick(...)` when it observes the change. Because v1 holds at most one ACTIVE path per train and `create_path(...)` requires `received_path_plan is None` while `extend_path(...)` keeps the same `path_id`, a new `path_id` can never appear while a live path is still present: the old path always transitions to `None` first, which drives the `RUNNING_MOVING → READY` authority-clear transition; a fresh path is then accepted from `READY` by a new `depart()` call.
- **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract.** `TrainController.depart()` accepts an existing authority. It is called directly by `RouteExecutor` whenever live authority exists for the current target. Returns `(success: bool, reason: Optional[str])`. Shared acceptance preconditions: `received_path_plan is not None`, `eb.active == False`, `state in {READY, RUNNING_MOVING}` (which already implies powered), and the train is not pending power-off. The `state in {READY, RUNNING_MOVING}` condition admits the case where the reactive speed-based state evaluation has already transitioned the controller into `RUNNING_MOVING` ahead of Phase 1's `depart()` call; in that case the call is an idempotent success that does not change `state` but still applies any side effects (e.g. invoking `ato.go()` to set `ato.running = True` if `control_mode == "ato"` and that has not already been done by the reactive transition). Cab acceptance is mode-specific:
  - In ATO (`control_mode == "ato"`): if no cab is currently selected, `depart()` auto-selects one from `received_path_plan.resource_sequence` against `TrainSpan.end_a` / `end_b` (final tie-break: `end_a`) before transitioning. ATO depart never fails for "no cab"; the auto-selection is part of the contract.
  - In manual (`control_mode == "manual"`): a cab must be pre-selected by the operator. If `cab is None`, `depart()` rejects with `success=False` and a diagnostic `reason` such as `"manual depart requires a selected cab"`. Manual depart never auto-selects.
  Failure leaves controller state unchanged. From `state == READY`, success transitions the controller to `RUNNING_MOVING`. In ATO with `received_path_plan is not None`, success also calls `TrainController.ato.go()` which sets `ato.running = True`. In manual mode, success transitions to `RUNNING_MOVING` without starting physics movement; the driver sees that depart has been accepted and applies handle/power-percent commands. All other sites that describe `depart()` effects must cross-reference this contract by name and must not restate the precondition or effect lists.
- `TrainController.on_path_complete()` is valid only after `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` verifies that the post-physics controller stop check has already passed, verifies the active path requested-target check, and commits path completion. It transitions the controller to the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection), must not clear `received_path_plan`, and must not enter dwell itself.
- `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` is called by the Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`. Acceptance preconditions: `state == READY`, `eb.active == False`. The Phase 1 caller additionally enforces that `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, the route's next non-finished walk-position has `task_kind == "dwell"`, and the predecessor walk-position's `finished_at` is set; these are caller-side gates. The dwell row's `started_at` is the derived read of the predecessor stop's `finished_at`, so this controller call does not need to (and does not) write `started_at`. On success, transitions the controller to `RUNNING_AT_STATION` and must not create movement or mutate `received_path_plan`. Returns `(success: bool, reason: Optional[str])`; on rejection the call leaves controller state unchanged and emits a diagnostic through `NotificationCenter`.
- `TrainController.finish_dwell()` is valid only from `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. Acceptance preconditions: `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`. EB latch state is not a precondition; `finish_dwell()` may run with `eb.active == True` because the canonical caller `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` invokes it as part of the Phase-3 power-off / abort / deactivate teardown sequence where EB may still be latched (the OFF cleanup that follows clears `eb.active`). On success, transitions back to the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection), preserves cab selection, does not call `TrainController.depart()`, and does not mutate `received_path_plan`. Returns `(success: bool, reason: Optional[str])`; on rejection the call leaves controller state unchanged and emits a diagnostic through `NotificationCenter`. The normal callers are the same train's Phase 1 dwell-complete step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` and `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` invoked by Phase-3 abort/power-off/deactivate consumers.

**Read accessors**

- `get_lookahead()` is the canonical accessor for UI and debug consumers. Any consumer asking for target speed or speed-limit reason should sample this payload at distance `0`.
- `get_current_span()` is the canonical controller accessor for occupied-span queries. It reads `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`, the v1 storage home for live physical occupancy and end locators. The payload is general and geometry-first: at minimum it must expose `occupied_blocks` and `occupied_tiles` in physical span order from one physical end of the train to the other, plus explicit end locators for both physical ends. The ordered occupancy is the block-level ownership view; the end locators are the tile-level precise geometry view. `end_a` and `end_b` are the train's two **physical** ends and are cab-independent: they are stable geometric labels for the two ends of the consist and do NOT imply head/tail. The "head" end is whichever physical end corresponds to the currently selected cab in the controller's travel frame; without a selected cab, `current_span` still carries both physical ends but no head/tail labeling. `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` consumers that need head-side reasoning derive it from controller cab/travel state plus the span; they must not assume `end_a == head`. `TrainPhysics` is the only normal producer of movement `span_update` values, while `TrainController` is the only normal writer of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`; `RouteExecutor` / `PathDispatcher` must use `TrainController.get_current_span()` whenever they need the train's current span or physical ends.

**TrainSpan schema**

This is the canonical v1 shape of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` and the payload returned by `TrainController.get_current_span()`.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `end_a` | `SpanEndLocator` | Stable physical end A of the consist. It is cab-independent and does not imply head/tail. |
| `end_b` | `SpanEndLocator` | Stable physical end B of the consist. It is cab-independent and does not imply head/tail. |
| `occupied_tiles` | `List[Tuple[int, int]]` | Tile-level coverage in physical order from `end_a` to `end_b`. |
| `occupied_blocks` | `List[str]` | Unique block IDs currently covered by the train, in first-seen `end_a` -> `end_b` order. |

`SpanEndLocator` carries exactly:

- `tile` — current tile coordinate for that physical end
- `entry_edge` — `"N"`/`"E"`/`"S"`/`"W"` edge used as the end's current in-tile distance frame
- `distance_cm` — precise in-tile distance from `entry_edge`, in centimeters

Consumers that need the forward/head side derive it from selected cab/travel state plus `end_a` / `end_b`; `TrainSpan` itself never relabels `end_a` or `end_b` as head/tail.

`PhysicsSpanUpdate` is the canonical payload from `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` into `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`. It carries exactly:

- `new_span: TrainSpan`
- `current_speed_kmh: float`
- `added_tiles: List[TileTraversal]` in physical traversal order for tiles newly covered by the updated span
- `removed_tiles: List[TileTraversal]` in physical vacate order for tiles no longer covered by the updated span

`TileTraversal` carries exactly:

- `tile_xy` — tile coordinate
- `entry_edge` — `"N"`/`"E"`/`"S"`/`"W"` traversal-frame entry edge
- `exit_edge` — `"N"`/`"E"`/`"S"`/`"W"` traversal-frame exit edge; for tail-vacated block tiles this is the vacated block's forward exit edge for the traversal by which the train left that block, which `TrainController` reads as the block tail-vacate `direction`
- `resource_type` — `"block"` or `"switch"` when the tile belongs to a known resource, otherwise `None`
- `resource_id` — block or switch ID for that resource, otherwise `None`
- `block_id` — owning block ID when applicable, otherwise `None`

`TrainPhysics` computes this delta by comparing the previous controller-owned span it read at the start of the movement step with the newly integrated span. `TrainController` is the only normal writer of the stored `current_span`: it applies `span_update.new_span`, derives resource entry/release observations from `added_tiles` / `removed_tiles`, packages them into one `ControllerProgressReport`, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) for later delivery to `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` in Phase 3.

`TrainPhysics.tick(...)` must emit a `PhysicsSpanUpdate` on every tick, even when `added_tiles` and `removed_tiles` are both empty and the integrated `new_span` / `current_speed_kmh` are unchanged. When nothing changed physically, this is an explicit no-change callback. The always-callback rule keeps `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` / `current_speed_kmh` canonical and gives Phase 3 a deterministic consume point for local progress consumption, the post-physics stop check, and latches such as `pending_power_off`.

- One shared neighbor/edge helper, such as `RailwayNetwork.get_connection_edges(tile_a, tile_b) -> Optional[Tuple[str, str]]`, answers two questions at once: are the two tiles track-connected, and if so, which edge on each tile connects to the other. The helper resolves both ordinary grid adjacency (the two tiles share a cardinal edge whose `track_bits` connect) and tunnel adjacency (the two tiles are linked through `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` — see **Tunnels** below). `TrainPhysics` and traversal helpers use this instead of re-deriving neighbor-edge pairs ad hoc. The lower-level neighbor lookup `RailwayNetwork.get_neighbor(x, y, edge) -> Optional[TrackSegment]` follows the same two-step resolution: it returns the grid neighbour first, then falls through to `RailwayNetwork.tunnels.get((x, y, edge))` when the grid cell is empty.

**Tunnels**

- `RailwayNetwork.tunnels: Dict[Tuple[int, int, str], Tuple[int, int, str]]` pairs two dead-end edges so they behave as a single tile-to-tile connection. The map is symmetric: every pair is stored in both directions so lookups are O(1).
- The sole writer is the helper pair `RailwayNetwork.add_tunnel(a, b)` and `RailwayNetwork.remove_tunnel(endpoint)`; no other site assigns to `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` directly. `add_tunnel` enforces the tunnel invariants:
  - Endpoints are distinct.
  - Each endpoint references an existing tile whose `track_bits` include the named edge.
  - Each endpoint is a dead-end at write time — no grid neighbour connects back on the opposite edge. A tunnel fills a dead-end; it does not override real grid geometry.
  - Neither endpoint already participates in another tunnel.
  - The two edges may be any pair, opposite or not (W↔E, N↔S, N↔W, …). A non-opposite pair is needed in layouts such as zig-zag siding loops where, for example, a north-facing dead-end on one side connects to a west-facing dead-end on the other. `TrackSegment._update_dead_ends` uses `RailwayNetwork.get_connection_edges(...)` rather than the cardinal opposite, so the connectivity check follows the actual recorded pair on each tile.
- `RailwayNetwork.propagate_from_tile(...)` runs `_sweep_stale_tunnels()` at the top of every propagation. Any tunnel whose endpoint loses its track, loses the named edge from `track_bits`, or gains a real grid neighbour that connects back, is dropped from `tunnels`. The propagation that triggered the sweep then sees the cleaned table and recomputes dead-end ports through it. `RailwayNetwork.remove_track(x, y)` calls the sweep explicitly because the removed-tile path does not always touch a grid neighbour propagation.
- `TrackSegment.dead_end_ports` is computed through `RailwayNetwork.get_neighbor(...)`, so a tunneled edge is automatically excluded. Other consumers of dead-end status (the `TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM` trigger, `TrainController.travel_direction` end-of-line fall-through) read through the same `dead_end_ports` field and inherit tunnel awareness without their own table lookups.
- `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` is serialised by `to_dict()` and `to_layout_dict()` as a `tunnels` list of `{"a": {"x", "y", "edge"}, "b": {"x", "y", "edge"}}` records, one per pair, and reloaded by `from_dict()` after all tracks are in place. Malformed entries on load are logged and skipped without aborting the load.
- Blocks remain grid-contiguous: a tunneled edge is always a block boundary, and `Block.ordered_tiles` does not walk through a tunnel.
- One shared backend network-graph candidate-query / walk-metrics home lives in the common network/pathfinding layer, such as `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...) -> List[GraphWalkCandidate]`. Thin adapter methods such as `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...)` or `RailwayPathfinder.measure_along_walk(...)` may still exist, but they must all delegate to this same operational graph home. The candidate query takes tile-precise locators, an explicit switch-handling mode (`respect current state` versus `topology-only` search for later acquisition), the caller's `manual_override` flag (default `False`; `True` only when the requesting run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`), always skips disabled blocks/switches, also skips manual blocks/switches when `manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home), and returns zero or more directed walk candidates with whatever ordered tile/block walk payload the caller requested.
- `GraphWalkCandidate` carries exactly this payload. **This subsection is the canonical home for the `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` candidate comparison order. All other sites that compare candidates must cross-reference this subsection by name rather than restating the comparison rule.**

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `ordered_walk` | `List[GraphWalkStep]` | Canonical directed walk over graph steps in traversal order. Each step contains enough resource/tile/frame data for dispatcher acquisition, route target comparison, and later distance projection. |
  | `ordered_tiles` | `Optional[List[Tuple[int, int]]]` | Ordered tile IDs for callers that requested tile payload. `None` when `return_tiles=False`. |
  | `ordered_blocks` | `Optional[List[str]]` | Ordered block IDs visited by the walk, with consecutive duplicate block steps collapsed only when they represent the same directed block traversal. `None` when `return_blocks=False`. |
  | `start_locator` | `GraphLocator` | The resolved tile-precise start locator used by the graph search, including block/tile membership and optional in-tile position when supplied by the caller. |
  | `terminal_locator` | `GraphLocator` | The resolved tile-precise terminal locator reached by the candidate. |
  | `terminal_block_id` | `str` | Block ID of the terminal block reached by the candidate. |
  | `terminal_block_direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Terminal block-relative traversal direction derived from the candidate's directed frame against `Block.ordered_tiles`. |
  | `terminal_frame` | `DirectedBlockFrame` | Terminal directed block frame, including path tiles plus `entry_edge` / `exit_edge` metadata needed by dispatcher/controller projection helpers. |
  | `tile_count` | `int` | Number of tile steps in the directed walk; first comparison key. |
  | `walk_length_cm` | `float` | Centimeter length along the directed walk; second comparison key. |
  | `candidate_rank` | `int` | Stable deterministic tie-break assigned by `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` after `tile_count` and `walk_length_cm`; third comparison key. |

  Every caller that compares candidates must choose by `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank)`. `walk_length_cm` is the common physical-distance metric regardless of caller input type: a waypoint/station/platform input is first resolved into one or more concrete terminal `GraphLocator` values and compared by the same walk distance, while a direct block/tile input already supplies the terminal locator used for the same distance calculation. `candidate_rank` is only for exact ties after those graph metrics. It is assigned by the shared graph home using one deterministic ordering of the directed walk identity: ordered resource IDs, ordered tile coordinates, entry/exit edge sequence, terminal locator identity, and then any remaining stable insertion order from the graph query. Callers must not replace `candidate_rank` with waypoint-specific, UI-order, or layer-local heuristics. If those metrics still remain exactly tied across the remaining current-span end choices, use `end_a` as the final deterministic dead-heat tie-break outside `GraphWalkCandidate`; no other layer-local tie-break is allowed.

- `GraphLocator` carries exactly this payload:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `tile` | `Tuple[int, int]` | Tile coordinate anchoring the locator |
  | `resource_type` | `Optional["block" \| "switch"]` | Resource type containing the locator, when known |
  | `resource_id` | `Optional[str]` | Resource ID containing the locator, when known |
  | `block_id` | `Optional[str]` | Convenience block ID when the locator is inside a block; `None` for switch-only locators |
  | `edge` | `Optional["N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"]` | Tile edge when the locator is anchored at a graph boundary or caller knows the relevant edge |
  | `distance_cm` | `Optional[float]` | In-tile distance from `edge` along the directed traversal when the caller has a precise position; `None` for tile-only searches |
  | `physical_end` | `Optional["end_a" \| "end_b"]` | Set only for current-train-span start locators so the caller can apply the final `end_a` dead-heat tie-break outside the graph query |

- `DirectedBlockFrame` carries exactly this payload:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `block_id` | `str` | Block whose directed traversal is represented |
  | `block_direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Direction relative to `Block.ordered_tiles` |
  | `path_tiles` | `List[path_tile entry]` | Ordered tile slice through the block using the canonical path-tile entry schema |
  | `entry_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge by which the traversal enters the block frame |
  | `exit_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge by which the traversal exits the block frame |

- `GraphWalkStep` carries exactly this payload:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `step_index` | `int` | Zero-based step index inside `ordered_walk` |
  | `resource_type` | `"block" \| "switch"` | Resource traversed by this step |
  | `resource_id` | `str` | Block or switch ID for the traversed resource |
  | `tile` | `Tuple[int, int]` | Tile coordinate for this graph step |
  | `entry_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge used to enter this tile/resource step |
  | `exit_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge used to exit this tile/resource step |
  | `block_id` | `Optional[str]` | Block ID when `resource_type == "block"` |
  | `block_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse"]` | Block-relative traversal direction when the step is inside a block |
  | `switch_required_state` | `Optional[int]` | Required switch state for switch traversal when topology search determines one |
  | `length_cm` | `float` | Physical length contribution of this step |

- Operational candidate comparison, filtering, and multi-candidate enumeration live only at this backend home. The frontend editor pathfinders in `static/js/pathfinders.js` (`ConstructionPathfinder.findPath(...)` and `ExistingTrackPathfinder.findPathOnExistingTracks(...)`) answer connectivity for the track editor and return a single path. They never sort by `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)`, never apply the disabled-block / disabled-switch / manual-block / manual-switch filters, and never apply `Block.allowed_travel_directions` or `requested_destination_direction`. The comparison order has exactly one implementation site and each operational filter has exactly one application site. Callers that need ranked candidates or filter-aware paths — including UI features — route through `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` rather than re-implementing comparison or filtering on the client. The frontend and backend must agree on one shared primitive: entering a tile on one segment of a switch limits the valid exits to that segment's paired edge. The frontend encodes this in `ConstructionPathfinder.getExitsForEntry(trackBits, entry)`; the backend encodes it in `TrackSegment.get_exits(entry, use_switch_state=False)`.

**Pathfinding-home migration**

- The current operational backend path search lives in `PathDispatcher.find_paths(start_block_id, end_block_id, train_id=None) -> List[PathSegment]` / `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_paths(...)`. That contract is insufficient for the v1 contracts above because it is block-to-block and distance-sorted only; it does not support direction-aware terminal rejection, locator-level (tile-precise) start positions, explicit switch-handling mode, the canonical `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, final end_a dead-heat tie-break)` comparison rule, or measure-along-walk projection. The refactor must extend or extract that existing backend pathfinding home so it exposes at least:
  - `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(request: GraphWalkQuery) -> List[GraphWalkCandidate]` — direction-aware network-graph search that accepts a `GraphWalkQuery` request payload (defined below). It accepts tile-precise start and end locators (not just block IDs), always skips disabled blocks/switches, also skips manual blocks/switches when `request.manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home), either respects current switch state or searches topology-only according to `request.switch_handling`, rejects candidates whose terminal traversal does not satisfy `request.requested_destination_direction`, also rejects candidates whose traversal violates `Block.allowed_travel_directions` when `request.manual_override == False` (per the same canonical home), and returns comparable walk candidates plus whichever ordered tile/block walk payload the request flags asked for.

  `GraphWalkQuery` carries exactly:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `start_locator` | `GraphLocator` | Tile-precise start locator. |
  | `end_locator` | `GraphLocator` | Tile-precise end locator. |
  | `switch_handling` | `"respect_current_state" \| "topology_only"` | Whether the search respects current `Switch.position` or searches topology-only for later acquisition. |
  | `requested_destination_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse"]` | Optional terminal direction filter; candidates whose terminal traversal does not match are rejected. |
  | `exclude_resources` | `Optional[Set[str]]` | Optional set of resource IDs to exclude from the search (used by retry / extension logic). |
  | `manual_override` | `bool = False` | Operator-override flag per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home. When `False`, manual blocks/switches are filtered out exactly like disabled ones and `Block.allowed_travel_directions` is enforced. When `True` (forwarded from a run whose route stores `Route.manual_override = True` — operator-initiated routes), manual resources participate normally and `Block.allowed_travel_directions` is not enforced; disabled resources are still filtered. |
  | `return_tiles` | `bool = True` | Whether to populate `GraphWalkCandidate.ordered_tiles`. |
  | `return_blocks` | `bool = True` | Whether to populate `GraphWalkCandidate.ordered_blocks`. |
  - `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...) -> Optional[GraphWalkCandidate]` — optional thin adapter over `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` for callers that specifically need the chosen ordered walk payload.
  - `RailwayPathfinder.measure_along_walk(ordered_walk, locator_a, locator_b) -> float` — in-walk centimeter distance projection between two locators on an already-chosen walk, used by lookahead and platform-end pair comparison.
  - `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints(start_block_id, include_stations=True) -> List[str]` — topological reachability enumeration that returns every block (and optionally every station) reachable from `start_block_id`. The walk steps tile-by-tile, so switch tiles do not act as graph barriers. The walk does not consult current switch state, does not apply `Block.allowed_travel_directions`, and knows nothing about any train; callers that need direction filtering use `find_ordered_walk` / `query_graph_walks` instead. Used by the route editor's block-to-block feasibility highlighting, where there is no placed train and any travel direction is allowed.
