  - `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints_for_span(head_seeds, occupied_tiles, occupied_blocks, include_stations=True) -> List[str]` — span-aware reachability for one placed train. `head_seeds` is the list of `(tile, outward_edge)` pairs for the train's two physical span ends, where `outward_edge` is the network edge pointing away from the body at that end. The walk starts only from those seeds in those outward directions, steps tile-by-tile through switch tiles exactly like `get_reachable_endpoints`, and never steps onto a tile in `occupied_tiles`; `occupied_blocks` are removed from the result. So it returns exactly the blocks (and stations) the train can reach driving forward out of its head or in reverse out of its tail, never back through its own body or through a switch its body is sitting on. `PathDispatcher.get_reachable_endpoints_for_train(train_id, include_stations=True)` resolves the train's current span and its two end-outward edges and delegates here; the Quick Route tool's destination highlighting calls it. Each end's outward edge is a purely geometric value — the network edge of that end's tile pointing away from the consist body — computed from the span geometry (`end_a` / `end_b` tile + `entry_edge`) alone, independent of cab selection. It is not derived from `travel_direction` (which is cab-dependent, describes only the cab/head end, and is `None` when no cab is selected); both span ends always have a well-defined outward edge, so the tool works regardless of whether a cab is selected.
  - the `GraphWalkCandidate` table above as the only candidate payload contract.
- Every layer that needs pathfinding shares this single home: `RouteExecutor` platform-end selection, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` start-side and forward candidate selection, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` suffix selection, and the boundary-projection helpers all route through it.
- The implementation must use the following function table as the canonical operational pathfinding surface. If the shared graph home is implemented as methods on `PathDispatcher` rather than an extracted `RailwayPathfinder` class, keep the inputs/outputs and single-home rule identical.

  | Function | Inputs | Output | Required callers / need | Current nearby implementation |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `RailwayNetwork.get_connection_edges(tile_a, tile_b)` | tile coordinates that are either grid-adjacent or linked through `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` | `Optional[Tuple[str, str]]` edge on `tile_a`, edge on `tile_b` | `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`, graph traversal, directed frame construction | Implemented; consolidates the grid-adjacency check and the tunnel fallback used by `RailwayNetwork.get_neighbor(...)`. See the **Tunnels** canonical home above. |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(request)` | `request: GraphWalkQuery` | `List[GraphWalkCandidate]` sorted/comparable by candidate metrics | `RouteExecutor` platform-end selection, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` start-side selection, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` candidate selection, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` suffix selection | Closest backend code is `PathDispatcher.find_paths(...)`, `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_paths(...)`, `PathDispatcher._find_extension_paths(...)`, and `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_extension_paths(...)`; these must be extended/extracted. `static/js/pathfinders.js` has `ConstructionPathfinder.findPath(...)` and `ExistingTrackPathfinder.findPathOnExistingTracks(...)`, but those are editor/construction helpers and are not the runtime operational home. |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...)` | Same as `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` plus selection policy when needed | `Optional[GraphWalkCandidate]` chosen by canonical comparison | Thin convenience adapter for callers that need one chosen walk rather than all candidates | No exact current equivalent; closest is `PathDispatcher.find_paths(...)[0]` after sorting, but that lacks new frame semantics |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.measure_along_walk(ordered_walk, locator_a, locator_b)` | chosen `ordered_walk` and two `GraphLocator` values on that walk | signed or absolute centimeter distance as specified by caller | lookahead projection, platform-end pair comparison, graph-nearest route reconciliation | Current `PathSegment.distance_cm` is whole-path only and is not sufficient for locator-to-locator projection |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints(start_block_id, include_stations=True)` | starting block id, station-inclusion flag | `List[str]` of reachable block ids (plus station ids when included) | route-editor block-to-block feasibility highlighting (no placed train, any direction) | Lives at the pathfinder home; `PathDispatcher.get_reachable_endpoints(...)` is a thin wrapper that delegates here so existing callers keep their entry point |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints_for_span(head_seeds, occupied_tiles, occupied_blocks, include_stations=True)` | the two span ends as `(tile, outward_edge)` seeds, the train's occupied tiles and blocks, station-inclusion flag | `List[str]` of reachable block ids (plus station ids when included), excluding the train's own occupied blocks | Quick Route tool destination highlighting, via `PathDispatcher.get_reachable_endpoints_for_train(train_id, include_stations=True)` | Span-aware companion to `get_reachable_endpoints`: same tile-by-tile switch traversal, but seeded from the train's head/tail outward edges (resolved through its `TrainController`) and forbidden from entering the body's occupied tiles |
  | `RailwayNetwork.project_authority_boundary_delta(head_locator, authority_end, safety_margin_cm)` | `head_locator` (a `GraphLocator` carrying `tile`, `edge`, `distance_cm`) plus `authority_end` (carrying `block_id` and `terminal_frame: DirectedBlockFrame`) | signed centimeter delta; positive means head is past safety-margin boundary, negative means still short | controller braking diagnostics and stop-boundary projection | No exact current equivalent; must use shared graph/geometry helpers rather than dispatcher path records |

**Manual mode and movement-authority boundary**

- In `simulation` submode, the driver should call `set_handle()`, the controller should store `handle_position`, and `TrainPhysics` should integrate it into speed while ATC can still override with braking.
- In `power_percent` submode, the driver should call `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent()`, the controller should store `manual.manual_power_percent`, and `TrainPhysics` should convert it through `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` in the same tick whenever no braking override is active.
- In that same `power_percent` submode, the converted power-percent command is the absolute voltage/speed command and overrides the normal acceleration/deceleration-rate fields while no safety braking override is active. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` must set the `span_update.current_speed_kmh` for that tick to the calibrated speed implied by `manual.manual_power_percent`; the model assumes negligible momentum under direct voltage control, so there is no service-rate ramp between manual power-percent settings. Service braking and EB do not use a handle-based braking flow in `power_percent` submode: when controller safety logic requests braking (ATC override, service braking, or EB), the controller clamps the manual target speed down to the ATC limit at distance 0 and forces `manual_power_percent = 0` for that tick (with EB additionally engaging `eb.active`). ATC outranks the manual command: a positive ATC cap holds the train at that cap, while a zero cap (EB, no cab, or no/at-end authority) yields the calibrated zero-speed/zero-voltage outcome from `power_speed_curve`. There is no signed negative power-percent command.
- In manual mode with a received path plan, add `_calculate_authority_boundary_limit()` so the controller brakes to the current authority end boundary using the controller safety margin.
- Without a received path plan, manual mode receives the same no-authority empty lookahead defined in step `22a9` (`atc_limit = 0` at distance 0, `atc_limit_reason_code = no_authority`). There is no fallback to the current-block boundary; if a manual-mode train is rolling when authority is removed, the ATC overspeed branch escalates to EB per step `826a`.

**Completion ownership and timing**

- `TrainController` is the sole owner of post-physics usable-authority stop interpretation and `PathDispatcher` the sole owner of path completion commit. The controller exposes whether the train is now stopped at the usable end of live authority; dispatcher decides whether that completed stop applies to the active path and then mutates path/resource/route state.
- Let `TrainController` react to dispatcher-committed completion through `on_path_complete()`, but stop letting the controller decide independently that a path-backed run is complete.
- `PathDispatcher` is the sole owner of the `Path` record underlying `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan`; `on_path_complete()` is a reaction hook, not an authority-mutating hook.
- Path completion is emitted only after the train has already moved for that tick, the post-physics controller stop check says the train is now stopped at the usable end of live authority under the canonical usable-authority stop predicate, and `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` accepts it for the active path by verifying both that `Path.authority_end_block_id == Path.requested_destination_block_id` and that the terminal authority traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`. The controller safety margin remains a braking/diagnostic target; it is not a separate centimeter-tolerance completion gate.

**Controller invariants**

- There should be no `self.target_speed` field once lookahead-at-distance-0 becomes the canonical source of target speed.
- Controller-owned live control/physical state and train-owned persistent/configuration state should be cleanly separated.
- Controller ATO destination state comes exclusively from `received_path_plan`; there is no fallback read against `Train`.
- `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` should preserve the 1:1 train/controller pairing without recreating authority from train-side runtime mirrors. Its job is only "make sure a controller object exists and is registered for this `train_id`." In v1, controller existence is a hard precondition for any dispatcher path creation/extension: callers must ensure the controller exists before `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`. `SimulationController` must not construct or write a path payload itself, and there is no late-bind recovery hook such as `PathDispatcher.on_controller_available(train_id)` — `received_path_plan` is a property that resolves live, so no late controller write is needed.

## TrainPhysics

**Movement-authority input in `TrainPhysics`**

- `TrainPhysics` should continue to execute physical integration (speed update, span/end-locator update, occupancy tracing, and tail tracing), but the canonical live storage is `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` and `TrainController.physics_sync.current_speed_kmh`. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` computes each `span_update` (`new_span`, `current_speed_kmh`, ordered `added_tiles`, ordered `removed_tiles`, and traversal frame metadata) and passes it to `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`, which applies the controller-owned storage write. `TrainPhysics` should not own path identity, dispatcher authority state, progress interpretation, completion, or a separate canonical position store on `Train`.
- To choose exits on ambiguous geometry, `TrainPhysics` normally needs a controller-provided, read-only authority-geometry projection derived from `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan.resource_sequence`. The minimum movement data is the ordered `resource_sequence` / `path_tiles` geometry from that active plan, plus enough local direction context to choose the correct next edge through the current tile.
- `TrainPhysics` does **not** need dispatcher ownership fields such as `path_id` to step the train, and it does not need requested/authorized centimeter stop targets for physical integration. Centimeter stop-target fields are not part of the v1 path/controller contract; controller braking logic uses authority-boundary geometry plus the controller safety margin.
- `TrainPhysics` obtains that geometry projection from `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan`; it is the only path-geometry movement input.
- `TrainPhysics` should read the current live position through the controller-owned span and should write movement only by sending `span_update` to `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`, not through any train-entity position field. Initial placement is established by the API `SimulationController.add_train(...)` flow writing `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before the first tick. (Persistence and save-data migration are Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.)
- Exception for cancellation/revocation braking: if the dispatcher-owned path has already transitioned out of `ACTIVE` while the train is still rolling, `TrainPhysics` must still keep moving the train according to speed and the current live network state. That post-revocation movement relies on the physical `Switch.position` / live track geometry that exists in the network at that moment, not on retained authority locks or a second copy of path ownership surviving in the controller.
- Once live switch state ownership moves to `Switch`, `TrainPhysics._advance_end()` and tail tracing must call the updated `TrackSegment` traversal helpers using the resolver input defined by the `TrackSegment` helper contract above.

**Block occupancy**

- Route normal tail-vacated transitions through `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, which routes block releases through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` so `OCCUPIED -> CAUTION -> FREE` stays in one state machine.
- `TrainPhysics` must not write `Block` fields directly and must not call block-state mutators for normal occupancy, CAUTION, or release. It computes movement `span_update` values, and the controller/dispatcher feedback chain performs all block writes through `BlockManager`.
- Block tail-vacate observations come from the Step-0 detector (debounced no-current; see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), never from tile-level span arithmetic: `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` includes a block in `tail_vacated_resources` only when the detector slice has confirmed that block clear, so `PathDispatcher` calls `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` only on detector-confirmed clears.
- Normal CAUTION clearing happens inside `on_block_tail_vacated()` via `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`. There is no separate `clear_block()` / `release_block()` step.

**Tail-vacate flow in `TrainPhysics`**

- `TrainPhysics` does not call `BlockManager` mutators directly; CAUTION transitions go through `on_block_tail_vacated()` via the detector-confirmed tail-vacate path (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection).
- On tail-vacate, `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` includes a typed resource-release observation in `tail_vacated_resources`, packages it into the same `ControllerProgressReport`, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) for the train's Phase 3 consume pass. For blocks, the controller reports the vacate only when the Step-0 detector slice has confirmed the block clear (debounced no-current per **Power**, Block occupancy detection); the calculated span is not the block-vacate authority. For switches, tile-level vacate of the switch resource in the calculated span is sufficient. Block IDs arrive directly on the detector slice; switch IDs come from `TrackSegment.switch_id` on switch tiles via the same tile-lookup mechanism used for entry detection. The controller report carries block tail-vacate `direction` as the vacated block's forward `exit_edge` for the traversal by which the train left that block. During Phase 3, `PathDispatcher` routes the release through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)`: blocks go through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` so `OCCUPIED -> CAUTION -> FREE` stays in one state machine; switches go through `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)`, which removes the matching `Switch.physical_occupants[route]` entry first and then, only when no entries remain for that train, clears train-owned protection sourced from that vacated switch.
- `TrainPhysics` does not track its own `_caution_blocks`; `BlockManager` owns the full release lifecycle.

**Voltage behavior**

- `track_voltage`, `power_percent`, and `speed_kmh` are three different values. **Canonical home for `track_voltage` is `TrainPhysics`**: it is computed each tick from the authoritative source for the active mode (controller/physics speed solution in ATO/manual-simulation; in manual `power_percent` mode, `manual.manual_power_percent` is mapped to `track_voltage` through the inverse of `SpeedProfile.voltage_power_curve` — the stage-1 `(track_voltage, power_percent)` curve — while the calibrated `speed_kmh` for that same operating point comes from `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve`). `track_voltage` is always reached through `voltage_power_curve`, never through `power_speed_curve` (which has no voltage axis). `track_voltage` is not a stored field on `TrainController` or persisted on `Train`; it is per-tick derived state owned by `TrainPhysics` and emitted to `BlockPowerController` as that tick's hardware output.
- `TrainPhysics` should pass the current `track_voltage` value to `BlockPowerController` as the software-to-hardware output layer for block voltage emission.
- The active mode determines which value is authoritative. In ATO and manual `simulation` mode, `speed_kmh` is the controller/physics source of truth and `power_percent` / `track_voltage` are derived from that current operating point. In manual `power_percent` mode, `manual.manual_power_percent` is the source of truth and `speed_kmh` / `track_voltage` are derived from that commanded power level in the same movement update; the manual power command sets the absolute calibrated voltage/speed point instead of being integrated through the normal service acceleration/deceleration rates.
- In manual `power_percent` mode, that authority applies only while no controller braking override is active. There is no handle-based speed-up/speed-down integration in this submode: without a braking override, `TrainPhysics` uses the calibrated `power_percent -> speed_kmh` result directly as the movement speed for that tick. If ATC/service braking or EB is active, the controller clamps the manual target speed down to the ATC limit at distance 0 and forces `manual_power_percent = 0` for that tick (with EB additionally engaging `eb.active`); ATC outranks the manual command. A positive ATC cap holds the train at that cap; a zero cap (EB, no cab, or no/at-end authority) yields the calibrated zero-power / zero-speed outcome rather than a handle-based braking command.
- In `power_percent` manual mode, `TrainPhysics` should use the profile's voltage/power/speed calibration directly rather than reading removed scalar target-speed fields or applying the service acceleration/deceleration rates to the manual-power command. The calibration must be usable in both directions so physics can convert between the authoritative value for the active mode and the derived outputs it needs to maintain. These mappings are owned by `SpeedProfile` and should be documented exactly this way:
  - `track_voltage <-> power_percent`
  - `power_percent <-> speed_kmh`
- When `TrainPhysics` needs `track_voltage`, it derives it from the current authoritative mode input: `speed_kmh -> power_percent -> track_voltage` in ATO and manual `simulation` mode, or `manual_power_percent -> track_voltage` plus the calibrated absolute `speed_kmh` command in manual `power_percent` mode. In manual `power_percent` mode without a braking override, that calibrated `speed_kmh` is written into the same `PhysicsSpanUpdate.current_speed_kmh` that carries the movement result.
- Do not introduce extra names for these concepts: the doc speaks in voltage, power percent, and speed, and the three mappings stay distinct.

**Physics invariants**

- `TrainPhysics` should not own manual-mode policy; it should execute controller commands and maintain physical state only.
- Future authority is held by `Block.reserved_by_path` / `Block.reserved_for_train` on the block side and `Switch.locks` on the switch side; `TrainPhysics` holds no path-lock state of its own. Movement is geometry-driven; once the train enters a resource, switch integrity during traversal is enforced by `Switch.occupied_by` plus any `Switch.train_protection_claims`.

## SpeedProfile

`SpeedProfile` carries motion-rate data plus per-train calibration in the vocabulary the rest of the system should use directly: voltage, power percent, and speed. The calibration defines bidirectional mappings between `track_voltage` and `power_percent`, and between `power_percent` and calibrated `speed_kmh`.
- `SpeedProfile` fields:
  - `profile_id`: unique profile identifier.
  - `name`: display name for UI/debug/profile selection.
  - `max_voltage`: top of the usable `track_voltage` range for the first mapping stage.
  - `voltage_power_curve`: stored calibration mapping `(track_voltage, power_percent)` for the first stage. Its v1 default is linear across the usable range, from `lighting_voltage` (0%) to `max_voltage` (100%).
  - `power_speed_curve`: stored calibration mapping `(power_percent, speed_kmh)` for the second stage.
  - `lighting_voltage`: the single voltage at which the train is lit but not yet moving. The stopped-but-lit states output it, and it is also the 0%-power point (bottom) of `voltage_power_curve`: at exactly this voltage power is 0 and the train holds, and just above it the train begins to move. It is therefore the minimum `track_voltage` that produces movement.
  - `acceleration_rate`: service acceleration rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `deceleration_rate`: service braking rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `emergency_brake_rate`: emergency-brake deceleration rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `friction_rate`: passive/coasting deceleration rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `operational_decel_ratio`: comfort-braking fraction used when ATO builds operational braking curves.
  - `air_resistance_enabled`: whether the drag model is active.
  - `drag_coefficient`: drag strength used by the drag model.
  - `reference_speed_kmh`: reference speed used to scale the drag model.

**SpeedProfile constraint**

- `SpeedProfile` is not a speed limit object. It expresses motion-rate data and per-train calibration (`lighting_voltage`, `max_voltage`, `voltage_power_curve`, `power_speed_curve`), not any form of speed ceiling. Train maximum speed and block maximum speed remain separate, independently enforced constraints.

**Speed-profile addition**

- Calibration wording uses three explicit values: `track_voltage`, `power_percent`, and `speed_kmh`.
- The calibration has two bidirectional mappings and both should be stated directly in the doc:
  1. `track_voltage <-> power_percent`
  2. `power_percent <-> speed_kmh`
- `voltage_power_curve` is the stored `(track_voltage, power_percent)` mapping used by the first step. `lighting_voltage` is the minimum `track_voltage` that produces movement; below it the train does not move (and it is the voltage the stopped-but-lit states output). `max_voltage` is the top of the usable range. In v1 the default `voltage_power_curve` is linear across that usable range, from `lighting_voltage` (0%) to `max_voltage` (100%), so reversing from `power_percent` back to `track_voltage` uses the inverse interpolation of this same stored curve.
- `power_speed_curve` is the stored `(power_percent, speed_kmh)` mapping used by the second step. `power_percent` is the intermediate normalized control value between voltage and speed; it is not a second name for either of them. In v1 this may be linear by default, but it is still stored explicitly as profile calibration data rather than inferred ad hoc elsewhere. Reverse lookup from `speed_kmh` to `power_percent` uses the inverse interpolation of this same stored curve.
- The motion-rate fields (`acceleration_rate`, `deceleration_rate`) govern ATO, manual `simulation` handle integration, service/emergency braking, and coasting; they do not shape manual `power_percent` response (that submode applies the calibrated speed immediately, see TrainPhysics).
- Every train should have a default `SpeedProfile` carrying these calibration fields so the `power_percent` manual mode always has a defined voltage/power/speed mapping, even before per-train tuning is customized.

## Power

**Hardware interface boundary**

- The runtime drives and reads physical hardware only through abstract interfaces addressed by logical id (`block_id`, `switch_id`), never by pin or wire: `OutputInterface` for block track voltage, `SwitchOutputInterface` for switch throw, and `InputInterface` for hardware sensor reads. Each has a simulated default for in-memory runs (`SimulatedOutputInterface`, `SimulatedSwitchOutputInterface`, `SimulatedInputInterface`) and a real-hardware implementation behind the same contract. `BlockPowerController` and `SwitchPowerController` (below) are the only callers of the output interfaces and hold no wiring knowledge themselves.
- **No wiring knowledge above the interface.** No runtime, control, route, physics, or manager code names a GPIO pin, expander chip, shift-register bit, or bus type. A concrete implementation owns the map from logical id to physical channel and is the only place that knows the wiring. Direct GPIO, a 74HC595 / 74HC165 shift-register chain, and an I²C expander such as MCP23017 are interchangeable implementations of the same interfaces; choosing one changes which implementation is constructed and nothing else. (Per **Out of Scope**, v1 keeps that map in code, not a config file.)
- **Simulated fault injection.** The default simulated interfaces model happy hardware: every write succeeds and every read agrees with the command, so a simulated throw always confirms (see **Switch power-controller role**). A simulated interface may instead be built to inject a failed or late throw, an out-of-range readback, or a refused write. This is the v1 way to exercise the unconfirmed-throw path and the safe-state path in software, with no real hardware and without modeling fault dynamics (held **Out of Scope**).
- **Input / sensing boundary.** `InputInterface` is the read side for sensors that no software output drives — block current detection and switch position feedback. (The block track-voltage readback that throw verification consumes is the output interface reading back its own output, `OutputInterface.get_actual_voltage`, not an `InputInterface` read.) Block current detection is the block-occupancy authority; its read, debounce, attribution, and position reconciliation are defined in **Block occupancy detection** below. Switch position feedback stays diagnostics only — available to UI/debug read models on demand and never authority — because switch-alignment authority is the commanded `Switch.position`. Treating any sensor as a fault-detection, verification, or recovery source is **Out of Scope** for v1.
- **Safe state / fail-safe.** Each output interface defines a safe state — every block `track voltage` at `0` and every switch motor de-energized — reached through an `enter_safe_state()` call. The runtime issues that call when it stops driving outputs (simulation stop or process shutdown); the simulated interface zeroes its stored outputs, and real hardware de-energizes the track and point motors. Because a crashed process cannot issue any command, a real output implementation also arms a hardware watchdog that drops the same outputs to the safe state when the control loop stops refreshing them. The watchdog timing lives in the implementation; the design models only the safe-state command and the rule that loss of control reaches the safe state. This loss-of-control fail-safe is separate from the fault dynamics held **Out of Scope**.

**Block occupancy detection**

- Each electrically-isolated block has a current detector. Current present on a block means a train is standing on it; an open circuit means no train is. This detection is the authority for `Block.occupied_by` — it replaces any guess from the calculated train position. Switch occupancy is not detected this way: switch-alignment authority stays the commanded `Switch.position` (see **Out of Scope**), and a switch's physical entry/exit still comes from the train's calculated traversal.
- **One global read per tick.** At the top of each tick the runtime reads every block's detector at once through `InputInterface.read_block_currents()`, which returns a plain `block_id -> current present` map with no train identity (a real detector only knows on/off per block). The simulated default `SimulatedInputInterface` builds that map from the model — a block reads current when some train's live span covers it — so a run with no real hardware still drives occupancy "by calculation". The read reflects where trains were at the end of the previous tick, so a block the head just entered is confirmed one tick later; that one-tick lag is the honest sensor latency.
- **Debounce with on/off thresholds.** Real contacts flicker: a block a train is standing on can briefly read open. `BlockOccupancyDetector` keeps a per-block counter and confirms a block OCCUPIED only after `OCCUPANCY_ON_THRESHOLD` consecutive current reads, and CLEAR only after `OCCUPANCY_OFF_THRESHOLD` consecutive no-current reads. The bias is fail-safe: occupy quickly, clear slowly, so a momentary open circuit inside the train's span does not drop the block. Both thresholds live in `constants.py`. Each tick the detector compares the whole confirmed-occupied set against the previous one, so a fast train that enters or clears several blocks in one tick is reported in one pass.
- **Placement and restore are adopted, not re-detected.** Some blocks become occupied outside the detector: `place_train` sets `Block.occupied_by` directly when a train is put on the track (as does runtime state restore). At the top of each tick the detector adopts every already-occupied block as already confirmed, so a block a train was placed on is not reported as a fresh entry. Such a block still clears normally once its current drops for the off-window.
- **Attribution.** The raw read carries no train identity, so each confirmed change is matched to a train from the live authority model. A newly OCCUPIED block is matched in order: first the train it is reserved for (`Block.reserved_for_train`, the reserved-ahead corridor); failing that, the train whose live span currently covers it. A newly CLEAR block is matched to its current holder (`Block.occupied_by`). A confirmed entry that matches no known train at all is attributed to an **unknown train**: `Block.occupied_by` is set to the reserved sentinel `UNKNOWN_TRAIN_ID` (a value no real train uses) and surfaced to UI/diagnostics as an unmodeled occupant, rather than committed to a wrong train or silently dropped. Such a block then participates in availability like any occupied block — it blocks reservation and clean entry there — until its current clears for the off-window, at which point the unknown occupant is removed. (Under the simulated detector this never arises: its current map is built from live train spans, so every confirmed-occupied block already matches a modeled train. An unknown occupant appears only with real hardware reading current on a block no software train covers.)
- **The confirmed change flows through the normal Phase-3 path.** The detector hands each train its entered/vacated block sets; the controller places them in its Phase-3 progress report alongside the switch events it still derives from the calculated traversal, and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` commits them exactly as before (see the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**): block entry through `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)`, tail-vacate through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)`. The detector changes only the source of the block entered/vacated observations; every consumer downstream is unchanged. Because the off-debounce confirms a clear several ticks after the tail physically left the block, the controller captures that block's forward exit edge the tick the tail clears it and holds it until the debounced vacate fires, so `on_block_tail_vacated` receives the same caution `direction` it would have under an immediate precise-traversal release.
- **Physical detection outranks the calculated position.** When the detector confirms a block entered or vacated but the calculated span has not caught up, `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(...)` slides the rigid span forward one tile at a time until the head has reached every confirmed-entered block and the tail has cleared every confirmed-vacated block — to the block seam at least. It only ever moves the train forward, never backward. `reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(...)` computes the corrected span, but `TrainController` remains the committing writer of `current_span` (the canonical owner per **Read accessors**): the Step-0 preamble supplies the detected entered/vacated slice and the forward-snap target, and the controller applies the corrected span, so the single-writer rule for `current_span` holds even though the snap originates in the preamble. Under the simulated sensor the calculated span already agrees, so this never moves; it becomes active only with real hardware, or an early/late sensor, that leads the calculated position.
- **Not a latch.** The debounce counter is a filter that turns a noisy current stream into a confirmed value, and the snap is an immediate one-directional position correction. Neither defers a command across ticks waiting on a gating predicate, so neither is a latch and neither appears in the **Latch table**.
- **Two views of occupancy.** The calculated span's `occupied_blocks` is where the train geometrically is; `Block.occupied_by` is what the detector confirms. They agree except inside the debounce windows — a just-entered block is still `reserved_for_train` until entry confirms, and a just-cleared block stays `occupied_by` for the fail-safe window. Block track voltage tracks the calculated occupied-plus-reserved set, not the debounced set, so the rail under the train is never dead during the clear-debounce window (see **Block power-controller role**).

**Block power-controller role**

- `BlockPowerController` is focused on the software-to-hardware boundary only: it accepts a voltage number and applies it to block outputs.
- `TrainPhysics` computes `track_voltage` from the current authoritative operating input for the active mode: `speed_kmh -> power_percent -> track_voltage` in ATO and manual `simulation` mode, or `manual_power_percent -> track_voltage` plus the calibrated absolute `speed_kmh` command in manual `power_percent` mode. In manual `power_percent` mode without a braking override, the calibrated speed is effective immediately in `PhysicsSpanUpdate.current_speed_kmh`. `BlockPowerController` applies that already-computed voltage to the hardware-facing block outputs.
- **Energized set: the train's occupied blocks plus its reserved-ahead corridor.** Each tick `TrainPhysics` drives the train's single `track_voltage` onto every block that train holds: the blocks it occupies and the reserved-ahead authority corridor it is about to cross into (`Block.reserved_for_train` for that train, the future reservations written by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`). Pre-energizing the reserved corridor at the same voltage as the occupied blocks means the loco never crosses a block boundary into a dead block — the block ahead is already at running voltage, so it does not lose speed at the insulated joint. All held blocks of one train carry the same value.
- **Idle blocks are de-energized when their holder clears.** A block that no train holds — no `occupied_by` and no `reserved_for_train` — carries `0` track voltage. When a train vacates a block (its tail clears) or a path releases the block's reservation, `BlockManager` queues that block; after every train has emitted its `track_voltage` for the tick, `SimulationController` drains the queue (`BlockManager.take_blocks_to_deenergize()`) and drives each queued block that is still unheld to `0`. A block re-reserved in the same tick stays energized, because the drain re-checks the block before zeroing it. This drops a block in the train's wake back to `0` the moment its reservation is released and the train has vacated it, so the energized set stays exactly the union of every train's occupied-plus-reserved blocks. It is the routine idle output, distinct from the loss-of-control **safe state** (Hardware interface boundary above) that zeroes every output at once.
- Do not let it own manual-mode state or read `manual_target_speed`.
- The block-voltage read surface (`BlockPowerController.get_block_voltage(block_id)` over `OutputInterface.get_actual_voltage(block_id)`) is the source the switch throw continuity check consumes. On real hardware that read returns a measured value (a sensor/ADC reading) and electrical continuity through aligned switches is physical. In simulation the raw per-block read returns the last value written for that block; the continuity-aware node read used by throw verification is defined in the **Switch power-controller role** below.

**Switch power-controller role**

- `SwitchPowerController` is the software-to-hardware boundary for switch *throw* output — the switch-side counterpart to `BlockPowerController`. It accepts a target alignment (`0 = MAIN`, `1 = DIVERGE`, matching `Switch.position`) and drives the point motor. In a DC point motor the target maps to drive *polarity*: one polarity throws toward MAIN, the reverse toward DIVERGE. This `throw voltage` is a separate electrical output from the block `track_voltage` emitted by `BlockPowerController`; the two are different wires and are never compared to each other.
- Switch throw goes through the abstract `SwitchOutputInterface` per the **Hardware interface boundary** above — a `SimulatedSwitchOutputInterface` for in-memory runs and a real implementation (such as an H-bridge) for hardware. `SwitchPowerController` owns no routing, lock, or availability policy; it drives the motor and reports the throw result.
- The throw is emitted whenever the canonical `Switch.position` mutation workflow (see the **Switch entity** section) actually changes the commanded alignment. A no-op acquisition that finds the switch already in the requested state does not re-drive the motor.
- **Throw continuity verification.** After a throw, `SwitchManager` confirms the switch physically moved by checking electrical continuity between the switch's common (entry) leg and the leg it was just thrown toward. When a switch is aligned to a branch, that branch is electrically bonded to the entry leg and the open branch is isolated, so the entry and the aligned branch carry the same `track voltage`. The check compares the entry block's track voltage to the commanded target branch block's track voltage and confirms the throw only when they match.
  - The three blocks (entry, MAIN branch, DIVERGE branch) are derived from network geometry: the switch tile's common leg is the entry edge, its two switchable exits are the branch edges, and each edge resolves through its neighbour tile to a block via the `RailwayNetwork` tile-to-block index.
  - The check requires a single common entry leg. A tile that carries multiple independent through-routes and so has no single common entry — a scissors or double crossover, for example — offers no entry-vs-branch comparison, so verification is skipped for it. This follows from the tile geometry; it is not a per-`switch_type` branch.
  - The comparison is sign- and tolerance-aware (track voltage may be negative for reverse polarity). It is conclusive only when exactly one of the two compared blocks is energized and the other is unpowered, so the only way the entry voltage can appear on the branch block is through the switch. If both blocks are independently energized, or neither is, the result is indeterminate: verification is skipped and the throw is accepted without a continuity verdict. The check reads the track voltages currently applied to those blocks (the most recent `BlockPowerController` emission). The entry leg is energized whenever the holding train occupies or has reserved it; the branch leg is acquired — and the switch thrown and verified — in the same acquisition step, before that tick's voltage emission energizes the newly-reserved branch (per the energized-set rule in **Block power-controller role**), so the branch is still independently unpowered at the verdict even though the rest of the train's reserved corridor is energized. The comparison is therefore meaningful while a train is driving the entry side, and stays single-sided at verification time.
- **Unconfirmed throw.** Verification behaves the same way for both callers, and never rolls back `Switch.position` or auto-retries in v1; the commanded alignment stays authoritative for routing and geometry. On a mismatch it reports the throw as failed through the channel the caller already uses and emits a `NotificationCenter` warning: the manual/API entry `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)` returns `(False, reason)` so the operator's `command_result` notification (see the **Switch entity** section) reports the throw as unconfirmed, and a dispatcher-acquisition throw fails that switch's lock acquisition, which rolls back through the ordinary `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` failure path (the rollback releases the locks; it does not restore an earlier `Switch.position`). A confirmed throw — or one skipped as not-verifiable or indeterminate — returns success.
- **Simulation vs real hardware.** The verification logic is identical in both; the only difference is where the block readings come from. On real hardware the continuity read is a measured value (sensor/ADC) and can disagree with the command when the physical switch did not move. In simulation there is no measured voltage, so the reading is derived: the continuity-aware read bonds two blocks when the switch between them is aligned — by its commanded `Switch.position` — to the branch that joins them, and returns the single energized source voltage on each connected group of bonded blocks. Because that bond follows the same `Switch.position` the check is verifying against, a simulated reading is always as-expected and a simulated throw always confirms. The failed-throw path is therefore reached only on real hardware (or with a fault-injecting simulated interface, per the **Hardware interface boundary** above); the simulation models happy hardware.

## Data Flow To UI

**API payload ownership**

- Each runtime/entity owner should expose its own API payload through its own `to_dict()` (or equivalent read-model method) rather than merging changed live fields back into another entity's payload. `Train.to_dict()` owns persistent/configuration train data. `TrainController.to_dict()` owns live onboard/control/physical diagnostics. Dispatcher-owned path progress is exposed through `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)`.
- In v1, any composite runtime snapshot that currently returns the network/train view must expose three sibling payload groups with this exact ownership split: `trains` as the list of `Train.to_dict()` payloads, `controllers_by_train_id` as a map `train_id -> TrainController.to_dict()`, and `dispatcher_progress_by_train_id` as a map `train_id -> {path_id, status, current_resource_index, authority_end_block_id, requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction, waiting_for_block_id, resource_sequence}` resolved from `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)` (each `resource_sequence` entry carries `sequence_index, resource_type, resource_id, entry_edge, exit_edge`; `waiting_for_block_id` is the block the train is held waiting on for the "show stuck" overlay, else `None`). Other detail endpoints may present the same owned data in a different wrapper, but they must preserve this ownership split and must not merge controller-owned or dispatcher-owned live fields back into `Train.to_dict()`.

**`Train.to_dict()` payload**

- `to_dict()` should expose all canonical fields owned by the `Train` entity for convenience.
- Export only persistent/configuration train state from `Train.to_dict()`, because those are the fields owned by `Train`.
- `Train.to_dict()` exports only the persistent/configuration fields owned by `Train`. Live physical/control diagnostics (position, span, speed, state, target speed, ATO info, lookahead, path/destination IDs, reserved blocks) are not in this payload — they live on the controller payload.
- Read live operational diagnostics from `TrainController.to_dict()` instead.

**`TrainController.to_dict()` payload**

- `to_dict()` should expose all canonical fields owned by the `TrainController` entity for convenience.
- Export all live operational state, including `handle_position`, `manual.manual_mode_type`, and `manual.manual_power_percent`.
- Export live physical state from the controller-owned storage, including `current_span` and `current_speed_kmh`, for UI/debug consumers that previously read position or occupied tiles from `Train.to_dict()`.
- Export ATO state using `ato_destination_block_id` derived from `received_path_plan.authority_end_block_id`.
- Export `received_path_plan` when present using the canonical **received_path_plan schema**. This is the controller/UI projection of active dispatcher authority, and its nested authority geometry must continue to follow the canonical **resource_sequence entry schema** and **path_tile entry schema**.
- Do not make `TrainController.to_dict()` the owner of live path-progress state in v1. `PathDispatcher` remains the sole owner of `current_resource_index`; if UI needs that progress projection, expose it through a dispatcher-owned read model such as `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)` (the full projection is listed above in this section), resolved from the dispatcher's own active-path registry for that `train_id`, rather than copying ownership into `TrainController` or writing per-tick sequence-index updates into the controller.
- UI/debug consumers that need active authority geometry should read `received_path_plan.resource_sequence`; consumers that need current physical occupancy should read `TrainController.get_current_span()`.
- Expose the full canonical lookahead payload as the single source of truth for current target speed, ATC limit, speed-limit reason, and forward curve rendering.

**UI data-flow anchor**

- Treat controller payload as the source of truth for live operational/manual/physical state, and train payload as the source for persistent/configuration state.
- A UI asking "what is the train's target speed right now?" should call `controller.get_lookahead()` and sample at distance `0`.

**Signals (display-only)**

- Signals are display-only UI state: `RailwayNetwork.update_signals_from_blocks()` produces aspect values for the UI once per tick after dispatcher feedback (see the **Per-train tick transaction**), and no controller or physics layer consumes them as a speed restriction. The CAUTION speed restriction is owned by `TrainController.CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` through the lookahead / ATC path (see **TrainController**, ATC speed limit).

## End-To-End ATO Path Run

**End-to-end path-authority projection**

- `PathDispatcher` stores the active dispatcher-owned `Path`; `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` reads the canonical **received_path_plan schema** projection from that active record. `controller.assigned_path_id` is a derived read of that projection's `path_id`.
- `RouteExecutor` should ensure the train's `TrainController` exists before path authority is created or extended in Phase 1.
- `RouteExecutor` should not write `assigned_path_id` or `ato_destination_block_id` directly.

**End-to-end ATO run**

This is a high-level walk-through; canonical contracts live in the relevant sections (see **Active-path completion procedure**, **Planning Kernel**, **Route-run state**, **Phase Table**).

1. Phase 1: covered-task index advance, resolve the next task via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to get one concrete `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)`, and call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` (or `extend_path(...)` if a live path exists), using `acquisition_mode = route.acquisition_mode`. If a resolved pass-through (`waypoint`) target block is already covered, the index advance skips it; if a resolved stop target block is already covered, the stop remains the current target and uses ordinary same-block stop authority/completion.
2. Phase 2: controller lookahead / ATC / ATO command generation, then physics integrates and emits boundary-by-boundary `PhysicsSpanUpdate` callbacks.
3. Phase 3: drain and clear the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`; movement-derived cancellation commits inline); run the post-physics usable-authority stop check; on success call `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` per the **Active-path completion procedure**; then `pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, and `pending_route_deactivate` in the canonical order. `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption (and any latch that depends on it via the inclusive-latch rule) follows the gating in the **Latch Table** row `c832`. (Dwell-complete, route-deviation cleanup, and terminal route completion are Phase 1 steps on the next eligible tick, not Phase 3.)
4. Best-effort retries call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on the same `path_id` until the live authority reaches the requested target. `blocked_no_growth` keeps the live path; structural `failure_kind` (`no_viable_path`, `invalid_reverse`) latches cancel and triggers cancel-and-replan.
5. Stop arrival advances the stop task via `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` (which writes its `finished_at`). The next tick's Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` transitions the controller to `RUNNING_AT_STATION` automatically when the route's next task is `dwell`. The Phase 1 dwell-complete step checks `RouteTask.should_depart(...)`, then calls `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` for the dwell row (writing its `finished_at` and advancing the derived `current_waypoint_index`), then calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()`. When `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` then returns `None`, Phase 1 calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` per the terminal route completion contract (state `FINISHED`, the run preserved as a historical row); otherwise it leaves the next target to a later Phase 1.

**End-to-end manual-arrival note**

- Manual trains use the same dispatcher-committed arrival and Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` as ATO trains. The only difference is departure behavior: in manual mode `TrainController.depart()` marks the current authority ready to go and does not call `TrainController.ato.go()` or start movement by itself.

**End-to-end post-EB restart note**

- EB recovery follows the canonical **EB workflow state machine** defined in the **TrainController** section. The pre-EB path is never reused; after EB release, the next eligible Phase 1 creates fresh authority and calls `TrainController.depart()` directly per the canonical depart contract.

## Logic Review Checklist

**Review checklist**

- Is the controller reading the canonical **received_path_plan schema** projection when path-end stopping matters?
- Does each `resource_sequence` entry carry the ordered `path_tiles` slice using the canonical **path_tile entry schema**, with explicit `entry_edge` / `exit_edge` on both the resource entry and each nested tile entry, needed for controller lookahead and UI geometry?
- Is controller lookahead the only source of target speed and speed-limit reason?
- In manual mode, does ATC enforce the end of the assigned path as a hard stop through `_calculate_authority_boundary_limit()`?
- Can any path-complete event still fire while the train is braking or creeping toward the terminal authority end, instead of waiting for the post-physics controller stop check to say the train is stopped at the usable end of live authority and `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` to verify that the active authority reaches the requested target before committing completion?
- Does `_ato_calculate_handle()` read only the current tick's canonical lookahead payload, not stale scalar fields?
- Does `RouteExecutor` read controller state rather than mirrored runtime state on `Train`?
- Does `RouteExecutor` resolve waypoint-authored block-relative direction preferences into one concrete `requested_destination_direction` before calling `PathDispatcher.create_path()` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path()`, without relying on platform-level direction fields?
- Do `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` use the same shared pathfinder/current-span workflow, including the same deterministic tie-break, for start-side and platform-end selection?
- Do `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` obtain head/tail/current-span data only through `TrainController.get_current_span()`, with `TrainPhysics` remaining the movement-update producer and `TrainController` remaining the writer for controller-owned span state?
- For a pass-through waypoint whose resolved target block is already physically covered, does Phase 1's covered-task index advance skip it, while a stop task whose block is already covered remains the current target and uses ordinary same-block stop authority/completion plus dwell exit?
- Does `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` fail with `failure_kind = invalid_request` for a requested destination block the train's span already covers, leaving covered stops to Phase 1's covered-task index advance?
- Are repeated resources rejected during both `create_path()` and `extend_path()`?
- In `best_effort` mode, does the system keep one live `path_id`, extend `resource_sequence` in place, and avoid treating a truncated-but-live authority as a creation failure?
- Does `best_effort` creation fail instead of storing a live authority when the head is on a switch and no forward block can be acquired?
- Does `best_effort` truncation hold the live authority one block back instead of stopping it on a two-way block entered straight off a switch (the junction-fouling exclusion), while leaving one-way blocks — and two-way blocks in `CAUTION` — exempt so trains may pack tight up to them?
- Do dispatcher progress/completion/release callbacks all identify active authority by `train_id` rather than by caller-supplied `path_id`, with `PathDispatcher` resolving active progress from its own database rather than from physics or train mirrors, while delayed release of a still-occupied block or switch resolves from the resource itself after authority release?
- Are multi-train routes using per-train `RouteRun` objects as the only canonical progress store, with no route-level runtime progress summary fields?
- Does Phase 1's covered-task index advance loop resolve `PlatformTarget` and `StationTarget` task targets as needed, advance past already-covered `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks, and avoid advancing past `task_kind == "stop"` tasks solely because the target block is covered?
- On active-run plan replacement (operator retargeting and route swap follow abort + new assignment, never a `route_id` rewrite) and on live-path target re-resolution, does `RouteExecutor` clear the affected `path_cancelled` handoff state through the canonical write contract and replan from the train's current physical position without synthetic progress jumps, while keeping target identity in `task_runs[i].resolved_target` rather than in `current_route_status`, and without keeping any stored planning cursor outside `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`?
- Does stop completion require the post-physics usable-authority stop check, and do controller deviation cases produce `path_cancellation` with `initiator = "deviation"` and the structural distinction carried on the event payload's `deviation_kind` field?
- Does the normal `CAUTION -> FREE` reevaluation require either (a) the forward neighbour (reached by the connectivity walk from `block.caution.exit_edge`) to be non-`OCCUPIED`, or (b) the `OCCUPIED` forward neighbour to hold a train whose current `travel_direction` points back out through the chain-entry edge of its block (cab-flip case), while still allowing the explicit completion-time `force` exception in `release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)`? (`occupied_by` is always `None` in `CAUTION` — guaranteed by `on_block_tail_vacated()` clearing it — so a separate non-occupied check on the CAUTION block itself is vacuous. `reserved_by_path` is explicitly not a gating condition; if `block.caution` has cleared but `reserved_by_path` remains set, the computed block state is `RESERVED`, not `FREE`.)
- Can any completion or cancellation path still unlock a block or switch that the train physically occupies at full stop?
- After `TrainController.eb.release()`, does the system return to the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection) and restart through fresh path creation followed by a Phase 1 `TrainController.depart()` call per the canonical depart contract?
- Do train-owned flank-protection claims record `source_switch_id` so tail-vacate can release only the protection created from the vacated main switch?

**Notes**

- Ordinary switch release happens when the train tail vacates the switch tile, not on full path completion. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` reports the vacated resource in `tail_vacated_resources`, and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` releases it through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(..., route)`: blocks go through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)`, while switches go through `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)`, which removes the matching `Switch.physical_occupants[route]` entry first and then, only when no entries remain for that train, releases train-owned protection sourced from that vacated switch through `Switch.release_train_protection_from(...)`. The neighbour-side `source_switch_id` filter protects claims created by other occupied main switches. Both completion and cancellation must respect physical occupancy: if a switch is still physically occupied, its train-owned protection stays in place until the later tail-vacate event.
- `assigned_path_id` moves from `Train` to `TrainController` as a read-only derived property of `received_path_plan.path_id`. The underlying `received_path_plan` is itself a read-only projection over `self.simulation_controller.path_dispatcher.get_active_path(train_id)` (which derives over `Path.status == ACTIVE`), so updates and releases surface automatically without any controller-side write step.
